# Body Contact and Battles

> **Rule set:** Written to NHL rules. Where IIHF, USA Hockey, Hockey Canada or typical rec-league
> rules differ in a way that changes how you play, it is flagged inline. This document contains more
> of those flags than any other in this guide, because **whether body checking is legal at all
> depends entirely on which league you are in.** **Playing in Britain? The IIHF flags are yours** —
> every level of UK hockey runs the IIHF book, amended locally; see
> [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md), and read **"If you play in Britain"** in §1
> before you play a shift.
>
> **Related:** [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md) · [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md) · [Skating](skating.md) · [Puck Handling](puck_handling.md) · [Defender](../positions/defender.md) · [Risk Management](../hockey-iq/risk_management.md) · [Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md)

## Which book you are under decides what is legal — and the boards do not care

Two of the seven ideas in [Core Principles](../foundation/core_principles.md) govern this page, and they answer opposite halves of it. **Which book you are under, and where you sit inside it, decides what is legal for you.** Your age group, your league and the country whose rule book they play settle whether you may body check or be body checked; nothing visible on the ice tells you which, so ask before your first game rather than after your first hit. **The boards do not care which book you are under, or how fast you are going.** How you meet a wall is decided several strides out, in a non-checking league exactly as in a checking one, because a wall does not need an opponent to hurt you. Core Principles treats each of the two as a principle in its own right, with a worked example and the evidence behind it — [which book you are under](../foundation/core_principles.md#2-which-book-you-are-under-and-where-you-sit-inside-it-decides-what-is-legal) and [choosing how you meet the boards](../foundation/core_principles.md#7-choose-how-you-meet-the-boards-before-you-have-to).

## Key focus

**Find out what your league allows before you play, because body *contact* and a body *check* are not the same act, and only one of them is legal in almost every league.** Body contact — two players leaning on each other while both chase the puck — is legal in every league bar one: the CARHA adult book, whose Rule 49(a) reaches deliberate bodying, pushing and shoving. A body check is a different act that many leagues ban outright: USA Hockey bars it at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's classifications and in all non-check adult classifications; Hockey Canada's ban reaches U13 and below, throughout female hockey, and any division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved as non-check; and the IIHF rulebook, which Britain plays under, permits only a restricted form instead, for women's hockey. **If you play in Britain, neither rule book states an age at all** — the answer is in your competition's Rules of Competition, and [Which Contact Rules Are You Actually Playing Under?](#1-which-contact-rules-are-you-actually-playing-under) sets out the four IHUK regulations read here, what they settle, and the British leagues they do not reach. ⚠️ **Asking your own league is the only test that works: get the answer in writing, and until you have it, do not hit anyone.**

**When the wall is coming and you cannot avoid it, meet it with your skates parallel to the boards and take the contact on your forearm and hip, head up and chin off your chest.** Never turn your back to the wall yourself, however protective that feels, and never duck — a back turned makes the contact a hit from behind, and a chin tucked to your chest straightens the cervical spine, so a vertebra can fracture at walking speed. Both prohibitions are absolute in every league, checking or not, because a wall does not need an opponent to hurt you.

**Never hit a player who cannot see you coming, and chase a beaten carrier with your stick and your feet rather than your body.** Checking from behind has no free tier in any of the six rule books read for this document: the NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL write no minor option at all, so the lightest call available there is a major with an automatic game misconduct; USA Hockey's floor is a minor plus a misconduct, the only rung in the six that leaves you on the ice; and CARHA's and Hockey Canada's floors each pair a minor with a *game* misconduct, so even their cheapest version ends your night. Play it as though the onus for avoiding it were absolute — USA Hockey's and Hockey Canada's own rules are written that way, and the NHL's states no onus at all.

**Take the puck with your route and your stick, not with your body — angling and stick checks are legal in every league, and they are how good defenders actually take pucks.** Angling is steering an opponent to where you want them by choosing your skating route rather than by hitting them: pick your line early, hold it, and add nothing once you arrive — an arm, a shoulder or a hip extended after the angle is what turns it back into a check. Veering across into somebody at the last instant to make the contact happen is not steering either, however gentle the contact turns out to be. An angle has to start before you need it, because a gap cannot be closed from a standstill. On the stick, the rule most players are taught holds up: stick on the puck is legal, stick on their hands or their body is a penalty.

**You are allowed the ice you are standing on, so you can defend a whole shift without ever making a deliberate hit.** **In a CARHA adult league that entitlement is narrower — Rule 49(a) reaches a player who stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, so hold a spot you already took and do not lean or body to keep it.** You are not required to move to let an opponent past; you may block the path of an opponent you are in front of and moving in the same direction as; and you may use your body position to lengthen their route to the puck — provided your stick is not used to make you bigger, your free hand stays off them, and you do not take advantage of that position to deliver an otherwise illegal check. Standing in the place your opponent needs to be is a complete defensive act.

**Arrive at a battle with position rather than just with speed, and get low and wide when you get there.** Whoever arrives first decides where the battle happens, but arriving first with your body in the wrong place is worse than arriving second with it in the right one — so land with your body between the opponent and the puck and the wall on the far side. Then feet wider than your shoulders, knees deeply bent, backside low, chest up: a centre of gravity below your opponent's is very hard to move, and a low wide stance can still shuffle, pivot and step out where a stiff-legged one can only fall over. Leverage often beats strength — though not against a large enough strength gap, and that is coaching consensus rather than a measured law.

**If a player may have hurt their head or neck, nobody moves and somebody phones, and their helmet stays on — no exception.** Do not help them up.

## Overview

Most hockey games are decided not by pretty passing plays but by who wins the scrappy, three-second contests for a loose puck along the wall, in the corner and at the front of the net — the coaching account of where games are won rather than anything counted. This document is about winning those without getting hurt, ejected or penalised.

**Find out what your league allows before you play.** Body *contact* — two players leaning on each other while both chase the same puck — is legal everywhere bar one book: CARHA's adult rules penalise deliberate bodying, pushing and shoving, and §1 sets out what that leaves you. A body *check*, deliberately using your body to separate an opponent from the puck, is a different act that many leagues ban outright: younger age groups, any adult classification a local association has made non-check, and all girls' and women's play under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada — though the IIHF rulebook, which Britain plays under, permits a restricted form instead. **If you play in Britain, the answer is not in either rule book but in your competition's Rules of Competition** — four of IHUK's were read here, covering junior, U10, NIHL Division 1 and 2 and the WNIHL, and they settle nothing for the EIHL, BUIHA, NIHL National, the PNIHL, the SNL or Scottish domestic hockey outside the IHUK junior structure. ⚠️ **Nor does asking whether IHUK runs your league answer it**, because IHUK's In-House Rules reach NIHL National, the SNL and BUIHA Varsity and none of those four documents does — so ask your league, get it in writing, and until you have an answer, do not hit anyone. And "non-check" does not mean "non-contact" — USA Hockey says *"Non-check hockey does not mean no contact"*.

**Nearly all of this is legal wherever you play.** *Angling* — steering an opponent by the route you skate rather than by hitting them — is legal everywhere. So is the *stick lift*: your blade under their stick, **below their bottom hand**, lifting their blade off the ice. **Take it low in every book**, because the books diverge above the hands and a lift taken under the shaft is outside every one of them. A clean lift for the puck is not a chop, and **a chop at the hands is never the cheaper option**: in every one of the six rule books read here it can reach a **major plus a game misconduct with nobody injured** (NHL Rule 61.3 with 61.5). Which book reaches the hands through hooking and which through slashing, and which of them needs an injury before a hooking major, is in [Stick Checks](#3-stick-checks--legal-effective-and-under-used). Three things here are restricted, and §1 sets them out: the body check, the net-front walk-out, and the pin in women's hockey.

**Both sides of a collision have a rule that does not bend.** Never hit a player from behind, never hit one turned toward the wall, and never make contact with an opponent's head, face or neck. Checking from behind has no lesser option under the NHL's rulebook, the IIHF's and the PWHL's, where the lightest call available is a major with an automatic game misconduct, and the onus is on the player delivering it — USA Hockey's Rule 608 and Hockey Canada's rule say so in those words, while the NHL's checking-from-behind rule states no onus at all. **Play it as though the onus were absolute.** Where a book does soften it, that softening is written into its *boarding* rule rather than into the rule against checking from behind, and [Checking from behind](#checking-from-behind-and-what-the-onus-does-and-does-not-mean) sets out which books write it and which do not. **The head is not a matter of degree either, and a player facing you is not a licence to hit high** — IIHF Rule 48.1: *"There is no clean check to the head or neck… This rule supersedes all similar actions regarding hits to the head and neck."* USA Hockey Rule 620 reaches any contact to the head, face or neck with any part of the body, equipment or stick, and its Standard of Play puts the onus on the checker *"regardless as to size differential, to not make contact in the head/neck area of the opponent."* And never turn your back to the wall yourself, however protective that feels, and never duck. Head up, chin off your chest — and when the wall is coming and you cannot avoid it, meet it with your skates parallel to the boards and take the contact on your forearm and hip. **The turned back and the tucked chin are two prohibitions against two different injuries.** A back turned makes the contact a hit from behind, and USA Hockey's warning on those is that *"Even a light hit from behind could inflict a severe head or neck injury."* A chin tucked to the chest straightens the cervical spine, and it is the **injury** from that head-down, head-first collision with the boards or a goal post — not the turn — that USA Hockey says *"can occur at walking speed."* Turning into a hit deliberately to draw the call earns you nothing either: the NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL all waive the checking-from-behind penalty when the turn was intentional, and the IIHF adds that other penalties may still be assessed.

**If a player may have hurt their head or neck, nobody moves and somebody phones, and their helmet stays on — no exception.** Do not help them up.

---

## 1. Which Contact Rules Are You Actually Playing Under?

Before you read another word, find out which category your league falls into — and then check the game in front of you, because the two can come apart: under **USA Hockey Rule 604(b)**, a checking team playing a non-checking one is in a no-checking game whatever its own classification says. Getting this wrong is how good players end up in the penalty box, and how careless players hurt someone.

### The two categories

```facts
Key: Body contact and body checking are not the same thing — one is legal in every league, though the CARHA adult book penalises the deliberate kind; the other only in specific classifications
Rule: Hockey Canada defines body contact as incidental contact of two opposing players pursuing the puck in the same direction — no penalty
Rule: USA Hockey defines a body check as intentional physical contact by a skater to an opponent in control of the puck
Rule: Hockey Canada's mechanical test for a check — opposite-directional force, leaving your skating lane, or extending an arm, shoulder or hip after angling
Key: Body contact is legal at every age in every league but CARHA's adult book, which penalises deliberate bodying, pushing or shoving; body checking is legal only in specific classifications
```

**Body contact** is incidental or positional contact between two players who are both playing the puck — leaning on each other, rubbing shoulders as you skate the same direction, boxing someone out at the net front, sealing a player off along the wall. Hockey Canada's rulebook defines body contact as "incidental contact of two opposing players in pursuit of the puck on the ice in the same direction" and states plainly that no penalty is assessed for it. **Body contact is legal in every league, at every age, in every division — with one published exception.** If your league runs the CARHA adult book, Rule 49(a) reaches a player who intentionally bodies, pushes or shoves an opponent, so deliberate leaning is not free there; the rule is set out in full [below](#where-body-checking-is-and-is-not-permitted).

**Body checking** is a deliberate act: you use your body to separate an opponent from the puck. USA Hockey defines a body check as "intentional physical contact, from the front, diagonally from the front or straight from the side, by a skater to an opponent who is in control of the puck." Hockey Canada adds the mechanical test — body checking occurs "when a player makes deliberate contact with an opposing player with opposite-directional force, when a player leaves their skating lane to make contact, or when a player extends an arm, shoulder, or hip after angling an opponent." **Body checking is legal only in specific classifications.**

### Where body checking is and is not permitted

```facts
Rule: USA Hockey Rule 604(a) bans body checking at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's classifications and in all non-check adult classifications — and the same rule lets a local governing body prohibit it in any classification, so check what your own association has decided
Rule: Hockey Canada bans body checking at U13 and below, throughout female hockey, and in "any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada" — so being above the U13 line does not settle it (Hockey Canada Rule 7.3)
Rule: When a checking team plays a non-checking team, body checking is prohibited outright (USA Hockey Rule 604(b))
Rule: A third USA Hockey prohibition sits outside Rule 604 — body checking is "prohibited during all Blind Hockey games", penalised "under the guidelines within Rule 604", with no age or classification limb, so it reaches adult rosters the 604(a) list does not
Never: Assume your league permits checking — USA Hockey Rule 604(a) lets a local governing body prohibit it in any classification, so ask your own association and get the answer in writing
Rule: CARHA's book has no checking division at all — 49(a) makes a body check a minor or a major anywhere on the ice, for intentionally bodying, pushing or shoving an opponent, and a major where the contact injures (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: CARHA 49(c) assesses no penalty for accidental contact, so the line there is intent rather than touch — but 49(a) also reaches a player who "does not avert body contact with an opponent" (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: CARHA's accident defence stops at the head — 50(a) is a minor for accidentally contacting an opponent in the head, face or neck with any part of the body or equipment other than a stick (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: A CARHA major for a body check ends your night — Rule 30(a) rules it off the ice for the rest of the game and Rule 32(d) adds an automatic one-game suspension (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: CARHA Rule 32(a) ejects you on three penalties in a game — its Note 1 tallies any combination of minors, bench minors and majors, and Rule 49(b)'s Note counts a boarding double minor as two of the three, so boarding plus one more minor is your night (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Key: "Non-check" does not mean "non-contact" — USA Hockey says a Competitive Contact game can be very physical
```

| League / level | Body checking? | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **NHL** | Yes | NHL rulebook |
| **USA Hockey — 14U and older boys'/men's check classifications** | Yes, **unless** your local governing body has prohibited it — 604(a) lets it do that in any classification | USA Hockey Rule 604(a) |
| **USA Hockey — 12U and below, all Girls'/Women's classifications, and all non-check Adult classifications** | **No** | USA Hockey Rule 604(a) |
| **Hockey Canada — U13 and below** | **No** | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 |
| **Hockey Canada — female hockey** | **No** | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 |
| **Hockey Canada — any division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved** | **No** | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 (the rulebook does not list which. Ask your league) |
| **Hockey Canada — U15 and above (male)** | Yes, **unless** your division is one of those Member-approved above | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 |
| **IIHF women's international** | Restricted — see below | IIHF Rule 101.1 |
| **Women's hockey in Britain — the rule-book layer** | Restricted, same as IIHF; your league may go further, and WNIHL does | IIHF Rule 101.1, unamended by the In-House Rules |
| **PWHL** | Restricted — see below | PWHL Rule 52.1 |
| **British hockey — the four IHUK regulations read here** | **Junior U10 and U12 no, U14 and above yes; NIHL Division 1 and 2 yes; WNIHL no** — set by the competition regulations, since neither rule book states an age | IHUK Rules of Competition 2026-27 |
| **Adult rec / beer leagues** | **Ask your league** — your association may have barred it whatever the age list says | USA Hockey Rule 604(a); Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 |

Six things that catch people out:

- **Under Hockey Canada, being above the U13 line does not mean you are in a checking division.** Rule 7.3 has a third limb people miss: it applies "only in divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey, **and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada**." The rulebook does not say which divisions those are, so an adult or U15 division can be non-check by approval and you will not find it in the playing rules. Ask your league.
- **A local governing body can ban checking in any classification — including one your age would otherwise put you in.** The sentence immediately after the 12U / girls' / non-check-adult list in **USA Hockey Rule 604(a)** reads: *"A local governing body may prohibit body checking in any classification and is encouraged to provide Competitive Contact options at all levels of recreational play."* So clearing 14U tells you what the national rule permits; it does not tell you what your own association has done with that permission, and the association's decision is the one the officials will enforce. **And being an adult in a checking country does not put you in a checking class by default.** USA Hockey's own Glossary, under *Age Classifications*, names them: *"Adult classifications shall include Adult non-check, Adult U.S., Adult Elite and Adult Women"*. One of those four is non-check in its name, and another — Adult Women — is non-check under 604(a)'s girls'/women's limb. Adult recreational hockey is widely played in non-check classifications, but **that is a general impression of how the adult game is organised rather than a measured share**, and how widely is not something a rulebook can tell you: 604(a) *encourages* local bodies to provide Competitive Contact options and does not record which ones did. Do not assume — ask, and get it in writing.
- **When a checking team plays a non-checking team, nobody checks — and it is not the coaches' call.** USA Hockey Rule 604(b) prohibits body checking outright in that game, and the Casebook puts exactly that question — a non-league fixture where *"[b]oth coaches agree to play the game under the Body Checking category rules"* — and answers *"No"*: *"Coaches do not have the authority to decide what rules they choose to play under on any given day"* (Rule 604 Situation 1).
- **One USA Hockey prohibition sits outside Rule 604 altogether, and it is the one that reaches adults.** It is in the book's *Disabled Hockey* section rather than its playing rules: *"Body checking is prohibited during all Blind Hockey games. Body checking shall be penalized under the guidelines within Rule 604 in the Official Rules of Ice Hockey."* It carries **no age or classification limb** at all, and the same section provides that a Blind Hockey roster *"can comprise all youth-aged players (players under 18), **all adult-aged players (18 and older)**, or mixed youth and adult-aged players"* — so it reaches adult rosters the 604(a) list does not. **Check the game, not the age group.** *(Only the Blind Hockey body-checking provision is described here; the Sled Hockey rules carry a separate Non-Contact Player provision this document does not cover)*.
- **"Non-check" does not mean "non-contact."** USA Hockey states it in as many words: "Non-check hockey does not mean no contact and the Competitive Contact Category game can be very physical."
- **One published adult book answers the question for you, and its answer is no.** If your league runs the **CARHA Hockey** rule book — **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, and no reader in minor hockey** — you are not in a checking division, because that book has none. **Rule 49(a)** assesses *"[a] Minor or Major penalty, at the discretion of the Referee, based upon the intent and the violence of the impact with the opposing player… to any player who intentionally bodies, pushes, shoves, stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent. When injury results from intentional body contact a Major penalty shall be assessed."* The book's own case situation prices a bodycheck at **centre ice** — nowhere near the boards, nobody hurt — at a minor or a major, and one **into the boards** at a double minor under **49(b)**, a major if the player is injured, and a match penalty if the referee judges the incident serious. Its referee's-signal page prints a single signal for *"Boarding and Body Checking"*, and **49(d)** adds a minor or a major for deliberate physical contact after the whistle. ⚠️ **What that book does not ban is contact as such** — **49(c)** provides that *"[w]here, in the opinion of the Referee, accidental contact has taken place, no penalty shall be assessed"*, so the line it draws is intent rather than touch, and battling for a loose puck is not what it is aimed at. **That defence stops at the head, though, so keep your hands, arms and shoulders below an opponent's collar even in a scramble you did not start:** **Rule 50(a)** assesses a minor to a player who *"accidentally contacts an opponent in the head, face or neck with any part of the player’s body or equipment other than a stick"* — accidentally, with no intent limb at all, where 49(c) excuses accidental body contact outright. *(Contact made with a stick goes to Rule 62 instead, by Rule 50's own Note.)* But read 49(a)'s third limb before you decide how much room that leaves: it reaches a player who *"does not avert body contact with an opponent"*, which is a duty to get out of the way rather than merely a ban on initiating. *(That the third limb is a duty is a reading of the rule's grammar; the book does not gloss it, and 49(c) is what keeps genuinely accidental contact outside it.)* ⚠️ **And a major there is the end of your night, plus the next game:** **Rule 30(a)** rules any player assessed a major *"off the ice for the remainder of the game (Major penalty plus Game Misconduct)"* — its only exception in the whole book is an accidental high stick — and **Rule 32(d)** adds an automatic one-game suspension to that game misconduct. **Rule 32(a)** ejects you from the game on **three penalties in one game**, and its Note 1 counts *"[a]ny combination of minor, bench minor, or major penalties"* toward a player's personal total, so in a book where every intentional bump is a minor, three of them is your night. ⚠️ **And a double minor there is two of the three, not one** — the Note under **Rule 49(b)**, the boarding conversion §5 sets out, makes it two *"penalties in the application of the Three Penalty Rule"*, so a boarding call plus one more minor ends your night in two rather than three. CARHA binds CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only and is not a general rec-hockey standard; it is set out here because it is a published answer to the question this section asks, in the kind of league many adult readers actually play in. Ask your league whether it is your book.

### If you play in Britain

```facts
Rule: Your book is the IIHF Rule Book, amended by the In-House Rules — the USA Hockey and Hockey Canada rows above are not yours
Rule: Neither the IIHF Rule Book nor the In-House Rules states an age at which body checking becomes legal — your competition's Rules of Competition carry it instead, and only four of IHUK's were read here: junior, U10, NIHL Division 1 and 2 and the WNIHL
Rule: IHUK's Rules of Competition 2026-27 set the format: junior U10 and U12 "non-checking" and U14, U16 and U19 "checking"; NIHL Division 1 and 2 "checking"; WNIHL "non-checking" at U16 and senior — and the U10 document adds, in terms, "Bodychecking is NOT allowed in U10 matches"
Never: Assume Britain mirrors USA Hockey's 12U or Hockey Canada's U13 — the British junior line falls between U12 and U14, and it comes from a competition regulation, not from either rule book
Action: Get your own division's contact status in writing from your league, and until you have an answer, do not hit anyone
Never: Settle it by asking whether IHUK runs your league — IHUK's In-House Rules reach NIHL National, the SNL and BUIHA Varsity, and no Rules of Competition for any of them was read for this guide
Never: Read IHUK's junior, U10, NIHL 1 and 2 and WNIHL Rules of Competition as settling the EIHL, BUIHA, NIHL National, the PNIHL, the SNL or Scottish domestic hockey outside the IHUK junior structure
Rule: British junior categories are U19/U16/U14/U12/U10 by birth year and unisex, "not classified as 'Mens' within the UK" (In-House Rules, Rule 200)
Rule: In women's hockey under EIH, Adult runs from the 14th birthday and Under 16 from the 10th (In-House Rules, Rule 100.1)
Rule: In British women's hockey at every age, adult included, any major penalty carries an automatic game misconduct and ejection — In-House Rule 101's ejection bullet applies "in all cases" and states no age scope (In-House Rules, Rule 101)
Rule: The age-scoped half of In-House Rule 101 is its other bullet, which applies IIHF Rule 201.1 to non-adult women's categories — it does not narrow the ejection bullet beside it
Key: Read junior hockey the same way at every age, U19 to U10 — In-House Rule 201 scopes the IIHF junior rules to all junior groups without naming 201.1, so treat a major as ending the game
```

Every age banding for body checking just set out is North American — they are USA Hockey's and Hockey Canada's — and none of them transfers to Britain. Of the levels listed, the IIHF one and the British ones are written for you; the rest are not. **Britain does have an answer, but it is in a different kind of document**, and finding it means looking past the rule book. Here is what maps, what does not, and where the British answer actually is.

**Your book is the IIHF Rule Book**, adopted by Ice Hockey UK and the BUIHA as the foundation of the game *"in England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland (the Home Countries)"*, with the **In-House Rules** sitting on top of it as amendments. (The superseded 2025-26 edition reached England and Scotland only.) So the IIHF rows and flags in this document — Rule 43 on checking from behind, Rule 42 on charging, Rule 48 on head contact, Rule 101.1 on women's hockey — are describing your game. The USA Hockey and Hockey Canada rows are not. See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md) for the full amendment list.

> **Neither rule book states an age at which body checking becomes legal in Britain, and this document will not invent one — the answer lives in a competition's own *Rules of Competition* instead, and four of IHUK's were read for this document.** ⚠️ **That is not a promise that one exists for your league**: four is all that was read, and for anything they do not cover, this document has no answer and neither do the rule books.
>
> The **IIHF Rule Book** contains no body-checking age restriction at all. It defines a legal body check in its glossary — *"a Player checks an opponent who is in possession of the puck, by using their hip or body from the front, diagonally from the front or straight from the side… only with the trunk of the body (hips and shoulders) and must be above the opponent's knees and at or below the shoulders"* — and restricts checking in exactly one place: **Rule 101.1**, in women's hockey. Its junior section (**Rules 200–202**) covers age eligibility for the U20 and U18 World Championships, facial protection, and one playing rule — **201.1**, that any major penalty in men's junior also brings an automatic game misconduct. **There is nothing about contact.**
>
> The **In-House Rules** do not add one. Their junior section (**Rules 200–201**) sets the British age categories and then says, in its entirety, that the IIHF junior playing rules *"apply to all junior age groups as defined in Rule 200 of the in-house rules."* Their women's section (**Rules 100–102**) sets the age boundaries, applies IIHF Rule 201.1 to *"all non-adult age categories of Women's Hockey"*, ejects any player assessed a major *"in all cases"* — that one carries no age scope — and restricts half visors. **Neither section mentions body checking, body contact, or non-contact play — and nor does any other section of the document.**
>
> **So do not assume Britain mirrors USA Hockey's 12U line or Hockey Canada's U13 line — the British line is in a different place, and it is not in either rule book.** **Where the answer lives is your competition's own Rules of Competition**, a document separate from the playing rules — and four of IHUK's, the junior, U10, NIHL 1 and 2 and WNIHL editions for 2026-27, put the answer in a table. Those four were read for this document, and they are the whole of the answer given here.
>
> **For IHUK junior hockey**, the *Junior Rules of Competition 2026-27* set the format for every age group: **U10** *"Cross-ice jamboree, non-checking"*, **U12** *"Full ice, non-checking, running clock"*, and **U14**, **U16** and **U19** each *"Full ice, checking, stop clock"*. So the British junior line falls **between U12 and U14** — a year later than USA Hockey's and a year earlier than Hockey Canada's, which is exactly why guessing from either was never going to land. The separate *U10 Rules of Competition 2026-27* says it in terms rather than by format label: *"Bodychecking is NOT allowed in U10 matches."* Those leagues are *"solely owned and operated by IHUK and operate between U10s through to U19s."*
>
> **In the senior game the league decides it, not your age.** The *NIHL 1 and 2 Rules of Competition 2026-27* sets NIHL Division 1 and 2 as *"Full ice, checking, stop clock"*. The *WNIHL Rules of Competition 2026-27* sets **both** WNIHL U16 and senior WNIHL as *"Full ice, non-checking, stop clock"* — a restriction tighter than IIHF Rule 101.1, which permits a limited form of bodychecking in women's hockey; where the two differ, plan around the tighter one. ⚠️ **And being a woman does not by itself put you in a non-checking game**: the NIHL document contemplates that *"[s]enior women may register WNIHL + NIHL in different clubs"*, and NIHL Division 1 and 2 is a checking format.
>
> **A competition regulation is the right place for this to live, and it does not conflict with the rule books.** Each of those documents states its own order of precedence, running from the IIHF Rule Book through the In-House Rules to the Rules of Competition itself — the WNIHL document inserts one more tier, *"IHUK Sex & Gender Participation Policy"*, between the IIHF book and the In-House Rules — and the sentence immediately above it says which end of that order wins: where the IIHF rules are supplemented or amended by the In-House Rules, *"the IHUK wording will take precedence for domestic competition."* **So it is the more local document that governs where it speaks, not the more general one.** **On body checking the precedence question does not even arise**, because there is nothing to conflict with: the IIHF book never grants a right to check — it defines what a legal check is and penalises illegal ones — and neither it nor the In-House Rules mentions checking by age at all. A competition declaring its games non-checking is adding a restriction to a silence, not overriding a permission.
>
> **What this does not settle, and it is a real gap rather than a formality.** **Four *Rules of Competition* were read for this document — the junior, U10, NIHL Division 1 and 2 and WNIHL editions for 2026-27 — and they are the whole of the British answer this document gives.** None of them sets a checking format for the **EIHL**, for **BUIHA** university hockey, for **NIHL National** — which the NIHL 1 and 2 document names as a tier above NIHL 1 and sets a filming standard for, but no format — for the **PNIHL**, which that same document sends to its own separate national *Rules of Competition*, for the **SNL**, or for Scottish domestic competitions outside the IHUK junior structure. No regulation for any of those was read here, so nothing in this document settles whether they are checking or non-checking. ⚠️ **That is "unanswered", not "different there" — do not read either way into it.**
>
> **And do not try to settle it by asking whether IHUK runs your league, because that question has the wrong shape.** The NIHL document states that *"NIHL leagues are solely owned and operated by IHUK"*, and the junior document says the same of the junior leagues — so a **NIHL National** player who answers "IHUK runs my league" has answered correctly and still has no checking format, because the document covering their tier is not one of the four and was not read. IHUK's own In-House Rules legislate by name for NIHL National, the SNL and BUIHA Varsity, none of which those four Rules of Competition reach. The In-House Rules also note that IHUK *"may also issue Rule Bulletins from time to time"*, and a bulletin can move a line mid-season. ⚠️ **So if one of those four documents covers you, take the format from the Rules of Competition for your own season; for anything else, ask your club or league secretary and get it in writing — and until you have an answer, do not hit anyone.**

**The age bandings themselves are different, so the North American numbers do not even translate.** British junior categories are set by **birth year**, not by the North American banding used elsewhere in this document: **U19, U16, U14, U12 and U10** (In-House Rules, Rule 200). The In-House Rules add a note that matters for anyone reading a USA Hockey "boys'/men's check classification" row and looking for its British equivalent: *"Junior age categories are unisex and are not classified as 'Mens' within the UK."* There is no British *boys'* checking division to map onto — where the British game is checking, it is checking for the whole age group rather than for one sex within it. In women's hockey, **Adult runs from the player's 14th birthday** and Under 16 from the 10th to the 16th (Rule 100.1).

**One consequence to plan around, and it is not confined to the age groups:** in British **women's** hockey at **every** age, adult included, **any foul that draws a major penalty also draws an automatic game misconduct and ejection.** In-House Rule 101 carries that as a bullet of its own — *"Any player who commits a foul that is assessed a Major Penalty will, **in all cases**, also receive an additional Game Misconduct Penalty and be ejected from the game"* — and it states no age scope at all. The bullet above it in the same rule, which applies IIHF Rule 201.1 to *"all non-adult age categories of Women's Hockey"*, is a separate provision and does not narrow it: the two are siblings under a heading that reads *"Women's Hockey Specific Playing Rules"*, so the heading is the only scope the ejection bullet has. For **junior** hockey, In-House Rule 201 scopes the IIHF junior playing rules to *"all junior age groups"* without naming 201.1, so read the same consequence across U19 to U10 as an interpretation rather than the rule's own words. Either way, treat a major for charging, boarding or checking from behind as ending the game, and confirm with your league.

### Women's hockey — the nuance that is usually got wrong

```facts
Key: "Women's hockey has no hitting" is too blunt — the answer depends on which book you are under
Rule: USA Hockey and Hockey Canada prohibit body checking in all girls'/women's classifications
Rule: IIHF women's rules allow bodychecking only where there is clear intention of playing or gaining possession of the puck, and subject to the exception the rule itself then sets out (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: Two players in pursuit of the puck may push and lean into each other only while "possession of the puck" remains "the sole object" of both — leaning on after that is an illegal hit (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: The PWHL permits angling with the body to separate an opponent from the puck (PWHL Rule 52.1)
Never: Step or glide into an opponent, or move into a puck carrier trying to avoid you — that is an illegal hit
Rule: IIHF women's rules forbid using the boards to eliminate, push or pin an opponent (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: A stationary player is entitled to their ice, and a puck carrier skating directly at her must avoid contact (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: An illegal hit in women's hockey is a minor, or a major plus an automatic game misconduct (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: Britain applies IIHF Rule 101.1, the women’s bodychecking rule, unamended, though the WNIHL's Rules of Competition label both its levels non-checking; the 14th-birthday Adult threshold is in neither 101.1 nor IIHF Rule 100.1, but a domestic amendment (In-House Rules, Rule 100.1)
Rule: IIHF Rule 100.1 sets women's Senior at eighteen by 31 December of the year the Championship Season ends, with a waiver for a 16th or 17th birthday that year, and Under 18 at fifteen to eighteen — four years above the British Adult line
```

The common statement "women's hockey has no hitting" is too blunt. What is accurate:

- **USA Hockey and Hockey Canada prohibit body checking in all girls'/women's classifications.** That part is simple and absolute.
- **IIHF women's rules permit a restricted form of bodychecking — and this is the rule women's hockey in Britain plays under.** IIHF Rule 101.1 states that in women's hockey, *"'bodychecking' is allowed when there is a clear intention of playing the puck or attempting to 'gain possession' of the puck"*, and subject to the exception the rule itself then sets out. Two players in pursuit of the puck *"are reasonably allowed to push and lean into each other provided that 'possession of the puck' remains the sole object of the two"* Players. What is banned is the *hit*: *"Any move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player will be assessed at least a minor penalty (2') for an 'illegal hit'"*, and the rule provides either a minor **or** a major plus an automatic game misconduct. Three further clauses are worth knowing exactly, because they decide most real contests:
  - **The boards.** Where two or more players are competing for possession, *"they are not allowed to use the boards to make contact with an opponent to eliminate her from the play, push her into the boards, or pin her along the boards."*
  - **Standing your ground is protected.** *"A Player, who is stationary, is entitled to that area of the ice. It is up to the opponent to avoid body contact with such a Player… the opponent is obliged to skate around the stationary Player."* And: *"Players are allowed to 'hold their ground' any time that they have established their position on the ice."* Everything in Section 4 of this document is available to you.
  - **The obligation runs both ways.** *"If a Player with the puck is skating directly at an opponent who is stationary, it is the obligation of the puck carrier to 'avoid contact'."* But if the carrier makes every effort to avoid contact and the stationary player moves into her, it is the *defender* who takes at least a minor.
- **The PWHL permits the same restricted form.** PWHL Rule 52.1: "In the PWHL, a form of Bodychecking is permitted. Players may angle their opponent by using their body in order to separate her opponent from the puck." Same-direction contact that dislodges the puck is legal; "opposite-directional force," leaving your skating lane to make contact, or extending an arm, shoulder or hip *after* angling are all illegal hits.

**In Britain, Rule 101.1 is your rule as written — the In-House Rules do not amend it.** What they do amend is who counts as an adult and what a major costs: **Adult women's hockey runs from the player's 14th birthday**, Under 16 from the 10th to the 16th, and — **at every age, adult included** — **any foul assessed a major penalty also carries an automatic game misconduct and ejection** (In-House Rules, Rules 100.1 and 101, whose ejection bullet applies *"in all cases"* and states no age scope). So an illegal hit serious enough to draw the major under 101.1 ends your game whatever your age. Do not read the Adult line as an exemption from it: what the 14th birthday changes is which age category you are in, not what a major costs you.

**That 14 is British, not international — do not cite it to the IIHF.** The IIHF's own Rule 100.1 defines its women's categories only by World Championship eligibility: "Senior" needs a player at least eighteen by 31 December of the year the Championship Season ends, with a waiver route for a 16th or 17th birthday in that year, and "Under 18" runs from fifteen to eighteen on the same date. Nothing in it addresses a fourteen-year-old in a domestic league, and Rule 101.1 — the bodychecking rule itself — carries no age at all. The threshold is the In-House Rules', and it sits four years below the IIHF's Senior line, which is why it is worth knowing whose rule it is. See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).

In practice, the women's-game standard is very close to the standard for **legal angling everywhere** — which is the subject of the next section.

### Before the puck drops — the warm-up is not outside the rules

```facts
Rule: Three books penalise the pre-game warm-up — USA Hockey 502(b), "Penalties may also be assessed during the warm-up period"; Hockey Canada 2.2(j), the penalty "served at the start of the game"; CARHA 27(b), for infractions "committed during the pre-game warm-up, when observed by the Referee"
Rule: IIHF Rule 5.6's "No penalties are to be assessed but a report of the incident must be submitted to the Proper Authorities" is the enforcement clause for that rule's own terms on warm-up equipment, roster and timing — identical in both editions, and not a general amnesty for warm-up conduct
Rule: A warm-up fight is penalised on the night under the IIHF — players fighting "other than during the periods of the game shall be assessed a major penalty for fighting and an automatic game misconduct" (Rule 46.8 in the 2026/27 book; the same rule is numbered 46.9 in 2025/26 Version 1.1)
Rule: In the EIHL the warm-up is a non-contact period by rule — Casebook 86.6(i), "Physical contact between players on opposing teams is not allowed during the warmup and players are not allowed to skate over the centre red line during this time"
Rule: The EIHL Casebook reverses IIHF 5.6 for the Elite League alone — referees "are authorised to call all penalties provided for in the rule book from the start of warm-up time until 30 minutes after the end of the game, if they see the offence take place" (86.6)
Rule: An EIHL warm-up violation reaches the bench — suspension and/or fine for players, team officials and the club, and the head coach "who instigated (started) or provoked such activity" is liable too (Casebook 86.6, with 5.6(1))
Never: Put a hand on an opponent, or cross the centre red line, in the warm-up — no book here treats it as off the record, and under the IIHF a warm-up fight is a fighting major and an automatic ejection
```

**Everything else in this document is about what you may do once the game has started. The warm-up is a separate question, and the books do not answer it the same way.** It is the one stretch of the night when you share the ice with opponents and there is usually no referee standing on it — which is exactly why a shove at the red line becomes a suspension rather than two minutes.

- **Three of the books simply penalise it.** **USA Hockey Rule 502(b)**, among the Referee's duties, states that *"[p]enalties may also be assessed during the warm-up period."* **Hockey Canada Rule 2.2(j)** is more explicit, and the price is paid by your team rather than only by you: the referee *"will penalize any player who commits a violation of the rules during the pre-game warm-up"*, and *"[t]he penalty will be served at the start of the game"* — so you open short-handed. ⚠️ **Its Note 1 is a concession about timing and not about the penalty:** a player may finish the warm-up before serving a minor, bench minor or misconduct, and that permission is not extended to a major, match or game misconduct. **Hockey Canada 4.1(a)** adds that a player penalised in the warm-up *"will automatically have their name placed on the Official Game Report"*. **CARHA Rule 27(b)** matches them with a seeing limb — penalties may be assessed *"for infractions of the rules committed during the pre-game warm-up, when observed by the Referee"* — and writes the same Official Game Report consequence. *(CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only.)*
- **The IIHF looks like the exception, and it is the book most British readers are under — but its Rule 5.6 is narrower than it reads.** **Rule 5.6**, word for word identical in the 2025/26 (Version 1.1) and 2026/27 editions, sets the terms of the on-ice warm-up: that *"all protective equipment must be properly worn"*, that a player registered for the Championship but not on the line-up sheet may take part, and that *"[n]o Player can warm-up on ice at the end of a period or at any stoppage of play."* It then ends: *"No penalties are to be assessed but a report of the incident must be submitted to the Proper Authorities."* ⚠️ **That sentence is the enforcement clause for those terms. It is not a general amnesty for anything done in the warm-up.**
- **A warm-up fight is penalised on the night under the IIHF, exactly as it would be in the game — and this is the part a British reader must not get wrong.** **Rule 46.8** in the 2026/27 book, headed *Fighting Other Than During the Periods of the Game*, reads: *"Players involved in fighting other than during the periods of the game shall be assessed a major penalty for fighting and an automatic game misconduct."* **The same rule is numbered 46.9 in the 2025/26 (Version 1.1) book**, which writes the tier as *"(5'+GMP)"* and routes an aggressor or an instigator to its own rules. Both editions add that a player who *"defends"* themselves may be treated as an unwilling combatant and have the game misconduct waived at the referee's discretion — ⚠️ **the ejection, not the major.** And the IIHF's own warm-up rule points that way rather than at 5.6: **Rule 86.6** confines each team to its own end of the rink and signposts *Fighting Prior the Drop of the Puck*, which in turn sends a fight before the start of a game or a period back to 46.8 in terms. **So "nothing is called in the warm-up" is false under the IIHF for the one thing most likely to happen in one.**
  - **The book does not reconcile those two rules, and this guide does not invent a reconciliation.** 5.6 says no penalties are to be assessed; 46.8 assesses a major plus an automatic game misconduct for a fight outside the periods of the game. Neither cross-refers to the other. **Read 5.6 as scoped to its own subject matter and the fighting rule as scoped to fighting** — that is the reading the rest of the book supports, and it is the safe one either way, because the alternative is planning on an amnesty the fighting rule contradicts. **For warm-up conduct the fighting rule does not reach, 5.6's route does apply**: nothing called on the night, and a report to the Proper Authorities — supplementary discipline, the branch that has no five-minute ceiling on it and can reach games rather than minutes. ⚠️ **Do not read "no penalties are to be assessed" as "nothing happens."**
- **The EIHL reverses that for the Elite League alone, and adds a contact rule of its own.** The **EIHL Casebook 26-27** supersedes the IIHF where it speaks, and under **Rule 86.6** it speaks here. Its first lettered limb is the one this document exists to flag: *"Physical contact between players on opposing teams is not allowed during the warmup and players are not allowed to skate over the centre red line during this time. Violations of these rules will be handled by the standby referee who oversees the warmup."* **So in that league the warm-up is a non-contact period by rule, whatever the game itself permits.** The same rule then reverses IIHF 5.6: *"Referees are authorised to call all penalties provided for in the rule book from the start of warm-up time until 30 minutes after the end of the game, if they see the offence take place."* And the cost is not only yours — *"players, team officials and the clubs will be subjected to a suspension and/or fine"*, and *"the Head Coach of the team who instigated (started) or provoked such activity is subject to a suspension or fine"*. Casebook **5.6(1)** covers the same ground from the other direction, reaching a player who crosses the centre red line *"(inclusive of body and/or equipment)"* to start *"any sort of unsportsmanlike behaviour (including shooting pucks at opponent's end)"*, and its warm-up window runs from the clock reading 40 minutes to the clock reading 20 minutes.
  - **Two things in that rule qualify each other, and quoting either alone gets it wrong.** The limb immediately after the contact ban says that violations of game rules in the warm-up *"cannot be enforced by on-ice officials because they are not on the ice to witness the violations first-hand, but these may be reviewed after the game by the proper authorities"* — while the authorisation above is conditioned *"if they see the offence take place"*, and a standby referee does oversee the warm-up. ⚠️ **Read together: what is seen can be called, and what is not seen is reviewed afterwards. Neither limb makes the warm-up free.**
  - **And the Casebook prints that authorisation twice, in two different forms.** The copy in its Preamble omits *"if they see the offence take place"* and so reads as unlimited; the copy at 86.6 carries it. ⚠️ **Quote the 86.6 version, which is the one printed with the rule.**
- **Scope this to the EIHL and no further.** The In-House Rules for 2026-27, which govern NIHL, the SNL, BUIHA Varsity, junior and women's hockey, were searched here for any warm-up conduct provision and contain none beyond a pre-warm-up equipment check — so in those competitions **the IIHF book stands unmodified, both halves of it**: Rule 5.6's report route for the warm-up terms that rule covers, **and a major plus an automatic game misconduct for a warm-up fight** (Rule 46.8 in the 2026/27 book, 46.9 in 2025/26). **There is no British relaxation of either.** See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).

### What this means for how you read the rest of this document

```facts
Key: Almost everything here applies in every league — angling, stick checks, body position, receiving a hit safely, battles, shot blocking
Priority: Two things need a full-checking game — Section 5's legal body check, and step 3 of the net-front box-out in Section 9, driving an opponent off their spot. Neither is free even in a checking game
Rule: Interference governs the net-front walk-out, because the screener has no puck — NHL 56.1 needs both limbs, "Moving laterally and without establishing body position, then making contact with the non-puck carrier", IIHF 56.1 the same words, and USA Hockey 625(a)(4) names it too
Rule: Women's hockey is not a full-checking league — IIHF Rule 101.1 allows bodychecking with a clear intention of playing the puck or an attempt to gain possession of it, subject to the exception the rule itself then sets out, and makes any move to step or glide into an opponent an illegal hit
Rule: A third thing is scoped, to the women's game — the pin along the boards in Sections 7 and 8, which IIHF Rule 101.1 bars; there you may pin the puck, not the player
Never: Treat anything here as a licence to hit, push or shove an opponent in a league that does not allow it
Key: Holding a position you have established is written as legal in the NHL, IIHF, USA Hockey, PWHL and CARHA — moving an opponent off theirs is not, and CARHA adds a duty to avert contact
Rule: The NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL write it in full — Rule 56.1 in the first two, 57.1 in the third — and USA Hockey in its Standard of Play summaries, scoped to no age or category: "entitled to the ice they occupy". Hockey Canada has no such sentence; a CARHA adult league grants the standing half only
Rule: The PWHL grants body position in its interference rule — 57.1, a player is "allowed the ice she is standing on (body position) and is not required to move in order to let an opponent proceed"
Rule: CARHA still lets you keep a spot you have taken — Note 2 to Rule 66(a) says defending players "are entitled to 'stand their ground' or 'shadow' the attacking players"; 49(a) reaches the leaning and bodying, not the standing (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: CARHA adds a duty to avert body contact — 49(a) reaches a player who "stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact" or "does not avert body contact with an opponent" (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
```

Almost everything here applies in **every league, including yours** — angling, stick checks, body position, receiving a hit safely, board and corner and net-front battles, loose-puck races, shot blocking and staying out of the box. **Three things are not.** The first is the section on **delivering a legal body check** (§5), which applies only if your league permits **full** body checking — women's hockey does not, and its own restricted standard is the one set out in §1. ⚠️ **And under USA Hockey Rule 604(b) it also depends on the fixture:** the section is not yours in a game where a checking team is playing a non-checking one, whatever your own classification says. The second is a single step inside the net-front box-out (§9): **driving an opponent off their spot — walking them out from under their arms — is a push, and it needs a full-checking game — Rule 604(b) reaches it as well.** ⚠️ **And a full-checking game does not make it free.** A body check is delivered on an opponent *in control of the puck*, and the player you are walking out of the net front does not have it — so what governs there is **interference**, not the checking rules. NHL and IIHF **Rule 56.1** let you block a path and hold the ice you are standing on, but penalise as interference *"Moving laterally and without establishing body position, then making contact with the non-puck carrier"* — the quoted wording is the NHL’s, the IIHF’s the same but for quotation marks round its defined terms — and **NHL 56.2(i)** writes the minor for a player who *"interferes with or impedes the progress of an opponent who is not in possession of the puck"*. **And USA Hockey's interference rule announces no category scope in its title the way Rule 604 does** — **Rule 625(a)(4)** names the act in terms — *"[a] defending player who changes their skating lane or foot speed in an effort to play the body of an opponent who is no longer in control of the puck"* — while its Note defines interference as impeding a non-puck carrier *"with no effort to play the puck, maintain normal foot speed or maintain an established skating lane"*, which is why simply holding a lane you established is not what either rule reaches. So establishing position **first** is not merely the good technique §5 teaches; in a checking league it is the thing that keeps the walk-out on the legal side of 56.1. In the books that bar checking it is a minor — USA Hockey Rule 604(c), and Hockey Canada Rule 7.3(a) in *"divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey, and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada"* — **and neither of those minors is the ceiling in its own book**, because each runs on to a major plus a game misconduct **with nobody hurt** and then to a match penalty; the rule numbers and the tiers are in [Defending the net front](#defending-the-net-front). **In women's hockey, where checking is restricted rather than barred, it is an illegal hit under IIHF Rule 101.1** — the walk-out is not an effort to play the puck, a stationary player *"is entitled to that area of the ice"*, and *"[a]ny move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player will be assessed at least a minor penalty (2') for an 'illegal hit'"*. **And that minor is not the ceiling in that book either:** Rule 101.1 assesses an illegal hit as either a *"Minor penalty (2')"* or a *"Major penalty (5') and automatic game misconduct penalty"*, and writes no injury condition into either. **The third is scoped to the women's game rather than to checking:** pinning an opponent along the boards (§7, and the corner in §8, which is a board battle) is barred by IIHF Rule 101.1, which forbids two or more players competing for the puck from using the boards *"to make contact with an opponent to eliminate her from the play, push her into the boards, or pin her along the boards"* — there you may pin the puck, not the player. All three are flagged where they appear. The principle underneath is the one to carry: holding a position you have already established is not what these rules penalise; moving an opponent off theirs is. **One adult book limits even that half, so a CARHA reader should not carry the principle across unaltered** — **CARHA Rule 49(a)** reaches a player who *"stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent"*, which is a duty to get out of the way rather than a licence to hold your spot by contact. **The standing half CARHA does grant, inside its interference rule:** Note 2 to Rule 66(a) says defending players *"are entitled to 'stand their ground' or 'shadow' the attacking players"* — so the line there runs between holding a spot you took, which Note 2 protects, and leaning or bodying to keep it, which 49(a) reaches. What keeps a battle legal there is **49(c)**, under which accidental contact draws no penalty. *(CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only.)* Nothing in this document is a licence to hit in a league that does not allow it.

---

## 2. Angling — Legal in Every League, If You Hold Your Lane and Add Nothing

**Angling is steering an opponent to where you want them by choosing your skating route, not by hitting them.** USA Hockey's glossary defines it as "a legal defensive skill used to direct/control the puck carrier to an area that closes the gap and creates an opening that is too small for the puck carrier."

It is legal everywhere. Hockey Canada's own interpretation of its no-checking rule says it outright: "A player may steer or direct an opposing player into the boards, without actually touching or body-checking them. This is acceptable." You are allowed to close off the boards and force a puck carrier to slow down or turn back inside — as long as you do not bump, push or shove them to do it. ⚠️ **The route is half of that test, not the free half — pick your line early and hold it.** Steering is what the books permit; veering across into an opponent at the last instant to make the contact happen is not, however gentle the contact turns out to be. USA Hockey's Casebook puts this exact play — a Team B player angles a carrier along the boards, contact occurs, the carrier is knocked into the boards and loses the puck — and rules it **no penalty**, *"provided the Team B player does not use any overt hip, shoulder or forearm action to make contact with the opponent and simply maintains their skating lane and focuses on closing the gap and gaining possession of the puck"* (Standard of Play, Situation 15). Hockey Canada draws the same line from the other side: the same contact becomes a body check *"when a player leaves their skating lane to make contact"*, or when a player *"extends an arm, shoulder, or hip after angling an opponent"* (Rule 7.3, whose Glossary entry adds *"to maximize physical contact"*; the full definition is in [§1](#the-two-categories)). And **IIHF Rule 101.1 — the British women's rule — makes the foul the movement itself**: *"Any move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player will be assessed at least a minor penalty (2') for an 'illegal hit'."* **The PWHL writes Hockey Canada's two limbs out in the same words**, at the Rule 52.1 quoted in [§1](#womens-hockey--the-nuance-that-is-usually-got-wrong) — so the test above is not one book's idiosyncrasy. ⚠️ **But "legal in every league" rests on different ground in different books, and it is worth knowing which.** Three books grant angling in terms: USA Hockey's glossary, Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 7.3(a), and PWHL Rule 52.1. **The NHL book never uses the word at all** — angling is legal there because nothing in it reaches a contactless skating route, not because the NHL says so. **So the angle is skated, not thrown. What keeps it legal is the lane you chose three strides ago, and the arm, shoulder or hip you do not add once you arrive.**

This is how good defenders actually take the puck. Not by hitting people.

![](diagram:rush-gap-and-angle)

**The picture above is borrowed from [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md), and the distances in its caption belong there rather than here.** They are gap-control targets for a defender retreating on a one-on-one. ⚠️ **This section sets no distance of its own**: what carries across is the geometry, and nothing numeric.

It is the same *principle* rather than the same skill — that defender is travelling backward on a rush, where the four things below are written for closing on a carrier in open ice. What carries across is the geometry: the defender's body is between the carrier and the middle, the shaded ice is what the route takes away, and the only lane left open is the wall. What it does not show — because no still picture can — is that the defender was already at speed before that instant. **A gap cannot be closed from a standstill** — which is the same reason an angle has to start before you need it.

### The four things you are managing

```facts
Action: Skate at the ice they want, not at the player. Take away their good ice and force them to bad ice
Position: Approach on an angle that leaves your momentum heading back toward your own net, not sideways across the ice
Technique: Arrive under control — slow your feet as the gap shrinks, knees bent so you can still change direction
Never: Cross over as you close — a crossover commits your weight, and a good attacker cuts back against it
Rule: Hold the skating lane you chose — USA Hockey's Casebook clears the angler who "simply maintains their skating lane", and Hockey Canada makes the same contact a body check "when a player leaves their skating lane to make contact" (Rule 7.3)
Technique: Stick on the ice in a sweeping manner, blade covering the inside lane while your body covers the outside
Goal: A gap too small for the puck carrier to cut inside
```

**1. Your route, not their route.** Skate at the ice they want, not at the player. If they are trying to get to the middle, your line takes the middle away and leaves them the wall. Coaches call the middle **good ice** and the outside **bad ice**; the whole aim is to make bad ice the only ice available. USA Hockey's own coaching write-up puts the objective as *"to take away an opponent's good ice and force them to bad ice"* — that sentence is the article's, not a quotation from anyone; the piece quotes Seth Appert, head coach of the U.S. National Under-18 Team at its National Team Development Program, separately and in his own words below.

**2. Momentum back toward your own net.** Approach on an angle that leaves you travelling *back* toward the goal you are defending, not sideways across the ice. Appert: "When you're skating to take away time and space from your opponent, one thing you need to have for the proper angle is momentum back toward your net." The reason is simple — if they get rid of the puck, you are already going the right way and you stay above them (between them and your net). If you angled flat across the ice and they beat you, you are now chasing.

![](diagram:angling-your-route)

**3. Closing speed.** Arrive under control. If you close at full speed, one inside cut beats you and you are 20 feet out of the play. Slow your feet as the gap shrinks and keep your knees bent so you can change direction. The gap you are aiming for is small enough that a cut inside is not available — that is what "creates an opening that is too small for the puck carrier" means.

- **Do not cross over as you close.** A crossover commits your weight to one direction, and a good attacker cuts back against a committed defender. Stay on an inside edge through the last few strides so you can still change direction. (Crossovers are for *building* the speed that gets you to the angle, not for arriving on it — see [Skating](skating.md).)

**4. Stick on the ice, blade in the passing lane.** Appert again: "you want your stick on the ice in a sweeping manner and protecting good ice." Your stick covers the inside — the lane back to the middle — while your body covers the outside. At the moment of contact, "if your opponent still has the puck, you want your stick on the puck."

The picture above draws the first two of these four: the route the defender skates, and the direction that route leaves them travelling. The other two — closing speed, and where the stick is — are not things a still picture can show.

### Where the angle ends

```facts
Action: In a non-checking league, end the angle with a seal — take away the last of their space and arrive stick-on-puck
Key: Both leaning, both playing the puck, both going the same direction is legal competitive contact
Action: In a checking league, the same angle ends with a rub-out or a check under the Section 5 rules
Priority: The angle comes first, the contact is the last step
Never: Veer across into them at the last instant, or add an arm, shoulder or hip once you arrive — either turns a legal angle into a body check
Never: Throw a hit without an angle — a hit from bad position is where most penalties come from, on the coaching account rather than a count of why penalties were taken
Rule: One adult book limits where the angle may end — CARHA 49(a) reaches a player who "stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact" or "does not avert body contact", so the seal is not automatically safe there (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
```

In a **non-checking league**, the angle ends with a seal: your body position takes away the last of their space, you arrive stick-on-puck, and you either take it or force a dump. Contact happens — you are both leaning, both playing the puck, both going the same direction — and that is legal competitive contact. ⚠️ **One published adult book draws that line further back, and if it is yours the seal is not automatically safe.** **CARHA Rule 49(a)** — **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, and never a general rec-hockey standard** — reaches a player who *"intentionally bodies, pushes, shoves, stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent"*, which is a duty to get out of the way rather than merely a ban on initiating. **Rule 49(c)** keeps genuinely accidental contact outside it — *"[w]here, in the opinion of the Referee, accidental contact has taken place, no penalty shall be assessed"* — so a scramble for a loose puck is not what the rule is aimed at; but a seal you deliberately ride through is inside its words. *(That the third limb is a duty is a reading of the rule's grammar; CARHA does not gloss it.)* What a penalty there costs is set out in [§1](#where-body-checking-is-and-is-not-permitted). **Ask your league whether CARHA is your book before you decide how much room this section leaves you.**

In a **checking league**, the same angle ends with a rub-out or a check, delivered under the rules in Section 5. Note the order: **the angle comes first, the contact is the last step.** A hit thrown without an angle is a hit thrown from bad position, which is where most penalties come from — **the coaching account rather than a count.** No published study codes *why* a penalty was taken; [Game Management, "Frustration penalties"](../systems/game_management.md#frustration-penalties) carries what is and is not measured about it, and the claim should not be restated without that qualification.

**Angling is a skating skill before it is a defensive skill.** See [Skating](skating.md) for the crossovers, pivots and edge control it demands, and [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md) for how angling combines with gap control on an oncoming attack.

---

## 3. Stick Checks — Legal, Effective, and Under-Used

A **stick check** is any attempt to win the puck with your stick rather than your body. All four below are legal skills in every league — but *where* you take them decides whether they stay legal, and the books do not draw that line in the same place. The rule most players are taught is this:

> **⚠️ Stick on the puck is legal. Stick on their hands or their body is a penalty.**

**You will usually hear a third clause in the middle of that — "or stick on their stick" — and it is not a universal rule: of every book read here it is in the NHL's, the IIHF's and the PWHL's only.** It comes from NHL Rule 55.1 (Hooking): "When a player is checking another in such a way that there is only stick-to-stick contact, such action is not to be penalized as hooking." The IIHF carries that sentence in the same words, bar a capitalised *"Player"* and quotation marks around *"hooking"*. 🇬🇧 **IIHF Rule 55.1 also carries a second sentence the NHL's does not, printed immediately before that one:** *"If the stick goes against the opponent's hands / or near the opponent's hands, it shall be penalized as 'hooking'."* It is in both current editions — 2025/26 Version 1.1 and 2026/27 — and it names the hands with no condition attached at all, so the book a British player is actually under is the plainest of them on this point — though the stick-to-stick sentence beside it pulls the other way, and the IIHF reconciles neither, which is set out under [The stick lift](#the-stick-lift) below. Note the condition even there: it exempts a player **checking** an opponent, not stick-on-stick contact generally. **USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and CARHA run the other way** — all three name the opponent's stick as a hookable surface, "any part of an opponent's body or stick" (USA Hockey Rule 623's Note; Hockey Canada Rule 8.2; CARHA's Glossary). So under those three books "it was only stick-on-stick" is not a defence. **The stick lift survives in every book that addresses it, but on different footings.** Hockey Canada permits it expressly — Interpretation 1 to 8.2(a), a lift *"for the purpose of making a play on the puck"* *"will be allowed"*. **USA Hockey permits it too, in its Casebook, and on a broader footing than Hockey Canada's** — asked whether a player may lift an opponent's stick without being penalised under the Standard of Play, it answers *"Yes. A player may lift the opponent's stick at any time provided they do not impede their progress"*, and calls it *"a good defensive play"* (Standard of Play, Situation 2). ⚠️ **Read what each permission is conditioned on, because it is not the same thing:** Hockey Canada's turns on your *purpose* — a play on the puck — and USA Hockey's on the *effect*, that you do not impede their progress. **Neither is permission to take it at the hands**: Rule 623 still needs *"a pulling or tugging motion"* and still reaches a stick on the hand or arm, and the Casebook's Rule 623, Situation 1 opens by ruling that hooking an opponent's stick *is* a penalty before it allows the lift — and under the IIHF book it is the hands sentence above that catches you, on the location alone, with no pulling, tugging or effect to be shown. Take it low. **CARHA is the one book here that addresses it neither way** — the word *lift* appears nowhere in it, so there is no permission to rely on and no prohibition to point at, while its hooking rule reaches the opponent's stick like USA Hockey's and Hockey Canada's. **That is a gap in the book, not a ruling either way, so in a CARHA league take the lift low and clean and expect it to be judged on the hooking rule.** And NHL Rule 61.1 (Slashing): "Any forceful or powerful chop with the stick on an opponent's body, the opponent's stick, or on or near the opponent's hands that, in the judgment of the Referee, is not an attempt to play the puck, shall be penalized as slashing."

Read that second one carefully. Chopping *at their stick* is still slashing if it is a chop rather than an attempt to play the puck. The difference is intent and violence, and referees are watching for it.

### The poke check

```facts
Technique: A short, straight jab of the blade at the puck, one hand or two, from a controlled distance
Read: Use it when you are square to a carrier with the puck out in front — receiving, looking up, or beginning a deke
Technique: Top hand on the knob, extend from the shoulder — do not lunge with your whole body
Action: Poke, recover, stay skating
Never: Poke from too far away — if your blade cannot reach the puck without lunging, you are too far away
```

**What it is:** A short, straight jab of the blade at the puck, one hand or two, from a controlled distance.

**When it works:** When you are square to the puck carrier and they have the puck out in front of them — receiving a pass, looking up, or beginning a deke. It is also the natural finish to an angle, when the carrier has run out of room.

**How to do it:** Keep your top hand on the knob and extend from the shoulder; do not lunge with your whole body. The moment you commit your feet, a good carrier steps around your stick and you have taken yourself out of the play. Poke, recover, stay skating.

**The mistake:** Poking from too far away, missing, and being beaten. If your blade cannot reach the puck without you lunging, you are too far away.

### The stick lift

```facts
Technique: Blade under their shaft low, below their bottom hand and a hand's width clear of the glove — lift crisply up, not sideways
Action: Step through as their blade comes off the ice and take the puck
Read: Lift late — as they are about to shoot, receive or move the puck, not while they are just carrying it
Read: A stick lift on an opponent bent low over the puck goes up at a face down at stick height — catching that face is a high stick however low your hands were. Take it under the shaft, or leave it alone
Position: Works from behind and beside an opponent, at the net front, in a board battle and on a backcheck
Never: Take the lift on a player who does not have the puck — what makes it legal is that it plays the puck; on anyone else it is restraint
Rule: Stick-to-stick is exempt from hooking only in the NHL, IIHF and PWHL books, and only while checking (NHL and IIHF 55.1; PWHL 56.1) — USA Hockey 623, Hockey Canada 8.2 and CARHA's Glossary name the opponent's stick as a hookable surface (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: IIHF 55.1 makes a stick against, or near, the opponent's hands hooking; its next sentence exempts stick-to-stick contact while checking, and the book reconciles neither. Below their bottom hand the two sentences never meet — no contact at the hands for the first to catch. Low is still the answer
Rule: The NHL's hooking rule writes no hands sentence but its slashing rule does — and so do IIHF 61.1 and PWHL 63.1, word for word, and USA Hockey 634, which drops the "attempt to play the puck" let-out; Hockey Canada 9.3 and CARHA 80 reach a chop there without naming the hands
Rule: Hockey Canada answers the missed stick lift directly, in junior and senior only: Interpretation 2 to Rule 9.5(a), item ii, makes an attempted lift that accidentally contacts the head or neck and causes injury a double minor. In minor and female hockey it is 7.6(a), a minor with no injury needed
```

**What it is:** You place your blade under the shaft of their stick, **below the bottom hand and with a clear gap above it**, and lift their blade off the ice — then take the puck out from underneath it.

**Why it is worth learning:**

- It takes the puck with **far less** penalty risk than a poke check (tripping) or a body check (a dozen possible penalties). **Not none — and no book here leaves the hands unprotected, the NHL included. But it is the IIHF, USA Hockey and Hockey Canada whose *hooking* rules reach a *lift* at the hands.
