The NHL, the PWHL and CARHA write no hands sentence into hooking at all, and their hands provision sits in slashing instead, which catches a chop rather than a lift.** 🇬🇧 IIHF Rule 55.1 carries a sentence the NHL's does not — *"If the stick goes against the opponent's hands / or near the opponent's hands, it shall be penalized as 'hooking'"* — and that is the book a British player is under. ⚠️ **Read 55.1's very next sentence too, because the book does not reconcile the two:** *"When a Player is checking another in such a way that there is only stick-to-stick contact, such action is not to be penalized as 'hooking'."* A lift taken under the shaft is stick-to-stick contact and nothing else, so the two sentences pull against each other, and **no IIHF interpretation reconciling them is held here.** Note the scope of the exemption even so — it covers a player **checking** an opponent, not stick-on-stick contact generally. Reading the hands sentence as the operative one is the cautious choice and the one this document makes; it may be stricter than the rule actually is. **That is not a reason to take the lift high.** An unresolved rule gets resolved on the night by the referee in front of you, and the reading that costs you nothing is the low one — below their bottom hand the two sentences never meet, because there is no contact at the hands for the first one to catch. **Low is still the answer.** **The books whose hooking rules stop short still reach the hands, by their own routes.** USA Hockey's *hooking* rule takes a narrower one than the IIHF's, catching a stick on the hand or arm only where it stops the opponent playing the puck; the NHL's and the PWHL's hooking rules write no hands sentence at all, and their *slashing* rules do the work instead, as do the IIHF's and USA Hockey's, while Hockey Canada's Rule 9.3 and CARHA's Rule 80 reach a chop at the hands without naming them. **A clean lift for the puck is not a chop**, so it sits outside NHL and IIHF Rule 61.1, whose slashing sentence excepts an attempt to play the puck — **and USA Hockey's Rule 634 writes no such exception, so there it is the force of the chop that decides.** Either way the answer is the same one: lift under the shaft, below their bottom hand, and you are outside the hands provision in every one of them. Each book's wording, and which of them needs an injury before a major, is in [Where the stick lift becomes a penalty](#where-the-stick-lift-becomes-a-penalty) below. Stick-on-stick is the other half of 55.1, and in the IIHF book the half that collides with its hands sentence: it is what NHL and IIHF Rule 55.1 and PWHL 56.1 protect, and only while checking — USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and CARHA name the opponent's stick as hookable instead.
- It works from **behind and beside** an opponent, where you cannot legally do anything else. You cannot restrain a player from behind — NHL Rule 56.1 says a player behind an opponent "may not use his stick, body or free hand in order to restrain his opponent" — but lifting the stick of a player *who has the puck* is playing the puck, not restraining a player.
- It is the correct tool at the **net front**, in a **board battle**, and on a **backcheck** — the three places where amateurs instead reach, hook, or cross-check and go to the box.
- It leaves you **balanced**. You never leave your feet or lean past your centre of gravity, so if it fails you are still in the play.

**How to do it:** Get your blade under their shaft *low*, **below their bottom hand and a hand's width clear of the glove** — that is also where the leverage is, because the fulcrum is their top hand and a contact point further down the shaft has the longer lever arm. Lift crisply — up, not sideways. As their blade comes off the ice, step through and take the puck with your other hand still on your stick. Do it late: lift as they are about to shoot, receive, or move the puck, not while they are just carrying it.

#### Where the stick lift becomes a penalty

```facts
Never: Lift the stick of a player who does not have the puck. That is interference
Never: Catch their glove or forearm on the way up, or yank the stick down or across. That is slashing, roughing, or holding the stick
Rule: Hockey Canada allows the lift "for the purpose of making a play on the puck" (Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.2(a)), but makes it hooking where it contacts the hands and impedes their ability to "pass, shoot, receive, or otherwise propel the puck" — receive is the one that catches you
Rule: A lift that misses an opponent bent low is a high stick however low your own hands were — the reference is the struck player's waist (NHL 60.3; IIHF 60.3 in the 2026/27 book; IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27, Situation 60.13). A minor, or a double minor where injury results
Rule: A missed lift catching a bent-over face is not capped at the double minor: IIHF 60.4, USA Hockey 621(b) and Hockey Canada 7.6(b)/7.7(b) reach a major plus a game misconduct with nobody injured; NHL 60.4's match penalty needs a deliberate or attempted injury
Rule: A fifth book is harsher — CARHA 62(b), CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, is a major for contact above an opponent's shoulders, accidental or not, injury or not, with no minor rung there and no windup carve-out; its Note adds a game misconduct when intentional or when it injures
Rule: A chop at the hands reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody injured in every book read here — NHL 61.3 with 61.5, IIHF 61.3, PWHL 63.3 with 63.5, USA Hockey 634(b), Hockey Canada 9.3(b), and CARHA 80(a) with 30(a), which makes that major an ejection (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: A hooking major needs an injury under NHL 55.3 and Hockey Canada 8.2(b), but not under IIHF 55.3 or USA Hockey 623(b) — so a chop at the hands is never the cheaper charge
Rule: Three minor penalties for stick infractions in the same game end your game under Hockey Canada Rule 4.8(b)(i) — a Game Ejection. The rule defines stick infractions as Rules 9.1 to 9.5, so slashing counts and hooking does not — and 9.5 is Junior/Senior only
Rule: Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b) counts a double minor as a single infraction towards its three-stick-minor Game Ejection, so a double minor for slashing puts one mark against you rather than two
Rule: A Hockey Canada Game Ejection is not a game misconduct and triggers no automatic suspension — Rule 4.8's Note 1 says so in terms, so a third stick minor costs the rest of that game and the book attaches nothing past the final whistle
Key: Penalties are counted as well as priced — three cheap stick minors end your game under Hockey Canada, and the other books run in-game counts of their own
```

Lifting a stick when the player does **not** have the puck is interference. Lifting at or near their hands is hooking under the IIHF book on the location alone — subject to the carve-out in 55.1's own next sentence, which the book does not reconcile with it and which is set out above — and under USA Hockey's Rule 623 where a stick placed on the hand or arm *"takes away the ability for the opponent to pass or shoot the puck with a normal amount of force"* — a condition the IIHF's sentence does not carry. **The NHL's *hooking* rule has no hands sentence, but do not read the book as silent on the hands:** NHL Rule 61.1 makes a forceful chop *"on or near the opponent's hands that, in the judgment of the Referee, is not an attempt to play the puck"* **slashing** — and the NHL is not alone in taking that route, because IIHF Rule 61.1 and PWHL Rule 63.1 are that sentence word for word and USA Hockey Rule 634 a shortened form of it with no *"attempt to play the puck"* let-out at all. A lift for the puck is an attempt to play the puck; a chop at the hands is not, under any of them. **Hockey Canada reaches it by an interpretation written about this exact technique:** Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.2(a) allows a player to *"lift an opposing player's stick, for the purpose of making a play on the puck"* — but *"if this action contacts the opponent's hands and impedes their ability to pass, shoot, **receive**, or otherwise propel the puck, then a penalty for Hooking must be assessed."* Note *receive*: taking a pass is the commonest moment a lift lands on the hands. Catching a glove on the way up is slashing or roughing in any of them. Yanking the stick down or across is holding the stick.

**And a lift that misses an opponent who is bent low over the puck is a high stick, however low your own hands were.** The reference point is the player you hit, not you: the NHL's Rule 60.3 and the IIHF's 2026/27 Rule 60.3 both write *"above the height of the opponent's waist"*, and the **IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27** answers this exact case at **Situation 60.13** — a stick-lift attempt on a player *"bent over digging the puck out of the corner"* which misses and hits them in the face while the striker's stick is *"only slightly above the height of their waist"* — with *"Yes, this is a minor (or double-minor) penalty for high-sticking."* **And the double minor is not the ceiling.** IIHF **60.4** lets the referee assess *"a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty"* on a player who *"recklessly endangers their opponent by 'high-sticking'"* — wording identical in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 IIHF books, so this tier does not turn on which edition you are under — and **USA Hockey 621(b)** makes the same pair compulsory: *"A major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of high sticking."* **Neither waits for an injury**, and Hockey Canada reaches that tier through its head-contact rules instead, on the degree of violence rather than on endangerment — **7.6(b)** in minor and female hockey, *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"*, and **7.7(b)** in junior and senior, *"for any Head Contact infraction with a high degree of violence"*. The NHL's **60.4** is a match penalty and needs more than a miss — it reaches a player who *"attempts to or deliberately injures an opponent with a high-stick"*. **Hockey Canada answers this same case directly, and its answer is division-scoped.** Interpretation 2 to Rule 9.5(a) lists the *"attempted restraining fouls"* that *"should be considered accidental high-sticks"*, and item ii is this one in terms: *"When an attempted lifting of the opponent's stick, which results in accidental contact to the head or neck area, causes injury, a double Minor will be assessed."* Item vi takes *"any aggressive, reckless, negligent or forceful action of the stick (e.g. pitchfork action in lifting the stick or hooking an opponent)"* that causes injury to a major plus a game misconduct. ⚠️ **Both are junior and senior only** — Rule 9.5 is titled *"High-Sticking – Junior/Senior"*, and items iv to vi are assessed under Rule 7.7, Head Contact – Junior/Senior. **Below that the case falls to Rule 7.6, and the trigger there is lower, not higher:** 7.6(a) assesses a minor *"to any player who accidentally contacts an opponent in the head, face, or neck, in minor and female hockey"*, with no injury required at all. So in Hockey Canada minor and female hockey a missed lift that merely brushes a bent-over face is already a penalty. ⚠️ **And a fifth book on disk is harsher than any of the four, in the kind of league many adult readers actually play in.** **CARHA Rule 62(b)** — binding **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only**, and no reader in minor hockey — assesses **a major penalty** to a player who *"accidentally or intentionally contacts an opponent above the normal height of the opponent's shoulders with a high stick"*, *"whether or not injury results."* **Above the shoulders there is no minor rung at all.** CARHA's 62(a) is a minor for a **different act** — checking or intimidating an opponent while carrying your stick above **their waist** — which needs no contact at all, and it is not a lower rung of 62(b): the book's own case situations price a swing at the puck *"above waist height of opponent – making contact with the player"* at a *"Minor Penalty"*, and the same swing *"striking opponent above the normal height of the player's shoulder"* at a *"Major Penalty"*. Nor is there a route back down through a head-contact rule: **CARHA Rule 50** is written for contact *"with any part of the player's body or equipment other than a stick"*, and its opening Note sends any stick contact back to Rule 62. 62(b)'s Note adds a game misconduct where the high stick was intentional, or where it was accidental and injures; **62(c)** is a match penalty. ⚠️ **And CARHA writes no windup or follow-through carve-out** — searched by concept as well as by phrase, and the second of those case situations refuses one on the facts, because a swing *at the puck* is exactly what it describes — so the accident that is a minor or a double minor under the four books is a five-minute major there. **Find out which book your league runs before you take a lift at a bent-over opponent.** **None of that is a reason to stop lifting sticks; it is the reason to take this particular one under the shaft or not at all.** Editions, rule numbers and the rest of the divergence are in [The stick above the shoulders](#the-stick-above-the-shoulders).

**Which charge you take is the reason to lift rather than chop, even where you think you would get away with it.** Under the NHL and Hockey Canada a hooking major needs an **injury** — NHL 55.3 reaches a player who *"injures an opponent by 'hooking'"*, Hockey Canada 8.2(b) one who *"injures an opponent by a hooking or butt-end hooking infraction"* — where a slashing major needs only severity of contact (NHL 61.3) or *"the degree of violence of the impact"* (Hockey Canada 9.3(b)); and Hockey Canada 8.2(c) bars a match penalty for hooking outright — *"No Match penalty may be assessed for a hooking infraction"* — while 9.3(c) provides one for slashing. **Under the IIHF and USA Hockey the two rules run level, and both run high:** each reaches a major **plus a game misconduct** for recklessly endangering an opponent with nobody hurt — IIHF 55.3 and 61.3 at the referee's discretion, USA Hockey 623(b) and 634(b) where the major plus game misconduct *"shall be assessed"* — and **USA Hockey allows a match penalty above that on both** (623(c), 634(c)). ⚠️ **And a major plus a game misconduct is not the chop’s ceiling in any book read here but one.** Three more write a match penalty for slashing: **NHL 61.4**, where the referee *"may assess a match penalty if, in his judgment, the player attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent by slashing"*; **PWHL 63.4**, the same sentence in that book’s own pronouns; and **CARHA 80(d)** — *"A Match penalty shall be assessed to any player who deliberately attempts to, or deliberately injures an opponent with a slash"* — with **CARHA 48(h)** reaching a slash, a high stick and a cross-check alike on the same terms (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). **The IIHF is the one exception, and not because a chop is cheaper there: that book writes no match penalty for anything**, so the major plus the automatic game misconduct already named is its ceiling *inside the game*. **Above that the IIHF has moved the sanction off the ice rather than removed it** — 61.6 allows Supplementary Discipline above a slash, 20.7 requires the Referee to report every major to the Proper Authorities, and 28.1 lets them act *"whether or not such offense has been penalized by the Referee(s)"*. What that costs is set by the IIHF Disciplinary Code, which is not held here and has not been read.

**And in Hockey Canada a chop can end your game a third way, because the minors are counted as well as priced.** Rule 4.8(b)(i) assesses a **Game Ejection** *"[w]hen a player is assessed three Minor penalties for 'stick infractions' in the same game"*, and defines those in the same clause as *"Rule 9.1 — Butt-Ending, Rule 9.2 — Cross-Checking, Rule 9.3 — Slashing, Rule 9.4 — Spearing, and Rule 9.5 — High-sticking — Junior/Senior."* So a slashing minor counts toward it and a hooking minor, which lives in Section 8 with the restraining fouls, does not — Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.1(a) says exactly that about butt-end hooking. **Read the last item on that list with the scope printed in its own title, because it is not everybody's.** Rule 9.5 is headed *"High-Sticking – Junior/Senior"* and its penalty clause opens *"In Junior and Senior divisions"*, so in minor and female hockey only four of those five stick infractions reach you at all. That does not make a high stick free below Junior: there it is Head Contact under Rule 7.6, which carries a count of its own — three of those in the same game bring the same Game Ejection under Rule 4.8(b)(ii), on a separate tally. So a minor-division player has two counts running rather than one, and the high stick is on the other one. **Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b) softens the count in one respect and one only:** *"For the purposes of accumulation towards a Game Ejection penalty, a double Minor penalty will be considered one infraction."* So a double minor for slashing is one mark against you, not two — and the third mark still ends your night, whichever way you got there. **What it does not do is follow you into the next game.** A Game Ejection is a lesser thing than a game misconduct, and the book says so: *"A Game Ejection is not equal to a Game Misconduct penalty and a player receiving a Game Ejection penalty does not trigger any automatic suspension"* — automatic under the rulebook only, since your own league's supplementary discipline may still act on it. (Rule 4.8's Note 1, printed after 4.8(c) without being tied to a sub-clause.) So the automatic suspension in [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight) is not triggered by this one; the rulebook itself attaches nothing further past the final whistle.

**The NHL, IIHF and USA Hockey books were each searched for a stick-specific count of their own, and none of them carries one. Read that negative narrowly.** It means those three books do not single stick minors out; it does **not** mean your penalties stop mattering once you have paid for each of them. Every one of the four escalates within a single game on a counter of some kind, on different triggers and different numbers, and this document does not set the other three out — [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) does. **Find your own book's count before you take a third cheap stick minor**, because the price of the first two is not what the third one costs.

### The sweep check

```facts
Technique: A low, wide arc — blade flat, shaft nearly parallel to the ice, sweeping through the puck or the passing lane
Read: Use it when beaten to the outside, defending a pass across the slot, or covering the pass on a 2-on-1
Technique: Bottom hand slides down the shaft, get low, sweep through the ice — not through the air
Never: Sweep at knee or waist height — that is a hook or a slash
Never: Lean your body after the sweep. Sweep with your stick instead and keep your feet under you
```

**What it is:** A low, wide arc of your stick across the ice — blade flat, shaft nearly parallel to the ice — sweeping through the puck or the passing lane.

**When it works:** When you are beaten to the outside and reaching across, when you are defending a pass across the slot, or when you are down low and want to take away a lane without committing your body. Defencemen use it on a 2-on-1 to cover the pass while the goalie takes the shooter.

**How to do it:** Bottom hand slides down the shaft, get low, sweep through the ice — not through the air. The blade must stay on the ice; a sweep at knee or waist height is a hook or a slash.

**The mistake:** Sweeping with your body leaning after it. Sweep with your stick and keep your feet under you.

### The press-down (stick press)

```facts
Technique: Lay your blade or shaft on top of their blade, pinning it to the ice so they cannot shoot, pass or move the puck
Read: Use it in tight — net front, board battles, faceoff scrambles — when you want the whistle or a teammate to arrive
Key: The lift takes their blade up so you can take the puck; the press takes it down so they cannot use it
Never: Press with force, chop down repeatedly, or press on their hands — a press is a hold-and-pin, not a chop
```

**What it is:** You lay your blade or shaft down on top of their stick blade, pinning it to the ice so they cannot shoot, pass, or move the puck.

**When it works:** In tight — net front, board battles, faceoff scrambles, and any time the puck is sitting between you and an opponent and you want either the whistle or a teammate to arrive. It is the opposite tool to the lift: the lift takes their blade *up* so you can take the puck; the press takes their blade *down* so they cannot use it.

**Where it becomes a penalty:** Pressing down with force, repeatedly chopping down, or pressing down on their hands. A press is a hold-and-pin, not a chop.

> **All four of these work in non-contact leagues.** If you play in a beer league and want to become a genuinely better defensive player this season, learn the stick lift.

---

## 4. Body Position as Defence

You can defend an entire shift without ever making a deliberate hit. **Most of the books are on your side here — four of the six write the entitlement, and the two that do not are set out below.** The fullest passage is worth reading whole. NHL Rule 56.1 (Interference), under "Body Position":

> "A player is allowed the ice he is standing on (body position) and is not required to move in order to let an opponent proceed. A player may 'block' the path of an opponent provided he is in front of his opponent and moving in the same direction. ... A player is always entitled to use his body position to lengthen an opponent's path to the puck, provided his stick is not utilized (to make himself 'bigger'...); his free hand is not used and he does not take advantage of his body position to deliver an otherwise illegal check."

USA Hockey codifies the same idea for non-checking play, listing as legal competitive contact: a skater is entitled to the ice they occupy while maintaining skating speed and body position between an opponent and a loose puck; a skater may stand their ground and is not required to move; a skater may block an opponent when in front and moving in the same direction; and a skater may use body position while holding their skating lane to force an opponent onto a longer route to the puck.

**Four of the six books read for this document write that grant in full — the NHL and the IIHF at Rule 56.1, the PWHL at 57.1, and USA Hockey in the Standard of Play sentences just quoted. Two do not, so do not carry "the rulebook is on your side" into either of them — one writes nothing of the kind, and the other writes only the standing half of it.** Hockey Canada writes no body-position entitlement at all: its only body-position sentence is **Rule 8.1**'s arm strength move, which *assumes* you have position while permitting an arm action rather than granting the position itself — so there the entitlement is unwritten, ⚠️ **and Rule 8.3's interference minor reaches impeding an opponent** *"who is not in possession of the puck"* with no body-position carve-out anywhere in that book's restraining-fouls section. ⚠️ **And in a CARHA adult league it is narrower than anywhere else here: hold a spot you already took, and do not lean or body to keep it.** **Rule 49(a)** reaches a player who *"intentionally bodies, pushes, shoves, stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent"*, and makes a major mandatory *"[w]hen injury results from intentional body contact"* — a duty to avert contact rather than a licence to hold your spot by it. What keeps an ordinary battle legal there is **49(c)**, under which *"[w]here, in the opinion of the Referee, accidental contact has taken place, no penalty shall be assessed"*. **The standing half CARHA does grant, and it sits inside its interference rule:** Note 2 to **Rule 66(a)** says defending players *"are entitled to 'stand their ground' or 'shadow' the attacking players"* — so the line there runs between holding a spot you took, which Note 2 protects, and leaning or bodying to keep it, which 49(a) reaches. **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only; see §1.**

### What that gives you

```facts
Action: Take away ice — standing in the place your opponent needs to be is a complete defensive act
Technique: Box out — inside shoulder ahead of theirs, hips between them and the puck, and hold
Action: Seal along the wall — occupy the lane out and let the boards do the rest
Rule: A player who has lost position must skate to gain or re-establish it in order to make a check (NHL Rule 56.1)
Never: Use your stick to make yourself wider, your free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab, or the position as cover for a check that would otherwise be illegal — the three provisos of NHL and IIHF 56.1
Rule: The free hand may fend off an opponent or their stick, but may not hold their stick or body — NHL and IIHF Rule 56.1; Hockey Canada 8.1 permits blocking a stick but not holding it
Rule: USA Hockey writes the fend-off permission in its Casebook only, and conditions it — control the puck (Standard of Play Situation 6) or be reaching for it, engaged in a corner or in front of the goal (Situation 7), hold a position you "legally established", and never push off to create space
Rule: The entitlement is not universal — CARHA 49(a) reaches a player who "stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact" or "does not avert body contact", with a mandatory major on injury, though 66(a) Note 2 still lets a defender "stand their ground" (CARHA adult leagues only)
Rule: Hockey Canada writes no body-position grant — 8.1 assumes position while permitting an arm strength move, and 8.3's interference minor reaches impeding an opponent "who is not in possession of the puck", with no body-position carve-out in its restraining-fouls section
```

**Taking away ice.** Standing in the place your opponent needs to be is a complete defensive act. You do not have to touch them. Making a player skate 15 extra feet is often worth more than a hit, because it buys your teammates time to get back.

**Boxing out.** Get your inside shoulder ahead of theirs, put your hips between them and the puck, and hold. This is the basketball rebound principle and it is exactly how you defend the net front and how you win a loose puck in a crowd.

**Sealing.** Along the wall, put your body between the opponent and the ice they want, then let the boards do the rest. You are not pushing them into the boards — you are occupying the lane out.

**Skating to re-establish position.** The line you must not cross is in the same rule: a player behind an opponent who does not have the puck "must skate in order to gain or reestablish his proper position in order to make a check." If you have lost position, the only legal way to get it back is with your feet. Reaching with your stick is hooking; reaching with your free hand is holding.

**The three things that turn legal position into a penalty**, and they are the three provisos of one sentence in NHL and IIHF Rule 56.1: using your stick to make yourself wider, using your free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab, and — the limb that gets called at the wall — taking *"advantage of his body position to deliver an otherwise illegal check"* (the NHL's words; the IIHF's the same with *"their"* and quotation marks round its defined terms). A seal you were entitled to hold does not make a late hit, a charge or an arrival from behind legal. NHL and IIHF Rule 56.1 permit the free hand to "fend off" an opponent or their stick, but "may not be used to hold an opponent's stick or body." **USA Hockey writes the permission too — in its Casebook rather than its playing rules — and hangs conditions on it the NHL's sentence does not.** Standard of Play Situation 6 asks whether a player may take a hand off the stick and use it as a shield, and answers that *"[a] player who is in control of the puck is allowed to use a free hand to maintain body position that they legally established"*, the example being *"the player with the puck uses the free arm to fend off the opponent and maintain their body position."* ⚠️ **Read every condition before you take a hand off your stick.** You must be **in control of the puck** — or, under Situation 7, **reaching for it** while physically engaged in a corner or in front of the goal; the position you are holding must be one you **legally established**; and the arm may hold what you have, never take more. Situation 6 ends *"if they grab the stick, sweater or arm of the opponent, or use the free arm to push off and create space, a penalty should be assessed"*, and Situation 5 allows a net-front defender to steer with the stick or forearm only *"as long as the arm(s) are not extended."* Hockey Canada 8.1 permits blocking an opponent's stick with the hands, arms or body but not holding it.

---

## 5. Delivering a Legal Body Check

> **This section applies only in games that permit body checking.** If you play 12U or below, girls'/women's hockey under USA Hockey or Hockey Canada, U13 and below in Canada, any Canadian division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved as non-check, or a non-check adult league, everything below is a penalty, and it is not capped at a minor: USA Hockey 604 runs minor, major plus game misconduct, match, and Hockey Canada 7.3 the same shape. ⚠️ **And the bar is not only a property of your own league:** USA Hockey Rule 604(b) makes body checking prohibited outright *"[a]ny time a team from a Competitive Contact category … plays a team from a Body Checking category"*, so a single fixture against a non-checking team is a no-checking game whatever your own classification is, and everything below is a penalty in that game too. **And if your league runs the CARHA book, this section is not yours either** — that book has no checking division at all, its Rule 49(a) makes a body check a minor or a major anywhere on the ice and a major where it injures, and by Rule 30(a) that major rules you off the ice for the rest of the game, with Rule 32(d) adding an automatic one-game suspension (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only; see §1). Skip to Section 6 — **but read the last four sections of this one first: *Four fouls no division is exempt from*, *Boarding, and the player who cannot see you coming*, *Checking from behind, and what the onus does and does not mean*, and *And what these fouls cost beyond tonight*.** Checking from behind, head contact, charging and boarding are penalties in *every* division: Hockey Canada scopes 7.3 and USA Hockey scopes 604, and neither scopes those four — though Hockey Canada writes head contact twice, 7.6(a) penalising it in minor and female hockey and 7.7 in Junior and Senior, with both rules opening by banning head contact outright. **All four can end your game in at least one book**, and they can cost you games beyond it. ⚠️ **But if CARHA is your book, read those four sections knowing that they price these fouls in the NHL, IIHF, USA Hockey and Hockey Canada books and never in CARHA's.** Every act they prohibit is prohibited under CARHA too — it is the penalty ladders that are not yours, and they do not run the same way. **Your four rules are CARHA 49 (Boarding and Body Contact), 50 (Head Contact), 52 (Charging) and 53 (Checking from Behind), and 53 is the one to read first.** CARHA's floor for checking from behind is *"[a] Minor penalty plus a Game Misconduct"*, assessed *"to any player who intentionally pushes, body checks, or hits an opposing player from behind, anywhere on the ice"* — **so the lowest rung in the CARHA book still ends your game, and the relief USA Hockey's 608(a) gives is not in it.** Under **CARHA 53(a)** a major plus a game misconduct is compulsory *"[i]f a player is injured"*, and **53(b)** is a **Match penalty** where a player is *"pushed, hit or propelled in any manner from behind into the boards or goal net, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*, under a Note instructing referees *"not to substitute other penalties when a player is checked from behind in any manner."* **CARHA 52(a)** prices charging at a minor or a major, makes a major plus a game misconduct compulsory *"[i]f injury results"*, and adds a Note: *"If more than two steps or strides are taken, it shall be considered a charge."* **CARHA 50** covers contact to an opponent's head, face or neck *"with any part of the player's body or equipment other than a stick"* — a minor at 50(a) for contact that is *accidental*, a double minor or a major plus a game misconduct at 50(b) when it is intentional, and a match penalty at 50(c) for a deliberate attempt to injure; a stick that gets there goes to CARHA 62 instead, which §3 covers. **CARHA boarding is 49(b)**, and it works by conversion rather than by tier: where a player commits an infraction that causes an opponent to collide with the boards, the minor *"normally assessed under the rules will be waived and a Double Minor penalty shall be assessed"*, with the major or match applicable to that rule *"assessed in the normal manner."* ⚠️ **And that double minor is two penalties on the ejection count there, not one** — Rule 49(b)'s own Note reads *"The Double Minor penalty assessed under this rule shall count as"* two *"penalties in the application of the Three Penalty Rule (Rule 32 (a))"*, so under CARHA a boarding call plus one more minor rules you off the ice for the rest of the game. **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only; see §1.**
>
> **In Britain the answer is not in a rulebook but in your competition's Rules of Competition** — neither the IIHF Rule Book nor the In-House Rules sets a body-checking age, and IHUK's Rules of Competition put the junior checking line between U12 and U14, so ask your league which side of it your division sits before you read on (see "If you play in Britain" in §1). **Women's hockey in Britain runs IIHF Rule 101.1**, so the restricted standard above is yours and this section is not.

### A body check is the last step of a progression, not the first thing you learn

```facts
Never: Deliver anything in this section in a league that does not permit checking — everything below is a penalty there
Rule: A body check under USA Hockey 604(c) at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's and all non-check adult classifications is not capped at a minor — 604(d) is a major plus a game misconduct where it recklessly endangers, and 604(e) is a match
Rule: Hockey Canada prices a body check in divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey and in any other division a Member has approved — 7.3(a) is a minor, 7.3(b) is a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence with nobody hurt and mandatory on injury, and 7.3(c) is a match
Rule: Body checking is prohibited at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's classifications and in all non-check adult classifications (USA Hockey Rule 604(a))
Rule: Hockey Canada 7.3(a) bars body checking at U13 and below, throughout female hockey, and in any other division a Member has approved — so being above the U13 line does not settle it
Rule: In Britain the body-checking age is not in either rule book but in your competition's Rules of Competition — IHUK's put the junior checking line between U12 and U14. Ask your league; women's hockey runs IIHF Rule 101.1
Key: League legality is a floor, not a readiness test
Technique: The progression is skating, angling, stick checks, contact confidence, body blocking, take out/rub out, solid body checks
Priority: If you cannot angle, lift a stick cleanly or stay balanced through contact, the missing skill is the step you skipped
Key: Body play should begin to be taught at 8-and-Under, years before checking is legal
Priority: A body check is the fourth line of defence after angling, stick checks and body blocks — not the first tool to reach for
```

League legality is a floor, not a readiness test. USA Hockey's *Introduction To Body Contact* sets out body play as an ordered progression, and a solid body check is step seven of seven:

1. **Skating**
2. **Angling**
3. **Stick checks**
4. **Contact confidence** — *"being comfortable with incidental body contact – contact that occurs when competing for a puck"*
5. **Body blocking**
6. **Take out / rub out checks**
7. **Solid body checks**

The manual's own justification for the order: *"It is important that coaches teach this progression of body play skills. This will allow players to enjoy the game more and will also make it a safer sport."* It also notes that body play skills should **begin** to be taught at 8-and-Under, years before checking is legal, precisely because the earlier steps are what make the last one safe.

Two things follow. **For a player:** if you cannot yet angle a puck carrier off the puck, lift a stick cleanly, or stay balanced through incidental contact, the missing skill is not the check — it is the step you skipped, and it is the reason the check will go wrong. Sections 2, 3 and 4 of this document are steps 1–5. **For a coach:** the first four levels of body play, in USA Hockey's own ordering, are angling, stick checks, body blocks and body checks — a body check is the fourth line of defence, not the first one to reach for.

### What a legal check is, precisely

```facts
Rule: Not legal where checking is barred — USA Hockey 604(a) at 12U and below, all girls'/women's and non-check adult; Hockey Canada 7.3 at U13 and below, in female hockey, and in any other division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved
Rule: IIHF women's play permits bodychecking only with clear intention of playing or gaining possession of the puck, subject to the exception it sets out; any move to step or glide into an opponent is at least a minor (Rule 101.1). Britain's age is in IHUK's Rules of Competition, not either rule book
Rule: A body check is not legal in a CARHA-affiliated adult league either — that book has no checking division, 49(a) makes one a minor or a major anywhere on the ice and a major where it injures, and 30(a) costs you the rest of the game with 32(d) adding the next one
Goal: To gain possession of the puck — NOT to punish or intimidate an opponent
Targets: Where the game you are in permits a check at all — an opponent in control of the puck, hit from the front, diagonally from the front, or straight from the side
Technique: Trunk only — hips and shoulders. Hands, forearm, stick and elbow are all illegal
Position: Where the game you are in permits a check at all — contact above their knees and at or below their shoulders
Technique: Start with your stick on the puck — the blade of the player delivering the check must be below the knees (USA Hockey)
Rule: Where checking is barred, an illegal body check is intentional contact using overt hip, shoulder, forearm or torso action — USA Hockey Rule 604 Note 1, scoped by its own words to "the purpose of this rule" and by 604(a) to the Competitive Contact classifications
Key: If your stick is up, you are about to commit a cross-check, a high stick or a head-contact penalty
```

USA Hockey's **Glossary** entry for *Body Checking* is the most explicit definition in the sport, so use it as your checklist — it is the Glossary that carries it, not Rule 604. Its mirror image is **USA Hockey Rule 604, Note 1**, which defines the illegal version — but mind the scope: 604 is titled *"Body Checking (Competitive Contact Categories)"* and its Note opens *"For the purpose of this rule"*, so it governs the classifications where checking is **barred**. **In a checking league the hip and shoulder action it names is the *legal* check — the forearm never is, in any classification** (the Glossary and the Declaration bar it book-wide). Where checking is barred, it reads: *"an illegal body check is defined as when a player makes intentional physical contact with the opponent using overt hip, shoulder, forearm or torso action. This includes physically forcing the opponent off the puck and with no effort to legally play the puck."* Everything below is what keeps you on the legal side of that line:

- **Purpose:** to gain possession of the puck — "NOT to punish or intimidate an opponent."
- **Target:** an opponent **in control of the puck**.
- **Direction:** from the front, diagonally from the front, or straight from the side.
- **Your contact surface:** the **trunk only** — hips and shoulders. Hands, forearm, stick and elbow are all illegal.
- **Their contact area:** **above the knees and at or below the shoulders.**
- **Your stick:** "Proper body checking technique starts with stick on puck, therefore the stick blade of the player delivering the check must be below the knees."

**The IIHF book defines the same check in its own glossary**, and if you play in Britain this is the definition you are actually being officiated against: *"A legal body check is one in which a Player checks an opponent who is in possession of the puck, by using their hip or body from the front, diagonally from the front or straight from the side. Legitimate body checking must be done for the purpose of separating the opponent from the puck, only with the trunk of the body (hips and shoulders) and must be above the opponent's knees and at or below the shoulders."* Note what it does **not** contain: **no stick-below-the-knees clause.** That one is USA Hockey's coaching standard, and it is worth adopting anyway — but it is not a rule you are held to under the IIHF book.

**Two of the four books never define a legal check at all, and they are worth naming.** The
**NHL rulebook contains no positive definition of a body check** — the words appear in it
barely at all, and never as a definition of the legal act, which is why its glossary has no
entry for a check of any kind — and
**Hockey Canada** works the other way round, defining body-checking by directional force:
deliberate contact *"with opposite-directional force"*, leaving your skating lane, or extending
an arm, shoulder or hip after angling. That wording sits in **Rule 7.3**, which applies only
where checking is *banned* — so a Hockey Canada reader in a checking division gets no definition
from it at all. The book's **glossary** carries the same definition under *Body-Checking* with
no division scope, and adds the purpose 7.3 leaves out: the arm, shoulder or hip is extended
*"to maximize physical contact"*. Both arrive at the same place by prohibiting
rather than by describing. That is why the envelope above is worth playing to even where your
own book never writes it down: it is the most demanding of the four descriptions, so a check
that satisfies it does not offend the other three. That is a comparison drawn here between the
four definitions, not a rule any of them states, **and it does not travel past those four** — the two
further books on disk that restrict contact bar hits this envelope permits, so satisfying it is not a
licence outside a full-checking league under one of the four. ⚠️ **And it is a claim about those four definitions of a legal check, not about the fouls that sit beside them.** Clipping and kneeing are separate rules in all four books, neither is scoped to a division, and under Hockey Canada 7.8 a knee contact is kneeing *"on an otherwise legal hit"* — so satisfying the envelope is not the end of the test. [Delivering a Legal Body Check: Technique](#delivering-a-legal-body-check-technique) prices both.

That last point is the one that separates a coached player from an amateur. **The check starts with your stick on the puck.** If your stick is up, you are about to commit a cross-check, a high stick, or a head-contact penalty. If your stick is down on the puck, your hands are down, your elbows are down, and your shoulder arrives where it should.

### Delivering a Legal Body Check: Technique

```facts
Rule: Not legal where checking is barred — USA Hockey 604(a) at 12U and below, all girls'/women's and non-check adult; Hockey Canada 7.3 at U13 and below, in female hockey, and in any other division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved
Rule: IIHF women's play permits bodychecking only with clear intention of playing or gaining possession of the puck, subject to the exception it sets out; any move to step or glide into an opponent is at least a minor (Rule 101.1). Britain's age is in IHUK's Rules of Competition, not either rule book
Rule: A body check is not legal in a CARHA-affiliated adult league either — that book has no checking division, 49(a) makes one a minor or a major anywhere on the ice and a major where it injures, and 30(a) costs you the rest of the game with 32(d) adding the next one
Technique: Where you may check at all, play the body, not the head — the point of contact is the chest or upper body, square
Never: Extend the body upward or outward to reach — NHL Rule 48.1 counts that as evidence head contact was avoidable
Technique: Elbows down, hands down — an extended elbow is elbowing (NHL Rule 45.1) whether or not it injures
Rule: Getting low means your own base, not where you hit — lowering your body to check at or below their knees is clipping (NHL and IIHF 44.1 word for word, USA Hockey 639, Hockey Canada 8.7, drawn at the hips), and NHL 44.3 makes the major mandatory where it injures, 44.5 the game misconduct with it
Rule: Kneeing is leading with or extending your knee; Hockey Canada 7.8 is wider — an opponent's knee "on an otherwise legal hit" — a double Minor there, a minor plus a misconduct at USA Hockey 628(a), neither a bare minor; NHL and IIHF 50 start at one, and a major brings an automatic game misconduct
Action: Where you may check at all, hit through rather than at — skate through the contact with your feet moving so you finish standing over the puck
Rule: Charging is judged on distance travelled (NHL Rule 42.1); IIHF Rule 42.1 penalises building speed by "multiple strides", USA Hockey and CARHA 52 more than two (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only), Hockey Canada two or more
Rule: Never leave your feet to check — jumping is charging in its own right, whatever the stride count (NHL Rule 42.1, a player who "skates, jumps into or charges an opponent in any manner"; IIHF Rule 42.1, one who "jumps to check an opponent")
Technique: For an open-ice check, where the game you are in permits one, the opponent must be one stride away — drive the shoulder into the chest, do not lunge forward
Never: Hit a player who is turned toward the boards
```

**Play the body, not the head.** The point of contact is the chest or the upper body, square. Do not extend upward or outward to reach — NHL Rule 48.1 lists "unnecessary extension of the body upward or outward" as evidence that head contact was avoidable, and IIHF Rule 48.1 is blunter still: "There is no clean check to the head or neck."

**Elbows down, hands down.** An extended elbow is elbowing (NHL Rule 45.1) regardless of whether it causes injury. Hands and forearms into a check are roughing or head contact.

**And down has a floor. Getting low is about your own base, not about where you hit them.** The envelope above puts the legal contact area *above the knees and at or below the shoulders* — and it is the upper limit that this document keeps returning to. ⚠️ **The lower one has two separate fouls under it, and neither is scoped to a division.**

**Clipping is what the checker commits by going low, and the NHL and the IIHF write it in the same words.** **Rule 44.1** in both books: clipping is *"the act of throwing the body across or below the knees of an opponent from any direction"*, and then the sentence that reaches you — *"A Player may not deliver a check in a 'clipping' manner, nor lower their own body position to deliver a check on or below an opponent's knees."* Both add that an illegal *"low hit"* is a check delivered by a player *"who may or may not have both skates on the ice, with their sole intent to check the opponent in the area of their knees"* (the IIHF's wording is quoted throughout here, identical in its 2025/26 Version 1.1 and 2026/27 editions; the NHL's Rule 44.1 is the same text with masculine pronouns and a lower-case *player*, so *"their own body position"* reads *"his own body position"* there). ⚠️ **And the IIHF publishes the case it has in mind, which is a hip check.** **Situation 44.1** of its Situation Handbook — the same words in the 2025/26 Version 1.1 and the 2026/27 editions — has a defender skating backwards who *"lowers the body in an attempt to make a hip check"* and is *"so low that contact is in the area of A16’s knees"*, and answers: *"No. This would result in a penalty for clipping."* **So the low hip check is not a tradition the rule tolerates; it is the illustration the rule is written against.** **USA Hockey files it inside Rule 639 and requires deliberateness** — clipping there is *"the act of deliberately leaving the feet or lowering the body for the purpose of making contact with the opponent at or below the knees."* ⚠️ **Hockey Canada draws the line higher up the body than any of them**, at **Rule 8.7**: clipping is where a player *"uses their body to make contact below an opponent's hips"* — the hips, not the knees — *"This may take the form of a player lowering their body prior to making a check or being checked."* Its **Interpretation 1** is written at exactly the habit this section teaches: *"While players may attempt to lower their centre of gravity, in preparation for a hit, the onus is on the player lowering themselves to ensure that they do not commit a clipping infraction, by contacting their opponent at or below the knee area."*

**Kneeing is a different foul, and under Hockey Canada it does not need a bad check at all.** Three of the four books reach it only through intent or a lead: **NHL 50.1** makes kneeing *"the act of a player leading with his knee and in some cases extending his leg outwards to make contact with his opponent"*, and **IIHF 50.1** is that sentence in the plural — *"their knee"*, *"their leg"*, *"their opponent"*, identical in the 2025/26 Version 1.1 and 2026/27 editions — and **USA Hockey's Rule 628** Note reads *"leading with or extending their knee outwards for the purpose of making contact, or attempting to do so, with the opponent."* **Hockey Canada's Rule 7.8 is the outlier and it is the wider one:** kneeing is *"the act of a player making contact with an opponent where the knee is the principle point of contact"*, and — the limb that defeats the envelope above — *"[t]his may take the form of a player making contact with an opponent's knee **on an otherwise legal hit**, leading into a hit with their knee, or deliberately extending their leg outwards to make contact with an opponent."* ⚠️ **So under that book a check that satisfies every other part of the envelope is still kneeing if your knee is what arrives.**

**And neither foul is capped at a minor — for kneeing, two of the books write no bare minor at all.** ⚠️ Hockey Canada 7.8(a) **is a double Minor, 7.8(b) lets the referee assess a major plus a game misconduct** *"based on the degree of violence of the impact"* **with nobody hurt** and makes that pair compulsory where a kneeing infraction that would otherwise be a double minor injures, and **7.8(c)** is a match penalty — with **7.8(d)** providing that *"[n]o Misconduct penalty may be assessed for kneeing"*, so there is no ten-minute rung between them. **USA Hockey 628(a)** is a minor **plus a misconduct**, 628(b) a major plus a game misconduct for reckless endangerment, 628(c) a match penalty — and its **Summary of Penalties** files 628(a,b) under *"Minor plus Misconduct or Major plus Game Misconduct"*, with no bare-minor entry anywhere. **The NHL and the IIHF do start at a bare minor** (50.2 in each): the NHL runs 50.3 major, 50.5 a game misconduct automatic with that major, 50.4 a match; the IIHF stops at **50.3**, a major plus an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment, because it writes no match penalty anywhere in the book. **Clipping runs the same shape** — NHL 44.2 minor, **44.3 a major that is mandatory *"[i]f an injury occurs as a result of this 'clipping' check"***, 44.5 a game misconduct mandatory with it, 44.4 a match; IIHF 44.2 and 44.3, and no match; Hockey Canada 8.7(a) to (c), with 8.7(d) again barring a misconduct rung; USA Hockey inside 639(a), (b) and (c).

**Both bind your division.** Neither rule in any of the four books carries an age, division or classification clause — the same thing that is true of boarding, charging, checking from behind and head contact, and for the same reason: it is Hockey Canada's 7.3 and USA Hockey's 604 that are scoped, and nothing else. (USA Hockey's Casebook does write one carve-out, and it is for a different sport: *"There is no penalty for kneeing in sled hockey."*) **And both are on USA Hockey's progressive-suspension list** — **Rule 411(a)** names Rule 628 and Rule 639 among its aggressive infractions, so a third major in a season costs three further games there, the ladder set out under [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight). ⚠️ **Two more books on disk price them differently again.** **CARHA** — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — writes **no clipping rule at all**, and puts kneeing in with elbowing at **Rule 56(a)**: *"A Minor penalty or, at the discretion of the Referee, a Major penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty shall be assessed any player who fouls an opponent in any manner with their elbow or knee"*, with **56(b)** a major where it injures. ⚠️ **That rule writes no definition of kneeing at all** — no lead, no extension, no principal point of contact, just the knee — so its wording is wider than any of the four above, and **Rule 56 itself carries no match tier**. The **PWHL** carries the NHL's Rules 44 and 50 unrenumbered and almost word for word. 🇬🇧 **And for a British reader the IIHF text above is the whole of it.** The **Motorpoint EIHL Casebook 26-27** supersedes the IIHF where it speaks, and on these two fouls it does not speak — neither Rule 44 nor Rule 50 appears in its contents list or in its body, and the words *kneeing* and *clipping* appear nowhere in it, so under its own Preamble the IIHF book and Situation Handbook remain *"the official ruling"* for both. **Nor do the British competition regulations reach them:** IHUK’s *Rules of Competition* for the junior, NIHL, WNIHL and U10 competitions, and the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27, use neither word. ⚠️ **And there is no published interpretation of kneeing to soften the rule text above** — the IIHF Situation Handbook prints *"No situation."* under Rule 50 in both editions, where its Rule 44 carries the hip-check ruling quoted above. **The EIHL Casebook is revised in-season by email and on that league’s website**, and the copy read here carries three version stamps that disagree with each other, so an Elite League player should check the current release rather than this paragraph.

**So where this document tells you to get low, it means your centre of gravity.** [The wide low base](#the-wide-low-base) in §7, and *"get **under** them — your shoulder beneath their armpit"* in [§9](#defending-the-net-front), are instructions about your own hips and knees. They are not instructions about the height of the contact, and the moment your body drops to reach an opponent at or below their knees you have left the envelope above and entered Rule 44.

**Hit through, not at.** Skate through the contact with your legs, keeping your feet moving. Stopping at the point of contact and swinging your upper body is how you end up off balance and reaching. Driving *through* on your skates keeps you upright and — the actual point — leaves you standing over the puck when they are not.

**Time it, don't chase it.** Charging is defined by distance travelled and speed built up. NHL Rule 42.1: "Charging shall mean the actions of a player who, as a result of distance traveled, shall violently check an opponent in any manner." USA Hockey's enforcement standard puts a number on it — the **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking** (CHARGING): "A player cannot take more than two strides or travel an excessive distance to accelerate through a body check for the purpose of punishing the opponent." **Rule 607 (Charging)** carries the same test in its own words. **CARHA Rule 52's Note sets the identical threshold** — *"[i]f more than two steps or strides are taken, it shall be considered a charge"* (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). **Hockey Canada writes the two-stride test straight into the definition** — Rule 7.4 lists charging as, among other things, when a player *"builds up speed by taking two or more strides immediately prior to making contact"* or *"[t]ravels an excessive distance with the sole purpose of delivering a hit"* (wording identical in the 2024-2026 and 2026-2028 Playing Rules). **IIHF Rule 42.1 carries a stride element too, without a number on it:** charging is *"the actions of a Player who either jumps to check an opponent, builds up speed by taking multiple strides immediately prior to making contact and / or travels an excessive distance with the sole purpose of delivering such a hit and / or violently checks an opponent in any manner."* That is the test a British player is officiated against — closer to Hockey Canada's construction than to the NHL's, but with "multiple" left to the referee rather than a stride count. **If you are winding up from across the ice, you have already committed the penalty.**

**And never leave your feet.** The stride count is not the whole of the rule, and a player who learns charging as a distance test can miss the other half of it. Both books put jumping in the opening sentence: the NHL penalises "a player who **skates, jumps into** or charges an opponent in any manner" (Rule 42.1), and the IIHF definition quoted above begins with a player who *"either **jumps to check an opponent**"* (Rule 42.1). Leaving your feet is charging in its own right — it does not matter how short your approach was, and it does not become legal because you took only one stride.

**For an open-ice check, the working constraint is tighter than either rule.** *Introduction To Body Contact* calls the open-ice shoulder check a *"[h]igh risk check, possibility of only partial or no body contact with the puck carrier"* and sets the condition for attempting one at all: *"Opponent must be close enough so that only one stride forward is required to make contact (gap control)"*. One stride, not two. If you need more than one stride to get there, you do not have the gap. Close it with your feet first, or angle instead. The same entry adds: *"[d]rive shoulder into opponent's chest, do not lunge forward"*.

**Never hit a player who is turned toward the boards.** This is not a technique point, it is the line between hockey and a spinal injury. See below.

### The late hit — and the narrow window for finishing a check

```facts
Rule: Not legal where checking is barred — USA Hockey 604(a) at 12U and below, all girls'/women's and non-check adult; Hockey Canada 7.3 at U13 and below, in female hockey, and in any other division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved
Rule: IIHF women's play permits bodychecking only with clear intention of playing or gaining possession of the puck, subject to the exception it sets out; any move to step or glide into an opponent is at least a minor (Rule 101.1). Britain's age is in IHUK's Rules of Competition, not either rule book
Action: Where you may check at all, check the puck carrier and check them now — never decide to deliver a check after the puck has gone; whether one already under way may be finished depends on your book
Rule: The NHL allows a check "rendered immediately following his loss of possession" — immediately, not a beat later; USA Hockey's Standard of Play penalises the same act under ROUGHING and calls it "finishing" the check
Rule: The IIHF writes the NHL's condition and adds a second on top of it — the check must also fall inside an "objectively reasonable window of opportunity" (IIHF 56.1), so a British reader's window is the narrower of the two
Rule: Being inside the IIHF’s late-hit window is not enough — a bodycheck on an opponent who no longer controls the puck and is either unaware of the hit or unprepared for it is automatically reckless, however legal it would be on a non-vulnerable opponent (IIHF glossary, "Vulnerable Position")
Rule: A late hit is not capped at a minor — IIHF 56.1 makes it reckless endangerment and 56.5 a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt; Hockey Canada 8.3(b) reaches the same tier on the degree of violence of the impact, and mandatorily where it injures
Rule: USA Hockey 640(b) makes a minor of every avoidable check on a player who has released a shot or pass — but it is written "(except Adult Male Classifications)", and there 640 Note 2 directs a roughing penalty instead, with no classification exception
Rule: Finishing a check — the Casebook's unnumbered rule of thumb — needs four things: prior effort to gain possession, an arm and stick length when the puck went, the check already in delivery, and minimised force taking no advantage of a vulnerable or defenceless opponent. USA Hockey Situation 10
Never: Skate one or two more strides after the puck is gone and then decide to deliver the check — USA Hockey's Casebook rules that out in those words
Rule: USA Hockey 640(g), a major plus a game misconduct, and 640(h), a match penalty, reach only acts under 640(b–f); a late hit reaches them through 640(b), which excepts Adult Male Classifications, so whether they reach a late hit there the book does not say and this document does not know
Rule: USA Hockey Rule 602(a) is limited by no classification and tied to no named foul at all — a match penalty for anyone who "recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player"
```

**Check the puck carrier, and check them now.** A check delivered after the player has passed or shot is a late hit, and each of the four books writes a narrow window for one that was already under way. **A fifth book writes no window, because it never grants the hit — do not go looking for one there.** CARHA Rule 49(a) prices the body check itself, a minor or a major *“at the discretion of the Referee, based upon the intent and the violence of the impact”*, for a player who *“intentionally bodies, pushes, shoves, stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent”* — no possession, timing or vulnerability limb anywhere in it, and the major is mandatory *“[w]hen injury results from intentional body contact”*. 49(c)’s only relief is where *“accidental contact has taken place”* (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). USA Hockey's **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking** penalises, under ROUGHING, "delivering a late check to a player who has already relinquished control of the puck by a pass or shot. This is oftentimes referred to as 'finishing' the check." The NHL allows a check "rendered immediately following his loss of possession" — immediately, not a beat later. 🇬🇧 **The IIHF writes that condition and then adds a second on top of it**, so a British reader's window is the narrower of the two: a player in possession *"may be checked legally, provided the check is rendered immediately following them 'loss of control' **and** their opponent still finds themself within an 'objectively reasonable window of opportunity' to deliver such a check"* (56.1). ⚠️ **And inside that window you still have to look at the player.** The IIHF defines a **Vulnerable Position** as a skater *“no longer in control or possession of the puck”* who is *“either not aware of an impending hit or they are not prepared for the hit”* — which is the late hit’s own fact pattern — and then removes the argument: *“A bodycheck to an opponent who is vulnerable is automatically considered reckless even if that bodycheck would be considered legal to a non-vulnerable opponent.”* **If they have not seen you coming, or are not set for it, being inside the window does not save the hit.** ⚠️ **And the late hit is not capped at a minor.** IIHF 56.1 says a late hit *"constitutes reckless endangerment"* and **56.5 reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt**; Hockey Canada 8.3(b) reaches the same tier on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* with nobody hurt, and makes it mandatory where it injures. **And USA Hockey's window is the narrowest of the four — not the absence of one.** 640(b) makes a minor of every *avoidable* check on a player who has released a shot or pass — the rule says they *"are not eligible to be body checked"*, and the Declaration of Player Safety calls *"finishing the check"* an *"unacceptable action"* because it is *"meant to intimidate or punish the opponent with no intent to gain possession of the puck"* — wording the book's Glossary repeats under *"Late Body Check"* and widens, to *"no intent, or possibility, to gain possession"*. **Its separate Casebook is what says when contact already under way is not a late hit** — and, like Hockey Canada's 8.3, it measures that as a distance. The Casebook offers it as a *"general rule of thumb"* rather than as a test, and under it a check may be finished where the defending player *"first makes an effort to gain possession of the puck and is an arm's length plus stick length away from the opponent when the puck was dumped"*, the check *"was in the process of being delivered when control of the puck was relinquished"*, and the checker is *"expected to minimize the force used in this instance **and not take advantage of a vulnerable or defenseless opponent**"* (Standard of Play, Situation 10). ⚠️ **That is four conditions and you need all four to finish a check** — though **the passage numbers none of them**, and the last of the four is a separate sentence carrying two obligations at once. ⚠️ **They are not the whole of what that passage allows, either.** It goes on to a second and separate route to legal contact: *"Contact may also be legal provided the defending player maintains their normal skating lane and reasonable foot speed. In this instance, the obligation is on the attacking player to go around the defender."* **With a limit attached, and it is the one that catches you:** *"if the defender initiates contact by stopping or changing their skating lane to cut off the opponent, an interference penalty would be the correct call."* **And lateness is an express test there rather than merely evidence of avoidability:** *"It is not acceptable to skate one or two more strides after the puck is gone and then decide to deliver the check."* That named minor is written *"except Adult Male Classifications"* — but it is not a free hit there either: 640 Note 2 directs a roughing penalty for *"an avoidable body check to an opponent … who is no longer in control of the puck"* with no classification exception. ⚠️ **And the minor is not USA Hockey's ceiling either — the tier is written into Rule 640 itself, and it needs no argument from anywhere else.** **640(g)**: *"A major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of any actions falling under Rule 640(b, c, d, e or f)."* **640(h)**: *"A match penalty for reckless endangerment may also be assessed under sub-sections (b), (c), (d), (e) or (f) of this rule."* ⚠️ **Note which sub-sections that reaches.** Both get to the late hit through **(b)** — the one written *"(except Adult Male Classifications)"* — while (d), a check delivered *"with no effort to gain possession of the puck"* with the stick blade above the knees, and (e), a check on an opponent already *"physically engaged for possession of the puck"*, carry no such limit. **Whether 640(g) reaches a late hit in adult men's hockey, where (b) is not assessed, the book does not say, and this document does not know.** **Rule 602(a) does not depend on the answer**: *"A match penalty shall be assessed to any player or team official who recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player or team official"* — tied to no named foul at all. ⚠️ **And a 640(g) major does not end with the game.** Rule 640 is on Rule 411(a)'s aggressive-infractions list, so that major counts on the season tally: a third costs three further games, a fourth five, and a fifth a suspension until a hearing. The whole ladder is set out under [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight), below.

### Four fouls no division is exempt from

```facts
Rule: Boarding (7.2, 603), charging (7.4, 607) and checking from behind (7.5, 608) carry no division clause, and Hockey Canada's 7.6 and 7.7 both open by banning head contact outright — all four bind your division; 7.6(a) penalises it in minor and female hockey, 7.7 in Junior and Senior
Never: Make contact in an opponent's head, face or neck with any part of the body, equipment or stick — the onus is on the checker (USA Hockey Rule 620)
Rule: IIHF 48.1 opens "There is no clean check to the head or neck" and "supersedes all similar actions regarding hits to the head and neck" — an absolute the NHL's 48.1 does not write; both then bar a hit where the head was the main point of contact and it was avoidable
Never: Build speed into a check to punish rather than to win the puck — IIHF "multiple strides", USA Hockey and CARHA 52 more than two (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only), Hockey Canada two or more
Rule: Charging is building speed to punish, or a running or jumping approach, and is not capped at a minor in any book — NHL 42.2 minor, 42.3 major, 42.4 match; USA Hockey 607(a) writes no bare minor and 607(e) is a match penalty
Rule: Hockey Canada prices charging this way — 7.4(a) is a minor, 7.4(b) is a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence with nobody hurt and mandatory on injury, and 7.4(c) is a match
Rule: A fifth book ejects you for a charge that injures — CARHA 52(a) is a minor or a major at the referee's discretion, and "If injury results, a Major penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty shall be assessed"; 52(b) compels that pair in a goalkeeper's crease (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: A CARHA stride count picks the charging tier there and never whether you may hit at all — that book has no checking division, and 49(a) makes a body check a minor or a major anywhere on the ice (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: The IIHF writes no match penalty, so Britain’s charging ceiling inside the game is a major plus a game misconduct — unlike head contact at 48.3, 42.4 leaves that misconduct to the referee, except on a head or face injury in the EIHL and on any major in IIHF men’s junior and British women’s hockey
Rule: Head contact is not capped at a minor in any book — USA Hockey 620(a) writes no bare minor and 620(c) is a match; Hockey Canada reaches a match at 7.6(c) and 7.7(c); the NHL rules out a major and a game misconduct (48.3, 48.4) but 48.5 is a match penalty
Rule: USA Hockey's rule says a head-contact match penalty "may also be assessed" (620(c)); its Casebook makes it mandatory — a check using excessive force to intimidate or punish that contacts the head "must be assessed as a match penalty" (Rule 620, Situation 4)
Rule: Hockey Canada ejects for head contact with nobody hurt — 7.6(b) gives the referee a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence in minor and female hockey, and 7.7(b) assesses that pair flatly for high violence in Junior and Senior
Rule: NHL 48.5 needs intent — a match only where the player "attempted to or deliberately injured" the opponent — and the IIHF, Britain's book, writes no match penalty anywhere, so its head-contact ceiling inside the game is a major plus an automatic game misconduct (48.3)
Rule: The IIHF moves the sanction off the ice rather than removing it — 42.5 and 48.4 allow Supplementary Discipline above charging and head contact, 20.7 requires the Referee to report all majors, and 28.1 lets the Proper Authorities act "whether or not such offense has been penalized by the Referee(s)"
```

These are not ordinary penalties. Treat them as things you simply do not do.

**All four bind every division, including yours if you play non-check.** Hockey Canada scopes only 7.3 — the rule that bars body checking — and USA Hockey only 604; boarding (7.2, 603), charging (7.4, 607) and checking from behind (7.5, 608) carry no division or classification clause at all, and Hockey Canada's two head-contact rules both open by banning it outright — **7.6** *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck"* and **7.7**, for Junior and Senior, *"There is no legal contact to the head, face, or neck"* — each putting it on the players to avoid contact *"at all times"*. **They split so that minor and female hockey and Junior and Senior are priced separately, not so that either is left out**, and the tiers the two rules set are not the same.

**Checking to the head.** **The IIHF states the principle outright — IIHF Rule 48.1: *"There is no clean check to the head or neck."* The other books get there by describing the illegal hit instead.** NHL Rule 48.1 prohibits a hit where the head "was the main point of contact and such contact to the head was avoidable." USA Hockey Rule 620 goes further: any contact to the head, face or neck with any part of the body, equipment or stick, with the onus on the checker — Rule 620's own Note puts it as avoiding "placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger of potential injury", and the *"regardless as to size differential"* wording is the Standard of Play's, not the rule's. The **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking** (HEAD CONTACT) spells out how far that onus runs: "The onus is on the player delivering the check, regardless as to size differential, to not make contact in the head/neck area of the opponent." **And the tiers run further than the NHL's do.** Under the **NHL** an illegal check to the head is a bare minor and, short of intent, nothing else — **48.3** reads *"There is no provision for a major penalty for this rule"* and **48.4** the same for a game misconduct — with **48.5** a match penalty only where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured"* the opponent. **USA Hockey 620(a)** writes no bare minor at all — a minor plus a misconduct, or a major plus a game misconduct — and **620(c)** reaches a match penalty. ⚠️ **"Reaches" understates that one, because the playing rule and the Casebook are worded differently.** The rule says a match penalty *"may also be assessed"* for head contact; the Casebook says *must*, for exactly the hit this section is about. **Rule 620, Situation 4** asks what degree of force a match penalty needs, and answers that *"[a]ny check using excessive force for the purpose of intimidating or punishing the opponent that makes direct contact with the head area of the opponent must be assessed as a match penalty."* **Situation 3 does the same one rung down**: *"[a]ny time the Referee deems the contact to the head area to be intentional or a reckless act, a major plus game misconduct or match penalty must be assessed."* **So the discretion the rule appears to leave is not discretion about the deliberate hit.** It is about everything below it. **Hockey Canada splits the rule by division:** **7.6** covers minor and female hockey, where 7.6(a) is a bare minor, and **7.7** covers Junior and Senior, where the floor is a minor *and* a misconduct. ⚠️ **Between those floors and those match penalties both rules reach a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt — and the two clauses are not shaped alike.** **7.6(b)** leaves it to the referee: that pair *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"*, and then compulsory — *"must be assessed"* — where a head-contact infraction that would otherwise be a minor injures the player. **7.7(b)** gives the referee no discretion at all: in Junior and Senior *"a Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty will be assessed for any Head Contact infraction with a high degree of violence"*, and a further limb assesses the same pair where a player is *"fouled or hit illegally"* and, as a result, hits their head on the glass, ice or boards *"in such a manner that results in a head injury."* **Both also carry the cross-check limb** — a major plus a game misconduct for striking an opponent above the shoulders with a cross-check, *"whether or not injury results"* (§9 sets that out). Only then do both reach a match penalty, at 7.6(c) and 7.7(c). **So under either Hockey Canada rule you can be ejected for head contact without anyone being hurt.** **And in minor and female hockey those minors are counted as well as priced:** three head-contact penalties in one game bring a Game Ejection under Rule 4.8(b)(ii), which is set out in [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight) below. **The IIHF** starts at a discretionary minor (48.2) and stops at a major plus an automatic game misconduct (48.3) — **no match penalty, because its book contains none anywhere.**

**Charging.** Building speed into a check to punish rather than to win the puck, or a running or jumping approach. **The written threshold differs by book and the difference is one stride:** USA Hockey and CARHA Rule 52 penalise *more than* two strides of acceleration (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only), Hockey Canada penalises *two or more*, the **IIHF** — and so Britain — sets no number either, defining charging as a player who *"builds up speed by taking multiple strides"* (Rule 42.1), and the NHL sets no number at all. Under Hockey Canada, two strides is already charging. **And charging is not capped at a minor
in any book:** the NHL runs 42.2 minor, 42.3 major, **42.4 a match penalty**; USA Hockey 607(a)
writes no bare minor at all and reaches **607(e)**, where a match penalty *"may also be assessed for charging"*;
Hockey Canada 7.4(a) is a mandatory bare minor, but 7.4(b) lets the referee assess a major plus a
game misconduct *"based on the degree of violence of the impact"* **with nobody hurt** — and makes
that pair mandatory where a charge that would otherwise be a minor injures the opponent; **7.4(c)**
is a match penalty. **And the CARHA book has a rung the referee does not weigh either:** **52(a)** leaves the minor-or-major choice to the referee, then adds *"If injury results, a Major penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty shall be assessed"* — so under that book a charge that hurts somebody ends your night wherever on the ice you delivered it, and **52(b)** compels the same pair for charging a goalkeeper in the crease (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). **The IIHF is the exception, and it is Britain's book** — it
writes no match penalty for charging because it writes none anywhere in the rulebook, so the
ceiling there *inside the game* is a major plus a game misconduct, with 42.5 allowing Supplementary Discipline above
it at the Proper Authorities' discretion (Rule 42.4 — which, unlike its head-contact
equivalent at 48.3, does not make the game misconduct automatic — though in the **EIHL** it is
mandatory once the major is for a foul causing an injury to the face or head, which
[Boarding](#boarding-and-the-player-who-cannot-see-you-coming) sets out, along with the two other populations where any major carries the game misconduct with it).

### Boarding, and the player who cannot see you coming

