```facts
Rule: NHL Rule 41.1 is boarding — checking or pushing a defenceless opponent so they hit the boards "violently or dangerously" — and the definition names the icing/offside case in terms
Rule: Under USA Hockey boarding is not only a hit — its Rule 603 Note reaches a player who "pushes, trips or body checks" an opponent dangerously into the boards, and its Casebook prefers the boarding call to tripping (Rule 603, Situation 3)
Rule: Boarding is not capped at two minutes — NHL 41.2 minor, 41.3 major, 41.4 match, and 41.5 adds a game misconduct whenever a major follows an injury to the face or head; USA Hockey 603(a) writes no bare minor at all and 603(c) is a match penalty
Rule: Hockey Canada 7.2(b) reaches a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence of the impact with nobody hurt, and makes that pair mandatory where a board injures; 7.2(c) is a match penalty
Rule: Under the IIHF — Britain's book — boarding reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt (41.4) and no match penalty above it, only 41.5; unlike checking from behind at 43.3, that game misconduct is not automatic — except in the EIHL, in IIHF men's junior and in British women's hockey
Rule: The EIHL supersedes the IIHF here, and its Casebook prints one sentence under boarding, charging and elbowing alike: where a major under that rule is for a foul "resulting in an injury to the face or head of an opponent, a game misconduct shall be imposed"
Never: Hit a player playing an obvious icing or offside puck into the boards — boarding in NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 alike; USA Hockey reaches only the icing half, at 624(d)
Never: Arrive as the third man and hit someone already engaged in a battle — USA Hockey deems both players defenceless
Rule: Under IIHF, a check on an opponent who no longer has the puck and is either unaware of the hit or unprepared for it is automatically reckless, however clean it would otherwise be (IIHF glossary, "Vulnerable Position")
```

**Boarding.** NHL Rule 41.1: checking or pushing "a defenseless opponent in such a manner that causes the opponent to hit or impact the boards violently or dangerously." ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey, though, boarding is not only a hit — and this is the half of the rule most players never meet.** Three of the four books define it by the check: *"checks or pushes"* in NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 alike. **USA Hockey's Rule 603 Note names a third action, and it is a stick foul** — boarding is *"the action where a player pushes, trips or body checks an opponent causing them to go dangerously into the boards."* Its Casebook takes that literally: at **Rule 603, Situation 3** a trailing player *"trips the opponent and causes them to lose control and crash into the end boards"*, and the answer to whether boarding may be called is *"Yes… Even though a tripping penalty may also be appropriate, the boarding call is preferred to draw attention to the more aggressive infraction."* ⚠️ **So under USA Hockey a stick trip that sends somebody into the end boards is not a two-minute trip. It is boarding, the referee is told to prefer that call, and 603(a) writes no bare minor — so the floor is a minor plus a misconduct.** A trip near the end wall is not the cheap option. **Back to the NHL's rule, and the specific case it calls out:** "Any unnecessary contact with a player playing the puck on an obvious 'icing' or 'off-side' play which results in that player hitting or impacting the boards is 'boarding'." A player skating hard to touch up an icing is not looking for you — and that clause is in three books word for word: NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 all print *"off-side"* hyphenated, and the only difference between them is the capitalised *"Player"* the IIHF and Hockey Canada use; USA Hockey reaches only the icing half of it, at 624(d), without the offside half and without naming boarding. ⚠️ **And boarding is not capped at two minutes.** The NHL runs 41.2 minor, 41.3 major, 41.4 match, and **41.5 adds a game misconduct whenever a major follows an injury to the face or head**. USA Hockey 603(a) has no bare minor at all; Hockey Canada 7.2(b) assesses a major plus a game misconduct *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"* — **with nobody hurt** — and *then* makes that same pair mandatory where a board that would otherwise be a minor injures; and under the IIHF book — the British one — 41.4 reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt. **And the cost can outlive the game, for most of these fouls and not just boarding** — see [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight) below. **So on an icing race, take the puck and let him go.** The four-book comparison is in [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md). **And boarding reaches a match penalty in three of the four books:** NHL **41.4** where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent by boarding"*, **USA Hockey 603(c)** — a match penalty that *"may also be assessed for boarding"*, above a 603(a) that writes no bare minor at all, so the act it hangs on is the boarding itself — and **Hockey Canada 7.2(c)** where a player *"deliberately attempts to or deliberately injures an opponent by boarding"*. **The IIHF stops at a major plus a game misconduct (41.4), and that game misconduct is not automatic** — unlike its checking-from-behind and head-contact equivalents at 43.3 and 48.3. ⚠️ **And that discretion is not everyone's.** IIHF **201.1** makes the game misconduct automatic on *any* major in its Men's Junior U20 and U18 categories — *"in any case"* — and the **IHUK In-House Rules' Rule 101** writes the same for **women's hockey in Britain**, *"in all cases"*, with no age limb attached at all, so in either of those a major for boarding or charging ends your game ([If you play in Britain](#if-you-play-in-britain)). ⚠️ **One British league takes that discretion away from everyone, and it does so for three fouls at once.** The **EIHL Casebook 26-27** prints the same sentence under its **Rule 41 – Boarding**, **Rule 42 – Charging** and **Rule 42 – Elbowing** headings: *"When a major penalty is imposed under this rule for a foul resulting in an injury to the face or head of an opponent, a game misconduct shall be imposed."* ***Shall*, where IIHF 41.4, 42.4 and 45.4 all say the referee *"may"*** — and the IIHF's trigger is reckless endangerment, where the EIHL's is the injury. It can do that because its Preamble states that the Casebook's interpretations *"will replace or in specific cases, supersede, the ruling that is currently within the IIHF Rule Book"*, and that the IIHF book governs only *"[f]or all rules not mentioned in the EIHL Casebook"*. ⚠️ **The Casebook heads its elbowing section *"RULE 42 – ELBOWING"*, which is its own numbering and not the IIHF's** — elbowing is **Rule 45** in both the 2025/26 and 2026/27 IIHF books, and the Casebook prints *42* for charging and elbowing alike, in its contents list as well as its body. Quote the words, not that number. **This binds the Elite League**, and no British competition regulation held here adopts it — the word *Casebook* returns **zero** in the IHUK In-House Rules for 2026-27 and 2025-26, in the England Ice Hockey Rules and Regulations, and in all four IHUK *Rules of Competition* on disk.

**One narrow thing USA Hockey's boarding rule does permit — set out here because the rule sets it out, and not as anything to aim for.** The sentence immediately after 603(a) reads: *"'Rolling' an opponent along the boards where they are attempting to go through too small an opening is not considered boarding."* **Read carefully what that is and what it is not.** It is a limit on the word *boarding*, covering a player who tried to force a gap along the wall that was never there — the same idea as being entitled to the ice you are already standing on. ⚠️ **It is not a technique, it is not a licence to drive anybody into the wall, and it is the only book of the four that writes it**: no counterpart appears in NHL Rule 41, IIHF Rule 41 or Hockey Canada Rule 7.2. **And it limits that one word only.** It does nothing to checking from behind (608), head contact (620) or charging (607); it does not touch 603's own Note, which still reaches a push, a trip or a check that sends an opponent dangerously into the boards; and it does not reach 602(a)'s match penalty for recklessly endangering an opponent, which is tied to no named foul at all. **Nothing about knowing this sentence should change how you play the wall.**

**And one more that is easy to commit without realising:** USA Hockey's **Declaration of Player Safety, Fair Play & Respect** states that "when two or more players are physically engaged for control of the puck along the boards, they are considered to be vulnerable or defenseless," and any body check delivered to a player already engaged with another skater "is considered dangerous, careless or reckless (unacceptable) and must be penalized accordingly." ⚠️ **And the playing rule is wider than the Declaration, in exactly the limb that decides where it reaches you.** **Rule 640(e)** assesses a minor to any player who *"delivers a body check to an opponent who is physically engaged for possession of the puck with one or more other players"*, and its Note reads that *"[w]hen two or more players are physically engaged for possession of the puck they are considered vulnerable or defenseless and are not eligible to be body checked."* **Neither sentence names a location.** The Declaration's *definition* is the sentence scoped to the boards; the numbered rule is scoped nowhere, in either limb — and the Declaration's own operative sentence, the *"must be penalized accordingly"* one quoted just above, names no location either. ⚠️ **And the net front does not rest on inferring anything from that silence, because USA Hockey's Glossary states it.** Its defined term **Physical Engagement**, the book's own noun for what 640(e) calls being *"physically engaged for possession of the puck"*, reads: *"Two players who are in pursuit of the puck are allowed to use competitive contact provided that possession of the puck remains the sole objective of the two players. **This includes opposing players competing for the puck in front of the goal or along the boards.**"* So the rule reaches a net-front battle exactly as it reaches a wall battle. **Nor does it carry a classification limb**, unlike **640(b)** in the same rule, which is written *"(except Adult Male Classifications)"*. ⚠️ **And it is not capped at a minor:** **640(g)** assesses a major plus a game misconduct to a player who *"recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of any actions falling under Rule 640(b, c, d, e or f)"*, and **640(h)** allows a match penalty under the same sub-sections. ⚠️ **And USA Hockey tells the official to stop play before that hit ever lands.** Its Casebook at **Rule 632, Situation 1** puts two players engaged in the corner and a third who *"sees the two players physically engaged and skates in their direction with the clear intent to deliver a check to the opposing player"*, asks whether the Referee should stop play, and answers *"Yes."* The direction is unconditional: *"The Referee must stop play the instant they realize that unnecessary contact will result from allowing the play to continue. If contact is made in this instance, they must assess a penalty to the third player who entered the corner and made no effort to legally play the puck and instead body checked a vulnerable or defenseless opponent."* **Do not arrive as the third man and hit someone already in a battle.** Take the puck instead.

**The IIHF book gets to the same place by a different route, and a British reader should know which one binds them.** It has no board-battle clause, but it defines a **Vulnerable Position** in its glossary: *"A skater is considered to be in a vulnerable position when they are no longer in control or possession of the puck and they are either not aware of an impending hit or they are not prepared for the hit. A bodycheck to an opponent who is vulnerable is automatically considered reckless even if that bodycheck would be considered legal to a non-vulnerable opponent."* A player wrestling for a loose puck with someone else has neither possession nor any preparation for a third arrival, so that hit is reckless under the IIHF book too — arrived at through the state of the player rather than the fact of the battle. **Reckless matters, because recklessness is the trigger for the major-and-game-misconduct tier** in IIHF Rules 42.4, 43.3 and 48 — and in British women's hockey, at every age and adult play included, any major brings a game misconduct anyway (In-House Rule 101's ejection bullet applies *"in all cases"* and states no age scope), with junior hockey reading the same way on In-House Rule 201's scoping of the IIHF junior rules.

### Checking from behind, and what the onus does and does not mean

```facts
Never: Check from behind — the NHL, IIHF and PWHL write no minor at all (43.2) and their major carries an automatic game misconduct; USA Hockey's 608(a) and Hockey Canada's 7.5(a) floors are not bare minors
Rule: Checking from behind reaches a match penalty at NHL 43.4, discretionary, and mandatorily at USA Hockey 608(c), Hockey Canada 7.5(c) and CARHA 53(b); the IIHF writes none anywhere in its book, but 20.4 rules a major plus automatic game misconduct "off the ice for the balance of the game"
Rule: The onus for checking from behind is USA Hockey Rule 608's — "The onus is on the player delivering the check to not hit from behind" — and Hockey Canada's, which puts it on the checker "in all circumstances"; NHL Rule 43 states no onus at all
Rule: "He turned at the last second" is no defence unless the turn was deliberate — a player who intentionally turns to create contact with their back draws the checker no penalty for checking from behind (NHL, IIHF and PWHL Rule 43.1; the IIHF adds that other penalties may still be assessed)
Rule: The turn-into-a-hit waiver is NHL, IIHF and PWHL only (43.1) — USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write none, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to 7.5(a) penalises the checker anyway: "the responsibility is always on the player delivering the check to avoid making contact with the back"
Rule: An onus written into a boarding rule is not strict liability — NHL Rule 41.1, IIHF Rule 41.1, PWHL Rule 41.1 and Hockey Canada Rule 7.2 all let the referee weigh whether the opponent put themself in a vulnerable position, and whether the check was unavoidable
Rule: USA Hockey's boarding rule is the one that attaches no balance — "The onus is on the player delivering the check to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger", with no however-clause (USA Hockey Rule 603 Note; its Casebook weighs force against preparedness at 603 Situation 2)
Never: Rely on the vulnerability-and-unavoidability balance the boarding rules allow — the two checking-from-behind onus books attach no "however", and a spinal injury is not undone by a favourable call
Rule: A fifth book has no rung that leaves you on the ice — CARHA 53(a) is a minor plus a game misconduct at the floor, a major plus a game misconduct compulsory on injury, and 53(b) is a flat match penalty for driving a player from behind into the boards or goal net (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: From behind, the instrument does not lower the tier — IIHF 43.1, Britain's book, reaches a hit "in any manner (i.e., high-sticking, cross-checking, etc.)" and 43.2 writes no minor, so a stick driven high into a player's numbers from behind is not a Rule 60 high stick
Rule: IIHF 43.1 names a destination as well as an act — "from behind into the boards, the goal frame, or on open ice" — and IIHF 5.1 makes "Player" cover goalkeepers, so a goaltender driven into their own net from behind is reached there, where that book writes no minor, not by charging
Rule: A sixth book states the missing rung outright — PWHL 43.2, "There is no provision for a minor penalty for checking from behind", with the major at 43.3 and the game misconduct automatic at 43.5
```

**Checking from behind.** NHL Rule 43.1 defines it as a check "delivered on a player who is not aware of the impending hit, therefore unable to protect or defend himself," with contact on the back part of the body. **There is no minor penalty for it** (NHL Rule 43.2) — it is a major, and a game misconduct is automatic whenever the major is called (43.5). IIHF **Rule 43** matches this: 43.2 states that "[t]his infraction is considered serious and dangerous, therefore there is no option to award a minor penalty", and 43.3 provides a major plus an automatic game misconduct. ⚠️ **And 43.1 prices the act rather than the instrument** — it reaches a player who hits an opponent from behind into the boards, the goal frame or on open ice *"in any manner (i.e., high-sticking, cross-checking, etc.)"*, **so a stick that arrives high into a player's numbers from behind is checking from behind**, with no minor rung, rather than a Rule 60 high stick. ⚠️ **And it names a destination as well as an act.** The **goal frame** sits in that list beside the boards, and **IIHF Rule 5.1** makes any reference to *"Player"* cover *"both skaters and goalkeepers"* — so a goaltender driven into their own net from behind is reached by **IIHF Rule 43**, where that book leaves the referee no minor to award, rather than by its charging rule, where 42.2 still gives them one. *(That Rule 43 reaches a goaltender is a reading of how the book is assembled rather than a sentence the IIHF prints: its Appendix II glossary defines a vulnerable position for* "a skater", *and its Situation Handbook prints* "No situation." *under Rule 43.)* **USA Hockey Rule 608** assesses minor-plus-misconduct or major-plus-game-misconduct (608(a)), a major plus game misconduct where an opponent is recklessly endangered or sent head first into the boards (608(b)), and a match penalty for reckless endangerment of a vulnerable opponent (608(c)). **Two of the books here provide a minor for it, and neither writes a bare one.** USA Hockey's, above, arrives with a misconduct — the lowest floor of the four, and the only one that leaves you on the ice. Hockey Canada's Rule 7.5(a) assesses "a Minor penalty **and** a Game Misconduct penalty" for hitting an opponent from behind anywhere on the ice, 7.5(b) lets the referee assess a major and game misconduct instead, *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"* and **with nobody hurt** — and where a checking-from-behind infraction that "would otherwise call for a Minor penalty" injures the player, that pair "must be assessed" — and **7.5(c) is a mandatory match penalty**, in its own words *"A Match penalty **will** be assessed to any player who pushes, body-checks, cross-checks, or hits an opposing player in any manner from behind into the boards or goal frame, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*, with no violence threshold and no injury threshold at all. **The NHL has a match penalty here too** — 43.4, at the referee's discretion, where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent by checking from behind."* So a Canadian reader should not infer that two minutes is on the table: under all four books it costs you more than two minutes, and under three of them your game. ⚠️ **A fifth book has no rung at all that leaves you on the ice.** **CARHA Rule 53(a)** — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, and no reader in minor hockey — assesses *"[a] Minor penalty plus a Game Misconduct or a Major penalty plus a Game Misconduct at the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact… to any player who intentionally pushes, body checks, or hits an opposing player from behind, anywhere on the ice"*, makes the major and game misconduct compulsory *"[i]f a player is injured"*, and adds that *"[a] Match penalty could also be assessed under this rule."* **Rule 53(b)** is that match penalty written flat, and it names the stick first, for a player *"high sticked, cross-checked, body-checked, pushed, hit or propelled in any manner from behind into the boards or goal net, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"* — and its Note tells referees they are *"instructed not to substitute other penalties when a player is checked from behind in any manner. This rule must be strictly enforced."* So the lowest tier in that book ends the game, and the relief USA Hockey 608(a) gives is not in it. **The onus is on the checker — and it is the North American minor-hockey books that say so.** USA Hockey states it in those words, with no qualification, in **Rule 608** itself: "The onus is on the player delivering the check to not hit from behind." Hockey Canada states the same principle in its own checking-from-behind rule, which reads *"In all circumstances the responsibility is on the player delivering the check to avoid"* contact to an opposing player's back. (Hockey Canada does use "the onus is always", but in Rule 8.5, on goaltender interference, which is a different rule and not this one.) **The NHL's rule contains no onus clause at all** — Rule 43 gives the definition, the penalty tiers and the intentional-turn carve-out, and nothing else. The only place the NHL's rulebook puts an onus on a checker is **Rule 41.1, boarding**, quoted below — everywhere else the word appears it is about maintaining your equipment. Do not cite Rule 43 for the onus. "He turned at the last second" is not a defence unless the turn was deliberate — NHL Rule 43.1 waives the penalty only when a player "intentionally turns his body to create contact with his back," and IIHF Rule 43.1 has its own carve-out, in its own words, for a player who "intentionally turns their back towards an opponent and puts themself in a vulnerable position" — adding, as the NHL does not, that "other penalties may still be assessed." So under the IIHF book what the carve-out waives is the checking-from-behind penalty specifically, not the checker's whole liability. Note which way that carve-out cuts: **a player who turns his back deliberately to draw the call gets nothing.** It is not a way to win a power play, and it is the position that breaks necks. **And that waiver is the NHL's, the IIHF's and the PWHL's.** USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write no such clause — and USA Hockey's separate Casebook writes none either: its Rule 608 situations set out when each tier must be assessed and attach no exception to a player who turned, requiring a major plus a game misconduct or a match penalty *"[i]n every instance where a player forcefully checks an opponent who is standing along the boards (back toward the middle of the ice)"* — and **Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.5(a)** puts the question directly — *"What penalty is assessed when a player, who is about to be checked, turns and, as a result, creates contact with the back?"* — and answers it the other way: the penalty goes to the checker, because *"[t]he responsibility is always on the player delivering the check to avoid making contact with the back."*

**Two things the word "onus" does not mean.** It is not strict liability — and the statement of that sits in the NHL's **boarding** rule, which is the one place the NHL's book puts an onus on a checker at all. Rule 41.1: "The onus is on the player applying the check to ensure his opponent is not in a defenseless position and if so, he must avoid or minimize contact. **However**, in determining whether such contact could have been avoided, the circumstances of the check, including whether the opponent put himself in a vulnerable position immediately prior to or simultaneously with the check or whether the check was unavoidable can be considered. This balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule." So a boarding call is judged on a balance, not on outcome alone. **That balance is not the NHL's alone, and it is not in every book either — four of the six books read here write it, and two do not.** **IIHF Rule 41.1 — the British reader's boarding rule — writes the same passage almost word for word**, with *"The responsibility is on the Player applying the check"* where the NHL says "onus", and the same *"However, in determining whether such contact could have been avoided, the circumstances of the check, including whether the opponent put themself in a vulnerable position immediately prior to or simultaneously with the check or whether the check was unavoidable can be considered. This balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule."* **Hockey Canada Rule 7.2 writes it too** — *"The duty is on the Player applying the check to ensure their opponent is not in a defenseless position"*, followed by the same however-clause, ending *"may be considered"* rather than "can be considered" and without the NHL's and IIHF's closing sentence — and Hockey Canada's boarding rule carries no division scope, so that balance is available in minor and female hockey as much as anywhere else. **The PWHL writes it a fourth time**, at its own Rule 41.1 and in the feminine: the onus sentence, the same however-clause weighing *"whether the opponent put herself in a vulnerable position immediately prior to or simultaneously with the check or whether the check was unavoidable"*, and the same closing *"This balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule."* **USA Hockey's is the outlier, and it runs against you as the checker:** Rule 603's Note states the onus flat — *"The onus is on the player delivering the check to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger"* — and attaches no balancing sentence to it at all. **CARHA is the other book without the balance, and it has no onus sentence either**: its Rule 49 turns a boarding or body-contact call on *"the intent and the violence of the impact"* in the referee's view, and Rule 49(c) gives no penalty at all where the contact was accidental. ⚠️ **Its Casebook is not silent, though, and what it writes is a different weighing.** Rule 603, Situation 2 restates the onus first, scoped — *"For USA Hockey youth games, the onus is on the player delivering the check to do so in a manner that does not place the opponent in danger"* — then asks *"was the check reckless and/or what was the vulnerability of the player being checked?"* and works it both ways: a penalty *"may be assessed when a player was not necessarily hit that hard, but was considered to be in a vulnerable or defenseless position when unnecessarily hit"*, and *"a penalty may not be warranted when a player is hit much harder, but their preparedness for the hit causes them to go only mildly into the boards."* **The Casebook prints that weighing twice, and the second printing is narrower.** Standard of Play, Situation 19 asks *"was the check **necessary** and/or what was the vulnerability of the player being checked?"* — *necessary* where Situation 2 says *reckless* — and its no-penalty example carries a condition Situation 2's does not: a penalty *"may not be warranted when a player is hit much harder as an opponent clearly attempted to gain possession of the puck, but their preparedness for the hit causes them to go only mildly into the boards."* **So even USA Hockey's own relief turns on your having been going for the puck.** **That is force weighed against the opponent's preparedness — not the vulnerability-and-unavoidability balance the NHL, the IIHF, Hockey Canada and the PWHL write**, so it is not the excuse those books offer, and it is not in the playing rule. **And note the limit of all of it.** Those balancing sentences are written into the boarding rules; the two books that state an onus for *checking from behind* — USA Hockey 608 and Hockey Canada's — attach no "however" to it at all, and Hockey Canada's says "in all circumstances". So the qualification you are relying on may not exist in your rulebook. **None of that is a licence to lean on it.** You cannot know in advance how a referee will weigh the balance, you are the only one of the two players who chose to arrive, and the reason to give a turned player the ice is that a spinal injury is not undone by a favourable call. Play it as though the onus were absolute; just do not tell a teammate the rulebook says it is.

### And what these fouls cost beyond tonight

```facts
Rule: A game misconduct can cost you the next game — USA Hockey 404(b) suspends you for the first one, and a second major in the same game costs two: the two games are 403(b)'s and are additional to any other suspension from the incident, and 404(b) calls them a minimum
Rule: USA Hockey 411(a) adds a three-game suspension on a third major of the season across its aggressive-infractions list — seventeen rules, boarding, charging, checking from behind and head contact among them, ending with 639 and Rule 640 Unnecessary Roughness (Roughing)
Rule: A 640(g) late-hit major is an aggressive infraction and counts on that tally, and USA Hockey 411(a) does not stop at three — a fourth major in the category is a further five-game suspension, a fifth a suspension until a hearing. Fighting is not on that list — 411's Note sends it to Rule 615(f)
Rule: NHL 23.6 suspends a second game misconduct in the Physical Infractions Category inside 41 consecutive regular-season games, and 23.5 does the same for stick fouls
Rule: Hockey Canada 4.8(c) suspends a game misconduct given in the last 10 minutes, in overtime or after the game — a minimum of the next game or seven days
Rule: The IIHF Rule Book writes no automatic next-game suspension for a player — 23.2 gives the Proper Authorities "full power to impose further disciplinary measures" and 28.1 lets them act "at their discretion"
Rule: The IIHF Rule Book is not the IIHF's whole discipline — Rules 23.3 to 23.6 name four disciplinary categories, including the Stick and Physical Infractions categories the NHL tariffs, and refer them to the separate IIHF Disciplinary Code, which is not held here and has not been checked
Rule: In Hockey Canada's Minor and Female divisions, three Minor or double Minor penalties for Rule 7.6 head contact in the same game bring a Game Ejection (Rule 4.8(b)(ii)) — and Rule 4.8's Note 1 adds that a Game Ejection is not a game misconduct and triggers no automatic suspension
Rule: A double Minor counts once towards a Hockey Canada Game Ejection — Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b) reads "a double Minor penalty will be considered one infraction", so an intentional head-contact double Minor is one mark of the three, not two
Rule: CARHA counts a double minor as two where Hockey Canada counts it as one — Rule 32(a) ejects on three penalties in one game, and the Notes to Rule 49(b) boarding, 50(b) head contact and Rule 48 each make their double minor two penalties there (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Key: A game misconduct is never only tonight — every book read here lets it reach the next game or beyond, CARHA 32(d) and PWHL 23.6 included, and your own league's supplementary discipline sits on top
```

Every book read here lets a game misconduct reach
beyond the game it was given in, each on its own trigger — of the four set out first, three
automatically, and the IIHF's at the Proper Authorities' discretion. Under **USA Hockey Rule 404(b)** a player
incurring a game misconduct *"shall also be suspended for the team's next game"* — the
first one, not the second. **A game misconduct for a second major in the same game costs two
games, and two is a floor rather than a tariff.** **Rule 403(b)** sets the number: the game
misconduct for that second major suspends the player for their team's *"next two games"*, and
that *"two-game suspension is in addition to any other required suspensions incurred during the
same incident."* The word *"minimum"* belongs to 404(b) instead — the same sentence quoted just
above ends by saying that where the game misconduct is imposed under 403(b) *"there shall be a
minimum two-game suspension."*
**Rule 411(a)** adds a three-game suspension on a
player's third major of the season for any combination of its listed aggressive
infractions. That list runs to seventeen rules — boarding, charging, checking from
behind and head contact among them, and ending with **Rule 639**
(tripping, clipping, leg checking, slew footing) and **Rule 640 Unnecessary
Roughness (Roughing)**. The last entry is the one to notice, because it is the rule
the late hit lives in: a **640(g)** major is an aggressive infraction and counts on
that tally. **And the ladder does not stop at three.** A fourth major in the same
category is a further five-game suspension, and a fifth suspends the player *"until
a hearing is conducted by the proper authorities"* — with all of them *"in addition
to any other suspensions imposed through the official playing rules."* **Fighting is
not on that list**: Rule 411's own Note sends it to a separate progressive-suspension
rule at Rule 615(f).
The **NHL** counts by category rather than by season: two game misconducts in the
**Physical Infractions Category** before playing 41 consecutive regular-season games
without one brings an automatic suspension under **Rule 23.6**, and **Rule 23.5** does
the same for the Stick Infractions Category. **The PWHL runs that same counter on a much shorter clock.** Its own **Rule 23.6** suspends a player automatically once she incurs *"two (2) game misconduct penalties in the “Physical Infractions Category”"* before playing *"12 consecutive Regular Season League games without such penalty"* — where the NHL's clean slate takes 41. **Each subsequent one adds a game in both books.** **Hockey Canada Rule 4.8(c)** attaches to
*when* rather than to how many — a game misconduct *"in the last 10 minutes of regular
playing time, at any time in overtime, or after the conclusion of the game"* brings an
automatic suspension of *"a minimum of the next regular league/play-off game or seven
days."* The **IIHF** writes no automatic trigger at all. **Rule 23.2** sends a player incurring a game
misconduct to the Proper Authorities, *"which shall have full power to impose further
disciplinary measures"*, and **Rule 28.1** lets the Proper
Authorities investigate and assess additional discipline *"at their discretion"*,
*"whether or not such offense has been penalized by the Referee(s)"* — so a British
reader's exposure is discretionary rather than tariffed, which is not the same as
smaller. *(The book's only *"automatically suspended"* is **Rule 46.11**, and it is a different thing: a **Coach or other team personnel** drawn into an altercation is assessed a game misconduct *"and automatically suspended from the game, ordered to the dressing room"* — an ejection from the game in progress, not a tariff on the next one. The half of Rule 46.11 that covers **players** carries no suspension language at all.)* **That is true of the IIHF *Rule Book*, which is what was read here**; Rules 23.3
to 23.6 name four disciplinary categories — including the same Stick and Physical Infractions
categories the NHL tariffs — and refer them to the separate **IIHF Disciplinary Code**, which
is not held here and has not been checked. **Your own league's supplementary discipline is a further layer on top of all
of this, and none of these books governs it.**

**One Hockey Canada consequence runs the other way, and head contact is where it
bites.** Hockey Canada counts head-contact minors inside a single game. **Rule 4.8(b)**
assesses a **Game Ejection** *"in the following circumstances"*, and **4.8(b)(ii)** is one
of them: *"[w]hen a player, in Minor or Female divisions, is assessed three Minor or double
Minor penalties for Rule 7.6 – Head Contact in the same game."* The head-contact rule states
the same counter from its own end — **Rule 7.6(a)**: *"In minor and female hockey, any player
incurring three Head Contact penalties under Rule 7.6 (a) will be assessed a Game Ejection
penalty."* **Note the scope, because both places write it in:** minor and female hockey.
Junior and Senior head contact is **Rule 7.7**, and neither the counter nor 7.6(a) names it.
**A double minor counts once.** **Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b)** reads *"For the purposes
of accumulation towards a Game Ejection penalty, a double Minor penalty will be considered one
infraction"* — so the intentional head contact 7.6(a) prices at a double minor puts one mark
against you rather than two, and 4.8(b)(ii)'s *"three Minor or double Minor penalties"* lists
which penalties count rather than counting minutes. **And a Game Ejection is not a game
misconduct.** **Rule 4.8's Note 1** says so in terms — *"A Game Ejection is not equal to a
Game Misconduct penalty and a player receiving a Game Ejection penalty does not trigger any
automatic suspension"* (printed after 4.8(c) and not tied to a sub-clause). So a third
head-contact minor ends your night, and unlike the game misconducts set out above, the
rulebook attaches nothing past the final whistle. Your own league's supplementary discipline
may still act on it.

**One adult book counts the same thing the opposite way, and boarding is the foul that exposes it.**
**CARHA Rule 32(a)** — **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only** — rules a player *"assessed
THREE PENALTIES in one game"* off the ice for the remainder of the game, and its Note 1
counts *"[a]ny combination of minor, bench minor, or major penalties"* toward a player's
personal total.
⚠️ **Under CARHA a double minor is two of those three, not one.** The Note under **Rule
49(b)**, which converts the minor into a double minor where an infraction sends an opponent
into the boards, reads *"The Double Minor penalty assessed under this rule shall count as"*
two *"penalties in the application of the Three Penalty Rule (Rule 32 (a))"*, and the
Notes under **50(b)** (intentional head contact) and
**Rule 48** (attempt to injure, including butt-ending and spearing) say the same for their
own double minors. **So a CARHA boarding call plus one more minor ends your night in two
penalties rather than three** — the opposite of Hockey Canada's count, where a double Minor
is one infraction, and the reason to find out which book your league runs before you do the
arithmetic in your head. **And a minor you never serve still counts there:** where a goal
by the non-offending side wipes out a delayed minor, **Rule 39(b)'s Note 1** provides that
*"[a]lthough the penalty time is not served, the infraction shall be taken into account for
the purpose of applying the Three Penalty Rule."*
⚠️ **And CARHA does reach past tonight, which Rule 32(a) on its own does not.** **Rule 32(d)** makes a game misconduct assessed to a player *"for receiving a Major penalty as a result of a Rule infraction, other than the Accidental High Stick Rule"* carry an automatic *"one (1) game suspension"*, with the offender *"subject to further discipline action"* depending on the severity — and **Rule 32(b)** has any game misconduct there *"reported to the Chairman for further action."* **So the three-penalty count is CARHA's within-game limb, not the whole price.**

---

## 6. Receiving a Hit Safely

**Read this section whatever league you play in** — unexpected contact happens in non-check hockey too, and the boards and the goal posts are just as hard.

### Keep your head up. Always — you cannot brace for a hit you cannot see.

```facts
Never: Skate with your head down — not "look up sometimes", never; USA Hockey's Rule One is to keep it up "especially when it looks like you're going to take a hit"
Key: You cannot brace for something you cannot see; a body prepared for contact absorbs it
Key: USA Hockey's Heads Up Hockey programme calls keeping your head up "the single most important thing to know and do to prevent head injuries" — a ranking of the habit rather than of any one moment
Rule: Keeping your head up is worth something in the rulebook too — IIHF Rule 48.1 states that where a player skates with their head up and may reasonably be expecting contact, "an opponent does not have the right to hit them to the head or neck"
Rule: The NHL bars an avoidable head hit at its own Rule 48.1 — a hit "where the head was the main point of contact and such contact to the head was avoidable" — and writes no flat head-down carve-out and no head-up sentence, but its Rule 48.1(ii) still weighs a head-down posture against you
Rule: The NHL and the PWHL are not silent on a head-down posture, though neither writes a flat carve-out — NHL Rule 48.1(ii) and PWHL Rule 48.3(i) both weigh whether you assumed "a posture that made head contact on an otherwise full body check unavoidable", and the IIHF lists that same factor too
Rule: Under the IIHF book only — a square check with no upward motion on a puck carrier skating head down toward the checker is not an illegal check to the head, the checker must still avoid your head, and the IIHF's Rule 48.1 attaches no penalty to the head-down puck carrier
Rule: The IIHF's head-down carve-out is a list of conditions and not a general licence — a head-down player hit from the side or the blind side, or hit without the puck, sits outside it entirely, and it reaches only a puck carrier skating toward the checker (IIHF Rule 48.1)
Rule: Hockey Canada gives a checker no Head Contact penalty when the opponent ducks into the hit — Junior and Senior divisions only, and never where the head is low as the result of a legitimate hockey play such as shooting or taking a pass (Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a))
Rule: Below Junior and Senior, Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) — sparing the checker who catches a ducker's head — does not apply: Rule 7.6 governs in minor and female hockey, requiring head contact to be penalised regardless of the circumstances, so the checker is penalised too
Rule: Hockey Canada Rule 8.7 bars crouching to avoid a bodycheck in every division, and the ducker's own penalty is not capped at a minor — 8.7(a) is a minor, 8.7(b) is a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence with nobody hurt, and 8.7(c) is a match penalty
Rule: The IIHF prices the ducker too, but only conditionally — its Rule 44.1 (Clipping) bars a player who crouches "down near the boards to avoid being bodychecked and as a result, causes an opponent to tumble over them" — do not duck in any book: a chin tucked to the chest straightens the cervical spine
Action: Learn to carry the puck without staring at it — it is a stickhandling problem, and it is solved off the ice
```

**The head-up rule is USA Hockey's before it is this document's, and USA Hockey states it more plainly than any rulebook does.** Rule One of USA Hockey's *Heads Up Hockey* programme is one sentence — *"Keep your head up on the ice, especially when it looks like you're going to take a hit"* — and its programme guide tells coaches to teach it as *"the single most important thing to know and do to prevent head injuries"*. USA Hockey's guide also names the awareness half of that rule among its four basic safety techniques for taking a check — *"Know where your opponents are"* — on the ground that when players do, *"they'll be less likely to get checked without any warning."* And *Heads Up Hockey* names the reflex the rule is written against: *"most people duck when they see a hit coming, but doing so puts them in danger."*

**USA Hockey's superlative ranks the habit, not the moment.** *"[T]he single most important thing to know and do to prevent head injuries"* is USA Hockey's ranking rather than this document's, and ⚠️ **nothing in the safety material held here ranks game situations against one another by danger.**

Not "look up sometimes." Never skate with your head down. Two separate reasons, both serious:

1. **You cannot brace for something you cannot see.** A body prepared for contact absorbs it. A body that is surprised does not.
2. **Under the IIHF book, the rules protect you less when your head is down.** IIHF Rule 48.1 states that a player who checks an opponent "who is skating with the puck with their head down in the direction of the Player and does not use an upward motion or drive their body up into the opponent, shall not be penalized for an 'illegal check to the head'." Even under the IIHF book the checker is still obliged not to target your head; what that book declines to penalise is only the square hit with no upward motion, on a player who has the puck and is skating **toward** the checker. **All four of those are conditions, not description.** A head-down skater hit from the side or the blind side, or hit without the puck, is not inside that carve-out at all — the sentence reaches the carrier coming at you and nothing else. **And the same rule writes the other half of that pair — the sentence immediately before the carve-out in the book's own text, and it is the half in your favour:** IIHF 48.1 provides that *"When a Player is skating with their head up, whether they are in possession of the puck and may reasonably be expecting impending contact, an opponent does not have the right to hit them to the head or neck."* So the carve-out is written for the head-down carrier, and that sentence is written for the player whose head is up — including the one who can see the hit coming and is braced for it. Being ready for contact is not consent to be hit in the head. **Neither sentence is in the NHL book**, which bars an avoidable head hit at its own Rule 48.1 and writes neither the head-up sentence nor the flat carve-out. ⚠️ **Do not read that as the NHL ignoring your posture, because it does not.** NHL Rule 48.1(ii) tells the referee to weigh, in deciding whether the head contact was avoidable, *"[w]hether the opponent put himself in a vulnerable position by assuming a posture that made head contact on an otherwise full body check unavoidable"* — and the PWHL writes the same test at its Rule 48.3(i), where on automatic video review it can cut a major to a minor. The IIHF lists that factor too, in the same Rule 48.1. **So a head-down posture counts against you in three of the six books read here, not one; what is distinctive about the IIHF is that its sentence is flat — *"shall not be penalized"* — where the NHL's and the PWHL's are factors a referee weighs.** No such exception was found in USA Hockey's Playing Rules, nor anywhere in Hockey Canada's rules, in any division — Rule 7.6 and Rule 7.7 both, with their Interpretations, and where Hockey Canada's book mentions a head-down player at all it runs the other way, in Rule 7.5, which tells referees to strictly enforce checking from behind on *"a player along the boards, with their head down looking for the puck or 'digging the puck out,'"* who is *"hit from behind and driven head-first into the boards."* **And under Hockey Canada the book can do worse than decline to protect you — the *checker's* amnesty is confined to junior and senior.** **Rule 8.7's prohibition on crouching to avoid a check carries no division scope**, so the ducker's own exposure reaches every division. Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) provides that in *"Junior and Senior divisions"* a player who *"ducks to avoid a check"* and is hit in the head area by *"an otherwise legal check"* draws no Head Contact penalty against the checker, and that the referee *"may assess a penalty to the player who ducks to avoid a check under Rule 8.7 — Clipping"* — a minor under Hockey Canada Rule 8.7(a), and **the minor is the floor rather than the ceiling**: 8.7(b) reaches a major plus a game misconduct at the referee's discretion on *"the degree of violence of impact (including the impact with the ice or boards)"*, **with nobody hurt**, and mandatorily where the duck injures the checker; and 8.7(c) is a match penalty for an attempt to injure or a deliberate one. That Hockey Canada interpretation *"does not apply to a player who is in a vulnerable position as the result of a legitimate hockey play (e.g. shooting, making or receiving a pass, etc.)"*, so a head that is low because you are shooting or taking a pass is not a duck. Full treatment in [Common Mistakes](#common-mistakes).

Learning to carry the puck without staring at it is a stickhandling problem, and it is solved off the ice. See [Puck Handling](puck_handling.md).

### Do not turn your back to an oncoming checker near the boards

```facts
Never: Turn your back to a checker near the boards. Spine exposed, head first into the wall, hit from behind. USA Hockey warns them: "Even a light hit from behind could inflict a severe head or neck injury"
Key: A chin tucked to the chest straightens the cervical spine; the head stops, the body keeps going, and vertebrae fracture
Risk: The spinal injury from a head-down, head-first collision with the boards or a goal post can occur at walking speed — no speed is slow enough to make one safe
Action: Case 1, best of all — arrive on an angle, not perpendicular
Action: Case 2, contact coming — skates parallel to the boards, forearm and upper thigh to the wall, feet apart and knees bent for a low centre of gravity, lean into the impact
Action: Case 3, already facing the wall — turn skates parallel to it before contact if at all possible; only if you cannot, spread the load, stick and gloves up on the glass, arms out as a shock absorber
Never: Take the wall on your shoulder — never the tip, and not the whole shoulder if you can help it. Forearm and hip meet the wall. Never crouch or duck to avoid a check
Rule: Turning into a hit deliberately to draw a call earns nothing — a player who intentionally turns to create contact with their back draws the checker no penalty for checking from behind (NHL, IIHF and PWHL Rule 43.1; the IIHF adds that other penalties may still be assessed)
Rule: The turn-into-a-hit waiver is the NHL's, the IIHF's and the PWHL's (43.1 in all three) — USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write no such clause, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.5(a) puts this exact case and gives the checking-from-behind penalty to the checker anyway
Key: Head up, chin off your chest — hit the boards or a post with an arm, a leg or anything but your head first
```

**Never turn your back to the wall, at any speed and in any league.** It is worth understanding why it is so tempting: when contact is coming and you are facing the wall, turning away feels like protecting yourself. It is the opposite. Turning gives the checker your back and your unprotected spine, and puts your head first into the boards. **It also converts the collision into a hit from behind**, which is the one USA Hockey addresses to the checker in the plainest terms it uses anywhere: *"Even a light hit from behind could inflict a severe head or neck injury."* You cannot see it coming and you cannot brace for it — and read that sentence for what it says: **the warning is written to cover the light hit**, so a slow checker is not the safe case. **Neither this nor the head-down collision described below is the lesser hazard.** No source cited here ranks the two against each other; both prohibitions are absolute, and USA Hockey's walking-speed finding below is stated of the head-down collision rather than of the turn.

**And if you were thinking of turning on purpose to draw the call, the rulebook has already closed that door.** NHL Rule 43.1: "When a player intentionally turns his body to create contact with his back, no penalty shall be assessed." IIHF Rule 43.1 says the same at more length, for a player who "intentionally turns their back towards an opponent and puts themself in a vulnerable position immediately before a bodycheck, to create a 'checking from behind' situation" — though it adds that "other penalties may still be assessed" against the checker on some other ground. **PWHL Rule 43.1** carries the NHL's sentence word for word in its own she/her form. You take the whole risk and you do not get the power play. ⚠️ **That door is closed in the NHL, IIHF and PWHL books, and USA Hockey and Hockey Canada answer the question the other way.** They write no such carve-out at all: Hockey Canada's **Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.5(a)** puts exactly this case — *"a player, who is about to be checked, turns and, as a result, creates contact with the back"* — and still gives the checking-from-behind penalty to the checker, because *"[t]he responsibility is always on the player delivering the check to avoid making contact with the back"*; Rule 7.5 lists the same scenario among those referees are to *"strictly enforce"*. §5 sets both books out in full. **None of that is a reason to turn.** The call is not the point — the turn is the position that breaks necks, and a penalty to the other player does not undo a cervical fracture.

The standard teaching against it is the **"Heads Up, Don't Duck"** programme, begun in 1995 by Dr Alan Ashare and carried by the Hockey Equipment Certification Council and USA Hockey. Its whole content is one posture: head up, chin off your chest, whatever else is happening.

**Two facts from USA Hockey's *Heads Up Hockey* programme that make the hazard concrete.** First, the mechanism: the upper spinal column has a natural curve that gives the head and neck flexibility in a normal heads-up position, but *"when the head is flexed (chin toward the chest), this normal curve is removed, and the cervical spine becomes straight"*. In that head-down position, hitting the boards or a post head-on stops the head while the body keeps moving, *"compressing the spine. This force can produce a shock greater than the neck's discs and muscles can cushion, resulting in a fracture or break of one [or] more vertebrae. And if one breaks, it can cause compression of the spinal cord, resulting in paralysis below the level of the fracture."* (The guide prints "one *of* more"; the bracket is this document's correction of a typo in the original, not a change to its meaning.) The finding beneath it is one USA Hockey attributes rather than claims: *"According to research done among a wide range of hockey players,"* it says, almost all on-ice cervical spine injuries have been due to the head being slightly flexed on head-on contact with the boards or goal post — and the research it prints underneath is **Charles H. Tator and colleagues, *Spinal Injuries Due To Hockey*, Canadian Journal of Neurological Sciences 11:34–41.** Second, and this is the part players get wrong: **_"A player doesn't have to be going at full speed for this to happen — it can occur at walking speed."_** There is no speed slow enough to make a head-down collision with the wall safe.

**The paper USA Hockey credits for that "almost all" is a six-patient case series, and the paper itself does not carry the quantifier.** *Spinal Injuries Due To Hockey* is indexed as a case report. Its published abstract records six patients with cervical spinal injury due to hockey treated at a single Toronto unit — the Acute Spinal Cord Injury Unit, Sunnybrook Medical Centre — between 1974 and 1981, of whom *"four struck the boards with the neck flexed, and one struck another player with the neck flexed"*, and it closes *"[t]he reasons for the increase in spinal injuries in hockey are unknown."* So *"almost all"* and *"a wide range of hockey players"* are **USA Hockey's characterisation of the research**, not that paper's own finding. **The mechanism is not what is in doubt, and larger work by the same author points the same way.** A national survey of the 42 injured players reported to the Committee on Prevention of Spinal Injuries due to Hockey between 1976 and 1983 found that *"[s]trikes from behind and collisions with the boards were common mechanisms of injury"* and that *"[m]any of the players had suffered a burst fracture of the cervical spine following a blow to the top of the helmet when the neck was slightly flexed"* (Tator and Edmonds, *National survey of spinal injuries in hockey players*, **Canadian Medical Association Journal 1984;130:875–880**). And of 355 Canadian cases registered between 1943 and 2011, **78.9% were cervical-spine injuries** and **64.2% resulted from impact with the boards**, with a check or push from behind *"still the most common cause of injury"* at **36.0%** (Tator, Provvidenza and Cassidy, **Clinical Journal of Sport Medicine 2016;26:232–238**). ⚠️ **None of the three sets the head-down posture against every other posture**, so read *"almost all"* as USA Hockey's summary rather than as a measured proportion. **What none of this touches is the instruction.** Head up, chin off your chest, never duck and never turn your back — all three papers put the flexed neck and the boards at the centre of the injury, and nothing here gives a player a reason to arrive at the wall any other way. (The two 1984 papers and the 2016 series were read here in their published abstracts and bibliographic records; their full texts were not.)

**Hockey Canada's checking-from-behind rule, Rule 7.5, names four situations its officials are told to *"strictly enforce"* — and it does not confine itself to them, because it introduces the list with the words *"situations such as the following:"* and then runs items i to iv. So four is a floor, not a count.** **Two of the four are about the position the checked player is in**: a player who "turns and, as a result, creates contact with the back," and "a player along the boards, with their head down looking for the puck or 'digging the puck out,'" who "is hit from behind and driven head-first into the boards." ⚠️ **The other two are about what the checker does, with no turn and no bend by the player being hit at all**: a checker who "swings the elbow and forearm into the opponent's back with the intent of throwing the opponent into the boards," and one who "kicks" or "pulls" an opponent's feet out from behind "and then pushes the player into the boards." **Do not read the rule as reaching only the player who has put themself in a bad position.** Half of the list is something done to a player who did nothing, and the list is open-ended besides.

**What to do instead — USA Hockey's material, ordered here in three cases.** The full rule is here. [Skating → Arriving at the boards](skating.md#arriving-at-the-boards--head-up-skates-parallel-take-it-along-your-side) owns Case 1, because choosing your angle is a skating decision made several strides out, and it carries the parallel-skates posture with it — but it says in terms that *"everything after the route is contact, not skating"*, and **this is the document with all three cases in one place.**

**Case 1 — best of all: arrive on an angle, not perpendicular.** *"Skating into the boards at an angle means better puck control and less risk of injury… When players skate into the corners at an angle, the risk of hitting the boards with their helmets first is greatly reduced. A leg or side or an arm will absorb most of the impact"* (USA Hockey, *Introduction To Body Contact*, "Angling In"). Even better: **take the puck off the wall before you get there** — see "Get off the wall" below.

![](diagram:angle-into-the-corner)

The picture above draws Case 1 and only Case 1: the line, and where it is chosen. What happens once you arrive — skates parallel, forearm and hip, chin off your chest — is a posture rather than a position, and no rink diagram can show it.

**Case 2 — contact is coming and you can still choose how you meet the wall: skates parallel to the boards, and take it along your side.** The manual's teaching points for a player checked while skating parallel to the boards are *"[p]ress your forearm and upper thigh (hip area) against the boards"* and *"[k]eep a low center of gravity with your feet apart and knees bent"*; the angling-in list adds *"[l]ean into the impact"* and *"[a]bsorb the shock over the widest possible part of your body"*. **The manual names the opposite of that stance as a Common Error in its own words** — *"[s]tanding too upright with your feet together (not having a low center of gravity)"* — so a narrow stance is not a smaller version of the right one; it is the error. Your shoulder and hip turn toward the **checker**; your forearm and hip meet the **wall**.

- **Do not take the wall on the point of your shoulder — and in this case, do not lead with the shoulder at all.** USA Hockey returns to the shoulder and the boards more than once, and the sentences are not one claim repeated: they differ in what they cover and in how hard they say it. **The one printed among the teaching points for this case is broad and soft.** Among those same teaching points for a player checked while skating parallel to the boards, next to the forearm and hip, is *"[a]void hitting the boards with the shoulder"* — the whole shoulder, and the word is *avoid*, not *never*. The flat prohibition is the narrower one, and it comes from the manual's "Angling In" demonstration list: *"Never hit the boards or glass with the tip of your shoulder – it can cause injury"*. **And the manual states the broad instruction outright in its own checklist for taking a check** — among the points it tells a coach to cover when demonstrating taking a hit, beside keeping the skates parallel and the feet apart, is *"No shoulder contact on the boards, if possible"*, and USA Hockey's *Heads Up Hockey* guide prints the same sentence in the same list. Read together, those three give one instruction: the tip never, on any approach, and along your side the shoulder is not the surface you are meeting the wall with, if you can help it. Forearm and hip. ⚠️ **A fourth sentence in the same manual points a shoulder at the wall rather than away from it, and it is set down here rather than explained away.** It is a teaching point inside the drill *Partner’s Bump Along the Boards* — a controlled, alternating partner drill for 12-and-Under, 14-and-Under and 16/18-and-Under, in which the checker is told to use the outside skate to push the player into the boards *"with control"* — and it reads: *"The player being checked needs to keep the head up and turn the far shoulder toward the boards to prevent shoulder injury."* ⚠️ **Two things decide how much weight that sentence can carry, and neither of them is a way of dismissing it.** The first is that *"far"* is never defined. The phrase appears **once** in the manual, and the two places where the manual does name a side write *"outside shoulder"* and *"inside shoulder"* — both times for the player delivering the check, not the player taking it. **Far from the boards and far from the checker are opposite shoulders, and the sentence does not say which it means.** The second is that it is a drill point rather than the manual’s general instruction, and the manual gives its general one separately: the checklist for *"[w]hen demonstrating taking a hit"* carries *"Keep skates parallel to the boards"* and *"No shoulder contact on the boards, if possible"*. **Those two sit at different levels, and the general one is what this document teaches.** What the drill sentence asks for is recorded here and not resolved, because the manual does not settle it. The instruction this document gives you is the one above: skates parallel, forearm and hip.

**Case 3 — you are already facing the boards and cannot turn: spread the load across everything you have.** *"If checked while facing the boards, distribute the force of the impact over as wide a surface as possible, including putting your stick and gloves on the glass to cushion the blow"*; the listed common error is *"[n]ot getting your stick and gloves up and spread out on the glass"*. The teaching drill has players *"make contact with the boards and glass with their extended arms, forearms, side of body and legs… but with no head contact at all"*, with the arms out forming a *"three-part 'shock absorber'."* **This is damage limitation, not the position you are aiming for.** USA Hockey's first teaching point for a player checked while facing the boards is *"[i]f at all possible, try to turn skates parallel to the boards before contact is made"*.

**In every case, and non-negotiable:** head up, chin off your chest, and *"[h]it the boards or goal posts with an arm, a leg or anything but your head first"* (Look Up Line, "Heads Up, Don't Duck"). Never your back to the wall, never duck. A tucked head is what turns a hit into a neck injury.

> **Three pieces of rink-side advice you will hear that the primary coaching material does not support.**
>
> **"Take the wall on your shoulder."** Players and coaches say this constantly. USA Hockey's own material, quoted above, runs the other way — and it is the manual's **broad** sentences that answer this one, not its narrowest. The flat prohibition, *"[n]ever hit the boards or glass with the tip of your shoulder"*, governs the tip, a point; the folklore is about the whole shoulder. What meets the folklore as stated is *"[a]void hitting the boards with the shoulder"*, all of it, and the checklist for taking a check, *"No shoulder contact on the boards, if possible"* — both broad, and both hedged. So take it on the forearm and hip, and keep the shoulder off the wall if you can. (Your shoulder does face the *checker*; it is the boards it should not meet.)
>
> **"Get square to the boards."** No coaching material teaches chest-first squaring up as the orientation to aim for. It appears only as the Case 3 fallback for a player who is already facing the wall. The target orientation is **parallel**.
>
> **"Get your hands off the boards — forearms, not hands."** USA Hockey teaches the opposite only for the one case where you are already facing the boards and cannot turn: get your **stick and gloves up and spread out on the glass**, and it names failing to do so as the common error for that case. Skating parallel to the boards is a different case, and USA Hockey's teaching points for it carry no stick instruction at all — forearm and hip against the boards, feet apart, knees bent — with standing too upright, not the shoulder, as the Common Error it names there. **A second safety body echoes that Case 3 instruction, in its own words.** The Hockey Equipment Certification Council's *Heads Up, Don't Duck* page asks a player to *"[i]f possible, use your hands, arms, shoulders or facemask to cushion the impact."* ⚠️ **HECC names the shoulders there, and USA Hockey warns off the shoulder more than once — so set them beside one another rather than picking one.** USA Hockey's flat prohibition is narrow and carries no hedge: *"[n]ever hit the boards or glass with the tip of your shoulder"*, a point. **Its broader instructions cover the whole shoulder and are softer.** The teaching point written for the player checked while skating parallel to the boards is *"[a]void hitting the boards with the shoulder"*, all of it, and the word there is *avoid*, not *never*; and the checklist USA Hockey gives for taking a check — in *Introduction To Body Contact*, and again in the *Heads Up Hockey* guide — says *"No shoulder contact on the boards, if possible"*. HECC is doing neither: it is listing broad surfaces to spread an impact across, hedged with *"[i]f possible"*, and that sentence, as worded, names no particular case. **So the safe course is not this document's synthesis but USA Hockey's own instruction, hedged the way USA Hockey hedges it: no part of the shoulder leads if you can help it, and the tip never.** The related warning that *"a locked, straight arm into the boards is how wrists and collarbones break"* is widely repeated but **no hockey safety authority was found behind it** — *Introduction To Body Contact*, the *Heads Up Hockey Program Guide* and both organisations' *Heads Up, Don't Duck* pages were each searched for it, and none of the four carries it. Treat it as unverified coaching craft. *(The nearest real evidence is not about hockey at all. In a laboratory study of forward falls onto the hand, Chou and colleagues found that "the elbow mediolateral shear force was 68% larger" when the arm was extended rather than flexed at impact, and that elbow flexion "reduces the first peak impact force value and postpones the maximum peak value" — but they measured **no** significant difference in axial force at wrist, elbow or shoulder, and a vertical fall onto a floor is not a skater hitting a wall. **How small a fall matters here:** eleven volunteers were dropped onto force plates from **3 and 6 centimetres**, elbows extended at impact. That is a scale below anything you will meet on the ice. It is a reason to find the advice plausible, not a source for it.)* The reading that reconciles it with the manual is USA Hockey's own phrase: arms out as a **progressive shock absorber that bends and gives**, spreading load across two gloves and two forearms, rather than one locked strut taking your whole weight through one wrist.

Note the corresponding rule on the other side: you also cannot solve the problem by ducking. **How far that rule reaches depends on your book, and the difference is worth knowing.** ⚠️ **Only the last sentence of IIHF Rule 44.1 (Clipping) is conditional, and it is the only part of that rule that is about ducking at all.** The two sentences above it reach the **checker** and attach neither a location nor a consequence — clipping is *"the act of throwing the body across or below the knees of an opponent from any direction"*, and *"[a] Player may not deliver a check in a 'clipping' manner, nor lower their own body position to deliver a check on or below an opponent's knees"* ([§5 sets that half out](#delivering-a-legal-body-check-technique)). **The ducker's limb is the conditional one:** a player "may not crouch down near the boards to avoid being bodychecked **and as a result, causes an opponent to tumble over them**" — so for the player who ducks, under the IIHF book, it is the location and the consequence that make it an offence. **Hockey Canada's Rule 8.7 (Clipping) states it without either condition:** "Players may not crouch down to avoid being bodychecked." Full stop, anywhere on the ice, whether or not anyone falls over you. Do not carry the IIHF's conditions into a Hockey Canada game. ⚠️ **And under Hockey Canada, ducking can cost you the protection as well as earning you the penalty.** Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) provides that *"In Junior and Senior divisions, if a player ducks to avoid a check and, as a result, is hit in the head area with an otherwise legal check, this will not be considered Head Contact and no penalty will be assessed"* — the checker walks away from it. **Read the limit the interpretation writes on itself:** it *"does not apply to a player who is in a vulnerable position as the result of a legitimate hockey play (e.g. shooting, making or receiving a pass, etc.)"*, and it is confined to junior and senior — in minor and female hockey Rule 7.6 governs and penalises head contact *"regardless of the circumstances"*, so the checker is penalised there whether you ducked or not. Do not duck in any book, because the reason not to is the one above, not the penalty.

**Being hit from behind is a different problem, and what is taught for it is thinner than for the other cases — but it is not nothing.** **Start with the instruction USA Hockey writes for the contact you can see coming, because it is the one the fallback in the next sentence is the exception to: skate through the check.** Its *Introduction To Body Contact* lists *"[s]kate through the check, this provides a better chance of maintaining balance and staying in the play"* among the points to cover with a player taking a hit, and its *Heads Up Hockey* guide gives the safety half of the reason in the same breath — keep the legs moving through the impact and *"[t]hey'll not only avoid head injury, they'll have a better chance of maintaining their balance and staying in the play."* **Feet moving through the contact is the instruction. The sentence that follows is the fallback for when you cannot.** USA Hockey's *Heads Up Hockey* guide does give one for contact you cannot prepare for: *"if they get in a situation where they can't skate through a check, players should keep their arms up, knees bent and, of course, their heads up"*, with the same checklist as the boards cases — skates parallel, *"arms/legs as shock absorbers"*, *"feet apart, knees bent, low center of gravity"*. No published treatment gives a technique for a hit with **genuinely zero warning**; every one of them assumes you have at least registered that contact is coming. Beyond that it is handled on the other side of the collision — checking from behind is a major penalty with an automatic game misconduct under the NHL and IIHF, and never a bare minor under USA Hockey or Hockey Canada, set out in Section 5 above — and on your side by awareness: USA Hockey's only instruction is *"[c]heck over your shoulder to see where your opponent is"*. Knowing pressure is coming is what converts the situation into Case 1 or Case 2. See [Scanning and Anticipation](../hockey-iq/scanning_and_anticipation.md).

### Bracing and absorbing

```facts
Position: Wide base, knees bent, weight low — an athletic stance rides contact, a stiff upright one gets knocked over
Action: Skate through the check, which is USA Hockey's own instruction — meet contact with your legs moving rather than slowing or stopping; momentum you generate is momentum that does not go into you
Never: Skate at an oncoming opponent to meet a hit — you add your speed to theirs and neither controls the geometry. Keep moving on your own line. Under the IIHF women's rule 101.1 it is also an illegal hit
Position: Chest and shoulder first, hands and stick in front of your body, not out to the side
Technique: Absorb with your legs — let your knees and hips give rather than taking it all through a locked frame
```

- **Wide base, knees bent, weight low.** A stiff, upright player gets knocked over. A player in an athletic stance rides the contact.
- **Meet it, don't wait for it.** Skating into contact with your body prepared and your feet moving costs far less than standing still and being hit. Momentum you generate is momentum that does not go into you. ⚠️ **Meet it — do not go and get it.** Nothing here asks you to skate *at* an opponent. **The reason is mechanical before it is legal:** turning into a closing opponent adds your speed to theirs, and at that combined speed neither of you controls the geometry of the collision. USA Hockey's coaching material asks for the opposite — *"[a]lthough it may seem safer to slow down or stop if a hit is about to happen, your players' balance and momentum will be much better if they keep their legs moving and concentrate on skating right through the impact"* (*Heads Up Hockey Program Guide*) — which is your own line, not theirs. **And one book penalises the turn in itself — though mind whose rule it is.** IIHF Rule 101.1 makes *"[a]ny move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player"* at least a minor for an illegal hit, and **101.1 is the women's-hockey rule**, so it binds women's play including every British women's category and does not bind IIHF men's play. **The permission not to move is the wider rule — but it is not universal either, and three of the four books write it.** The IIHF's **Rule 56.1** grants that *"A Player is allowed the ice they are standing on (body position) and are not required to move in order to let an opponent proceed"*; the NHL writes the same clause at its own **56.1**; and USA Hockey puts it in the Summary of its **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis — Restraining Fouls**, which announces no age or category scope at all: *"Players are entitled to the ice they occupy as long as they are able to maintain their own foot speed and body position between opponent and puck."* For women's play 101.1 adds that *"Players are allowed to 'hold their ground' any time that they have established their position on the ice. No Player is required to move out of the way of an oncoming Player to avoid a collision."* ⚠️ **Hockey Canada is the fourth, and it writes no such sentence** — see §1. **And if your league permits checking, the charging rules do reach building speed into a collision — but the written thresholds are not the same, and the NHL sets none at all.** Hockey Canada 7.4 makes it charging where a player *"[b]uilds up speed by taking two or more strides immediately prior to making contact"*, and IIHF Rule 42.1 where they build speed *"by taking multiple strides immediately prior to making contact"*. USA Hockey needs a threshold **and** a purpose — charging is where a player *"takes more than two strides or travels an excessive distance to accelerate through a body check for the purpose of punishing the opponent"* (Rule 607, Note). And the NHL counts no strides at all, defining charging as the act of a player who *"as a result of distance traveled, shall violently check an opponent in any manner"* (Rule 42.1). **Never attribute a stride count to the NHL.** ⚠️ **And a fifth book writes the number with no purpose limb at all** — the Note to **CARHA Rule 52** reads *"If more than two steps or strides are taken, it shall be considered a charge"* (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only) — and that count picks the charging tier there rather than whether you may hit at all, because that book has no checking division. Being braced and moving on your own line is the whole of the point.
- **Chest and shoulder first, hands in front.** Keep your hands and stick in front of your body, not out to the side. This protects you and keeps you able to play the puck the instant contact ends.
- **Absorb with your legs.** Let your knees and hips give a little rather than taking it all through a locked frame.

### Protecting yourself along the wall

```facts
Priority: Arrive first if you possibly can — the player who arrives first controls the geometry of the collision
Position: Get low and get wide — a low centre of gravity along the boards is very hard to move
Technique: Skates parallel to the wall, forearm and hip to it — not the point of your shoulder, not the whole shoulder if you can help it, and never your back. Head up, chin off your chest
Never: Stop and admire the puck — standing still, bent over, back to the play is the posture the checking-from-behind rules describe
```

When you go to the wall for a puck with a checker arriving:

- **Arrive first if you possibly can** — the player who arrives first controls the geometry of the collision.
- **Get low and get wide.** A low centre of gravity along the boards is very hard to move.
- **Get your skates parallel to the wall and put your forearm and hip to it — not the point of your shoulder, not the whole shoulder if you can help it, and never your back.** Head up, chin off your chest. (Case 2 above.)
- **Do not stop and admire the puck.** Standing still, bent over, back to the play is the exact posture the checking-from-behind rules describe.

### Get off the wall

```facts
Goal: The best protection is not being pinned. Avoid the collision entirely and keep possession
Options: Chip the puck along the boards, kick it to a teammate, or take it off the wall toward open ice before contact arrives
Action: Know what is around you before the puck arrives
Action: Have a plan for the puck before you get there — a chip, a rim, a reverse, or a spin off the wall
Key: Players who arrive at the wall with no plan get pinned; players who arrive with a plan get out
```

The best protection is not being pinned. If you can chip the puck along the boards, kick it to a teammate, or take it off the wall toward open ice before contact arrives, you avoid the collision entirely and keep possession.
