Two habits make this possible: **know what is around you before the puck arrives** (see [Scanning and Anticipation](../hockey-iq/scanning_and_anticipation.md)), and **have a plan for the puck before you get there** — a chip, a rim, a reverse, or a spin off the wall. Players who arrive at the wall with no plan get pinned. Players who arrive with a plan get out.

### Talk

```facts
Action: Call loud, immediate and specific for a teammate going back with their head down and pressure coming
Convention: "Time!" means they have space; "Man on!" or their name plus "behind you!" means they do not
Key: The warning is what lets a teammate prepare for contact they would otherwise take blind
```

If a teammate is going back for a puck with their head down and pressure coming, the correct call is loud, immediate and specific. "Time!" means you have space. "Man on!" or their name plus "behind you!" means you do not. A player cannot brace for a hit they cannot see, and the call is what gives them the chance to. See [On-Ice Communication](../foundation/on_ice_communication.md).

### If it happens anyway — a suspected neck injury on the ice

```facts
Never: Help the player up — it is the most common and most damaging mistake after a spinal impact
Action: Call for immediate medical help first
Read: Symptoms after a spinal impact — pain at the injury, buzzing or tingling in arms or legs, loss of movement, radiating pain
Action: Ensure adequate airway and breathing, observing the ABCs — Airway, Breathing, Circulation
Action: Reassure the player, keep them warm, and instruct and immobilise them so they do not move — head and neck especially
Never: Take the helmet off — CRT6 writes that one flat, and unlike its instruction about moving the player it carries no exception
Never: Roll, lift or otherwise move the player unless you are trained to and it is required for airway support
Key: Do not wait for symptoms — CRT6 says assume a possible spinal cord injury in all cases of head injury
```

Everything above is prevention. This is what to do when prevention has failed, and it is short enough to memorise. **The most common and most damaging mistake is helping the player up.**

**Symptoms to look for after a spinal impact**, per USA Hockey: pain in the area of the injury; buzzing or tingling in the arms and/or legs; loss of movement in the arms or legs; radiating pain in the arms or legs.

**If you think a player may have sustained a spinal injury, your immediate role is to get help and keep the player calm and immobile.** After calling for immediate medical help, USA Hockey's instructions are:

- **Ensure adequate airway and breathing**, observing the ABCs — Airway, Breathing, Circulation.
- **Reassure the player.**
- **Do not allow the player to move. Instruct the player not to move and immobilize him or her to prevent motion, especially motion of the head and neck.**
- **Keep the player warm.**

**That list is USA Hockey's and it ends there — it says nothing about the helmet, and the helmet instruction is not theirs.** It is **CRT6's**, from the Concussion Recognition Tool's "Remember" box, and the two instructions in that box are not written the same way. Moving the player is qualified twice: *"Do not attempt to move the athlete (other than required for airway support) unless trained to do so."* ⚠️ **The helmet is written flat, with no exception attached to it at all**: *"Do not remove helmet (if present) or other equipment."*

**So do not roll or lift the player unless you are trained to and it is required for airway support — and do not take the helmet off at all.**

**Do not wait for these symptoms to appear before applying the rule.** CRT6 is the concussion tool that belongs on your phone, and the same "Remember" box says to *"assume a possible spinal cord injury in all cases of head injury"* — neck pain or tenderness is itself a red flag that means an ambulance. CRT6's full red-flag list, and what to do in the hours afterwards, is in [Conditioning and Recovery → Concussion](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md). Head injury and neck injury are the same call at the rink: nobody moves, somebody phones.


### And the case where they get up

```facts
Never: Assume a player is fine because they were not knocked out — loss of consciousness is not required for a concussion and is not typical of one
Action: A suspected concussion means off the ice, no return that day, and a medical professional before they play again
Never: Treat symptoms clearing up as the all-clear — CRT6 says no return to any activity risking head contact, a fall or a collision until assessed medically, "even if the symptoms resolve"
Never: Let the player decide whether they are fit to carry on
Never: Leave them on their own in the first three hours — CRT6 writes "at least", so three is a floor. Stay with them instead: worsening symptoms "should lead to immediate medical attention"
Never: Let them drive home, and never send them home on their own — CRT6 says no driving until a healthcare professional clears them, and that they need to be with a responsible adult
Read: The red flags, the symptoms short of them, and what has to happen in the hours afterwards are in Conditioning and Recovery; Britain's adopted list differs — UK and England Rules carries it
Convention: Where CRT6's ten red flags and Britain's fourteen differ, act on whichever fires first — neither document writes that rule, it is what the two lists leave you with, and it can only ever make you act earlier
```

A player who takes a hit to the head and stands up is the far commoner case, and
the standard is the same everywhere it is written down: **a suspected concussion means off the ice,
no return that day, and a medical professional before they play again.** You do
not have to have been knocked out, and loss of consciousness is not typical of a
concussion. Do not let the player decide.

**And feeling better is not the all-clear.** CRT6 prints this in red, and it is the sentence that
answers the player who shakes it off and asks for the next shift: *"Any athlete with a suspected
concussion should be - IMMEDIATELY REMOVED FROM PRACTICE OR PLAY and should NOT RETURN TO ANY
ACTIVITY WITH RISK OF HEAD CONTACT, FALL OR COLLISION, including SPORT ACTIVITY until ASSESSED
MEDICALLY, even if the symptoms resolve."* Note how wide that is — it is not only the rest of the
game, and it is not only hockey.

**Two of CRT6's instructions are about the car park rather than the ice, and in adult hockey they
are the two most likely to be skipped.** CRT6 lists things an athlete with a suspected concussion
**should not** do, and among them: they should **not** *"[b]e sent home by themselves"* — *"[t]hey
need to be with a responsible adult"* — and they should **not** *"[d]rive a motor vehicle until
cleared to do so by a healthcare professional"*. It also says they should not *"[b]e left alone
initially (at least for the first 3 hours)"*, and that *"[w]orsening of symptoms should lead to
immediate medical attention."* So somebody drives them, somebody stays with them, and the decision
about when they drive again belongs to a healthcare professional rather than to them or to you.
These are CRT6's words, not medical advice from this document; the assessment is a professional's.

CRT6's ten red flags — the ones that mean an ambulance — are in
[Conditioning and Recovery → Concussion](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md), with the
symptoms short of them and what has to happen in the hours afterwards.

**But ten is CRT6's count, and CRT6 is not the only red-flag list.** England Ice Hockey has
adopted the **UK Concussion Guidelines for Non-Elite (Grassroots) Sport**, whose red-flag list runs
to **fourteen** and differs from CRT6's in both directions. ⚠️ **So if you play in Britain, learn that
list as well as this one, and where the two differ, act on whichever fires first.** **Neither CRT6 nor
the British guidance writes that rule** — it is what the two lists leave you with when you set them
side by side, and it can only ever make you act earlier, never later. Conditioning and
Recovery sets out where the two part company, and
[UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md#what-medical-cover-you-can-expect-at-the-rink-and-what-to-ask-for) carries the British fourteen in full.

---

## 7. Board Battles

A **board battle** is a contest for a puck against the wall, usually between the goal line and the top of the circles. Most possessions in most games start or end with one — coaching emphasis rather than a counted share.

### Arrive first — and arrive with position, not just speed

```facts
Priority: The player who gets there first decides where the battle happens
Position: Body between the opponent and the puck at the moment of arrival, with the wall on the far side
Key: Arriving first with your body in the wrong place is worse than arriving second with it in the right one
```

The player who gets there first decides where the battle happens. But arriving first with your body in the wrong place is worse than arriving second with it in the right one. What you want at the moment of arrival is your **body between the opponent and the puck**, with the wall on the far side.

### The wide low base

```facts
Position: Feet wider than shoulders, knees bent deeply, backside low, chest up
Key: A centre of gravity below your opponent's is very hard to knock off the puck — your base is the variable you control, though a large enough strength gap still wins
Technique: Generate force with your legs — that is where leverage lives
Action: Stay mobile — a low wide stance can shuffle, pivot and step out; a stiff-legged stance can only fall over
Convention: Leverage often beats strength — but not always, and not against a large enough strength gap; coaching consensus, not a measured law
```

This is the whole technique in three words. Feet wider than shoulders, knees bent deeply, backside low, chest up. From that stance:

- You are very hard to knock off the puck, because your centre of gravity is below your opponent's — hard, not impossible, for the reason set out below.
- You can generate force with your legs, which is where leverage lives.
- You can move — a low wide stance can shuffle, pivot and step out. A stiff-legged stance can only fall over.

**Leverage often beats strength here.** A stronger player standing upright will frequently lose to a weaker player who is properly down in their base. **Not always, and a large enough strength gap wins anyway** — the point is that your base is the variable you actually control, and it is the one most players give away for free. This is good news for smaller players and bad news for anyone relying on being big. **Flagged as partly unverified:** that leverage beats strength is coaching consensus, true often enough to teach, but it is not a measured law, and **nothing found in the ice hockey biomechanics literature quantifies it** — a Europe PMC search on 2 September 2026 for hockey board battles, one-on-one contests and pushing force returned no study of any of them. The nearest quantification comes from **rugby scrummaging**, where it points the same way on joint angle and splits on stance height: Green and colleagues' review of the determinants of scrummaging force reports that *"[s]tatistically significant relationships have been presented between the extensions of the hip (r=−0.47), knee (r=−0.51) and the ankle (r=−0.70) and the individual scrummaging forces"* — negative correlations, meaning the more flexed the joint, the more force produced. **Whose measurement that is matters:** those correlations are **not Green's own finding**. In the review they carry reference [13] — **Wu WL, Chang JJ, Wu JH, et al. (2007),** *An investigation of rugby scrummaging posture and individual maximum pushing force*, J Strength Cond Res 21(1):251–258. Green's group's own study is titled, flatly, *"A lower body height and wider foot stance are positively associated with the generation of individual scrummaging forces in rugby."* ⚠️ **The review reprints those three numbers without two limits Wu attaches to them, and the two rugby studies do not agree about height.** Wu measured the correlations under **parallel feet** — in cross-feet postures it reports the opposite sign, *"a positive correlation between individual forward force and hip angle of the rear leg"* — and Wu's own headline result is that the **lowest** of its three stances produced the **least** force across ten players: maximum pushing force was *"significantly lower"* at 36% of body height than at 38% or 40%, which points the other way from Green's group's title. Read the pair as an optimum rather than a straight line, and as one more reason the stance advice above rests on coaching consensus rather than on either paper. Three honest caveats: that is a different sport with a braced, bilateral push rather than a skating contest; the same review notes that other researchers *"failed to show relationships between kinematics and scrummaging performance"*, and Wu found that foot position itself made no significant difference to peak force; and the review's **own** summing-up of that whole passage is that *"these findings do not provide conclusive relationships between force development and scrummaging body positions."* [Puck Handling](puck_handling.md) carries the same claim with the same caveat.

### Using the wall

```facts
Position: Puck between your skates and the wall, your body on the outside of it
Technique: Create a little space between you and the boards and use your body to protect that space
Goal: Halve the directions you can be attacked from — with the wall behind you it is only 180 degrees
```

The boards are a teammate that never gets tired. Blaine Buterbaugh, assistant hockey director at the Robert Morris University Sports Center, puts it well: "Nobody from outside of the rink is coming to steal your puck... if you can create a little bit of space between you and the boards and use your body to protect that space, you're only worried about 180 degrees, essentially, of people that are trying to steal your puck."

So: put the puck between your skates and the wall, put your body on the outside of it, and you have halved the number of directions you can be attacked from.

### Working the puck free with your feet

```facts
Key: In a real battle your stick is tied up and your hands are busy — your feet are free
Rule: You may kick the puck in all zones (NHL Rule 49.2), but you cannot score with a distinct kicking motion
Rule: A kicked puck that deflects into the net off a body is no goal; off a stick other than the goalkeeper's it is a good goal (NHL 49.2(i) and 49.2(ii), and IIHF 49.2(I) and 49.2(II) word for word)
Rule: A kicked puck deflecting in off a stick can be a good goal only in the NHL, IIHF and PWHL books (49.2) — Hockey Canada 6.10(b): "no goal can be scored as the result of a kicking motion by an attacking player", and USA Hockey's Casebook (627, Situation 4) disallows even the kicker's own stick
Rule: Kick the puck, never a player — NHL 49.3 makes kicking or attempting to kick an opponent a match penalty *whether or not an injury occurs*, and USA Hockey 627(a) makes even a skate push-off a major plus a game misconduct
Rule: No book here makes kicking a player a two-minute mistake, and all six books read here penalise the attempt — match penalties under USA Hockey 627(b), Hockey Canada 7.1(c)(iii), PWHL 49.3 and CARHA 48(c), CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only
Rule: The IIHF writes no match penalty at all, so the ceiling inside the game under Britain's book for kicking or attempting to kick an opponent is a major plus an automatic game misconduct, 49.4 allowing Supplementary Discipline above — and IIHF 49.3 leaves that major to the referee's discretion
Action: Kick it out with the inside of your skate — back to a supporting teammate, along the wall, or out to your own stick
Action: Trap it — skates wide with the puck under you means nobody else can have it either
Technique: Practise this deliberately — almost no amateur does, and it wins pucks every game
```

This is the part nobody teaches and everybody needs.

In a real board battle your stick is usually tied up, your hands are busy, and the puck is under a pile of skates. **Your feet are free.** Skates are legal tools for moving the puck — you can kick the puck in all zones (NHL Rule 49.2 permits it; you just cannot score with a distinct kicking motion). **And the deflection rules split on what it hits:** a kicked puck that deflects in off a *body* is no goal (49.2(i)), while one deflecting off a *stick* other than the goalkeeper's is a good goal (49.2(ii)). The IIHF book writes those two clauses word for word at 49.2(I) and 49.2(II). **But that good-goal limb belongs to those two books and the PWHL, which writes both clauses at 49.2(i) and 49.2(ii) as well, so do not carry it into a minor-hockey game.** **Hockey Canada** states the opposite principle outright at Rule 6.10(b) — *"Kicking the puck cannot result in a goal, unless a defending player kicks the puck into their own goal. This includes when an attacking player kicks the puck and it deflects off any other player or object and goes into the goal"* — and its Interpretation 1 to Rules 6.10(b) and 6.10(c) spells out that a kick deflecting off a teammate's *"skate, body or stick"* is **no goal**. **USA Hockey** writes no stick-versus-body split at all: Rule 617(c)(1) disallows a goal where *"[a]n attacking player kicks the puck directly into the goal or the kicked puck deflects off any player, including goalkeeper, prior to entering the goal"*, and its Casebook puts the exact case — a kicked puck deflecting off the kicker's own stick and in — at **Rule 627, Situation 4**, and answers *"No."* Under Hockey Canada and USA Hockey the kick is a puck-recovery tool and nothing more.

- **Kick it out.** Use the inside of your skate to shovel the puck out of the pile — backward to a supporting teammate, along the wall to open ice, or out to your own stick. This is the highest-percentage way to win a puck that is genuinely stuck.
- **Kick it to your stick.** A short flick of the skate blade to pop the puck onto your own blade is the fastest way to turn a stalemate into a possession.
- **Trap it.** Standing over the puck with your skates wide, with the puck under you, means nobody else can have it either.

Practise this deliberately. Almost no amateur does, and it wins pucks every single game.

**One line you cannot cross, and it is in the same rule.** NHL Rule 49.1 defines kicking as using the skate *"to propel the puck **or to contact an opponent**"*, and 49.3 makes kicking or attempting to kick a player a **match penalty** — ejection — *"[w]hether or not an injury occurs"*. ⚠️ **USA Hockey splits the two acts, and only one of them is kicking:** Rule 627(a)'s major plus game misconduct is for a **push-off**, which Note 2 defines as a skate used *"in a non-kicking motion"* — while **627(b) is a mandatory match penalty** for a player who *"kicks, attempts to kick or recklessly endangers an opponent by kicking."* So keep the motion small, keep it at the puck, and if you cannot see the puck, do not swing your blade at where you think it is.

**The other four books get there by different routes, and one of them is Britain's.** **The PWHL** writes the NHL's rule word for word at its own **Rule 49.3** — a match penalty for a player who *"kicks or attempts to kick another player"*, again *"[w]hether or not an injury occurs"* — the same five-minute sentence the NHL writes. **Hockey Canada** and **CARHA** write no kicking rule of their own, and the act runs through their attempt-to-injure rules in both: **Hockey Canada 7.1(c)(iii)**, Attempt to Injure or Deliberate Injury, where a match penalty covers *"Attempting to kick or deliberately kicking an opponent"*, and **CARHA Rule 48(c)** — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — under which *"[a] player who attempts to kick or deliberately kicks an opposing player shall be assessed a Match penalty."* **The IIHF writes no match penalty anywhere in its book**, so its **Rule 49.3** is headed *"Major Penalty and Game Misconduct Penalty"* — a discretionary major plus an automatic game misconduct, with no lesser tier available for kicking an opponent. **There is no book here in which kicking a player is a two-minute mistake.**

### When to just pin it

```facts
Read: Pin when you are outnumbered, tangled with an equally stuck opponent, or a turnover here is a chance against
Action: Pin the puck against the boards with your skate or your body and let the whistle come
Key: Under pressure, pin it; unpressured, play it — pinning is legal only while the puck is genuinely contested
Rule: Deliberately freezing the puck to cause a stoppage is a delay-of-game minor (NHL Rule 63.2(i); USA Hockey Rule 610(a))
Never: Pin because a change is due or you are protecting a lead — that is the delaying purpose both rules describe
Rule: IIHF women's rules prohibit pinning an opponent along the boards — pin the puck, not the player (IIHF Rule 101.1, and so in British women's hockey)
```

Sometimes you cannot win the battle and neither can they. If you are outnumbered, if you are tangled up with an opponent who is just as stuck as you are, or if a turnover here is a scoring chance against, **pin the puck against the boards with your skate or your body and let the whistle come.** A defensive-zone faceoff is a far better outcome than a turnover in the slot.

**The qualifier is part of the tactic, because the same action without it is a penalty.** Pinning is legal *while the puck is genuinely contested* — an opponent is on it or arriving, and neither of you can free it. That is how most board-battle whistles happen, and referees let it run. Freezing the puck when nobody is actually pressuring you, purely to buy a stoppage, is a minor:

- **NHL Rule 63.2(i)** assesses a minor for delay of game *"On any player, including the goalkeeper, who holds, freezes or plays the puck with his stick, skates or body in such a manner as to deliberately cause a stoppage of play."*
- **USA Hockey Rule 610(a)** names the intent in plainer terms: *"A minor penalty shall be assessed to any player or goalkeeper who deliberately freezes the puck along the boards or goal frame for the purpose of delaying the game."*

**Under pressure, pin it; unpressured, play it.** Note what that rules out as a *motive*: "a change is due" and "we are protecting a lead late" are precisely the delaying purpose both rules describe. They are reasons to get the puck out — rim it, chip it off the glass, or make the safe play up the wall — not reasons to sit on it and fish for a whistle. Pin because you cannot win the puck and cannot afford to lose it, never because you want the clock stopped. See **[Game Management](../systems/game_management.md)** for the legal ways to kill time and change lines.

Pinning is a legitimate tactic and a game-management decision, not a failure. Know the exception: **IIHF women's rules specifically prohibit pinning an opponent along the boards** (Rule 101.1) — there, you may pin the puck, but not the player. **That exception is live in Britain**, because women's hockey under England Ice Hockey and the SIHA runs Rule 101.1 unamended. [Defensive Zone Coverage](../systems/defensive_zone_coverage.md) carries the same "pin the puck, not the player" distinction with the same freezing qualifier; the two documents agree.

---

## 8. Corner Battles

Corner battles are board battles with two differences that change everything.

### They are 2-on-1 by default

```facts
Key: Two players usually beat one in a corner — the first only has to tie the puck up rather than win it outright. That "usually" is the coaching account, not a counted result
Action: First man in engages the carrier, takes body position and seals — pin the puck, not the player. Keep the puck the thing you are going for
Rule: In women's hockey the puck has to stay the object of the contact — IIHF 101.1, a women's-hockey rule, allows pushing and leaning only while "possession of the puck" remains "the sole object" of the two players
Never: Make the player, not the puck, the object of it — where checking is barred, USA Hockey 604 Note 1 counts forcing an opponent off the puck with no effort to legally play it as an illegal body check
Rule: The seal has a limit in a checking league too — USA Hockey 622's Note makes it holding to wrap "one or both arms around the opponent along the boards in a manner that pins them", a minor under 622(a), in a rule carrying no category qualifier at all
Rule: Under USA Hockey a pin from behind against the boards is checking from behind — its Casebook makes minimal body contact from behind to an opponent close to the wall, with board contact, a minor plus a misconduct (Rule 608, Situation 1)
Rule: Forcefully checking an opponent who is standing along the boards with their back to the middle of the ice draws a major plus a game misconduct or a match penalty under USA Hockey, "in every instance" (Casebook, Rule 608, Situation 1)
Rule: IIHF 101.1 bars two or more players competing for the puck from using the boards to eliminate, push or pin an opponent — so §7's women's exception is live in the corner too
Action: Second man in stays out of the pile and reads where the puck will squirt — he is the one who recovers it
Never: Dive into the same scrum as your teammate — two tangled players and a third opponent skating away with the puck
```

The corner is the one place on the ice where support is closest and most reliable. **Two players usually beat one in a corner** — not because of strength, but because the first player only has to *tie the puck up* rather than win it outright. That "usually" is the coaching account rather than a counted result. What the first player occupies is the puck; the opponent is not a target in their own right. The second player just picks up the loose puck.

This is why the "second man in" is a defined role rather than an accident:

- **First man in** engages the puck carrier, takes body position, and seals them off it. Their job is to make the puck stop moving, not necessarily to win it — **but the puck has to stay the thing you are going for, and what you pin is the puck rather than the player.** That is the safest standard everywhere, and it is what the books that write the restriction down are asking for — **but it is not a universal rule.** ⚠️ **One book writes an exception, and it is narrower than the sentence usually quoted out of it.** Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 hangs off **Rule 7.5(a), checking from behind** — the rule that assesses *"[a] Minor penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty … to any player who intentionally pushes, body-checks, cross-checks while in motion, or otherwise hits an opposing player from behind, anywhere on the ice"* — so it is a carve-out from that one rule, not a general permission. **Read all three of its sentences, because the first two are the ones that govern.** It opens: *"The intent of this rule is not to penalize accidental contact as a result of playing the puck. Provided that contact is marginal in nature and does not endanger the safety of the opponent, no penalty may be assessed."* Only then does it add that *"[p]inning a player to the boards at low speed and with minimal impact is permitted, even if contact is initiated from behind (except under Rule 7.3 – Body-checking, as applicable)."* So what it excuses never leaves the puck, and it is low speed, minimal impact, and endangering nobody. ⚠️ **And the divisions it excepts are the ones most at risk.** Rule 7.3 applies *"only in divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey, and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada"* — so at U13 and below, throughout female hockey, and in any division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved, this permission is not available to those players at all, and 7.3(a) makes an intentional bump, shove or push a minor instead. ⚠️ **And a second book penalises that same fact pattern by name.** USA Hockey's Casebook lists, among the cases where *"[t]he minor plus misconduct penalty must be assessed"* for checking from behind, that *"[a] player makes minimal body contact from behind to an opponent who is in close proximity to them, and board contact is made. This check should be called as a minor and a misconduct as the result of this 'pinch' against the boards from behind"* (Rule 608, Situation 1). ⚠️ **And that is the floor of the same Situation rather than its ceiling** — the passage goes on to require a major plus a game misconduct or a match penalty *"[i]n every instance where a player forcefully checks an opponent who is standing along the boards (back toward the middle of the ice)"*. Same contact, same wall, opposite answers in the two books. Play it anyway: going for the puck rather than the player is the conservative standard, and it is the habit that keeps a battle from becoming a boarding or checking-from-behind penalty. **And the two passages usually quoted for the restriction — IIHF 101.1 and USA Hockey's Rule 604, Note 1 — are each narrower than they look.** **IIHF Rule 101.1** — a **women's-hockey** rule — allows two players in pursuit of the puck to push and lean into each other only *"provided that 'possession of the puck' remains the sole object of the two"* Players; and **USA Hockey's Rule 604, Note 1** counts *"physically forcing the opponent off the puck and with no effort to legally play the puck"* as an illegal body check — but Rule 604 is titled *"Body Checking (Competitive Contact Categories)"* and its Note opens *"For the purpose of this rule"*, so that one reaches the classifications where checking is **barred** (604(a)). Occupying an opponent is not a purpose of its own.
- **Second man in** stays **out of the pile** and reads where the puck will squirt. They are the one who actually recovers it. If they dive into the same scrum, you have two players tangled up and nobody with the puck.

**A corner is a board battle — this section says so in its own first line — so §7's women's exception reaches it too.** IIHF Rule 101.1 does not allow two or more players competing for possession to use the boards *"to make contact with an opponent to eliminate her from the play, push her into the boards, or pin her along the boards."* So in IIHF women's hockey, and in British women's hockey at every age, the first player in seals and goes for the puck; she does not pin the player. An illegal hit there is a minor **or** a major (5') plus an automatic game misconduct (Rule 101.1), and In-House Rule 101 adds a game misconduct and ejection to any major *"in all cases"*. ⚠️ **And where body checking is barred rather than restricted** — USA Hockey 12U and below, all girls'/women's and all non-check adult classifications (Rule 604(a)); Hockey Canada U13 and below, female hockey and any other division a Member has approved (Rule 7.3) — a pin that becomes a bump, shove or push is a minor under **USA Hockey 604(c)** and **Hockey Canada 7.3(a)**, which writes it for a player who *"intentionally body-checks, bumps, shoves, or pushes any opposing player"* in the divisions it names. **Neither book caps it there;** the ladders are in [§5](#5-delivering-a-legal-body-check) and [Defending the net front](#defending-the-net-front). Seal, and take the puck.

**And if you play under body-checking rules, so that neither of those two is your book, the seal still has a limit — one that is not scoped to a category at all.** USA Hockey's **Rule 622** is headed simply *"Holding an Opponent"*, without Rule 604's *"(Competitive Contact Categories)"* qualifier, and its Note defines holding to include *"wrapping one or both arms around the opponent along the boards in a manner that pins them against the boards and prevents them from playing the puck or skating"* — a minor under **622(a)**. ⚠️ **Do not carry that sentence into another book as a quotation.** The boards wording is USA Hockey's alone; the other three reach a pin through general words instead. **NHL 54.1** defines holding as *"[a]ny action by a player that restrains or impedes the progress of an opposing player whether or not he is in possession of the puck"*, with the minor at 54.2 for holding *"by using his hands, arms or legs"* — and **IIHF 54.1** and **54.2** are the same two sentences in the IIHF's own pronouns (*"an opposing Player whether or not they are in possession"*, *"using their hands, arms or legs"*). **Hockey Canada 8.1(a)** gives a minor to a player who *"holds an opponent in any manner."* So the act is reachable in all four books and only one of them names the boards. ⚠️ **And a fifth book prices it higher than any of them: CARHA 63(a)** gives the referee *"a Major penalty"* for a hold *"with the hands, stick or in any other manner"* with nobody hurt, and **63(b)** makes the major mandatory where the hold injures — so under that book a seal that becomes a wrap is not capped at two minutes (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). ⚠️ **And the same three write a permission USA Hockey does not** — a whole-book search of its playing rules and its Casebook returns no such sentence. The one place it addresses the question, it bars the block rather than granting it: a skater may *"use their body position while maintaining their skating lane, to force an opponent to take a less direct route to the puck, so long as they do not use a hand or arm to hold or block the opponent"* — printed in the *Competitive Contact Category* section of both volumes. ⚠️ **Read that as the floor rather than as a rule for non-check hockey alone**, because there is no general USA Hockey arm permission above it for it to be cutting down; treating it as the floor is this document's reading, not something the book says. **Hockey Canada 8.1** and **IIHF 54.2** both allow a player to *"use their arm in a strength move, by blocking their opponent, provided they have body position and are not using their hands in a holding manner, when doing so"* (the IIHF prints *"body position"* in quotation marks), and the NHL writes the same sentence at 54.2 in the masculine. That is the line the seal has to stay on the right side of: **body position seals, and arms wrapped around a player are what turns a seal into a holding minor.** Play as though the call may not come either way.

The commonest amateur error in the corner is two teammates fighting the same opponent for the same puck while a third opponent skates away with it.

### You have less room and more escape routes to cover

```facts
Read: Boards on two sides help you seal, but give them the option to spin either way — up the wall or out behind the net
Action: Angle the carrier so the escape they have is the one you want them to take — toward the wall and up, away from the net
Never: Leave them an escape back toward the slot
```

The corner has boards on two sides, which helps you seal a player but also gives them the option to spin either way — up the wall or out behind the net. Angle the puck carrier so that the escape they have is the one you want them to take: **toward the wall and up, away from the net, never back toward the slot.**

![](diagram:corner-escape-routes)

The two escapes the corner gives them are drawn above. The one you are taking away is not drawn, because your body is where it would be.

### Whose corner is it?

```facts
Convention: Whose corner it is is a coaching choice, not a law of hockey. Find out which one your team plays
Convention: Under the house-default low zone collapse, the low corner belongs to the strong-side defenceman with the centre supporting
Convention: Under a low zone collapse the wingers stay high covering the points and do not come to the corner
Options: Under man-on-man, a winger follows their check into the corner and you get help low — but the point may be left open
Key: On the coaching account a defensive-zone breakdown is two players on different assumptions about who had whom, not an effort problem — the cause, not a ranking; nothing public counts breakdowns
Key: F1/F2/F3 are roles defined by order of arrival, not by position — the first forward to the puck is F1
```

**This is a coaching choice, not a law of hockey.** The default assumed here is a **low zone collapse** (zone-based defensive-zone coverage) with a **2-1-2 forecheck**. Under a low zone collapse, the low corner belongs to the strong-side defenceman with the centre supporting; the wingers stay high covering the points and do **not** come to the corner. Under **man-on-man** coverage, a winger follows their assigned check into the corner and you will get help low — but the point may be left open.

Find out which one your team plays. When a breakdown happens down here, it is almost never that somebody failed to work hard — on the coaching account it is two players operating on different assumptions about who had whom, both skating hard in opposite directions, and the corner is one of the places that ambiguity lives. ⚠️ **That is an account of the cause, not a ranking — nobody counts defensive-zone breakdowns, so no one error can honestly be called the commonest:** public tracking breaks goals down by how they were produced — by the play the **offence** made, not by the defensive breakdown that let them in — so there is no public tally for anyone to rank one against another. What the reasoning above should persuade you of is the mechanism, not the rank. [Defensive Zone Coverage](../systems/defensive_zone_coverage.md) owns this account and carries the limits on the tracking behind it; see also [Forechecking Systems](../systems/forechecking_systems.md).

**F1 / F2 / F3 are roles defined by order of arrival, not by position** — the first forward to the puck is F1 whoever they are.

---

## 9. Net-Front Battles

The area on top of the crease is the **densest scoring area on the ice** — and, contrary to the coaching commonplace, it is **not** where most penalties are taken. Both halves of that are computed for this document further down this section, from the NHL play-by-play feed over 400 games of 2024-25: **26.8%** of the 2,498 goals with coordinates come from within two stick-lengths of the net, a patch that is a tiny fraction of the rink, while only **265 of 2,967** penalties fall inside the net-front box — and the penalties that actually concentrate there are hooking (**2.10×**) and slashing (**1.76×**), not the cross-check. It is dangerous ice in both directions, but not for the reasons it is usually given. Mike Corbett, head coach at the University of Alabama in Huntsville, made the scoring point about his own team this way: *"Probably over 70 percent of our goals were scored within two stick-lengths of the net."* Note the scale this is a claim about: the crease front is a sub-area of the **slot**, and it is the slot as a whole that takes most goals — [Shooting](shooting.md) owns that figure and its limits.

> **At NHL level that number does not hold.** The NHL play-by-play feed used in §9 records coordinates for goals as well as penalties. ⚠️ **Computed from that feed** over the same 400 games of 2024-25 (2,498 goals with coordinates), and taking two stick-lengths as about **10.5 feet** (NHL Rule 10.1 caps a stick at 63 inches): **26.8% of goals were scored within two stick-lengths of the net**, 30.9% within twelve feet, 51.6% within twenty. You have to go out to roughly **thirty feet** — the top of the circles — before you capture 70% of goals.
>
> **So keep the principle and drop the number.** Corbett was describing one season at one NCAA programme, hedged it himself with "probably", and was making a coaching point about where his team scored, not publishing a distribution. The point that survives is that the net front is by far the densest scoring area on the ice — a quarter of all goals come from a patch a couple of stick-lengths wide, which is a tiny fraction of the rink. What does not survive is "70% within two stick-lengths"; do not repeat it as a statistic.

### Attacking the net front

```facts
Technique: Set up in the shot lane in front of the goalie, not beside them, off the red crease line, taking away their eyes — two hands on your stick, blade on the ice, to tip, to screen and to bury a rebound
Key: Screening stands you in the shot lane at close range and upright — know when the puck is about to be released, and go there with your head and face protection right rather than nearly right
Read: Track the puck while feeling the defender — eyes on the puck, body on the player
Technique: Know where the near post is before you plant — a defender walks you along a path with the goal frame at one edge and the goaltender at the other, and you travel it sideways or backwards
Technique: Take the shove braced — wide base, knees bent, weight low, knees and hips giving rather than a locked frame — but never against the goaltender
Never: Meet a goal post head first, and never duck. Head up and chin off your chest at the net front instead, because you can be moved by a defender you are feeling rather than watching
Rule: NHL and IIHF 69.3 — once a goalkeeper establishing position in the crease has made contact with you, give ground at once and re-set with both skates off the crease line; the rule glosses that duty as "give ground to the goalkeeper", and where you do not, "the goal will be disallowed"
Rule: NHL and IIHF 69.3 add a penalty "whether or not a goal is scored" for refusing to give ground — a minor in the NHL's words, "the appropriate penalty" in the IIHF's; their Situation 6 D, a table row, disallows the goal you scored, so refusing costs you the goal or the penalty, never nothing
Rule: Keep your skates off the red crease line, not just out of the blue paint. USA Hockey 625(b) bars a goal with an attacker in the crease, and its Note counts the line as crease, as do the IIHF at 1.7 and Hockey Canada's Glossary — but the NHL's and the IIHF's Situation 5 E allows the goal on it
Rule: In the crease or privileged area, a defender who shoves you into the goalkeeper need not cost you a penalty — USA Hockey's Casebook at 607, Situation 4: "no penalty should be assessed provided the attacking player has clearly made every attempt to avoid the contact"; the attempt saves you
```

- **Get there.** Corbett's first principle is willingness: "You have to have a commitment to getting there before you can play there."
- **Play in front of the goalie, not beside them — and in front of the blue paint, not in it.** "If you're going to get to that blue paint, you need to be able to play right in front of the goalie, and be able to take away the goalie's eyes... you need to play on the shot lane, because the goalie is finding that shot lane." **Read "get to that blue paint" as the ice you are attacking, not the ice you stand on:** the screen is set with both skates outside the crease and off its red line. ⚠️ **Standing in the shot lane is the job here, and it is the reverse of what a shot blocker is taught.** [Shot Blocking](#11-shot-blocking) has the head out of the lane and the padded surfaces facing the puck, because a blocker is protecting the parts of them that armour does not cover; a screener stands in that lane by design — at close range, upright, with a defender on their back, their eyes on the puck and their blade in that same lane to tip — **so knowing when the puck is about to be released is part of the job, and so is going there with your head and face protection right rather than nearly right** ([Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md)). [Playing Without the Puck](../hockey-iq/playing_without_the_puck.md) sets the screener and the blocker side by side and gives the limits of what is known about the injury risk of each.
- **Two hands on your stick, blade on the ice.** You are there to tip, to screen, and to bury a rebound. All three require your blade available.
- **Track the puck while feeling the defender.** Eyes on the puck, body on the player.
- **And that instruction is what makes this the hardest place on the ice to take contact.** Your eyes are deliberately on the puck, so a defender working under your arms to walk you sideways moves you from a body you are feeling rather than watching. **Two things follow.** Know where the near post is before you plant, because the path you get walked along has the goal frame at one edge and the goaltender at the other, and you will be travelling it sideways or backwards. And take the contact the way [Receiving a Hit Safely](#6-receiving-a-hit-safely) teaches it — **wide base, knees bent, weight low, knees and hips giving rather than a locked frame** — with your **head up and your chin off your chest, and never duck**, because the flexed neck is what turns a collision with a post into a spinal injury, and USA Hockey puts the goal post in that mechanism alongside the boards. **Ducking is that same posture arrived at on purpose, so it is the same injury** — the chin stays off your chest whether the contact is coming from a defender, a post or the wall. Its *Heads Up Hockey* principles put the instruction in one line: *"Hit the boards or goal posts with an arm, a leg, or anything but your head first"* (USA Hockey, *Introduction To Body Contact*). **That is not a checking-league point** — a legal box-out moves you along the same path. ⚠️ **And there is one body at the net front you must not take braced — the goaltender's.** Under **NHL and IIHF Rule 69.3**, once a goalkeeper attempting to establish position in the crease has made contact with you, an attacking player who *"does not immediately vacate his current position in the goal crease"* — which the rule itself glosses as *"give ground to the goalkeeper"* — loses any goal scored on the play, and the rule adds a penalty *"whether or not a goal is scored"* — a minor in the NHL's words, *"the appropriate penalty"* in the IIHF's, which caps nothing. ⚠️ **Rule 69 is quoted here in the NHL's words, and the IIHF's are not identical to them** — the IIHF text runs in the plural and puts quotation marks round its defined terms, a difference of grammar rather than of answer, except on the penalty just quoted, where the two books genuinely part. **Both books' own reference tables then take the goal off the board instead of assessing a penalty, where you did score** (Situation 6 D: *"Goal is disallowed. A minor penalty is not assessed (loss of goal only)"*), and put the penalty in the no-goal case, so refusing to give ground costs you the goal or costs you the penalty and never nothing. **Give ground the moment a goalie leans into you, then re-set the screen with both skates off the crease line, not merely out of the blue.** ⚠️ **Under the IIHF — Britain's book — Rule 1.7 puts the red crease line inside the crease, so a screen set on the line is set on the ice where this duty bites.** ⚠️ **And if a defender is the reason you ended up in the goaltender, USA Hockey does write you a way out of the penalty** — its *Casebook* at Rule 607, Situation 4 assesses none *"[i]n the case where a defending player has forced the attacking player into their own goalkeeper"*, but only *"provided the attacking player has clearly made every attempt to avoid the contact with the goalkeeper"*, so the attempt is the thing that saves you rather than the shove. ⚠️ **Read that relief's scope before you carry it to the other end of the zone.** Situation 4 is asked about charging a goalkeeper *"while in their crease or privileged area"*, which takes in the net front and the ice behind the net but not the corners. **Out there the Casebook's next situation is the one on point, and it is not a relief** — Situation 5 says a penalty *"should be assessed in every instance where unnecessary or avoidable contact is made with the goalkeeper – even when outside the privileged area."* **So being shoved is not a defence you carry into the corner; the attempt to avoid is what either situation asks for.** ⚠️ **Rule 69 is an NHL and IIHF provision, and USA Hockey and Hockey Canada run nothing like it — which does not mean they are easier on you.** Under USA Hockey a deliberate shove into a goaltender is charging rather than interference and **607(c)** writes no bare minor for it; under Hockey Canada a major plus a game misconduct is **mandatory** for any player who charges the goaltender. [What it costs to touch the goaltender](#what-it-costs-to-touch-the-goaltender) below prices every book here, and [where you may stand](#where-you-may-stand-when-you-screen) sets out the crease line this duty is measured from. [Offensive Zone Play](../systems/offensive_zone_play.md) sets out Rule 69 and the two tables in full.

### Where you may stand when you screen

```facts
Never: Put a hand, a glove or your stick blade up in front of the goalkeeper's face. Hands and blade down, and let your body do the screening — a screen is your body in their sightline, nothing else
Key: Legal is not safe at the net front — screen with your chin strap done up and a full cage's chin cup in contact with your chin rather than hanging below it, not merely with the equipment on
Rule: A screen set outside the crease without contact is normally legal under NHL and IIHF Rule 69 — their Situation 5 E allows the goal to a screener planted "outside the goal crease" who obstructs the goalkeeper's vision, and 5 C to one in motion there; both are appendix table rows, not rule text
Rule: Rule 69 is "enforced exclusively in accordance with the on-ice judgement of the Referee(s)" (NHL and IIHF 69.1). The red crease line splits the books: the IIHF, USA Hockey and Hockey Canada count it as crease, the NHL's and IIHF's Situation 5 E allows a goal on it — so screen off the crease line
Rule: Neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada reaches a screen set from outside the crease — USA Hockey writes no goaltender-vision provision in its playing rules or its Casebook, and Hockey Canada's two references to the goaltender's view, both at 8.5, reach only a player standing in the crease
Rule: USA Hockey 625(b) bars the goal on position — "No goal may be scored with an attacking player in the goal crease" — unless the puck preceded you in, the goalkeeper is out of the crease area, or a defender's actions put you there; that last relief reaches your position only, not what you do next
Rule: Hockey Canada 8.5 is not flat — a teammate's goal from outside the crease stands if you stay passive in it, and is disallowed once you play the puck, interfere with the play or obstruct the goalie's view or movements, and a screen obstructs the view by definition
Rule: A crease screen can cost the zone with no goal scored, and the books differ — USA Hockey 625(b) sends the faceoff out of the zone, but only with puck in the zone, possession yours and goalie in contact with the crease; IIHF 69.1 leaves it to the referee; NHL 69.6 only after a disallowed goal
Rule: One screening act is a minor with no contact and no crease — the IIHF Situation Handbook, at 69.3 and 75.4, treats a hand, glove or stick held in front of the goalkeeper's "eyes/face with the obvious intent of impairing the ability to see the puck/play" as unsportsmanlike conduct under Rule 75
Rule: Under Hockey Canada a crease screen with no contact costs the goal and nothing else — Note 1 to its Interpretation 3 to 8.5(a): "No penalty would be assessed unless the attacking player's body or stick makes actual physical contact with the goaltender"
Rule: Not every skate in the paint stops play — USA Hockey's Casebook at 625, Situation 10: play "should not be stopped because a player momentarily places their stick and/or skates in the goal crease, unless they interfere with the goalkeeper", but a goal scored while either is in there is disallowed
```

- **Screening is normally legal.** Standing outside the crease in the goalie's sightline without contact is not goaltender interference. What is illegal is impairing the goalie's ability to make a save **inside the crease**. ⚠️ **Rule 69.1's clause (1) is often quoted as though standing anywhere could cost you the goal, and read whole it does not say that** — it sits under *"Goals should be disallowed **only if**"*, which is a ceiling on when a goal may be taken off the board rather than a direction to take one off, and the sentence in 69.1 that actually disallows a goal reaches an attacker who *"enters the goal crease"*. ⚠️ **That sentence sits differently in the two books, and the reading above is cleaner in one of them than the other.** In the IIHF's text *"The overriding rationale…"* closes the paragraph before it and *"If an attacking Player enters the goal crease…"* stands as a paragraph of its own across a page break; in the NHL's it runs straight on from the rationale sentence, where it can be read as illustrating that rationale rather than as a provision standing on its own. **So take the structural reading as firm under the IIHF and loose under the NHL** — which is the reason the answer below comes from the reference tables rather than from the structure. 69.3's no-contact screen ground asks for a significant position *within* the crease, and 69.4 — headed *"Contact Outside the Goal Crease"*, and keyed to the **goalkeeper** being out there rather than to where you are standing — carries no positioning limb at all. ⚠️ **That is not the same as saying it reaches only contact:** one of its limbs penalises an attacker who prevents a goalkeeper who has played the puck outside the crease from returning to it *"due to the deliberate actions of an attacking player"*, and a deliberate action need not be a contact. **That much is a reading of the rule's structure rather than something either book states in terms — but the outcome is not left to the structure.** Both books answer the play outright in their own reference tables: **Situation 5 C** allows the goal where an attacking player skates in front of the goalkeeper *"outside the crease"* and *"impairs the goalkeeper's ability to defend"* their goal. Both books also add that Rule 69 *"will be enforced exclusively in accordance with the on-ice judgement of the Referee(s)"*. ⚠️ **And it does not extend to the red crease line at the edge of the paint — keep your skates off the line, not merely out of the blue.** The IIHF at Rule 1.7, USA Hockey in the Note to Rule 625(b) and Hockey Canada in its Glossary all count that line as part of the crease — **and under two of them, standing on the line is what costs you the goal**. Under **USA Hockey 625(b)**, *"No goal may be scored with an attacking player in the goal crease"*: a flat position bar with no impairment to judge, so a skate on the red line takes your teammate's goal off the board. Under **Hockey Canada 8.5** the goal survives only while the crease player does not *"obstruct the Goaltender's view or movements"* — and a screen obstructs the view by definition. ⚠️ **The NHL's rule text does not say which side of the line the crease starts on**, saying only that the area is *"outlined by the crease lines"* — **but its Reference Table 14 does, and the other way**: Situation 5 E allows the goal to an attacker who *"plants himself on the crease line or outside the goal crease"* and obstructs the goalkeeper's vision. ⚠️ **That row is printed in both books**, so the IIHF answers the same play in two directions — Rule 1.7 putting the line inside the crease, its own Situation 5 E allowing the goal on it — and nothing held here settles it. ⚠️ **And two books attach a cost even where no goal is scored.** Under **USA Hockey 625(b)** a face-off *"shall be conducted at the nearest neutral zone face-off spot"* where an attacking player *"stands, holds their stick, or skates through the goal crease"* — mandatory, but only with its three other conditions met together, the puck in the attacking zone, your team in possession and the goalkeeper in contact with the crease. **The IIHF writes the same consequence and leaves it to the referee**, at 69.1: *"If an attacking Player is in the goal crease and does not leave immediately, the Referee has the possibility to stop the game"*, with the face-off then taken *"at the nearest neutral zone face-off spot outside the attacking zone of the offending team"* — **and the IIHF is Britain's book.** **The NHL is the one that does not have it:** its 69.6 sends the face-off out of the zone only once a goal has already been disallowed. ⚠️ **And it is not every skate in the paint, which is a limb the rule text does not carry.** USA Hockey's *Casebook* at Rule 625, Situation 10 asks whether the Referee should require a Neutral Zone face-off *"every time an attacking player places their stick and/or skates in the opposing team's goal crease"* and answers **no**: *"Play should not be stopped because a player momentarily places their stick and/or skates in the goal crease, unless they interfere with the goalkeeper in any manner."* ⚠️ **Read its second half before you take comfort from the first, because that half is stricter than 625(b)'s own sentence and it reaches your stick:** *"if a goal is scored while an attacking player has their stick and/or skates in the opponent's goal crease while the goalkeeper is in the crease, the Referee shall have no alternative but to disallow the goal."* **So under USA Hockey a blade in the paint takes the goal off exactly as a skate does.** **Standing a skate's width further out costs you nothing in any of the four books and is clear in all of them**, which is why the instruction is off the crease line rather than merely out of the paint. [Rink Map](../foundation/rink_map.md) owns the collision and sets it out. **⚠️ Rule 69 is the NHL's and the IIHF's, and the PWHL runs the same premise at its Rule 71. USA Hockey and Hockey Canada do not run that premise** — **neither reaches a screen set from outside the crease** either, so out there they all leave the clean screen alone. USA Hockey writes no goaltender-vision provision at all — not in its playing rules, whose one *"protective screen"* at Rule 625(a)(1) is a screen set on a checker, and not in its separate Casebook, whose seventeen Rule 625 situations run to stick and bench interference, the crease bar and a goalkeeper's abandoned stick, and write nothing about a screen or the goaltender's view — and Hockey Canada's two references to the goaltender's view both reach only a player standing **in** the crease. **Inside the crease the two part company.** USA Hockey Rule 625(b) is a position bar rather than an impairment test — no goal with an attacker in the crease **whoever scored**, with no impairment to judge, unless the puck preceded them in or the goalkeeper is out of the crease area. ⚠️ **But 625(b) carries one carve-out beyond those two, and it is the one that matters at the net front:** *"if the attacking player has been physically interfered with by the actions of a defending player that causes them to be in the goal crease, play shall not be stopped and any legal goal scored shall be allowed."* So a defender who shoves you into the paint does not thereby kill your team's goal. ⚠️ **That relief reaches your position only, not what you do next** — it excuses where you are, and nothing about playing the puck or contacting the goaltender once you are in there; [Shooting](shooting.md) carries the limit and the penalty that survives it. **Hockey Canada Rule 8.5 is not flat:** it allows a teammate's goal from outside the crease *"provided that the player who was in the goal crease does not attempt to play the puck, interfere with the play or obstruct the Goaltender's view or movements"*, and its Interpretation 3 to 8.5(a) disallows the goal where a player standing in the crease impedes *"the goaltender's vision or movements"* — a counterpart to NHL 69.3, not to 69.1. ⚠️ **And that costs the goal and nothing else.** **Note 1** to the same Interpretation reads: *"No penalty would be assessed unless the attacking player's body or stick makes actual physical contact with the goaltender."* **So under Hockey Canada a crease screen with no contact takes a goal off the board and leaves you on the ice** — which is no reason to set up in the paint, because the goal it takes off is your own team's. [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) carries the full comparison. **Standing clear is only half of it**, because under two of the four books the contact is priced above a minor: [what it costs to touch the goaltender](#what-it-costs-to-touch-the-goaltender) is next. See [Offensive Zone Play](../systems/offensive_zone_play.md).
- **There is one screening act that takes a penalty with no contact and no crease involved, and it is an easy drift.** A screen is your body in the goaltender's sightline. ⚠️ **Putting a hand, a glove or your stick blade up in front of their face is a different act**, and the **IIHF Situation Handbook** answers it twice in all but one word — **Situation 69.3** and **Situation 75.4** both ask whether an attacking player may hold *"the hand/glove or stick directly in front of the goalkeeper's eyes/face with the obvious intent of impairing the ability to see the puck/play"*, the one difference being that 69.3 writes *"its hand/glove"* where 75.4 writes *"the"*, and both answer *"No. This shall be treated as unsportsmanlike conduct and penalized with the assessment of a minor penalty under Rule 75 if witnessed by the Referee(s)."* **Both the 2025/26 and the 2026/27 Handbooks carry it, at the same two numbers**, so the edition question that runs through this document does not arise here. Hands and blade down, and let your body do the screening.
- **And none of that makes the spot a safe one.** ⚠️ **Legal is not safe.** The screen puts you in the shot lane at close range and upright, so knowing when the release is coming is part of the job — and so is taking that ice with your head and face protection right rather than nearly right, chin strap done up and a full cage's chin cup in contact with your chin rather than hanging below it ([Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md)).

![](diagram:screen-the-goalie-sightline)

### What it costs to touch the goaltender

```facts
Never: A screen never has to touch anybody, and being shoved in is no defence — where a defender puts you into the goaltender, only an obvious attempt to avoid them keeps you out of the box
Key: The books do not price contact with a goaltender alike — find out which your league runs, because a braced shove runs from a minor to leaving the game
Rule: USA Hockey sends a braced shove straight to charging — 607(d) Note 1 penalises "deliberate body contact or check that is delivered to the goalkeeper" as charging, and 607(c) writes no bare minor for it "while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or privileged area"
Rule: USA Hockey's "privileged area" is the low zone between the dots, not a box around the paint (607(d) Note 2) — a net-front screener is inside it always, a corner forechecker never. 607(d) adds that a goalkeeper outside it is NOT "fair game" — unnecessary contact is a penalty in every case
Rule: USA Hockey's charging ladder has a rung the referee does not weigh — 607(b) provides that a major plus a game misconduct "shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of charging", and 607(e) adds a reckless-endangerment match penalty option for charging
Rule: Hockey Canada 8.5(a) is a minor and 8.5(b) does not stop there — a major plus a game misconduct at the referee's discretion "based on the degree of violence of impact" with nobody hurt, and mandatory both "to any player who charges the goaltender" and where the contact injures
Rule: Hockey Canada 8.5(c) is a match penalty for a player who "attempts to injure or deliberately injures a goaltender by Interference", and 8.5(d) bars any misconduct for the offence — so there is no ten-minute rung between the minor and the ejection
Rule: Hockey Canada puts the onus "always on the attacking player" — Interpretation 1 to 8.5(a) disallows the goal for contact you initiate "inside or outside of the goal crease", excepting only an attacker whom a defender's foul left unable to avoid it, and Interpretation 2 requires an effort even then
Rule: NHL and IIHF 69.1 allow the goal where incidental contact was "initiated outside of the goal crease" and you "made a reasonable effort to avoid" it — 69.4's separate permission is narrower, keyed to a goalkeeper "in the act of playing the puck outside" the crease
Rule: NHL and IIHF 69.4 protects a goalkeeper who has played the puck outside the crease and is then "prevented from returning" by "the deliberate actions of an attacking player" — a separate foul from 69.3, turning on intent rather than on contact
Rule: NHL and IIHF 69.2 is a penalty "(minor or major, as the Referee deems appropriate)" for deliberate contact inside or outside the crease, so both start at a minor; NHL 42.1 adds a major plus a game misconduct for charging a goalkeeper "within his goal crease", IIHF 42.4 for reckless endangerment
Rule: The route can remove the referee’s minor — a goaltender put into their own net from behind is IIHF 43, not 42: 43.1 names the goal frame, 43.2 writes “no option to award a minor penalty”, 43.3’s game misconduct is automatic with it. To the head it is 48: 48.2 keeps a minor, 48.3’s is automatic too
Rule: The EIHL Casebook adds a crease criterion to IIHF Rule 69 — "Contact in the blue paint area calls for disallowing a goal" — and does not repeat IIHF 69.7's allowance for incidental contact in a rebound or loose-puck scramble, nor say how the two fit
Rule: CARHA 52(b) compels a major plus a game misconduct for charging a goalkeeper "within the goal crease" and for any charge that "injures an opponent" anywhere; 66(b) makes an attacking player's deliberate contact "whether in or out of the goal crease" a minor (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
```

- **Where you may stand and whom you may touch are different questions with different answers, and the two books that leave your screen alone do not leave your contact alone.** ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey a braced shove into a goaltender is not an interference minor — it is charging.** **Note 1** to **Rule 607(d)** splits the two: *"any accidental or unavoidable contact that occurs with the goalkeeper shall be penalized under the Interference rule"*, but *"[a]ny deliberate body contact or check that is delivered to the goalkeeper shall be penalized as charging."* And **607(c)** writes no bare minor for that — *"A minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to a player who body checks or charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or privileged area"* — with **607(e)** adding that *"[a] match penalty for reckless endangerment may also be assessed for charging."* ⚠️ **Read that scope limb before you picture it, because the privileged area is not a small box around the paint.** **Note 2** to the same rule defines it as *"an area outlined by connecting the end zone face-off spots with an imaginary line and imaginary lines from each face-off spot running perpendicular to the end boards"* — the whole low zone between the dots, and the *Casebook* at Rule 607, Situation 6 is asked in terms whether it includes the area behind the goal line and answers *"Yes"*, the area *"extends to the closer end boards"*. **So it takes in the whole net front, the goal line and the ice behind the net.** ⚠️ **What it does not take in is the corners, and that is the half most often got wrong.** Those end-zone face-off spots are placed *"twenty-two feet"* from the imaginary line running goal to goal (Rule 104(d)), so the band is forty-four feet across — and USA Hockey's own recommended rink runs from eighty-five to one hundred feet wide (Rule 102(a)), which leaves at least twenty feet of ice outside the band at each side. **That outside ice is where the corners are.** **USA Hockey's own Glossary settles it from the other direction:** the *Goalkeeper's Warm-Up Area* is defined as the Privileged Area *"extended to include"* the ice between each end-zone face-off spot and where the goal line meets the side boards, so the corners are an extension of the privileged area rather than part of it. **A net-front screener is inside it always; a forechecker in the corner is outside it.** ⚠️ **Being outside the privileged area is not a licence to hit the goaltender, and 607(d) writes that into the same clause as the definition:** *"A goalkeeper is NOT 'fair game' because they are outside the privileged area. A penalty for interference or charging should be called in every case where an opposing player makes unnecessary contact with a goalkeeper."* The *Casebook* at Rule 607, Situation 5 is written on exactly that question: a goalkeeper *"can be legally checked when outside the privileged area"* — **but only in a game Rule 604 permits body checking in, and that is a question about the fixture as well as your age group, because 604(b) puts a checking team playing a non-checking one in a no-checking game whatever its own classification says. Where checking is barred, that hit is a minor under 604(c) in its own right, with 604(d)'s major plus game misconduct and 604(e)'s match penalty above it.** Even in a checking game, a penalty *"should be assessed in every instance where unnecessary or avoidable contact is made"* out beyond the privileged area. **What the privileged area decides is the tier, not whether a goaltender may be touched at all.** [Language and Glossary](../foundation/language_and_glossary.md) owns the term. ⚠️ **And one rung of USA Hockey's charging ladder is not the referee's to weigh:** **607(b)** provides that a major plus a game misconduct *"shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of charging"* — *shall*, not *may*. ⚠️ **Hockey Canada does start at a minor — and does not stop there.** **8.5(a)** is the minor, but **8.5(b)** reaches *"A Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty"* at the referee's discretion *"based on the degree of violence of impact"* with nobody hurt, and **mandatorily** — *"will be assessed"* — *"to any player who charges the goaltender"* and for any such foul that injures. **And no stride count is needed to get there:** Rule 7.4 counts a player who *"[v]iolently and unnecessarily checks an opponent in any manner"* as charging, alongside the two-stride and distance limbs. **8.5(d)** then rules out a misconduct, so there is no ten-minute rung beneath the ejection, and **8.5(c)** is a match penalty for a player who *"attempts to injure or deliberately injures a goaltender by Interference."* Its preamble puts the onus *"always on the attacking player"*, and **Interpretation 1 to 8.5(a)** takes the goal off for contact an attacker initiates *"regardless of whether the contact occurs inside or outside of the goal crease"*, excepting only an attacker whom a defending player's foul left *"unable to avoid contact with the goaltender."* ⚠️ **That exception is conditional, and the condition is the half that gets dropped.** **Interpretation 2 to the same rule** provides that where an attacking player is *"tripped, hooked, cross-checked, or otherwise interfered with, falls, and makes contact with the goaltender, there must be an effort by the attacking player to avoid making contact with the goaltender"*, and without that effort *"they must be penalized for interference with the goaltender"* — the referee penalising the defender's original foul as well, not instead. **Being fouled into a goaltender is not the excuse; the attempt to avoid them is.**
- **The NHL and the IIHF price it from a minor upward instead** — NHL **42.1** is *"[a] minor, major or a major and a game misconduct"* for charging a goalkeeper *"while the goalkeeper is within his goal crease"*, IIHF **42.4** reaches a major plus a game misconduct for recklessly endangering an opponent by charging with nobody hurt, and **69.2** in both books is a penalty *"(minor or major, as the Referee deems appropriate)"* for deliberate contact *"whether or not the goalkeeper is inside or outside the goal crease"*. ⚠️ **That discretion belongs to the charging rule, and the route you arrive by can take it away.** Land the same hit on a goaltender's back and drive them into their own net and the IIHF reaches it under **Rule 43**, checking from behind. **43.2** reads *"This infraction is considered serious and dangerous, therefore there is no option to award a minor penalty for “Checking from Behind”"*, and **43.3** puts *"an automatic game misconduct penalty"* on top of the major. **43.1** names *"the boards, the goal frame, or on open ice"* alike, and **Rule 5.1** makes any reference to *"Player"* cover *"both skaters and goalkeepers"*. **A hit to the head goes to Rule 48 instead** — 42.1 says charging *"is superseded by all similar actions regarding an “illegal hit to the head”, except those related to “fighting”"* — **but 48.2 keeps a minor available there**, so under that book checking from behind is the one route that removes the choice. **What has gone is the two minutes, not the ceiling — and that ceiling is the whole game:** a major plus a game misconduct is the most this book can do to you inside it, and **Rule 20.4** rules that pair *"off the ice for the balance of the game"*, a substitute after five minutes. ⚠️ **So "the IIHF writes no match penalty" is not the lighter answer it sounds like** — it is the same on-ice outcome **NHL Rule 21.1** writes into a match penalty, and **IIHF 43.4** routes anything beyond it to Rule 28 supplementary discipline. *(That Rule 43 reaches a goaltender is a reading of how the book is assembled rather than a sentence the IIHF prints: 43.1 names the goal frame and 5.1 covers goalkeepers, but its Appendix II glossary defines a vulnerable position for* "a skater", *and its Situation Handbook prints* "No situation." *under Rule 43.)* ⚠️ **What 69.2 reaches is contact you meant, and Rule 69.1 marks the other edge of it in terms:** *"Incidental contact with a goalkeeper will be permitted, and resulting goals allowed, when such contact is initiated outside of the goal crease, provided the attacking player has made a reasonable effort to avoid such contact"* — the NHL's words, the IIHF's the same but for quotation marks of its own round *"Incidental contact"* and a capital *"Player"*. ⚠️ **Rule 69.4 carries a second permission and it is the narrower one, so do not read either as the other:** it allows incidental contact *"when the goalkeeper is in the act of playing the puck outside his goal crease"* — the IIHF writing *"their goal crease"* — and only *"provided the attacking player has made a reasonable effort to avoid such unnecessary contact"*, the IIHF quoting *"reasonable effort"* as a term of its own. **Both permissions are bought with the same thing the penalty reliefs below are bought with — the effort to avoid — and neither is a licence to touch a goaltender on purpose.** ⚠️ **So USA Hockey's push-in relief — the 625(b) carve-out for a defender who shoves you into the paint — excuses where you ended up and nothing about what you do once you are there.** ⚠️ **The penalty has a separate relief, and the two are not one axis — they excuse different things and ask different things of you.** USA Hockey's *Casebook* at Rule 607, Situation 4 runs three rungs for charging a goalkeeper *"while in their crease or privileged area"*, and the top one is no penalty at all: *"In the case where a defending player has forced the attacking player into their own goalkeeper, no penalty should be assessed provided the attacking player has clearly made every attempt to avoid the contact with the goalkeeper."* Below it sits a minor plus misconduct where a player going hard to the goal *"makes an honest attempt to avoid the goalkeeper once it is realized contact is unavoidable"*, and below that *"the major plus game misconduct, or match penalty option"* where the player makes no attempt and *"recklessly endangers the goalkeeper by accelerating through the contact, or by extending the arms or dropping the shoulder to deliver the hit"*. **The thread running through all three rungs is the attempt to avoid the goaltender, so make one and make it obvious.** ⚠️ **And that is where it parts company with 625(b): the goal relief carries no avoidance condition at all, while this one turns on nothing else.** Being shoved in is enough to keep your team's goal; only the attempt to avoid keeps you out of the box. ⚠️ **Nor is Situation 4 written for the corners** — its question is scoped to the crease and the privileged area, and out beyond them Situation 5 asks for a penalty *"in every instance where unnecessary or avoidable contact is made with the goalkeeper"*. **Find out which book your league runs**, and in every one of them the instruction is the same: a screen never has to touch anybody. ⚠️ **And not touching anybody is not the whole of it, because one screening act is a minor with no contact and no crease involved** — a hand, a glove or your stick blade up in front of the goaltender's face, which the **IIHF Situation Handbook** makes unsportsmanlike conduct and a minor under **Rule 75** at Situations 69.3 and 75.4, quoted in full under [where you may stand](#where-you-may-stand-when-you-screen). **Hands and blade down; let your body do the screening.** ⚠️ **And one British league draws a harder line inside the paint than the book it plays under.** The **EIHL Casebook 26-27** heads its Rule 69 section *“EIHL Addition to the IIHF Rule Book”* and sets out *“the 3 main criteria to consider/answer”*: *“[i]s the goaltender able to play his position? If he is contacted does he have time to recover?”*; whether the contact was *“initiated by the defensive player or the cause of a player from the opposing team”*; and *“Was the contact made inside (or outside) of the crease? **Contact in the blue paint area calls for disallowing a goal.**”* ⚠️ **That last sentence is shorter than IIHF 69.1, which allows incidental contact and the goal where the contact was *“initiated outside of the goal crease”* and the attacker *“made a reasonable effort to avoid”* it — a relief keyed to where the contact started, not to where the goaltender was standing.** The Casebook prints no such relief, and its Preamble lets it supersede the IIHF ruling on any rule it names. ⚠️ **And there is a second, sharper tension the Casebook does not resolve, so this guide will not resolve it either.** **IIHF 69.7** permits *“incidental contact”* with the goalkeeper *“[i]n a rebound situation, or where a goalkeeper and attacking Player(s) are simultaneously attempting to play a loose puck, **whether inside or outside the goal crease**”*, and says *“any goal that is scored as a result thereof will be allowed.”* **That is the net drive this section teaches, and the Casebook's criterion 3 does not repeat the carve-out.** ⚠️ **Nor does it repeal it.** The section is headed an *Addition*, not a replacement; the Preamble's *“replace or in specific cases, supersede”* leaves the IIHF governing anything the Casebook does not mention, and the Casebook does not say whether its first two criteria qualify the third. **So the honest statement is the narrow one: the EIHL adds a crease-contact criterion the IIHF does not write, and nothing on disk says how it and 69.7 fit together.** **If you play in that league, ask your officials how they are calling a rebound scramble in the paint, and until you have an answer, go to the net expecting the crease criterion to be applied against you.** Elsewhere in Britain the contact limb of IIHF 69.1 and the 69.7 carve-out both stand unamended: the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 change Rule 69 in one respect only, that *“[o]n-ice video review is not available in EIH or SIHA competition”*. **And if CARHA
