is your book, charging the goaltender in the paint stops being the referee's call — and so does any charge that hurts somebody, wherever on the ice it happens.** **Rule 52(b)** assesses a major and a game misconduct — *shall*, not *may* — to *"any player who charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or who injures an opponent as a result of a charge"*, and **52(a)**'s Note counts *"more than two steps or strides"* as a charge, so do not arrive at the paint carrying speed you built up. ⚠️ **That is two limbs joined by an *or*, and only the first one is keyed to the crease.** The second — *"who injures an opponent as a result of a charge"* — carries no crease condition and no goaltender condition either, and **52(a)** writes the same pair into the general charging rule: *"If injury results, a Major penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty shall be assessed."* **So a charge that hurts anybody is a mandatory ejection under this book anywhere on the ice, and running a goaltender out beyond the paint buys you nothing.** **Rule 66(b)** also reaches contact that is not a charge: an attacking player who *"makes deliberate contact with a goalkeeper whether in or out of the goal crease"* draws a minor, and *"At the discretion of the Referee, a Major penalty may be imposed."* **That book writes no privileged area, so the tiering above is not yours — and outside the crease is not free either**: the injury limb reaches there in its own right, and 52(b)'s own Note provides that *"[a] goalkeeper is NOT 'fair game' just because they are outside the goal crease area."* Any major there costs you the rest of the game under **Rule 30(a)** plus the next one under **Rule 32(d)** (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). [Playing Without the Puck](../hockey-iq/playing_without_the_puck.md) sets out the contact rule in full.

### Defending the net front

```facts
Priority: Body position first — inside shoulder ahead of theirs, hips between them and the puck, box out
Action: Tie sticks, don't cross-check — a stick lift — lifted up rather than sideways — or a light press removes the tip and the rebound at far lower risk, taken below their bottom hand
Technique: Full-checking games only, and only from body position you established first — shoulder beneath their armpit, legs driving, walking them out sideways toward the corner, their skates staying on the ice
Rule: USA Hockey's Standard of Play entitles a player to "proper body position … in all age classifications"; interference reaches the drive off it — NHL and IIHF 56.1 need both limbs, "Moving laterally and without establishing body position", and USA Hockey 625(a)(4) one who "changes their skating lane"
Rule: Driving an opponent off an established spot is a push — a minor under USA Hockey 604(c) at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's and all non-check adult, and not capped there: 604(d) is a major plus a game misconduct for reckless endangerment, and 604(e) is a match
Rule: Hockey Canada prices the same push in divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey and in any other division a Member has approved — 7.3(a) is a minor, 7.3(b) is a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence with nobody hurt and mandatory on injury, and 7.3(c) is a match
Rule: In women's hockey it is an illegal hit under IIHF 101.1, where "any move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player" is at least a minor — and not capped there either: 101.1 provides a major (5') plus an automatic game misconduct
Convention: Leverage over strength — the lower player usually wins, though not always and not against a large enough strength gap. Coaching consensus, not a measured law: nothing found in the ice hockey biomechanics literature quantifies it
Read: Eyes on the puck, body on the player — you cannot beat them to a rebound you did not see leave the stick
Never: Walk a body across your own goalmouth, whatever you are allowed to do to them — that path has a goal post at one edge and your goaltender at the other. Take them toward the corner instead
Never: Back into your own goalie — you screen your netminder, and you arrive backwards onto a goaltender who is down, square to the shot and unable to see you coming
```

The order of operations, and the order matters:

**1. Body position first.** Inside shoulder ahead of theirs, hips between them and the puck, box out — the same idea as boxing out for a rebound in basketball. If you are established inside, they have to come through you and the rules let you stand your ground.

**2. Stick ties, not cross-checks.** Corbett: "I'm more of a guy to take away sticks... You have to be able to, one, control sticks more than anything, and then wait for that shot to come and get physical." A stick lift or a light press on their blade removes the tip and the rebound at far lower risk than a cross-check — **lifted up rather than sideways and taken below their bottom hand**; at or near the hands the IIHF's Rule 55.1 reaches it on the location alone — subject to the unresolved carve-out in that rule's own next sentence, in [Stick Checks](#3-stick-checks--legal-effective-and-under-used) — and it is hooking under USA Hockey's Rule 623 where it takes away their ability to pass or shoot with a normal amount of force, and under Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.2(a), which says a hooking penalty *"must be assessed"* where the lift *"contacts the opponent's hands and impedes their ability to pass, shoot, receive"*.

**3. Work under their arms — full-checking games only.** To move someone, get *under* them — your shoulder beneath their armpit, your legs driving, walking them out sideways — **toward the corner, and with their skates staying on the ice**. Pushing from above only pushes yourself backwards. **⚠️ This is the one step in this section that needs a full-checking game — and a full-checking game does not make it free.** ⚠️ **Read that as the game and not the league, because under USA Hockey the fixture decides it.** **Rule 604(b)** provides that *"[a]ny time a team from a Competitive Contact category … plays a team from a Body Checking category, body checking shall be prohibited under this rule"* — so a checking team playing a non-checking team is a no-checking game, and this step is not yours in it whatever your own classification says. Your age group tells you what your book allows; the team on the other bench can take it away for the night (§1). The player you are moving is a screener, so they do **not** have the puck, and a body check is delivered on an opponent in control of it (§5). What reaches you here is **interference**. NHL and IIHF **Rule 56.1** penalise *"Moving laterally and without establishing body position, then making contact with the non-puck carrier"*, and **NHL 56.2(i)** writes a minor for a player who *"interferes with or impedes the progress of an opponent who is not in possession of the puck"*; **USA Hockey Rule 625(a)(4)** names this exact act — *"[a] defending player who changes their skating lane or foot speed in an effort to play the body of an opponent who is no longer in control of the puck"* — **and unlike Rule 604, Rule 625 announces no Competitive Contact scope in its title, so it reaches your checking league too.** ⚠️ **Read both rules for what they leave alone, because neither of them reaches the box-out itself.** NHL and IIHF 56.1 need *both* limbs — *"Moving laterally **and** without establishing body position, then making contact with the non-puck carrier"* — so contact made from a position you did establish is outside it — the quoted wording in this passage is the NHL’s, the IIHF’s the same but for quotation marks round its defined terms; and Rule 625’s own Note defines interference as impeding a non-puck carrier *"with no effort to play the puck, maintain normal foot speed or maintain an established skating lane"*, so a player maintaining an established skating lane is outside that one too. ⚠️ **That is why step 1 comes first and is not optional.** Established body position is the thing 56.1 protects; driving from a position you never established is the thing it penalises, whatever your classification. Boxing out is the part the books protect — though USA Hockey's authority for it is not the passage usually quoted here. ⚠️ **The Note to Rule 604(c) is the one everyone cites, and it sits under a rule scoped to the categories where checking is barred.** The sentences that scope to nothing are in USA Hockey's **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis**: *"A player is entitled to use proper body position and competitive contact in all age classifications in order to gain a competitive advantage"* (Body Checking, Summary), and *"Players are entitled to the ice they occupy as long as they are able to maintain their own foot speed and body position between opponent and puck"* (Restraining Fouls, Summary). The Note to 604(c) still describes this exact situation better than either — it blesses players *"simply maintaining established body position"* and says that *"most often occurs when two players are physically engaged … in front of the goal or along the boards"*, and USA Hockey's **Declaration of Player Safety, Fair Play & Respect** sharpens the phrase to *"legally established body position"*, which is the point exactly. Take the description of the act from the Note to 604(c), and the entitlement itself from the Standard of Play, which is scoped to nothing. And the IIHF's **general** Rule 56.1 carries the NHL's clause in the plural — *"A Player is allowed the ice they are standing on (body position) and are not required to move in order to let an opponent proceed"*, against the NHL's *"A player is allowed the ice he is standing on (body position) and is not required to move…"* — with its **women's** Rule 101.1 adding that players *"are allowed to 'hold their ground' any time that they have established their position on the ice."* Driving them off that position is a different act, and Rule 604(c) draws the line in one sentence — *"Legal competitive contact shall not be penalized under this rule. However, deliberate physical contact with an opponent, with no effort to legally play the puck, shall be penalized"* — a minor in every Competitive Contact classification, which is 12U and below, all girls'/women's, and all non-check adult (Rule 604(a)). **Hockey Canada is blunter still:** Rule 7.3(a) writes a minor for a player who *"intentionally body-checks, bumps, shoves, or pushes any opposing player"* at U13 and below, in female hockey, and in any other division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved. ⚠️ **And neither of those two minors is the ceiling in its own book.** USA Hockey **604(d)** assesses a major plus a game misconduct to a player who *"recklessly endangers an opponent with a body check in a Competitive Contact category"*, and **604(e)** a match penalty; Hockey Canada **7.3(b)** reaches the same pair *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"* — **nobody has to be hurt** — and makes it compulsory where the check injures, with **7.3(c)** a match penalty for a player who *"deliberately attempts to or deliberately injures an opponent by body-checking."* And under **IIHF Rule 101.1** — which is the women's-hockey rule, and the rule women's hockey in Britain plays under — pushing and leaning are permitted only *"provided that 'possession of the puck' remains the sole object of the two"* Players — and the same rule goes on to bar the walk-out itself: *"Any move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player will be assessed at least a minor penalty (2') for an 'illegal hit'."* So in women's hockey, where checking is restricted rather than barred, walking a net-front opponent off her spot is an illegal hit, and 101.1 provides a major (5') plus an automatic game misconduct for the worse ones. **Unless the game you are in permits full body checking, stop at steps 1 and 2**: take the inside position and tie the stick. Neither is scoped to checking leagues, and holding an established position is written as legal in **three of the four books** — the NHL and the IIHF in the clause quoted above at Rule 56.1, and USA Hockey in the Standard of Play summaries quoted above, which apply *"in all age classifications"*. ⚠️ **Hockey Canada is the fourth and it writes no such sentence.** The only place its rulebook mentions body position at all is **Rule 8.1 (Holding)**, which permits a player to *"use their arm in a strength move, by blocking their opponent, provided they have body position and are not using their hands in a holding manner, when doing so"* — a sentence the NHL and the IIHF write too, at 54.2, and a proviso that **assumes** you have body position while permitting an *arm* action, rather than stating that holding a position is legal. So under Hockey Canada the entitlement is unwritten rather than written-legal: the boxing-out itself is not something its book grants you in terms, while Rule 7.3(a) does expressly make the walk-out a minor in the divisions it names. See §1 for which book is yours.

**Step 3 has a direction, and it is the safety half of it: walk them toward the corner, not across your own goalmouth.** Whether you may move them at all turns on the game you are in rather than on your league alone; which way you move them decides what they hit, and that half is the one nobody teaches. A body moved across the front of your own net travels sideways or backwards through a space with a steel goal frame at one edge and your goaltender at the other, and it is the player being moved who meets the post. **USA Hockey puts the goal post in the spinal-injury mechanism alongside the boards** — with the chin tucked to the chest the cervical spine straightens, and *"when a player hits the boards or a goal post head on, the head stops suddenly, but the body's movement continues, compressing the spine"*, and the injury that follows *"can occur at walking speed"* (*Heads Up Hockey Program Guide*). [Receiving a Hit Safely](#6-receiving-a-hit-safely) is written for the player on the other end of this, and it is worth reading from both sides. **Taking them toward the corner is coaching caution drawn from that hazard rather than a rule anyone writes** — but the rulebooks do name the goal frame in the same breath as the boards, so the worst version of this is priced, and not at two minutes: driving a player from behind into the goal frame is a major plus a game misconduct under **USA Hockey 608(b)**, which reaches a check from behind that *"recklessly endangers an opponent, or causes them to go head first into the boards or goal frame"* — its first limb carrying no board or frame condition at all; a **match penalty** under **Hockey Canada 7.5(c)**, which does carry one, for the same hit *"into the boards or goal frame, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*; and checking from behind under **IIHF Rule 43.1**, which reaches a hit *"from behind into the boards, the goal frame, or on open ice"* and writes no minor for it at all (43.2). Charging carries the goal frame in identical words in three books — *"A 'charge' may be the result of a check into the boards, into the goal frame or in open ice"* (NHL 42.1, IIHF 42.1, Hockey Canada 7.4). The tiers are in [Checking from behind](#checking-from-behind-and-what-the-onus-does-and-does-not-mean). ⚠️ **And drive from under the armpit without taking their skates off the ice.** A player whose edges are gone cannot brace and cannot choose what meets the post first. **No book here writes a penalty for that lift on its own:** the IIHF's *"[a] Player who extends their body upward or outward in order to reach their opponent"* is **Rule 48.1(III)**, a limb of the illegal-check-to-the-head rule that reaches you only *"when a Player checks an opponent"* and only where the head or neck is what gets contacted — though that second limit is this document's reading of the rule's title and preamble rather than something the sub-clause states, because the sub-clause joins that half with *"or"* to a second one about using any part of the upper body *"to contact an opponent's head or neck"*, and writes no head-or-neck requirement into the first. So a box-out is outside it either way. And a whitespace-flattened whole-book search of USA Hockey's Playing Rules, USA Hockey's Casebook and Hockey Canada's Playing Rules returns no upward-or-outward extension clause in any of the three. ⚠️ **The NHL and the PWHL do write those words, and not as a definition — as a test.** Their **48.1** lists *"unnecessary extension of the body upward or outward"* among the circumstances that decide whether contact with an opponent's head was avoidable, so where the head is what your lift reaches, the lift is evidence against you — and the **PWHL's 48.2** makes that rule *"a major penalty"* where the NHL's 48.2 is a minor. **So the reason to keep them on their edges is the collision first; the tariff arrives only if their head is what you reach.**

![](diagram:net-front-walk-out-direction)

The picture above draws the direction and nothing else. The only route on it belongs to the player being moved, because theirs is the path that decides what gets hit; where the defender's shoulder is cannot be drawn at all. **And whose job the net front is in the first place is a coaching choice, not a law of hockey** — the picture draws a defenceman because that is the commonest case, but under the house-default low zone collapse it is specifically the *weak-side* defenceman's, and under **man-on-man** it belongs to whoever's check went there, which can as easily be a forward. Find out which your team plays; [Defensive Zone Coverage](../systems/defensive_zone_coverage.md) owns the comparison and sets out who has the net front under each.

**4. Leverage over strength.** Same principle as the board battle, with the same caveats: the lower player usually wins, though not always and not against a large enough strength gap, and it is coaching consensus rather than a measured law.

**5. Eyes on the puck, body on the player.** Feel them; watch the shot. You cannot beat them to a rebound you did not see leave the stick, and a defender who turns to look at the player they are boxing out has stopped defending the dangerous thing.

**6. Do not back into your own goalie.** Being pushed into the crease screens your own netminder and turns saves into goals — you have done the attacker's job for them. ⚠️ **And the goal is not the only cost.** You arrive there backwards, on a goaltender who is usually down, square to the shooter and unable to see you, with the post beside the pair of you — so it is a collision as well as a screen, and it is the reason to fight the shove early rather than ride it into the paint. **That second half is coaching caution: no source here counts injuries from it.** See [Goaltender](../positions/goaltender.md), which is written from the other side of it, and [Receiving a Hit Safely](#6-receiving-a-hit-safely) for how to take the shove itself.

### The cross-check problem

```facts
Rule: Cross-checking is using the shaft of the stick between the two hands to forcefully check an opponent (NHL Rule 59.1)
Never: Believe a blade on the ice makes a shaft shove legal — that wording is USA Hockey Rule 609 only, not NHL, IIHF or Hockey Canada
Rule: USA Hockey's Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking gives the net-front shaft-and-arms shove to create space as the first of its two cross-checking examples (the playing rule itself, 609, gives none)
Key: Cross-checking is the smallest of the seven infractions broken out at the net front, and no likelier there than league-wide — the emphasis is real, the frequency ordinary
Key: Net-front penalty counts are computed for this document from the NHL play-by-play feed, 400 games of 2024-25, not a published finding — hooking and slashing are the fouls that concentrate there
Rule: A cross-check is not capped at a minor in any book, and no major needs an injury — NHL 59.3 on the severity of the contact, with an automatic game misconduct at 59.5 and a match penalty at 59.4; USA Hockey 609(b) and 609(c) reach both tiers for reckless endangerment
Rule: Under the IIHF book, Britain's, a cross-check reaches a major plus an automatic game misconduct at 59.3 for recklessly endangering an opponent, nobody hurt — identical in the 2025/26 v1.1 and 2026/27 editions, and no match tier sits above it, only Supplementary Discipline (59.6)
Rule: Hockey Canada 9.2(b) makes a cross-check a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence of impact with nobody hurt, and mandatory where it injures; 9.2(c) is a match penalty; and Interpretation 2 to 9.2(b) sends one above the shoulders to Rule 7.6, "whether or not injury results"
Rule: CARHA 54(b) is a major plus a game misconduct for a cross-check above the shoulders, "whether or not injury results", and 54(c) the same where you crosscheck a goalkeeper in the crease or injure anyone; 54(d) is a match penalty (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: A cross-check to the back is often not cross-checking at all — NHL 43.3, IIHF 43.1 and PWHL 43.3 name it inside checking from behind, where all three write no minor rung whatever (43.2); Hockey Canada Interpretation 1 to 7.5(a) and CARHA 53(b) route it there too, and USA Hockey by force
Action: Control the stick instead of the body — a stick lift achieves what a cross-check is trying to achieve, lifted up rather than sideways, and is legal when the lift is taken below their bottom hand
Read: Establish position early and quietly; engage physically when the shot comes, not before
```

**Repeated cross-checking to the back is the penalty officials are specifically told to look for at the net front.** USA Hockey defines cross-checking at **Rule 609** as "using the shaft of the stick between the two hands to forcefully check an opponent with no portion of the stick on the ice", and its enforcement standard is written separately, in the **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking**. That document gives exactly this scenario as the first of its two cross-checking examples: "When a player in front of the goal uses the shaft of the stick and extension of the arms to contact an opponent in a dangerous manner to create space." (The second is the same shove on a player protecting the puck along the boards. Rule 609 itself carries no examples at all.)

> **What the call data actually shows.** The NHL's public play-by-play feed records every penalty with both its infraction type and its rink coordinates, so this is checkable. **Computed from** `api-web.nhle.com/v1/gamecenter/{id}/play-by-play`, games 2024020001–2024020400 of the 2024-25 regular season: **2,967 penalties**, of which **265** fall inside a net-front box 10 to 25 feet out from the goal line and within 10 feet either side of centre.
>
> | Infraction | Share of net-front calls | League-wide share | Enrichment |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | Hooking | 25.3% | 12.1% | **2.10×** |
> | Slashing | 15.1% | 8.6% | **1.76×** |
> | Interference | 10.6% | 8.3% | 1.27× |
> | Tripping | 10.2% | 16.6% | 0.61× |
> | Roughing | 8.3% | 12.0% | 0.69× |
> | Holding | 7.2% | 8.6% | 0.83× |
> | **Cross-checking** | **5.7%** | **5.2%** | **1.08×** |
>
> **Two things follow, and neither is the folklore.** First, **cross-checking is not the most-called penalty at the net front** — of the seven infraction types broken out it is the smallest, and the other 17.6% of net-front calls were never broken out at all. Second, and less comfortably for the claim above, **it is not meaningfully over-represented there either**: at 1.08× it is called in that box at almost exactly its league-wide rate. The penalties that actually concentrate at the net front are **hooking and slashing** — stick fouls on a player trying to get to a puck — not cross-checking, and not roughing (0.69×, which is a post-whistle penalty spread all over the ice rather than a net-front one).
>
> **And a third thing does not follow: these seven figures will not add up into a bigger claim.** The league-wide column ranks seven infraction types one at a time, and that is all it does. They are 71.4% of the calls between them; the other 28.6% were never broken out, and the infractions outside the seven are of both kinds — high-sticking and spearing on one side, boarding, charging and elbowing on the other. ⚠️ **The biggest of the seven cannot be sorted into either kind.** Tripping is a stick offence in no book, and that is the NHL classifying its own rules rather than an inference from how the offence is committed: **Rule 23.5's *"Stick Infractions Category"* is butt-ending, cross-checking, hooking, slashing and spearing, and tripping is not in it.** Nor is it in the IIHF's Section 08 or Hockey Canada's Section 9, which both begin at butt-ending. And the offence does go both ways: NHL Rule 57.1 names *"the stick, knee, foot, arm, hand or elbow"* and 57.2 *"his stick or any portion of his body"*, so the feed's single tripping type covers a stick trip and a leg trip alike. Group tripping's 16.6% with the stick fouls and the stick fouls lead these seven, 42.5% to 28.9%. Leave it out and the others lead, 45.5% to 25.9%. **The same seven figures answer "are stick fouls the biggest group of calls?" both ways, which means they do not answer it.** What they support is the ranking of one infraction against another, and nothing above that.
>
> So treat "the penalty officials are told to look for at the net front" as exactly what USA Hockey's Standard of Play makes it — **an enforcement emphasis, an instruction to officials about what to watch for** — and not as a claim about how often it is actually called. The rule below is still worth knowing precisely, because the emphasis is real even where the frequency is ordinary.

The rule itself is narrow and easy to break by accident. NHL Rule 59.1: *"The action of using the shaft of the stick between the two hands to forcefully check an opponent."* That is the whole definition — **there is no blade-on-ice element in it.**

> **A common and costly misconception.** You will hear that keeping your blade on the ice means it cannot be a cross-check. That is **not** the NHL rule, nor the IIHF rule (Rule 59), nor Hockey Canada's, nor the PWHL's, nor CARHA's — under every one of them a two-handed shaft shove is a cross-check whatever your blade is doing. The blade-on-ice phrasing comes from **USA Hockey Rule 609 and its Standard of Play emphasis material**, where it is an officiating cue for one association, not a definition of the offence. ⚠️ **USA Hockey is the only book here that carries it**, so unless that is the book you are playing under, you shove with the shaft and you are exposed regardless of your blade.

**And a cross-check is not a two-minute foul in any book here.** Every one of them writes a major above the minor, and none of those majors needs an injury.

- **NHL.** 59.2 makes the minor discretionary *"based on the severity of the contact"*, and **59.3 makes a major discretionary on the same test** — the severity of the contact, not the consequence. **59.5 then attaches an automatic game misconduct to every cross-checking major:** *"When a major penalty is assessed for cross-checking, an automatic game misconduct penalty shall be imposed"*. **59.4** is a match penalty where, in the referee's judgment, *"the player attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent by cross-checking."* **The PWHL writes the same four rungs, renumbered to its Rule 60** — a discretionary minor at 60.2 and a discretionary major at 60.3, both *"based on the severity of the contact"*, an automatic game misconduct with the major at 60.5, and a discretionary match penalty at 60.4.
- 🇬🇧 **IIHF — your book in Britain.** **59.3** is headed *"Major Penalty and Game Misconduct Penalty"*, and the referee may assess *"a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty if, in their judgment, the Player recklessly endangers their opponent by a 'cross-checking' and the Player would not be sufficiently sanctioned by imposing a minor penalty."* **Nobody has to be hurt.** The wording is word for word identical in the 2025/26 (Version 1.1) and 2026/27 editions, so the edition question that bites on high-sticking does not bite here. There is no tier above it on the ice, because the IIHF book writes no match penalty anywhere — 59.6 allows Rule 28 supplementary discipline above it, at the Proper Authorities' discretion.
- **USA Hockey.** **609(b)**: *"A major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of cross-checking."* **609(c)**: *"A match penalty for reckless endangerment may also be assessed for cross-checking."* Neither turns on injury either.
- **Hockey Canada.** **9.2(b)** gives the referee a major plus a game misconduct *"based on the degree of violence of impact"*, and makes the same tier compulsory — *"will be assessed"* — for any player *"who injures an opponent by a cross-checking infraction that would otherwise call for a Minor penalty"*; **9.2(e)** requires the game misconduct with any such major; **9.2(c)** is a match penalty for a player who *"attempts to injure or deliberately injures an opponent by cross-checking."* One thing this book does differently is worth knowing at the net front: its definition of the foul leaves out the word *"forcefully"* that the NHL, the IIHF and USA Hockey carry — CARHA's leaves it out too, with no interpretation to put it back — and **Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.2(a)** hands the force test back to the referee, who *"should consider whether the player is 'pushing' or 'striking' an opponent with the shaft of their stick. If a pushing motion is used, a penalty may not be necessary. However, a striking motion must be penalized."* So what is weighed there is the force, not whether contact happened: a push is still a cross-check on the book's own definition, and still the referee's to call.
- **CARHA — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, and the book that takes the discretion away twice.** **54(a)** is a minor or, at the referee's discretion, a major plus a game misconduct. Above that it stops asking. **54(b)** assesses a major plus a game misconduct to any player who strikes an opponent *"above the normal height of the opponents shoulders with a cross-check"*, *"whether or not injury results"* — so the shove that rides up is priced there in the cross-checking rule itself rather than routed out to head contact the way Hockey Canada routes it. **54(c)** assesses the same pair *"to any player who crosschecks a goalkeeper while they are in the goal crease, or who injures an opponent as a result of a cross-check"* — ⚠️ **two limbs joined by *"or"*, so the injury limb carries no crease and no goalkeeper in it at all.** **54(d)** is a match penalty for a player who *"deliberately attempts to or deliberately injures an opponent with a cross-check."* **Carry the crease limb to the net front:** under that book, shaft-shoving the goaltender in his own crease is not a call the referee can keep at two minutes.

**And Hockey Canada takes the referee's discretion away for the cross-check that most concerns a net-front player.** Its Interpretation 2 to Rule 9.2(b): *"A Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty for Head Contact **must** be assessed to any player who strikes an opponent with a cross-check above the normal height of the shoulders."* That one leaves Rule 9.2 altogether and is dealt with under **Rule 7.6 – Head Contact**. A shove that starts between the shoulder blades and rides up is not a worse cross-check under that book; it is a different and harsher foul. ⚠️ **And the rule it lands in prices it with nobody hurt, in terms.** Both of Hockey Canada's head-contact rules carry the same sentence — **7.6(b)** for minor and female hockey and **7.7(b)** for junior and senior: *"Any player who strikes an opponent above the normal height of their shoulders with a cross-check will be penalized with a Major penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty, whether or not injury results."* Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.6(b) narrows what counts — it *"is not intended to penalize players who make minimal, accidental contact with an opponent's head with their stick"*, but is aimed at *"a deliberate cross-checking action"* that strikes the head or neck — and that is the only softening either rule offers. There is no injury threshold to fall short of.

🇬🇧 **In Britain the same contact is routed the other way — and it is a labelling direction, not a change to what the foul costs.** The In-House Rules' **Section 4** tells officials to name the foul they are actually calling rather than reaching for a catch-all label, and gives this case in terms: *"If one player cross checks another player in the head or neck area, we want the penalty to be called Cross Checking, not Checking to the Head."* So where Hockey Canada sends a cross-check above the shoulders out of its cross-checking rule and into head contact, Britain keeps it in cross-checking. **That does not make it cheaper.** Under the IIHF book the two ladders are written the same way — a discretionary minor, then a major plus an automatic game misconduct for recklessly endangering an opponent: **48.2** and **48.3** for an illegal check to the head or neck, **59.2** and **59.3** for cross-checking. The label changes what the call is named, and what your league's supplementary discipline sees, not the rungs the referee has.

**And the shove that most often turns serious at the net front and on the wall is not priced as a cross-check at all, so never put the shaft into a player's numbers.** ⚠️ **Three books write the cross-check into their checking-from-behind rule in terms, and not one of the three has a minor rung in it.** **NHL 43.3** assesses a major to *"[a]ny player who cross-checks, pushes or charges an opponent from behind who is unable to protect or defend himself"*, *"anywhere on the playing surface"*, while **43.2** provides that *"[t]here is no provision for a minor penalty for checking from behind"* and 43.5 makes the game misconduct automatic with the major. **PWHL 43.3 and 43.2 are those two sentences word for word** but for the pronoun and the cross-reference each points at. And **IIHF 43.1** — Britain's book — reaches it from the definition instead: *"A Player who hits an opponent from behind into the boards, the goal frame, or on open ice in any manner (i.e., high-sticking, cross-checking, etc.) shall be penalized according to this rule."* ⚠️ **The other three draw the line for you, each in its own way.** **Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 7.5(a)** is asked that exact question and answers it on motion and on destination: *"If the player is stationary and cross-checks an opponent from behind, then a penalty for Cross-checking should be assessed. If the player is in motion and cross-checks an opponent from behind, then a penalty for Checking from Behind will be called. If a player is cross-checked from behind into the boards or the goal, then Checking from Behind will be called."* **CARHA 53(b)** names the cross-check inside its flat match penalty, for a player *"cross-checked… in any manner from behind into the boards or goal net, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*, and its Note instructs referees *"not to substitute other penalties when a player is checked from behind in any manner"* (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). **USA Hockey draws the same line by force, and draws it on the board battle this section is about** — its *Casebook* at **Rule 609, Situation 2** describes a player protecting the puck *"by having their back turned to center ice"* and an opponent who will *"extend the arms and make contact to the back of the opponent with the stick, but not use enough force to be considered a check from behind. This action should still be considered a cross-checking violation."* ⚠️ **Read which way that Casebook answer points: the light shove is the cross-check, and the forceful one is the other rule** — **608(a)**, whose floor is a minor *plus* a misconduct, with **608(b)**'s major plus a game misconduct for a player who *"recklessly endangers an opponent, or causes them to go head first into the boards or goal frame, as a result of checking from behind."* **So on the wall and at the net front the tier is decided by where their numbers are pointing, not by how hard you thought you pushed.** [Checking from behind](#checking-from-behind-and-what-the-onus-does-and-does-not-mean) prices that rule in all six books.

The real defence is not a stick position, it's a different action: **control the stick instead of the body.** A stick lift achieves what a cross-check is trying to achieve, and is legal **when the lift is taken — lifted up rather than sideways, not chopped — below their bottom hand** — at or near the hands the IIHF's Rule 55.1 reaches it on the location alone — subject to the unresolved carve-out in that rule's own next sentence, in [Stick Checks](#3-stick-checks--legal-effective-and-under-used) — and it is hooking under USA Hockey's Rule 623 where it takes away their ability to pass or shoot with a normal amount of force, and under Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.2(a), which says a hooking penalty *"must be assessed"* where the lift *"contacts the opponent's hands and impedes their ability to pass, shoot, receive"*.

Corbett's warning about timing is worth repeating: "if you get physical before the puck is on its way, you're going to get a penalty." Establish position early and quietly; engage physically when the shot comes, not before.

---

## 10. Loose Puck Races and 50-50 Pucks

A **50-50 puck** is any puck that neither team possesses and both can reach — a rim around the boards, a dump-in, a rebound, a puck flipped out of the zone.

### Arrive with body position, not just with speed

```facts
Position: Skate the inside lane, between the opponent and the puck — half a step behind but inside usually wins it
Rule: USA Hockey: a skater is entitled to the ice they occupy so long as they maintain their skating speed and body position between an opponent and the loose puck — and, separately, is entitled to stand their ground and not move if an opponent wishes to skate through (Standard of Play)
Rule: The NHL attaches no speed condition — a player "is allowed the ice he is standing on (body position) and is not required to move in order to let an opponent proceed" (NHL Rule 56.1)
Technique: Approach on an arc so you arrive already turned, and turn your hips so your backside is between opponent and puck
Position: Get low and wide, feet outside your shoulders, and keep your feet moving — a stationary player on the wall gets pinned
Never: Use a stick, arm, hand or skates to hold or obstruct, your free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab, or the position itself as cover for a check that would otherwise be illegal
Rule: The free hand may fend off an opponent or their stick, but may not hold their stick or body — NHL and IIHF Rule 56.1; Hockey Canada 8.1 permits blocking a stick but not holding it
Rule: USA Hockey writes the fend-off permission in its Casebook only, and conditions it — control the puck (Standard of Play Situation 6) or be reaching for it, engaged in a corner or in front of the goal (Situation 7), hold a position you "legally established", and never push off to create space
```

Winning the race is not the same as winning the puck. The player who arrives half a step behind but *on the inside*, between the opponent and the puck, usually ends up with it.

![](diagram:inside-lane-longer-route)

The rules are explicit that this is legal. USA Hockey: "A skater is entitled to the ice they occupy so long as they maintain their skating speed and body position between an opponent and the loose puck," and "A skater can use their body position while maintaining their skating lane, to force an opponent to take a less direct route to the puck, so long as they do not use a hand or arm to hold or block the opponent." USA Hockey states the stationary case as a separate entitlement of its own, in the bullet directly beneath the first of those two: "A skater is entitled to stand their ground and is not required to move if an opponent wishes to skate through that area of the ice."

**The NHL grants the entitlement too, and attaches no speed condition at all** — so do not read USA Hockey's skating-speed wording as meaning that stopping forfeits your ice. NHL Rule 56.1: a player "is allowed the ice he is standing on (body position) and is not required to move in order to let an opponent proceed," and he "is always entitled to use his body position to lengthen an opponent's path to the puck, provided his stick is not utilized… his free hand is not used and he does not take advantage of his body position to deliver an otherwise illegal check." Maintaining skating speed is one of USA Hockey's routes to the entitlement, not a condition on it.

USA Hockey states the same limit in its body-checking guidance: "Two skaters who use competitive contact for position as they skate to a loose puck are within their rights to do so, unless one uses their stick, arm, or skates to obstruct the opponent's ability to skate to the puck."

**So: skate the inside lane. Make them go the long way round. Do not use your hands.**

The mechanics of the arrival itself, in the order they happen: **approach on an arc**, not straight at the puck, so that you get there already turned; **turn your hips** so your backside is between the opponent and the puck rather than arriving square; **get low and wide**, feet outside your shoulders; **keep your feet moving**, because a stationary player on the wall gets pinned while one with momentum can escape along the boards or spin out to the middle; and **fend with the free hand, never hold** — NHL and IIHF Rule 56.1 permit the free hand to "fend off" an opponent or their stick but not to hold, pull, tug or grab — USA Hockey writes its version in the Casebook instead, and conditions it on controlling the puck or — where two players are *"physically engaged in the corner or in front of the goal"*, which is the only place Situation 7 is written for — reaching for it, on a position you legally established, and on never pushing off to create space (Standard of Play Situations 5, 6 and 7); Hockey Canada 8.1 permits blocking a stick but not holding it (§4 above).

### First to arrive, last to commit

```facts
Convention: Get there early, then decide late — a coaching heuristic in common use, not a law, with no named originator
Action: Arrive early, slow into a wide balanced stance, then read the pressure and chip, reverse, protect or turn up ice
Never: Arrive at speed already committed — a full-speed reach, a spin or a blind bank gives away all your options
Risk: Winning the race and immediately swinging at the puck turns a won race into a turnover
Read: The limit — in your own slot, or with an opponent bearing down on you, there is no beat to wait; get it out
Key: Upright, balanced and looking around is far harder to hurt than stretched out at full extension with your head down
```

The idea is this: **get there early, then decide late.** A player who arrives at speed with their body already committed to one play — a full-speed reach for the puck, a spin, a blind bank — has given away all their options and is easy to defend. A player who arrives early, slows into a wide balanced stance, and *then* reads the pressure can chip, reverse, protect, or turn up ice as the situation dictates.

The failure it is designed to prevent is specific: a player wins the race, immediately swings at the puck, misses it or shoots it into a shin pad, and turns a won race into a turnover. Arriving early and then taking body position and **waiting a beat** is very often the better play, because your support is still arriving and every tenth of a second improves your options. Its limit is equally specific: in your own slot, or with an opponent bearing down on you, there is no beat to wait. Get it out.

This is also the safest way to arrive. A player who is upright, balanced and looking around is far harder to hurt than one stretched out at full extension with their head down.

**Offered as a coaching heuristic, not as a law** — the phrase is in common use, with no named originator behind it.

### Protecting yourself on the boards

```facts
Action: Decide before you get there whether you are taking the puck off the wall, chipping it along, or protecting it
Never: Arrive bent over with your back to the play. Get side-on instead
Never: Turn your back to the wall in this race, and never duck. Puck races to the wall are where checking-from-behind and boarding injuries happen, so everything in Section 6 applies here
Rule: Unnecessary contact into the boards with a player playing an obvious icing or offside puck is boarding — NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 all write that clause; USA Hockey reaches only the icing half, at 624(d). That protects you, but do not rely on it
Technique: Get your skates parallel to the wall, head up and chin off your chest, and take the contact on your forearm and hip
Never: Meet the wall on the point of your shoulder. Take it on your forearm and hip instead — and not on the whole shoulder either, if you can help it
```

Everything in Section 6 applies at exactly this moment, because puck races to the wall are where checking-from-behind and boarding injuries happen. The specific points:

- **Decide before you get there** whether you are taking the puck off the wall, chipping it along, or protecting it. Arrive with the decision made.
- **Never arrive bent over with your back to the play.** Get side-on.
- **Remember the icing rule protection.** The NHL, the IIHF and Hockey Canada all class unnecessary contact with a player playing an obvious icing or offside puck, resulting in impact with the boards, as **boarding**. That protects you — but do not rely on it. Get your skates parallel to the wall, head up and chin off your chest, and take the contact on your forearm and hip — **not the point of your shoulder, not the whole shoulder if you can help it**, and never your back.

### Chipping and the escape

```facts
Technique: The one-touch — arrive and immediately put the puck somewhere useful
Options: Chip it past the defender to your own speed, bank it off the wall to a teammate, or rim it around
Goal: Convert a coin flip into a possession, and take yourself out of the collision
Read: Rim when a checker is sealing you against the wall; reverse when one has over-committed and skated past you
Action: Pick by the checker's momentum, and pick before you arrive
```

The highest-value skill in a 50-50 race is the one-touch: arriving and immediately putting the puck somewhere useful — chipped past the defender to your own speed, banked off the wall to a teammate, or rimmed around. It converts a coin flip into a possession, and it takes you out of the collision.

**Rim and reverse are opposite plays and they are chosen by where the pressure is, so do not treat them as the same escape.** A **rim** sends the puck *onward* around the boards, with the flow of play, and beats a checker who is sealing you against the wall. A **reverse** sends it *back against* the flow, into the ice the checker has just left, and beats one who has over-committed and skated past you. Pick by the checker's momentum, and pick before you arrive.

---

## 11. Shot Blocking

Blocking shots is genuinely useful and genuinely dangerous. Both facts matter.

### Shot Blocking: Technique

```facts
Position: Get close — the closer to the shooter, the more net you take away and the less the puck can accelerate or deviate; that getting close is also safer is coaching craft, not a measured finding
Technique: Stay on your feet where you can — a standing block leaves you in the play
Technique: Present your padded surfaces — shin guards front-on, thigh, hip, body square to the puck
Position: Head up and out of the shot lane, feet on the ice, hands behind your body with the backs of the gloves out — tucked in behind your shin pads is the going-down form, not a standing one
Never: Turn your back, turn sideways, or lift a leg — all three expose what armour does not cover and blind you to the puck
Never: Lead with your stick — a puck deflected off an outstretched blade goes upward, often into your own face. That mechanism is coaching craft rather than anything measured here
Never: Lunge. Close with your feet instead, because a slide that misses is a defender out of the play
```

**Get close.** The closer you are to the shooter, the more net you take away and the less time the puck has to accelerate or change direction. Blocking from ten feet away is far safer and more effective than blocking from three feet in front of your own goalie, where a deflection is a goal. ⚠️ **The "safer" half of that is coaching craft rather than a measured finding.** The geometry is plain enough — less net for the shooter to see past, and less distance for the puck to rise or deviate. What nothing held here measures is *injury* against blocking distance, and **no governing body's safety material stands behind this claim or the stick-deflection one below** — the only shot-blocking source this document cites for it is a coaching site rather than a rulebook or a safety programme. The instruction is retained on that footing, and labelled rather than dropped.

![](diagram:shot-block-get-close)

**Stay on your feet where you can.** A standing block leaves you in the play, and a puck blocked on your feet often becomes a breakaway going the other way. A dropped block takes you out of the play whether it works or not, and if you miss, the shooter walks around you into a 2-on-1.

**Present your padded surfaces.** Shin guards front-on, thigh, hip. Keep your body square to the puck.

**Protect what is not padded.** Keep your head up and out of the shot lane, keep your feet on the ice, and keep your hands behind your body with the backs of the gloves out. ⚠️ **The other hand position you will hear — tucked in behind your shin pads — belongs to a block taken on the ice, and the two are not interchangeable.** Stand up and try it: a player on their feet cannot get their hands behind their shin pads without folding forward at the waist, which brings the head down into the low shot lane and costs them the one cue that never bends. **So on your feet the hands go behind your body; behind the shin pads is what you have once you are down.** Going to the ice is set out in [Defender](../positions/defender.md), which owns it — this section is written for the block you take standing. Never turn your back, never turn sideways, never lift a leg — all three expose the parts of you that armour does not cover, and turning away means you cannot see the puck.

**Do not lead with your stick.** A puck deflected off an outstretched blade goes upward, often into your own face. Take your stick out of the lane or keep it flat on the ice beside you. ⚠️ **That the deflection goes upward is coaching craft on the same footing** — widely taught, and measured by nothing held here.

**Close with your feet, not by lunging.** A lunge is a slide, and a slide that misses is a defender out of the play with no way to recover.

### When *not* to block

```facts
Never: Block the shot on a 2-on-1 — your job as the lone defender is the pass, and the goalie has the shooter
Options: A timed slide at the pass, where that is your team's 2-on-1 method, is a different act from going to the ice at the shot — head up either way, and ask your coach how they want it done
Rule: USA Hockey Casebook 639, Situation 4 — a slide that brings an opponent down is tripping "regardless of who gains possession of the puck", and the sole exemption is a knee-drop shot block whose "momentum carries them into the player shooting the puck"
Rule: USA Hockey 639(b) makes "a major penalty plus a game misconduct penalty" mandatory for tripping a referee judges to recklessly endanger an opponent — nobody has to be hurt for it to be called
Never: Block from a screening position — a deflection off a body the goalie could not see is one of the most common ways soft goals are conceded, on the coaching account rather than a count
Priority: Either commit fully and take the shot, or get out of the lane — never stand half in the way with your eyes closed
Never: Dive to block a low-danger point shot while an unmarked opponent stands at the goalmouth
Read: Stay on your feet until the puck is actually released — committing early is what a shot fake is designed to draw
Convention: How much your team blocks is a coaching choice — find out which yours plays, and ask your goalie what they want
Key: Blocking puts your shins, feet and hands in the puck's path deliberately — shin guards that cover shin and ankle, gloves not worn through in the palm
```

This is the part that gets skipped, and it is where blocking costs goals.

- **On a 2-on-1.** Your job as the lone defender is the pass, not the shot — the goalie has the shooter. Sliding to block on a 2-on-1 takes you out of the passing lane and turns a manageable situation into a tap-in. **Note precisely what that forbids: going to the ice at the *shot*.** Some teams do coach a timed slide at the *pass* as their 2-on-1 method, and where that is the system the slide is the plan — but that is a slide at the *pass*, not a drop in front of the *shot*, and it is a different action on a different axis — nothing in this section's orientation guidance should be assumed to transfer to it. **And it is not a free mistake if you misjudge it.** Under USA Hockey a player who leaves their feet and slides into an opponent, bringing them down, is tripping *"regardless of who gains possession of the puck"* — and the **only** exception the Casebook names is narrower than a shot block: it is *"when a player drops to their knees to block a shot **and their momentum carries them into the player shooting the puck, causing them to fall**"* (Rule 639, Situation 4). ⚠️ **That is not a general licence for going to the ice** — this section teaches the block taken standing, and [Defender](../positions/defender.md) owns the going-down form. Rule 639(b) then makes *"a major penalty plus a game misconduct penalty"* mandatory for one a referee judges to recklessly endanger an opponent, and nobody has to be hurt for that to be called. See [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md), which sets out both systems and says plainly that the going-down technique for the pass-lane slide is not settled teaching there.
- **When you are screening your own goalie.** If your goalie cannot see the puck because you are in front of it, do not add a deflection to the problem. A blocked shot that changes direction off a defender the goalie could not see is one of the most common ways soft goals are conceded — the coaching account, not a counted breakdown. Either commit fully and take the shot, or get out of the lane and let your goalie see it — **the one thing you cannot do is stand half in the way with your eyes closed.**
- **When you would leave a better scoring chance unguarded.** Diving to block a low-danger point shot while an unmarked opponent stands at the goalmouth is a losing trade.
- **When the shooter is faking.** Committing early to a block is exactly what a shot fake is designed to draw. Stay on your feet until the puck is actually released.

**How much your team blocks shots is a coaching choice.** Some systems ask defenders to block aggressively and collapse in front of the goalie; others ask them to take away the lane while staying on their feet and let the goalie see everything. Neither is right in the abstract. Find out which your team plays and, in particular, ask your goalie what they want — see [Goaltender](../positions/goaltender.md) and [On-Ice Communication](../foundation/on_ice_communication.md).

**Blocking puts your body in the puck's path on purpose, so your equipment is being asked to do its actual job.** Shot blockers should have shin guards that actually cover the shin and ankle, and gloves that are not worn through in the palm. See [Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md).

---

## 12. Playing Physical Without Taking Penalties

### The discipline point

```facts
Key: Most amateur penalties are not dirty — they are late, frustrated or lazy; the coaching account, since no published study codes why a penalty was taken
Rule: Illegal means are acts that establish, maintain or restore body position other than by skating (NHL Rule 56.1)
Action: If you lost position, the only legal fix is your feet — skate more, reach less
Never: Retaliate after the whistle — retaliating converts one penalty into two
Rule: Under USA Hockey Rule 615(a) only, a player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be penalised for fighting — neither current IIHF edition writes that protection, so do not assume it under the British book
Convention: That the retaliator is the one who gets caught is dressing-room lore — the written standard aims the extra penalty at the instigator
```

Here is the standard coaching account of amateur penalties: **most of them are not dirty, they are late, frustrated or lazy.** ⚠️ **Take that as a lens for your own game rather than a fact about the population.** No published study codes *why* a penalty was taken, so the frustration half of it can be neither confirmed nor refuted from evidence; what *is* measured is only that amateur penalties are mostly aggression-type infractions rather than obstruction fouls. [Game Management, "Frustration penalties"](../systems/game_management.md#frustration-penalties) carries the full working, and this claim should never be restated without the qualification attached.

- **Late** — you were beaten, so you reached. Hooking, holding, tripping, and interference are overwhelmingly penalties of a player who has lost position. NHL Rule 56.1 names the mechanism precisely: "Illegal means are acts which allow a player or goalkeeper to establish, maintain or restore body position other than by skating." If you lost position, the only legal fix is your feet.
- **Frustrated** — the retaliation after the whistle, the extra cross-check in the scrum, the shove back. USA Hockey instructs officials that "any avoidable contact after the whistle shall be penalized strictly, including scrum situations around the goal or along the boards," with an **additional** penalty for whoever instigates. Note which way that cuts: the written standard targets the *instigator* for the extra penalty, and USA Hockey Rule 615(a) says in terms that *"[a] player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be assessed a penalty for fighting under this rule."* The folk wisdom that the retaliator is the one who gets caught is **not** what the rulebook says, and there is no published call data either way — so treat it as dressing-room lore, not a rule. ⚠️ **That protection is USA Hockey's alone, and a British reader does not have it.** Neither current IIHF edition writes anything like 615(a); the 2025/26 book says the opposite in terms, at Rule 46.6 — *"A Player who retaliates to being punched will be assessed at least a minor penalty (“Roughing”) or a major penalty (“Fighting”)"* — and the 2026/27 book writes no such protection either. What is certain under every book is that retaliating converts one penalty into two.
- **Lazy** — the stick that stays on an opponent's hip because you cannot be bothered to skate.

The fix is unglamorous and entirely within your control: **skate more, reach less.** The coaching claim that goes with it — that better conditioning reduces your penalty minutes, because a tired player reaches — is **reasoning, not a measured effect**, and the one study that turned up when this guide searched for the link did not support it. Haugen and colleagues tested roughly 40 to 60 defenders and 70 to 100 forwards from Norway's top men's league every pre-season from 2008 to 2017, and set those results against each player's season mean penalty minutes per game. Every endurance measure came back at essentially zero — the 3000-metre run at −0.01, chin-ups at 0.00, hanging sit-ups at 0.07, box jumps at −0.09 — and the largest correlation with any fitness test was a small **positive** one, with bench-press strength, at **0.23**, which points the opposite way from the coaching claim. ⚠️ **Read what that does and does not settle.** It compares *different players* across whole seasons. It does not test whether *you* reach more in the last twenty seconds of a long shift, which is what the coaching claim is actually about, and no study measuring it turned up in a Europe PMC search pairing hockey penalty minutes with fitness or with fatigue. So skate more and reach less remains the right instruction — it is simply not one that anything found here has measured. See [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md) and [Mental Game](../off-the-ice/mental_game.md).

### What actually gets called

```facts
Rule: Stick-to-stick is exempt from hooking in the NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL only, and only while checking (NHL and IIHF 55.1; PWHL 56.1) — USA Hockey 623, Hockey Canada 8.2 and CARHA's Glossary make the opponent's stick hookable (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: The shaft above your upper hand used to hold or hook is a minor under NHL and IIHF 55.2, PWHL 56.2 and Hockey Canada 8.2 — and a double minor at the referee's discretion under CARHA 48(f), which files it under Attempt to Injure rather than hooking (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: USA Hockey's hooking rule reaches only your own stick's blade (623's Note), so the shaft above your upper hand is butt-ending under Rule 606 instead: a major plus a game misconduct, which is what the book prints and not a forecast of the call
Rule: Slashing is a forceful chop on the body, on their stick, or near their hands that is not an attempt to play the puck (NHL 61.1, IIHF 61.1 word for word) — USA Hockey 634 drops that let-out, so there the force of the chop decides; Hockey Canada 9.3 exempts only a tap on the stick to gain possession
Rule: Cross-checking has no blade-on-ice element outside USA Hockey Rule 609 — it is absent from NHL and IIHF Rule 59.1
Rule: Charging thresholds differ — IIHF "multiple strides" (Rule 42.1), USA Hockey more than two, Hockey Canada two or more, the NHL no stated number
Rule: Interference runs above a minor in three books — NHL 56.4 a major on violence; IIHF 56.5 a major plus a game misconduct on reckless endangerment; Hockey Canada 8.3(b) that pair on the degree of violence with nobody hurt, 8.3(c) a match. USA Hockey's ladder is outside Rule 625, at 602(a)
Rule: Kneeing and clipping carry no division clause in any of the four books and neither is capped at a minor — Hockey Canada 7.8(a) is a double Minor and 7.8(d) bars the misconduct rung, USA Hockey 628(a) is a minor plus a misconduct, and both 628 and 639 are on Rule 411's suspension list
```

| Infraction | The test the official applies |
|---|---|
| **Hooking** | Using the stick to restrain. Stick-to-stick is exempt in the NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL only, and only while checking (55.1; PWHL 56.1). |
| **Slashing** | A forceful chop on the body, on their stick, or on or near their hands. The "not an attempt to play the puck" let-out is NHL and IIHF 61.1 **only** — USA Hockey 634 writes none. |
| **Holding** | Using the free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab. You may fend off (NHL and IIHF 56.1); the arm strength move is NHL and IIHF **54.2** and Hockey Canada 8.1, the same but for pronouns. |
| **Interference** | Impeding an opponent who does **not** have the puck; checking a player who has already released the puck; a "pick" on a player unaware of the hit. |
| **Cross-checking** | The shaft between two hands used forcefully to check an opponent (NHL 59.1). The blade-on-ice element is USA Hockey 609's alone — NHL and IIHF 59.1 lack it. |
| **Roughing** | A punching or slamming motion, normally at the head or face. USA Hockey 640 reaches wider — a careless hand, arm or stick in a check, and late checks. Hands are no legal check surface. |
| **High-sticking** | Stick contact above an opponent's shoulders. ⚠️ **The waist proviso (NHL 60.1, IIHF 2026/27) is no defence over a crouching opponent — 60.2 writes none.** |
| **Tripping** | Using a stick, knee, foot, arm, hand or elbow to take an opponent's feet away. ⚠️ **No fall required (USA Hockey 639 Note 1, Hockey Canada 8.6(a)); NHL and IIHF unresolved.** |
| **Slew-footing** | A leg or foot taking an opponent's feet from under them (NHL and IIHF 52.1; PWHL 53.1). ⚠️ **No book prices this at two minutes; three of the four eject you, and so does the PWHL.** |
| **Charging** | Distance travelled, speed built up, or jumping in. ⚠️ **No stride count in the NHL; IIHF multiple (42.1), USA Hockey over two, Hockey Canada two or more.** |
| **Clipping** | Lowering your own body to check at or below an opponent's knees (NHL and IIHF 44.1). ⚠️ **Hockey Canada 8.7 draws it at the hips; a major is mandatory on injury under NHL 44.3.** |
| **Kneeing** | The knee as the point of contact. ⚠️ **Hockey Canada 7.8 reaches a knee hit "on an otherwise legal hit", at a double minor; USA Hockey 628(a) writes no bare minor either.** |

**Two more of them catch players who were not trying to foul anybody, and both are worth spelling out. Interference** is impeding an opponent who does **not** have the puck, checking a player who has already released it, or setting a *pick* on a player who is unaware of the hit. **A minor is the floor rather than the ceiling in three books** — NHL Rule 56.4 allows a major on the degree of violence, and Hockey Canada 8.3(b) reaches the same tier on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* with nobody hurt and makes it mandatory where it injures, with 8.3(c) a match penalty for an attempt to injure or a deliberate one; under the IIHF book **56.5 reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt for a *late hit*, because 56.1 defines one as reckless endangerment**, while for a *pick* it needs reckless endangerment shown. ⚠️ **USA Hockey is the exception and the exception matters:** Rule 625 caps interference at a minor and is not on Rule 411's list — but 602(a)'s match penalty for a player who *"recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player"* is tied to no named foul at all and sits outside 625, and a **late hit** there is not interference at all: it is 640(b) — the sub-section written *"except Adult Male Classifications"* — which 640(g) and 640(h) take to a major plus a game misconduct and a match penalty, both reaching only acts under **640(b–f)**, so their reach in adult men's hockey is unstated. **Roughing** is a punching or slamming motion, normally at the head or face. ⚠️ **USA Hockey's Rule 640 reaches wider than that, and the extra limb has a threshold in it worth hearing.** Note 2 says a roughing penalty *"should also be assessed to a player who uses their hands, arms or stick in a careless or reckless manner while delivering a check, or delivers an avoidable body check to an opponent after the whistle or who is no longer in control of the puck."* **So it is not the hands or arms in a check that make it *roughing* — it is carelessness or recklessness with them**, and the late-hit limb needs the check to have been avoidable. ⚠️ **That is a question about which foul gets called, and it is not permission to check with your hands or forearms.** They are not a legal contact surface to begin with. USA Hockey says so twice over, in its **Glossary** and in its **Declaration of Player Safety, Fair Play & Respect**, in the same words each time: *"The use of the hands, forearm, stick or elbow in delivering a body check is unacceptable and not within the guidelines of a legal body check."* The **IIHF glossary** — the British reader's — allows a legal check *"only with the trunk of the body (hips and shoulders)"*. **That trunk-only envelope is stated in terms by two books of the four**, because the NHL writes no positive definition of a legal check and Hockey Canada prohibits rather than defines, as [§5 sets out](#what-a-legal-check-is-precisely). **And roughing is the low end of what a hand or forearm in a check can cost you anywhere:** elbowing, an illegal body check where checking is barred, and head contact are separate offences, and their ladders all run above it. ⚠️ **Their floors do not, so do not read the ladder off the bottom rung** — USA Hockey 611(a) elbowing and 604(c) an illegal body check are each a bare minor, against roughing's minor or double minor at 640(a); only head contact starts higher, at 620(a)'s *"minor plus a misconduct or major plus a game misconduct"*. And none of them makes Note 2's careless-or-reckless wording the trigger for its own base penalty, though **611(b)** and **620(b)** both reach the major-plus-game-misconduct tier on recklessness, 620(b) on carelessness too. **The sub-sections are worth reading whole, because that first limb of Note 2 is in none of them.** **Rule 640(a)** carries the roughing minor or double minor but names *Note 1*; **640(b)**, the avoidable check on a player *"no longer in control of the puck"*, is written *"except Adult Male Classifications"*; **640(c)**, avoidable physical contact after the whistle, carries no such exception; and **640(d)** reaches a check delivered with no effort to gain the puck where the blade of the stick is above the knees. ⚠️ **So the adult-male carve-out in (b) is not a free hit and does not switch the rest of Rule 640 off** — Note 2 directs a roughing penalty with no classification exception of its own, though putting that limb on 640(a)'s tier is a reading rather than the book's words, since 640(a) names Note 1.

**And in this group too, a minor is usually the floor rather than the ceiling** — [the cross-check ladder](#the-cross-check-problem) is the worked example, and the rest of this list runs the same way. A **hooking** major needs an **injury** under the NHL (55.3) and Hockey Canada (8.2(b)), but reaches a major plus a game misconduct for reckless endangerment with nobody hurt under the IIHF (55.3) and USA Hockey (623(b)). ⚠️ **And the stick-on-stick exemption is a three-book rule, with three books running the other way.** It is the NHL's and the IIHF's 55.1 and the PWHL's 56.1, and it holds only while you are checking the puck carrier — **USA Hockey 623, Hockey Canada 8.2 and CARHA's Glossary make the opponent's stick hookable**, so under those three books a stick laid on theirs is the offence itself. [§3](#3-stick-checks--legal-effective-and-under-used) works that through. ⚠️ **A second hooking clause runs the other way round, and the book it leaves out is the one you would go and check.** **NHL 55.2**, **IIHF 55.2** and **PWHL 56.2** all assess a minor to a player who *"uses the shaft of the stick above the upper hand to hold or hook an opponent"*, and **Hockey Canada 8.2** writes the same act into its own hooking rule, naming it *"[b]utt-end hooking"* — the shaft above the upper hand used *"to restrain an opponent"* — and directing that it be penalised *"as 'hooking', rather than under Rule 9.1 — Butt-Ending, which requires a jabbing motion with the shaft of the stick"*, which puts it at a minor under 8.2(a). ⚠️ **USA Hockey is the exception, and there it is not hooking at all.** Rule 623's Note defines hooking as impeding an opponent *"with a pulling or tugging motion by applying the blade of the stick"* — the blade, not the shaft — so the act falls outside that rule by the rule's own words. The phrase *"above the upper hand"* occurs twice in USA Hockey's playing rules and twice in the Casebook volume, and every one of the four is **Rule 606**, butt-ending, or a sled-hockey restatement of it: it is in no part of Rule 623, in none of that rule's Casebook situations, and in no Appendix I entry. **Rule 606's Note reaches a player who *"uses the shaft of the stick, above the upper hand, to check an opposing player in any manner"*, a limb that needs no jab**, and **606(a) is a major plus a game misconduct**, with 606(b) adding that a match penalty for reckless endangerment *"may also be assessed for butt-ending"* on top. A game misconduct also costs the team's next game (**404(b)**), and **Rule 606 is on Rule 411's progressive-suspension list where Rule 623 is not**. ⚠️ **That is what these books print, and not a forecast of what the official in front of you will call** — but it means the same act is priced at two minutes under the NHL, the IIHF, the PWHL and Hockey Canada, and at an ejection under USA Hockey. ⚠️ **And one more book prices it a third way, and files it where you would not look for it.** **CARHA Rule 48(f)** — **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only** — sits inside *Attempt to Injure or Deliberate Injury* rather than in that book's hooking rule, and provides that *"[a]t the discretion of the referee, a Double Minor penalty shall be assessed to any player or team official who uses the shaft of the stick above the upper hand, to hold or hook an opponent"* — the same act in near-identical words, at a double minor, and at the referee's discretion rather than automatically. **That book prints no duration for a double minor anywhere**, so what is verified here is the label and not a count of minutes. **So the shaft above your upper hand is a restraining tool in none of them; only the price of using it as one changes.** **NHL 61.1 writes what is *not* called too:** *non-aggressive* contact to the pants or the front of the shin pads. A **slashing** major, and with it a game misconduct, is reachable with nobody injured in every book read here — NHL 61.3 with 61.5, IIHF 61.3, PWHL 63.3 with 63.5, USA Hockey 634(b) and Hockey Canada 9.3(b), and under **CARHA 80(a)** a slash is *"[a] Minor or Major penalty, at the discretion of the Referee"* while **Rule 30(a)** rules a player assessed any major *"off the ice for the remainder of the game (Major penalty plus Game Misconduct)"*, its only exception an accidental high stick (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). ⚠️ **And Hockey Canada's slashing rule is narrower than any of the others, and forecloses the "I was playing the puck" defence in terms.** Rule 9.3 defines slashing as *"the action of hitting an opponent with a stick while holding the stick with one or both hands"*, and the only exemption it writes is for the stick: *"Tapping the stick of the puck-carrier is not considered slashing if it is for the sole purpose of gaining possession of the puck."* A chop on the body or on the hands has no let-out there at all, and 9.3 also penalises a player *"who, on the pretext of playing the puck, makes a wild swing at the puck with the object of intimidating their opponent."*

#### Holding, and the arm you are allowed

```facts
Rule: Holding is the free hand used to hold, pull, tug or grab — you may fend off, not hold (NHL and IIHF 56.1); Hockey Canada 8.1 permits a hand to block an opponent's stick, never to hold it
Rule: USA Hockey writes the fend-off permission in its Casebook only — control the puck (Standard of Play Situation 6), or be reaching for it while physically engaged in a corner or in front of the goal (Situation 7); keep a position legally established, and never push off to create space
Rule: Of the NHL, IIHF, USA Hockey and Hockey Canada, only Hockey Canada's 8.1(b) takes holding itself above a minor — a major plus a game misconduct, mandatory where a holding infraction injures — and 8.1(c) bars a match penalty; the NHL and IIHF stop inside Rule 54, USA Hockey inside 622(a)
Rule: CARHA 63(a) leaves a major to the referee for a hold of any kind with nobody hurt, and 63(b) makes the major mandatory on injury — and a CARHA major for holding ends your night under Rule 30(a), plus a one-game suspension under Rule 32(d) (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: NHL 21.1 and USA Hockey 602(a) need no named foul — 21.1 reaches a match penalty for deliberately injuring an opponent, or attempting to, "in any manner", and 602(a) reaches one for recklessly endangering or attempting to injure any opponent; the IIHF writes no match penalty at all, only Rule 28
Never: Touch an opponent's facemask — USA Hockey 622(b) makes it a major plus a game misconduct and 622(c) a match penalty; the NHL and IIHF put it at 75.2 and send the referee on to Rule 21
```

**Only one of the four books takes holding itself above a minor, and one version of it is not a holding penalty at all.** The rule itself is **Rule 54** (NHL and IIHF), **622** (USA Hockey) — which names using a free hand to clutch, grab or hold the stick as holding outright — and **8.1** (Hockey Canada). ⚠️ **The books write more than one permission for the free arm, and they do not all write all of them, so know which one you are leaning on.** The NHL's and the IIHF's 56.1 let a free hand *"fend off"* an opponent or their stick, and add in the same sentence that it *"may not be used to hold an opponent's stick or body."* **USA Hockey writes that one in its Casebook rather than its playing rules, and it writes it in two situations rather than one.** Standard of Play **Situation 6** allows a player *"in control of the puck"* a free hand to *"maintain body position that they legally established"*, and ends *"if they grab the stick, sweater or arm of the opponent, or use the free arm to push off and create space, a penalty should be assessed."* **Situation 7 is the limb that gets missed, and it does not require the puck at all:** where two players are *"physically engaged in the corner or in front of the goal"* it is legal *"for a player to take one hand off the stick when reaching for the puck and using the free arm to steer the opponent or maintain position"*, with the same stopping point — the line is crossed *"when the free arm is used to impede the opponent, pushes off with enough force to knock the opponent off of the puck or create space."* **So do not read USA Hockey's permission as control-of-the-puck only**, and do not read Situation 7 as reaching open ice: it is written for the corner and the goal front. Its Casebook uses the phrase *"fend off"* itself, so the words are not the NHL's and the IIHF's alone; what is narrower there is the permission behind them. ⚠️ **The arm in a strength move is a further permission again, and it is the one three of the four books share word for word.** **NHL 54.2**, **IIHF 54.2** and **Hockey Canada 8.1** each read: *"A player is permitted to use their arm in a strength move, by blocking their opponent, provided they have body position and are not using their hands in a holding manner, when doing so."* That is Hockey Canada's wording; the NHL's is the same sentence with masculine pronouns, and the IIHF's puts *"body position"* in quotation marks. **So if your book is the NHL's or the IIHF's — which is every British reader's — that permission is yours, and there is no need to go looking for a substitute.** **USA Hockey is the one book that writes no strength-move sentence at all**, in its playing rules or its Casebook. Hockey Canada 8.1 adds that players *"are permitted to block an opponent's stick with their hands, arms, or body"* — but not to hold that stick, which is its own announced call. And **Hockey Canada 8.1(b)** makes a major plus a game misconduct **mandatory** where holding injures an opponent — **the only major any of the four books writes for holding itself**, because NHL and IIHF Rule 54 stop at 54.2's minor and USA Hockey's Rule 622 puts its minor at 622(a) and reserves 622(b) and 622(c) for the facemask;
