Hockey Canada's own **8.1(c)** then bars a match penalty for holding, so that major plus game misconduct is the ceiling as well as the exception. ⚠️ **And a fifth book on disk reaches the major without waiting for an injury.** **CARHA Rule 63(a)** — **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only** — reads *"A Minor penalty or, at the discretion of the Referee, a Major penalty shall be assessed any player who holds an opponent with the hands, stick or in any other manner"*, so there the major is a discretion the referee already holds on an ordinary hold; **63(b)** then makes a major **mandatory** for *"any player who injures an opponent by 'holding'"*. ⚠️ **And a major there ends your night and costs you the next game**: **Rule 30(a)** rules any player assessed a major *"off the ice for the remainder of the game (Major penalty plus Game Misconduct)"*, and **Rule 32(d)** adds an automatic one-game suspension on top. **CARHA is a fifth book and not one of the four**, and it is the one many adult readers are actually under. ⚠️ **But in two of these four books a rule's own ceiling is not the game's.** **NHL Rule 21.1** imposes a match penalty on *"any player who deliberately attempts to injure or who deliberately injures an opponent in any manner"*, and **USA Hockey's Rule 602(a)** assesses one to *"any player or team official who recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player or team official"*. **Neither is tied to a named foul**, so a hold that deliberately injures is reachable at a match penalty under both, whatever Rule 54 and Rule 622 stop at. **The other two books do not open that route.** Hockey Canada's **8.1(c)** says in terms that *"No Match penalty may be assessed for a holding infraction"*, and its general **7.1(c)** reaches a deliberate or attempted injury only *"in any manner not otherwise covered within the playing rules"* — though that clause's own examples include acts the book penalises elsewhere, so it is 8.1(c)'s flat bar that settles holding. 🇬🇧 **And the British reader's book writes no match penalty at all:** the IIHF's Rule 21 is *Dangerous Actions*, a major plus an automatic game misconduct, and it reaches only *"an action that is not covered by the playing rules"*. ⚠️ **A hand on an opponent's cage is a different offence — inside the same rule under USA Hockey, outside it in the other three, and unpriced in none of them** — USA Hockey 622(b) is a major plus a game misconduct and 622(c) a match penalty that *"may also be assessed for grabbing or holding the facemask"*; Hockey Canada files it under Rule 7.1 Attempt to Injure; and the NHL and the IIHF put it at **75.2**, where *"grabbing hold of a face mask"* is named as unsportsmanlike conduct and carries a minor, with the referee sent on to **Rule 21** — *"[i]f warranted, and specifically when injury results"* in the NHL's words, and in the IIHF's where the offence is *"serious and severe, based on the degree of force"*. **Never touch a facemask.**

#### Tripping, and how narrow the relief is

```facts
Rule: Tripping requires no fall under USA Hockey Rule 639 Note 1 ("lose balance or fall") or Hockey Canada 8.6(a); the NHL and IIHF books are unresolved — 57.1 in each is "trip or fall" and 57.2, the clause imposing the minor, "trip and fall" — play it as though the stumble is enough
Rule: A stick through an opponent's skates is tripping exactly as a leg is — 57.1's list names the stick first in both books, the NHL's "stick, knee, foot, arm, hand or elbow" and the IIHF's "stick, or any part of their body"
Rule: Playing the puck first saves the penalty shot, not the minor (NHL 57.3, IIHF 24.8) — USA Hockey's Note to 639(a) relieves only a player "clearly hook-checking or poke-checking the puck", and no book's relief reaches slew-footing
```

**Tripping is the most-called of the seven infractions counted in [§9](#the-cross-check-problem)**, at 16.6% of all calls in that 400-game sample, ahead of hooking's 12.1% — **and the relief you think you have is narrower than it sounds.** ⚠️ **Read that as a ranking of those seven taken one at a time, and not of the book or of any group inside it:** they account for 71.4% of the calls between them and the rest were not broken out, so nothing here establishes tripping as the most-called penalty overall — nor stick fouls as the most-called *kind* of penalty, because the biggest of the seven is tripping and no book files tripping among its stick fouls. ⚠️ **That is about where the books file the offence, not about how you commit it** — 57.1’s own list, quoted below, names the stick first, so a stick through an opponent’s skates is tripping exactly as a leg is. USA Hockey's Casebook is explicit that the fouled player need not go down: Rule 639, Situation 1 asks whether they must fall to the ice and answers *"No"*. **Hockey Canada does not require a fall either** — 8.6(a) assesses a minor to *"any player who trips an opposing player"*, and Rule 8.6 defines tripping as a stick or body placed so as to cause an opponent *"to trip or fall"*, which is a disjunction and not a condition. ⚠️ **The NHL's book and the IIHF's leave the question unresolved, because their own two clauses do not agree** — 57.1 in each bars placing something so as to cause an opponent *"to trip **or** fall"*, the NHL naming *"the stick, knee, foot, arm, hand or elbow"* and the IIHF *"the stick, or any part of their body"*, while 57.2, the clause that actually imposes the minor, reads *"trip **and** fall"* in both. Neither book says which governs, and both current IIHF editions word it the same way. **Play it as though the stumble is enough.** ⚠️ **Playing the puck first does not save you the minor in the NHL or the IIHF either: it saves the penalty shot only** (NHL 57.3, IIHF 24.8). USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write relief on different terms, and **neither reaches slew-footing** — USA Hockey's, the Note to 639(a), relieves only a player *"clearly hook-checking or poke-checking the puck"*, which is a **stick** check and not a leg through the skates; and Hockey Canada's 8.6 says in terms that it does not reach clipping or slew-footing, quoted in full under [Slew-footing](#slew-footing--the-foul-that-grows-out-of-a-legal-battle-position).

#### The stick above the shoulders

```facts
Rule: The windup and follow-through carve-out is the NHL's, the IIHF's and the PWHL's only (60.1; PWHL 61.1 — all three exclude a wild swing at a bouncing puck) — USA Hockey writes none in its playing rules or its Casebook
Rule: Hockey Canada's follow-through rule is junior and senior only — Interpretation 1 to 9.5(a) is written for those divisions alone. In minor and female hockey the same contact is Rule 7.6 Head Contact, a minor at 7.6(a) for accidental contact, with no injury and no stick height in the test
Rule: The waist is the struck player's, not yours, and no book's waist floor licenses a stick on a crouching opponent — "above the height of the opponent's waist" is NHL 60.3's wording, IIHF 60.3 has it in the 2026/27 book only, and 60.2 bars contact above the shoulders with no proviso at all
Rule: USA Hockey's playing rule and its own Casebook disagree on what a high stick is — 621's Note requires contact above the opponent's shoulders; Casebook 621 Situation 2 says contact is neither required nor need be above shoulder height, and penalises a stick raised carelessly or to intimidate
Rule: A high stick is not capped at a double minor — IIHF 60.4 reaches a major plus an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment and USA Hockey 621(b) is a major plus a game misconduct, 621(c) is a match penalty for the same, and NHL 60.4 is a match penalty for a deliberate or attempted injury
Rule: Hockey Canada 9.5 is "High-Sticking – Junior/Senior", and it bars its own major and match — 9.5(b) and 9.5(c) send them to 7.7(b) and 7.7(c); minor and female hockey goes to 7.6 Head Contact. Neither 7.6(b) nor 7.7(b) needs an injury; 7.6(c) and 7.7(c) are match penalties
Technique: Take the stick lift under the shaft and low against an opponent bent low over the puck — the shaft is nearer their face than it looks, and a lift that misses rides up into it
Priority: Where USA Hockey's playing rule and its Casebook disagree, take the wider reading — it is the only one safe under both, and a stick landing below the shoulders can still be high-sticking
```

**The waist floor runs through 60.1 and 60.3, and which book has it depends on which IIHF edition you are under.** The NHL book has it at both, and the *contact* test is recent: the NHL's own **2024-2025** book still read *"A 'high stick' is one which is carried above the height of the opponent's shoulders"*, so **⚠️ what 60.1 asks now is what the stick touched, not how it was carried**. So does the **IIHF Official Rulebook 2026/27**: its 60.1 reads *"A 'high stick' is one which contacts an opponent above the shoulders, provided their shoulders are at waist level or higher"* — the NHL's sentence word for word — and its 60.3 carries the parenthesis *"(above the height of the opponent's waist)"*. The **IIHF Official Rule Book 2025/26 (Version 1.1)** has it at neither: there 60.1 tests a stick *carried* above the opponent's shoulders, 60.3 a stick *carried or held* above the shoulders, and no waist wording appears anywhere in Rule 60. 🇬🇧 **Which of those is yours in Britain is worth asking**, because the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 name *"the latest IIHF Official Rule Book (Version 1.1, published July 2025)"* as their foundation — and Version 1.1 of July 2025 is the 2025/26 book, not the 2026/27 one. **USA Hockey 621 and Hockey Canada 9.5 write no waist floor in either case.**

**And the waist that counts is the waist of the player you hit, not yours.** The NHL's **60.3** and the IIHF's 2026/27 **60.3** say so in terms — *"above the height of the opponent's waist"* — and the **IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27** puts the case at **Situation 60.13**: a player bent over digging the puck out of the corner, and an opponent's stick-lift attempt that misses and catches them in the face while the striker's stick is *"only slightly above the height of their waist."* The answer is *"Yes, this is a minor (or double-minor) penalty for high-sticking. The key here is that the opposing player was hit in the neck/face/head with the opponent's stick, at or above the height of the waist of the player being struck."* That Situation is in the **2026/27** Handbook and not in the 2025/26 one, and the Handbook ranks itself below the book — its Foreword reads *"in case of any contradictions, the valid IIHF Rulebook is always the basis."* Here it does not need to override anything: under the 2025/26 IIHF book a bent-over opponent is caught by Rule 60 more simply still, because Rule 60 there sets no floor at all — though that edition carries 60.1's carve-out for the centre bent over at a face-off in the same words as the 2026/27 one, which is set out below. **So a stick below your own waist is a defence in no book.** The practical consequence is for the stick lift in [§3](#the-stick-lift): against an opponent bent low over the puck, the shaft you are lifting under is nearer their face than it looks, and a lift that misses rides up into it. Take it under the shaft and low, and accept that you will not get every one of them.

**60.2 is word-for-word identical in the NHL book and in both IIHF editions** — *"Any contact made by a stick on an opponent above the shoulders is prohibited and a minor penalty shall be imposed"* — and it is the clause that actually imposes the minor, which is why the floor above it is no basis for planning a stick on a crouching opponent. Under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada, and under the 2025/26 IIHF book, no waist floor stands between a crouching opponent and the high-sticking rule at all. ⚠️ **That is not the same as absolute protection, and the exception is printed inside the definition itself.** IIHF 60.1 permits *"accidental contact"* committed as a normal *"windup or follow through"* of a *"shooting motion"*, and permits *"accidental contact on the opposing center who is bent over during the course of a 'face-off'"* — a crouching opponent, named and excused. That sentence is identical in both IIHF editions and word for word the NHL's 60.1, and it is confined to the centre at a faceoff and to accidents; it is not a general licence over a bent-over player. **The other two books do not write it, and one of them runs the other way:** USA Hockey's playing rule at 621 carries no equivalent sentence, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.5(a) assesses *"a Minor penalty for High-sticking"* where a junior or senior player high-sticks the puck and *"then, on the follow through, hits an opposing player above the shoulder and no injury has resulted"*, adding that it *"would also apply on a follow through of shooting the puck."* ⚠️ **But the absence of a waist floor is not full protection under those two books either.** Neither writes a bare *"above the shoulders"* test: USA Hockey's 621 Note measures against *"the normal height of the opponent's shoulders"*, and Hockey Canada's 9.5 and 9.5(a) against *"the normal height of the shoulder[s]"*. ⚠️ **Whether "normal" means the shoulders where they actually are or those of a player standing upright is a question neither book answers for these rules, so do not read it either way.** Hockey Canada defines the phrase exactly once in the whole book — Rule 6.9(a) Note 1, *"The 'normal' height of the shoulders refers to when a player is standing fully upright"* — but 6.9 is high-sticking **the puck**, and the book nowhere says that definition travels to 9.5 — its own 6.9(c) sends a puck high-stick, or an attempted one, that contacts an opponent to **Rule 7.6 Head Contact** instead, and only in minor and female divisions; USA Hockey defines *"normal"* nowhere at all, in its playing rules or in its Casebook. ⚠️ **And USA Hockey's playing rule and its own Casebook do not describe the same offence.** 621's Note is two-limbed — high sticking is where a player *"carries the stick above the normal height of the opponent's shoulders **and** makes contact with the opponent"* — but the Casebook asks *"Must contact with an opponent occur in order for the Referee to assess a penalty for high sticking?"*, answers *"No."*, and adds that *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"* and that *"[w]henever a player raises their stick above their shoulders in an attempt to intimidate an opponent or is careless in their actions, high sticking must be called"* (Rule 621, Situation 2). **Both of the Note's limbs fall away there — the contact, and the height at which it lands.** The two texts sit in one printed volume, the *Official Rules and Casebook of Ice Hockey 2025-29*, which carries the playing rule word for word and then reads it wider in its situations; **this document records that rather than picking a side.** ⚠️ **Play the wider reading, because it is the only one that is safe under both:** a stick raised above your shoulders can be a minor with no contact at all, and a stick that catches an opponent *below* their shoulders can still be high-sticking. Carelessness alone satisfies the Casebook's trigger, so no intent to intimidate is needed — but one of those two states does have to be there, so this is not a bar on carrying a stick high in open ice. **A routing point sits in the same Casebook, and it raises the price rather than lowering it:** where the stick reaches the head *"during the process of delivering a check"*, the call is not high-sticking at all but Rule 620 Head Contact, whose floor is a minor **plus a misconduct** (Rule 621, Situation 1). ⚠️ **What reaches a crouching opponent in those two books is head-contact law, and it does not turn on stick height at all.** USA Hockey Rule 620 makes head contact *"the action of a player contacting an opponent in the head, face or neck with any part of the player's body, equipment or stick"*, puts the onus *"on the player delivering the check"*, and starts at a minor **plus a misconduct** (620(a)). Hockey Canada Rule 7.6 opens *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck"* and requires that *"[r]egardless of the circumstances, players must be penalized for contact with an opponent's head, face, or neck at all times."* **And do not read Hockey Canada 9.5 as its general high-sticking rule** — it is titled "High-Sticking – Junior/Senior". In Hockey Canada minor and female hockey, contact above the shoulders falls to Rule 7.6 Head Contact instead — and that rule is **easier to trigger, not harder**. **Rule 7.6(a)** assesses a minor to any player who *"accidentally contacts an opponent in the head, face, or neck, in minor and female hockey"*, and a double minor where a player *"uses any part of their body or equipment to intentionally contact an opponent in the head, face, or neck"* in those same divisions: no injury has to result, and nothing in either limb turns on how high the stick was carried. So the reader who is told that Hockey Canada's high-sticking rule stops at junior should not hear that as protection — below junior the same contact is caught by a rule with a lower threshold and, through 7.6(b) and 7.6(c), a higher ceiling. **And a high stick is not capped at a double minor:** IIHF 60.4 reaches a major plus an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment and USA Hockey 621(b) a major plus a game misconduct, USA Hockey 621(c) and NHL 60.4 reach a match penalty, and Hockey Canada reaches both tiers by a different route. ⚠️ **And a fifth book on disk writes no double minor at all, so the rung a double minor would occupy there is a major.** **CARHA Rule 62(b)** — **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only** — makes contact with an opponent above the normal height of their shoulders a **major** from the first instance, accidental or intentional, *"whether or not injury results"*, with no route back down. ⚠️ **And Rule 62 has a rung below that one which is not about contact at all:** **62(a)** is a minor for a player who *"checks an opponent or intimidates an opponent while carrying their stick above the waist height of their opponent"* — their waist, not yours, and nothing has to be touched. **CARHA Rule 50** covers head contact *"with any part of the player's body or equipment other than a stick"*, and its opening Note sends stick contact to Rule 62. **62(b)'s own Note** adds a game misconduct where the contact was intentional, or accidental and injuring, and 62(c) is a match penalty. ⚠️ **Back under Hockey Canada now — CARHA is an adult book and has no junior or senior divisions at all.** In Hockey Canada junior and senior, 9.5(b) and 9.5(c) bar the major and match labels under high-sticking outright — *"No Major penalty may be assessed for high-sticking"* — and send them to 7.7(b) and 7.7(c). In Hockey Canada minor and female hockey there is no high-sticking rule at all: 7.6 Head Contact *"supersedes any other rules"* bar fighting, so the same contact is 7.6(b), a major plus a game misconduct, or 7.6(c), a match penalty. ⚠️ **And neither of those majors waits for an injury.** **7.6(b)** opens *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact, the Referee may assess a Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty for Head Contact"* and only then makes the same pair compulsory where *"a player is injured, as the result of a Head Contact infraction that would otherwise call for a Minor penalty"*; **7.7(b)** assesses it *"for any Head Contact infraction with a high degree of violence"*, and adds a third route the injury reading hides — a player fouled or hit illegally who, as a result, hits their head on the glass, ice or boards. **7.7(b) reaches the boards case only where a head injury results; its high-degree-of-violence limb does not.** Both (c) clauses are match penalties for hitting a player in the head *"in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*, or for a deliberate or attempted injury by head contact.

### Slew-footing — the foul that grows out of a legal battle position

```facts
Rule: Slew-footing is a leg or foot used to take an opponent's feet from under them, classically while an arm or elbow pushes their upper body backward at the same time — NHL and IIHF 52.1, USA Hockey 639 Note 4, Hockey Canada 8.8
Rule: The leg alone is slew-footing in every book read here, USA Hockey included — 639 Note 4 opens "Slew Footing is the act of a player using their leg or foot to knock or kick an opponent's feet from under them", and PWHL 53.1 writes the same first form, with 53.2 a mandatory match penalty
Never: Put a leg behind an opponent in a battle. Keep your leg in front of you instead, and win the puck with your hips and your skates
Rule: NHL Rule 52.2 is a mandatory match penalty with no minor or major tier beneath it — "Any player who is guilty of slew-footing shall be assessed a match penalty"
Rule: IIHF Rule 52.2 — the British book — reads "The Referee shall, at their discretion, assess a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty"; Rule 52 writes no lesser tier, and no match penalty anywhere in the book — 52.3 allows Supplementary Discipline instead
Rule: USA Hockey 639(a) excludes slew footing from the minor and 639(b) sets the floor at a major plus a game misconduct; its Casebook at Rule 639, Situation 5 says "[a] minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing"
Rule: USA Hockey's floor is not its ceiling either — 639(c) allows a match penalty for reckless endangerment "under this rule", and the same Casebook Situation 5 closes that "a match penalty must be considered" any time one of these actions is deemed to recklessly endanger the opponent
Rule: Hockey Canada 8.8(a) is a double minor and 8.8(b) bars a major — but that is a floor, not a ceiling: 8.8(c) reaches a match penalty on the degree of violence, and mandatorily where the foul injures
Rule: Hockey Canada defines it widest, reaching a leg that knocks, drags or sweeps the feet away (8.8), and its Interpretation 1 to 8.8(a) urges "serious consideration for a Match penalty" where the offender is in motion
Rule: "I played the puck first" is a defence to slew-footing in none of the six books read here — every puck-first relief there sits inside a tripping rule: USA Hockey 639(a) is written "except slew footing", Hockey Canada 8.6 excludes 8.8 in terms, and NHL and IIHF Rule 52 and PWHL Rule 53 write none
Rule: CARHA is a fifth book and the only one whose slew-footing clause sits inside its tripping rule — 86(b)'s Note ignores the trip where a player already falling or sliding knocks the puck clear before body contact, which is not the act 86(b) describes (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Never: Move a player who has gone down backward and is not getting up, and never take their helmet off. Somebody calls for immediate medical help instead — that is the spinal-injury section above
```

**This is the one that starts from something legal.** [§4](#4-body-position-as-defence) is about getting your body between an opponent and the puck, and the free arm that fends them off is permitted on conditions. Slew-footing is what that becomes when **a leg goes through their skates** — whether or not an arm is pushing their upper body backward at the same time. NHL and IIHF Rule 52.1 write it as two forms — the leg alone, *or* the leg with that arm push — and **both forms count**; each definition ends on a player made to *"fall violently to the ice"*. **Hockey Canada's Rule 8.8 writes the same two forms and widens the verb**: slew footing occurs when a player *"uses a leg or a foot to knock, drag, or sweep an opposing player's feet from under them, or pushes another player's upper body backward with an arm or elbow and at the same time, with a forward motion of their leg, knocks, drags, or sweeps that player's feet from under them."* ⚠️ **USA Hockey writes it as one sentence rather than as two routes — and it still reaches the leg on its own.** Rule 639 Note 4 opens with a complete definition, *"Slew Footing is the act of a player using their leg or foot to knock or kick an opponent's feet from under them"*, which is the IIHF's first form but for that book's capital *Player*, and the NHL's with its pronouns made plural; only then does it add *"[t]his is done by pushing an opponent's upper body backward with an arm or elbow and at the same time using a forward motion of their leg causing the opponent to fall to the ice"*, describing the classic version rather than narrowing the offence. **Its Casebook settles it.** Rule 639, Situation 5 calls *"[e]xtending the leg behind a player and taking their feet out from under them, **especially when used in conjunction with** a push to the chest area (slew foot)"* a dangerous action that *"must be penalized with a major plus game misconduct or match penalty"* — *especially when used in conjunction with* makes the arm push an aggravation, not a requirement, and the act it describes at base is the leg alone. **So a leg swept through an upright opponent's skates with no arm push is slew-footing in all four books — and in the PWHL, whose Rule 53.1 writes that same first form and whose 53.2 assesses a match penalty to any player *"guilty of slew–footing"*, with no lesser tier beneath it**, and under USA Hockey it is priced at 639(b) — *"[t]he minimum penalty to be assessed for slew footing is a major plus game misconduct penalty"* — with 639(c) reaching a match. **It is not the minor at 639(a),** which is written *"except slew footing"*.

**Why it is priced where it is.** A player whose feet leave in front of them goes down backward with nothing to break the fall — *that is this guide's reading, not a sentence any rulebook writes.* What the books do write is the *"fall violently to the ice"* above, and four tariffs that agree on one thing: **none of them prices this at a bare minor — and three of the four eject you at the floor**, as does the PWHL.

- **NHL Rule 52.2 — a mandatory match penalty**, no discretion, no lesser tier: *"Any player who is guilty of slew-footing shall be assessed a match penalty."*
- **🇬🇧 IIHF Rule 52.2 — your book in Britain** — *"The Referee **shall**, at their discretion, assess a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty if a Player is guilty of 'slew-footing' on an opposing Player."* Note the frame — *shall assess*, where the IIHF writes *may assess* in its neighbouring rules — and Rule 52 writes no lesser tier beneath it: 52.1 defines the foul and 52.2 is its only penalty clause. That is still an ejection; the IIHF book writes no match penalty for anything, and 52.3 allows Supplementary Discipline above it, at the Proper Authorities' discretion, rather than capping it there.
- **USA Hockey Rule 639** takes it out of the minor in terms — 639(a) covers the tripping family *"except slew footing"* — and 639(b) sets the floor at *"a major plus game misconduct penalty"*, with 639(c) reaching a match penalty for reckless endangerment *"under this rule"* — so it is not the slew-footing limb's ceiling alone. Its Casebook is blunter, at Rule 639, Situation 5: *"[a] minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing."* ⚠️ **And that Situation does not stop there — its last sentence is stricter than the rule it interprets.** Where 639(c) says a match penalty *"may also be assessed"*, the Casebook directs it: *"Any time one of these actions has been deemed to recklessly endanger the opponent, a match penalty must be considered."*
- **Hockey Canada is the one book that starts small, and the double minor is a floor rather than a ceiling.** 8.8(a) assesses *"a double Minor penalty"* and 8.8(b) says *"[n]o Major penalty may be assessed for slew-footing"* — but ⚠️ **8.8(c) goes straight past the major to a match penalty**, at the referee's discretion on *"the degree of violence of impact (including the impact with the ice, goal, or boards)"*, and **mandatory** where the foul injures. Its Interpretation 1 to 8.8(a) adds that *"[s]erious consideration for a Match penalty should be paid when an offending player is 'in motion' (skating) while committing this foul"*, so skating into it is an aggravating factor. **Read the double minor as the bottom of a ladder, not as the price.**

**A fifth book writes it with no arm push in the sentence at all.** **CARHA Rule 86(b)** — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — assesses *"[a]t the discretion of the Referee, a Match penalty … to any player who uses their feet to knock an opponent's skates out from under them with a kicking or leg dragging motion from behind ('slew footing')"*, and its glossary defines the foul in the same terms. **Neither sentence mentions an arm or an elbow** — that book describes the leg on its own and nothing else. ⚠️ **Note the words *from behind*, which CARHA writes into the rule where the other four books' definitions do not** — USA Hockey's Casebook uses the same description, but its playing rule does not. ⚠️ **And CARHA files the foul inside its *tripping* rule, which is the one place in any of these books a puck-first sentence comes near it.** The Note under **86(b)** ignores the trip only where a player *already falling or sliding along the ice* knocks the puck off an opponent's stick *"prior to making actual body contact"* — which is not the act 86(b) describes, and not a licence to put your leg through somebody's skates.

**So: keep your leg in front of you.** Win the battle with your hips, your base and your skates on the puck — [§7](#7-board-battles) is the whole method — and if you have lost position, the only legal way to get it back is with your feet. ⚠️ **And "I played the puck first" is not a defence.** Every puck-first relief the six books read here write sits inside a *tripping* rule and stops there: USA Hockey's is the Note to **639(a)**, and 639(a) itself is written *"except slew footing"*; **Hockey Canada's Rule 8.6** says so in terms — *"This rule does not apply to actions defined under Rule 8.7 – Clipping or Rule 8.8 – Slew-Footing"* — so its Interpretations 1 and 2 to 8.6(a), which excuse a player who touches the puck first, do not reach 8.8; and **NHL and IIHF Rule 52, and the PWHL’s Rule 53, write no relief of any kind.** [Risk Management](../hockey-iq/risk_management.md#slew-footing-the-one-the-carve-outs-never-reach) covers this from the other side. ⚠️ **And if someone goes down backward and is not getting up, nobody moves them, nobody takes the helmet off, and somebody calls for immediate medical help** — that is [the spinal-injury section above](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice).

### The habits that keep you out of the box

```facts
Action: Blade on the puck, never on the player — USA Hockey penalises a stick in front of the legs to impede them "even if on the ice", with no effort to legally play the puck
Rule: Hands and forearms are no part of a legal body check — USA Hockey's Glossary and Declaration bar "the hands, forearm, stick or elbow in delivering a body check", and the IIHF permits the trunk only; a careless one is roughing under 640 Note 2, but the check was already illegal
Action: Move your feet instead of reaching — a stick that has to reach is a stick that hooks, holds or trips
Priority: Take the puck, not the player — if your first thought is the body, your stick is somewhere it should not be
Never: Stay in a scrum. Play the whistle and then stop instead; retaliating turns their penalty into two
Never: Put a hand on an opponent's cage or facemask — USA Hockey 622(b) is a major plus a game misconduct, 622(c) is a match penalty, and Hockey Canada files it under Rule 7.1 Attempt to Injure
Rule: The NHL and the IIHF price the facemask grab too, outside Rule 54 — 75.2 in each names "grabbing hold of a face mask" as unsportsmanlike conduct and a minor, and sends the referee on to Rule 21 where it injures or the force was serious. No book here leaves it unpriced
Never: Take a penalty out of frustration at a referee — it is the cheapest goal you will ever give up
Action: Know your league's standard — watch how the first five minutes are being called and adjust
```

- **Blade on the puck, never on the player.** Almost every stick penalty starts with the blade off the ice — but a blade *on* the ice is no safe harbour either: USA Hockey's Standard of Play penalises placing the stick in front of an opponent's legs to impede them "even if on the ice", where there is "no effort to legally play the puck".
- **Hit with your trunk, never with your hands or forearms.** A hand or a forearm in a check is not a lighter version of a legal one; it is outside the definition altogether. **USA Hockey's Glossary and its Declaration of Player Safety, Fair Play & Respect** both write that *"[t]he use of the hands, forearm, stick or elbow in delivering a body check is unacceptable and not within the guidelines of a legal body check"*, and the **IIHF glossary** — the British reader's — allows a legal check *"only with the trunk of the body (hips and shoulders)"*. USA Hockey Rule 640 Note 2 makes a careless or reckless one **roughing**, but that names the foul it gets called under rather than telling you the check was legal, and elbowing and head contact are separate offences whose ladders run above it — head contact from a higher floor too, at USA Hockey 620(a)'s minor plus a misconduct. The full envelope is in [What a legal check is, precisely](#what-a-legal-check-is-precisely).
- **Move your feet instead of reaching.** A stick that has to reach is a stick that hooks, holds or trips. Coaches name reaching as the biggest driver of penalty minutes in amateur hockey — **coaching emphasis, not a counted ranking**.
- **Take the puck, not the player.** In a battle, aim for the puck. If your first thought is the body, your stick is somewhere it should not be.
- **Play the whistle, then stop.** Nothing good happens in a scrum. Both the instigator and the retaliator can be penalised — and USA Hockey's standard actually directs the *extra* penalty at the instigator, with Rule 615(a) protecting a player who does not retaliate. The common claim that the retaliator is called more often is unsourced; what is not in doubt is that retaliating turns their penalty into two.
- **Never put a hand on an opponent's cage or facemask.** It is not a scrum reflex you can afford. **USA Hockey 622(b)** assesses *"a major plus a game misconduct penalty"* to a player who *"uses their hand to rub, grab or hold the facemask of an opponent"*, and **622(c)** adds that a match penalty for reckless endangerment *"may also be assessed for grabbing or holding the facemask."* **Hockey Canada files it not under holding at all**, but under **Rule 7.1 – Attempt to Injure or Deliberate Injury**: 7.1(a) is a double minor for grabbing *"the facial protector, helmet, chin strap, or throat protector of an opponent"* with *"a minimal degree of violence"*, 7.1(b) a major plus a game misconduct at the referee's discretion *"based upon the degree of violence"* — and **mandatory** where the grab injures — and 7.1(c) a match penalty for one carrying *"a moderate or high degree of violence"*, or used *"to gain an advantage or to inflict punishment or injury."* **And the NHL and the IIHF — the British reader's book — price it outside Rule 54 altogether**, at **75.2**, which names *"grabbing hold of a face mask"* as unsportsmanlike conduct and assesses a minor, the NHL adding that *"[i]f warranted, and specifically when injury results, the Referee may apply Rule 21 – Match Penalties"* and the IIHF that a physical offence *"serious and severe, based on the degree of force"* goes to its own Rule 21. **No book here leaves it unpriced**, so there is no rule set in which this is a free reflex.
- **Never take a penalty out of frustration at a referee.** It is the cheapest goal you will ever give up.
- **Know your league's standard.** Beer leagues, youth associations and international play call the same rulebook very differently. Watch how the first five minutes are being called and adjust.

### Fighting — and why the amateur rules are much harsher than the NHL's

```facts
Rule: Fighting in the NHL is a five-minute major (Rule 46.14); an instigator takes that major plus an instigating minor and a ten-minute misconduct (46.11); an aggressor a major plus a game misconduct (46.2)
Rule: The third man into an altercation faces a game misconduct at the referee's discretion — except where a match penalty is being imposed in the original altercation (NHL Rule 46.16)
Rule: USA Hockey counts an attempted punch, contact or not, as fighting — a major plus a game misconduct (Rule 615)
Rule: A player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be penalised for fighting under USA Hockey Rule 615(a) — neither current IIHF edition writes that protection, and under both, punching back can reach a major for fighting (2025/26 Rule 46.6; 2026/27 Rules 46.1 and 46.12)
Never: Drop your stick or remove your gloves in an altercation you were not part of — a minor under USA Hockey 615(a), plus a game misconduct if you are judged the instigator of a subsequent altercation
Rule: Under IIHF Rule 46 an instigator takes 2+5+game misconduct and the third player in a game misconduct — the numbers moved between editions (2025/26: 46.3, 46.7; 2026/27: 46.10, 46.14). Which edition binds a British player is unresolved, and 2026/27's narrower third-man exception is the safe reading
Rule: In NIHL (all leagues), SNL and BUIHA Varsity, fighting is a five-minute major — and in NIHL, NIHL1 and NIHL2 a fighting major in the last five minutes of regulation or in overtime also carries an automatic game misconduct (In-House Rules, Section 6)
Rule: The British fighting major carries a relief neither IIHF edition writes in those words — in NIHL (all leagues), SNL and BUIHA Varsity an unwilling combatant may be given, at the referee's discretion, "a minor OR a major penalty (2 OR a standalone 5 without a game misconduct)"
Rule: In British women's hockey at every age, adult included, any major brings an automatic game misconduct — a fighting major ends your game (In-House Rule 101's ejection bullet applies "in all cases" and states no age scope)
Key: Junior hockey at every age reads the same way, on In-House Rule 201's scoping of the IIHF junior rules — an inference, since 201 does not name 201.1, so treat a fighting major as ending your game
Key: Rec and beer leagues are commonly harsher again — a general observation about how such leagues are run, not a sourced survey. Read your own league's sheet, the only version that binds you
Rule: CARHA 59(b) assesses a game misconduct to any player "acting as a peacemaker" in a fight, and Hockey Canada 7.10(e)(ii) to one who "acts as a peacemaker" — Hockey Canada's only where one fighting penalty has already been assessed (its Interpretation 6) — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only
Rule: USA Hockey refuses the peacemaker defence by name: 615(d) is a mandatory game misconduct for the first to intervene in an altercation, and Casebook Situation 24 applies it "[r]egardless of the reason"
Action: If a scrum starts, hands down, gloves on — skate away and let the officials sort it out
```

This document is about legal contact, and fighting is not it. But it belongs here, because the moment a battle turns into a shove-fest the rules that apply are ones most players know only from watching the NHL — and **they are not the rules you play under.**

- **NHL (Rule 46).** Fighting is a **five-minute major** — 46.14 reads simply, *"A major penalty shall be imposed on any player who fights."* A player judged to have started it takes more than an extra minor: an **instigator** *"shall be assessed an instigating minor penalty, a major penalty for fighting **and a ten-minute misconduct**"* (46.11) — and it escalates, because a second instigator penalty in the same game replaces that ten-minute misconduct with a **game misconduct**, and a third in one regular season brings an automatic game misconduct of its own. An **aggressor** — one who *"continues throwing and landing punches"* at an opponent *"no longer in a position to defend himself"* — *"shall be assessed a major penalty for fighting and a game misconduct"* (46.2). And the **third man into an altercation** faces a game misconduct *"at the discretion of the Referee"* (46.16), which also catches *"subsequent players who elect to intervene"*. ⚠️ **That last one carries an exception, and it is written into the same sentence:** the game misconduct applies *"except when a match penalty is being imposed in the original altercation."* Do not plan around it — you cannot know from outside a fight whether a match penalty is coming — but do not read the rule as a flat ban on ever stepping in either. It is still the trap: stepping in to help a teammate can end your night.
- **USA Hockey (Rule 615) is far stricter, and the threshold is much lower.** Its Note defines fighting as *"[a] punch, **or an attempted punch**, thrown by any player in the direction of an opponent, **regardless as to whether contact is made**"*. You do not have to connect. And 615(a) assesses *"a major plus a game misconduct penalty… to any player who engages in fighting"* — so under USA Hockey a thrown punch is not five minutes, it is **an ejection**, plus a further minor if you instigated it, plus the progressive suspensions that follow a game misconduct. There is also a minor for any player who *"drops their stick and/or removes their glove(s) during an altercation and is not a participant in the original altercation"* — i.e. for the third man arriving hot. ⚠️ **That minor is not the ceiling either, and the next sentence of the same rule says so:** *"A game misconduct penalty shall be added if, in the judgment of the Referee, such player is deemed to be the instigator of a subsequent altercation."* So arriving hot to somebody else's scrum can end your game without your ever having been in the first one.
- **615(a) also protects you for doing nothing:** *"[a] player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be assessed a penalty for fighting under this rule."* That clause is worth knowing by heart, because it means the disciplined response is also the one the rulebook rewards.
- **Going in to break it up is not a defence in any of the six rulebooks read here, and three of them price the peacemaker by name.** **USA Hockey 615(d)** is flat and mandatory: *"A game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player or goalkeeper who is the first to intervene in an altercation then in progress. This penalty is in addition to any other penalty incurred in the same incident."* ⚠️ **And an *altercation* there is a lower bar than a fight** — its Casebook defines one as *"[a]ny physical interaction between two or more opposing players where at least one penalty assessed"*, citing 615(c, d and e) (Rule 615, Situation 21). **A shoving match somebody gets two minutes for is enough to make you the first man in.** Its Casebook answers the peacemaker question by name and refuses the intent defence — a player *"first to enter an altercation, acting only as a peacemaker"* is caught anyway, because *"[r]egardless of the reason, the first player to enter any altercation shall be assessed a game misconduct penalty"* (Rule 615, Situation 24) — and Situation 25 adds that a third player who then fights takes **both** the fighting major plus game misconduct **and** the first-to-intervene game misconduct. **CARHA's adult book and Hockey Canada name the act rather than the order of arrival.** **CARHA 59(b)** assesses a game misconduct to *"[a]ny player joining in a fight, acting as a peacemaker, or taking part in another fight during the same stoppage of play"*, and **Hockey Canada 7.10(e)(ii)** to a player who *"joins in a fight or acts as a peacemaker"* — so under those two the fourth player in is caught as squarely as the third, where USA Hockey's 615(d) reaches only the first. ⚠️ **That is not a licence to be the fourth man in under USA Hockey**, because 615(a) writes a minor for a player who *"drops their stick and/or removes their glove(s) during an altercation and is not a participant in the original altercation"*, and a thrown or attempted punch is a major plus a game misconduct there whoever threw it. ⚠️ **Hockey Canada's carries a limit USA Hockey's does not:** its Interpretation 6 applies that game misconduct *"only to situations where at least one player has been assessed a fighting penalty"*. **And two of the three discretionary books ask nothing about why you came either** — neither the NHL's 46.16 nor the PWHL's 46.15 writes an intent limb at all, and the only peacemaker waiver written in any of the six is the 2026/27 IIHF book's, at 46.14, which needs an aggressor penalty in the original altercation and the referee's judgement besides. ⚠️ **And one case in Hockey Canada's book is priced above all of them:** 7.10(f) makes a gross misconduct of intervening *"as a peacemaker in a fight involving a team official"*. **So there is no rule set here in which stepping in to separate them is free.**
- **IIHF (Rule 46) — the British base, and the one fighting rule here that has just been rewritten.** The book opens by saying that *"'Fighting' is not part of international ice hockey's DNA."* **The tiers are the same in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions; the numbers are not**, so quote a number with its edition or not at all. An **instigator** takes a minor plus a major for fighting plus an automatic game misconduct — 2'+5'+GMP — at **46.3** in 2025/26 and **46.10** in 2026/27; an **aggressor** the same, at **46.4** and **46.2**; the **third player in** a game misconduct at the referee's discretion, catching *"subsequent Players who elect to intervene"*, at **46.7** and **46.14**.
  - **The third-man exception narrowed, and that one is about you rather than about the fighters.** The 2025/26 book waives the game misconduct outright *"except when a game misconduct penalty is being imposed in the original altercation"* (46.7). The 2026/27 book waives it only *"[i]f an aggressor penalty is being imposed in the original altercation"* **and** the referee judges the third man in *"is doing so with the purpose of intervening to stop the ongoing altercation with an 'unwilling combatant' and not to start a secondary altercation"* (46.14). ⚠️ **Under the current book, skating in to break it up is more exposed, not less.**
  - **Neither edition writes USA Hockey's flat protection for the player who does not retaliate.** What the 2025/26 book writes is the opposite case, at **46.6** — *"Defender – Unwilling Combatant"* — covering the player who *does* punch back: *"A Player who retaliates to being punched will be assessed at least a minor penalty ('Roughing') or a major penalty ('Fighting', without a game misconduct penalty), **if the criteria for 'fighting back' with a few punches is not fulfilled**."* Read that against the same rule's opening paragraph, which does shelter a player who *"'defends' themself with a 'few punches'"* as an unwilling combatant — so the minor waits for a retaliation that goes past a few punches rather than for the first one.
  - **The 2026/27 book deletes the *Defender – Unwilling Combatant* heading** — 46.6 is *Helmets* there — and keeps only a note, repeated under 46.2, 46.8, 46.10 and 46.12, that a player who *"'defends' themselves against an aggressor or an instigator may be considered as an 'unwilling combatant' and the automatic game misconduct penalty may be waived at the discretion of the referee."* **Note what that waives and what it does not: the game misconduct, not the fighting major.** The sentence about retaliating to a punch is not in that book at all — so what a retaliation costs there falls back on **46.1**, which deems a fight to have occurred once a player *"punches or attempts to punch an opponent repeatedly"*, and **46.12**, a major plus an automatic game misconduct for any player who fights. **Neither edition makes punching back safe. The 2025/26 book prices it at a minor or a major; the 2026/27 book leaves the major and only lets a referee lift the ejection.**
  - **Which edition binds a British player is not answerable from the British document.** The IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 adopt *"the latest IIHF Official Rule Book (Version 1.1, published July 2025)"* — that is the **2025/26** book — while their own cover says *"the Current IIHF Rule Book"*. ⚠️ **Until that is resolved, take the harsher reading of each point**: do not count on a non-retaliation protection, and do not count on the third-man exception.
- **The IHUK In-House Rules amend it.** In **NIHL (all leagues), SNL and BUIHA Varsity**, *"[f]ighting shall be penalised with a 5-minute Major Penalty."* ⚠️ **That sentence does not travel alone, and the half usually dropped is the one that helps you.** The same bullet continues: *“An unwilling combatant may be awarded (at the referee’s discretion) a minor OR a major penalty (2 OR a standalone 5 without a game misconduct)”* — **a bare five with no ejection**, which is relief neither IIHF edition gives in those words. **It is discretionary, so it is not something to count on**, and it is scoped to the same three competitions and no further. And in **NIHL, NIHL1 and NIHL2** there is an additional provision: *"Any fighting major assessed in the last 5 minutes of regulation time (including overtime) will also carry an automatic Game Misconduct."* A further British consequence follows from elsewhere in the In-House Rules, and it is not limited to the age groups: in **women's hockey at every age, adult included**, any major penalty already brings an automatic game misconduct and ejection — Rule 101 carries that as a bullet of its own, applying *"in all cases"* with no age scope stated — so there, a fighting major *is* an ejection. **Read junior hockey the same way at every age group**, on In-House Rule 201's scoping of the IIHF junior playing rules to *"all junior age groups"* — though note that Rule 201 does not name 201.1, so that reading is an inference rather than the rule's own words. See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).
- **One British league runs a different fighting rule again, and it is the one most British readers watch rather than play in.** The **EIHL Casebook 26-27** supersedes IIHF Rule 46 for the Elite League, and its on-ice tiers are lower — **but it is not simply a relaxation, because it moves part of the cost off the penalty sheet.** It has its own section below: [The EIHL's fighting rules](#the-eihls-fighting-rules--lower-on-the-sheet-not-lower-in-cost). ⚠️ **None of it is your rule unless you are in that league.**
- **Rec and beer leagues are commonly harsher again** — many eject and suspend for any fight regardless of who started it. *(That is a general observation about how such leagues are run rather than a sourced survey; there is no league-wide census of rec-league fighting policy. Read your own league's sheet, which is the only version that binds you.)*

**The practical version:** in every book that governs amateur hockey, throwing a punch — or even winding up to — ends your game. If a scrum starts, your hands stay down and your gloves stay on. Skate away and let the officials sort it out; that is not passivity, it is the only outcome that leaves you on the ice.

A team that plays hard, wins battles and takes no penalties is far more physically dominant than a team that hits everything and spends nine minutes a game shorthanded. See [Risk Management](../hockey-iq/risk_management.md).

### The EIHL's fighting rules — lower on the sheet, not lower in cost

```facts
Rule: The EIHL Casebook supersedes IIHF Rule 46 for the Elite League alone — its 46.1 penalises willing combatants with "at least a Major Penalty", noting that "for their 2nd Major Penalty an Automatic Game misconduct will be assessed", where IIHF 46.1 attaches that ejection to the first
Rule: The EIHL instigator is "2 minor + 5 major + 10 misconduct" under Casebook 46.10 where the IIHF's is 2+5+game misconduct — rising to "2 minor + 5 major + 20 Game misconduct" in the final five minutes of regulation, at any time in overtime, or on a second instigator penalty in the same game
Rule: Two EIHL provisions pull the other way — Casebook 46.2 imposes a game misconduct on any player assessed a fighting major "after the original altercation has started", waivable only where the opposing player was clearly the instigator of the altercation
Rule: Every EIHL game misconduct assessed under Casebook 46.2 is "reviewed and subject to further game suspensions through the EIHL Department of Player Safety" — the ejection starts a review rather than ending the matter
Rule: The EIHL Casebook keeps a season fighting count outside Rule 46.1, in its Automatic Supplementary Discipline section — a 10th fighting major in the regular season brings an automatic one-game suspension, a 14th "an automatic two game suspension for each additional fighting major"
Rule: An EIHL altercation outside the periods of the game draws "all appropriate penalties that may be imposed at the time" plus automatic supplementary discipline, and its instigator a minimum one-game suspension — where the IIHF answers the same moment with a major plus an automatic game misconduct
Rule: An EIHL instigator who is also the aggressor takes both tiers stacked — "an instigating (2) minor penalty, a (5) major penalty for fighting, a (10) ten-minute misconduct (instigator) and a (20) game misconduct penalty (aggressor)". The Casebook prints that rule and the altercation rule both as 46.2
Rule: Casebook 46.13 gives an unsportsmanlike minor to "[a] player who is involved in an altercation and does not have his sweater properly tied down" — involvement, not fighting, is the trigger, and the IIHF book the EIHL otherwise adopts writes no such limb
Never: Read the EIHL's lower on-ice tier as a lower price — what the Casebook takes off the penalty sheet it hands to the Department of Player Safety
```

**This is one British competition, and it is the one on television — which is exactly why its rules get carried into rinks they do not govern.** The **EIHL Casebook 26-27** supersedes IIHF Rule 46 for the Elite League, and read on the penalty sheet alone it departs *downward*.

- **The on-ice tiers are lower than the IIHF's.** Where IIHF 46.1 says a player willing to fight *"shall be assessed a major penalty for fighting and an automatic game misconduct penalty"*, the Casebook's **46.1** reads: *"Willing combatants will be penalised with at least a Major Penalty ('Fighting'). Note for their 2nd Major Penalty an Automatic Game misconduct will be assessed."* Its **46.10** prices an instigator at *"an instigating minor penalty, a major for fighting and a ten-minute misconduct. (2 minor + 5 major + 10 misconduct)"* — a ten-minute misconduct where IIHF 46.10 attaches an *automatic game misconduct*, and the NHL's 46.11 number exactly — rising to *"2 minor + 5 major + 20 Game misconduct"* for an instigator in the final five *"minutes of regulation time or at any time in overtime"* or for a second instigator penalty in the same game. ⚠️ **One player can be charged as both, and that composition is the Casebook's own.** A player *"deemed to be both the instigator and aggressor of an altercation"* takes *"an instigating (2) minor penalty, a (5) major penalty for fighting, a (10) ten-minute misconduct (instigator) and a (20) game misconduct penalty (aggressor)"* — **the two tiers stacked, not merged**, and reviewed by the Department of Player Safety on top. ⚠️ **That rule is printed as 46.2, and so is the altercation rule above it — the Casebook prints the number twice, for two different rules.** Quote the heading as well as the number, because the number alone does not identify which one you mean.
- **The Casebook does not say whether 46.1's "2nd Major Penalty" is counted within the game or across the season, so do not do arithmetic on it.** ⚠️ **It does keep a season fighting count elsewhere — it simply does not tie 46.1 to that count.** Its *Automatic Supplementary Discipline (DOPS)* section counts fighting majors across the EIHL regular season, and its own Note there reads: *"If a player is not the instigator, the fighting major is not included in the player's total number of fights."* **Two different tallies, in one book, neither cross-referring to the other. Plan on whichever is harsher.**
- **Two provisions pull the opposite way from the lower tiers, and neither appears on the penalty sheet you see at the time.** **Casebook 46.2 Altercation** — *"Fighting After the Original Altercation"* — imposes *"a game misconduct penalty… on any player who is assessed a major penalty for fighting after the original altercation has started"*, waivable at the referee's discretion only *"if the opposing player was clearly the instigator of the altercation."* ⚠️ **So being second into the night's fighting is an ejection in this league**, whatever 46.1's lower first tier says. And the same rule adds: *"All fighting penalties that involve a Game Misconduct penalty assessed under this rule will be reviewed and subject to further game suspensions through the EIHL Department of Player Safety."* **The ejection is the start of the process.**
- **And there is a season ladder.** Under *Automatic Supplementary Discipline (DOPS)*, a fighting major assessed *"for the 10th time during the EIHL regular season"* brings an automatic one-game suspension; a 14th brings *"an automatic two game suspension for each additional fighting major"*; and an instigator *"in any of the fights above the 10th game threshold"* draws a further game. The Casebook resets those to zero for the play-offs. ⚠️ **None of that is visible in the five minutes you are sitting.**
- **The Casebook also reaches an altercation outside the periods of the game**, in the same section: the teams involved *"shall be assessed all appropriate penalties that may be imposed at the time and shall also be subject to automatic supplementary discipline"*, and an instigator there *"shall be suspended for a minimum"* one *"game pending a review of the incident"*. **Read that with the warm-up rules in [§1](#before-the-puck-drops--the-warm-up-is-not-outside-the-rules).**
- **And there is a minor here that has nothing to do with punching, which is the one an ordinary player is likeliest to take.** Casebook **46.13 Jerseys** assesses *"a minor penalty for Unsportsmanlike Conduct"* to *"[a] player who is involved in an altercation and does not have his sweater properly tied down (the sweater is not removed)"*, *"in addition to other penalties to be assessed to the participants of an altercation"*. **Being *involved in* an altercation is the trigger, not fighting in one** — and the IIHF book the Casebook otherwise adopts writes no such limb. Deliberately removing your jersey before an altercation, or wearing a modified one, is that minor **plus a game misconduct**; so is fighting and losing the jersey off your torso, unless the opponent or a linesman took it off, or the tie-down held — in which case *"the game misconduct is not applicable"* and the kit goes to the League office to be examined. ⚠️ **So tie down before you leave the room.** In this league a scrum you were merely in can cost two minutes for a fastener.
- **And the Casebook settles the edition question for the EIHL alone**: its Preamble names *"the official 2026 - 2027 IIHF Rulebook and IIHF Situation Handbook"* as the residual authority, so an EIHL reader is under the **2026/27** book. ⚠️ **That says nothing about the NIHL, the SNL or BUIHA, where the question in the section above stands open.**

**So the televised version is closer to the truth in the EIHL than anywhere else in this section — and it is still not your rule unless you are in that league, and it is still not cheap.**

### Leaving the bench or the box during an altercation

```facts
Rule: USA Hockey 629(a) is a major plus a game misconduct to any player who leaves either bench "during an altercation", "in addition to any other penalties" — and a USA Hockey major is served with "no on-ice substitute" permitted (403(a)), so the team kills it short-handed
Rule: USA Hockey's Casebook puts that on every player who leaves, "not just the first player to do so" (Rule 629, Situation 2) — so "only the first man off the bench gets it" is not USA Hockey's rule
Rule: In USA Hockey Casebook 629, Situation 1 a player whose penalty had expired left the penalty bench during an altercation, stayed out of it and skated to his own bench — "Each player would be assessed a major plus game misconduct penalty"
Rule: The IIHF rules that pattern the same way — a player with 0:45 left who leaves the box during a fight and "just skates around the altercation" takes "an additional minor penalty plus a game misconduct" (Situation Handbook 70.1, on Rule 70.4)
Rule: The prohibition is in every book, in wording close but not identical — NHL 70.1 bars leaving "the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation"; IIHF 70.1 names the two places separately, "the Players' bench or penalty box"
Rule: "The first or second player to leave" is the NHL's and the IIHF's PLAYERS'-bench tier (NHL 70.3 and 70.6; IIHF 70.3 and 70.6) — out of the penalty box both books catch every player, and USA Hockey 629(a) catches every player off either bench
Rule: Hockey Canada 10.4(e): "A Game Misconduct penalty will be assessed to any player who leaves the players' or Penalty Benches while a fight is occurring on the ice or for the purposes of starting a fight" — double minor plus game misconduct first off, game misconduct for a later player penalised
Rule: CARHA 70(b) is the first player off either bench, but 70(c) adds an automatic game misconduct to any other player penalised for what they did — including one out of the penalty box, who "would not have to be the first to leave a bench". CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only
Rule: A penalised player who leaves the box during a stoppage and during an altercation takes a minor plus a game misconduct under the NHL (70.4, 70.6) and the IIHF (70.4), and 629(a)'s major plus game misconduct under USA Hockey
Rule: USA Hockey 629(a) treats the altercation as concluded once the referee enters the referee's crease or, with no penalties, signals a face-off location — until then, leaving the bench is the offence
Rule: The EIHL adds a suspension the IIHF does not, the night’s penalty unchanged — leaving either bench "to join an altercation" draws a 20 or a 2 + 20 and "shall be automatically suspended for the minimum of three" games, "pending a review of the incident" (Casebook, Fines and Suspensions (2))
Never: Leave the bench or the penalty box while an altercation is going on — not to help a teammate, not to change, and not even to skate to your own bench once your penalty has expired
```

**The instruction above is *hands down, gloves on, skate away*. There is one place on the ice where skating away is itself the major, and it is worth knowing exactly where.** If an altercation starts while you are on the players' bench or in the penalty box, this rule is not about what you do once you are on the ice. It is about the fact that you left the bench at all. **It is scoped to no division and no age** — unlike body checking, it binds you whatever category you play.

- **USA Hockey is the strictest of the books read here on the night, and it is the book most North American amateurs are under.** Rule 629(a): *"A major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who leaves the players' bench or the penalty bench during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation. These penalties are in addition to any other penalties that may be assessed during the incident."* ⚠️ **Price that properly, because "a major plus a game misconduct" is two separate bills and only one of them is yours.** A USA Hockey major is served on the ice — **Rule 403(a)**: the player *"shall be ruled off the ice for five minutes, or the designated major penalty time, during which time **no on-ice substitute shall be permitted**"* — so your team kills it a skater short while you are already in the dressing room, and 629(a)'s *"in addition to any other penalties"* means anything you did once you got there is added on top. *(Rule 403's own Note lets an affiliate shorten a major to three or four minutes in shorter periods, except for Adults; it does not let anyone shorten the ejection.)* ⚠️ **Two things players commonly believe about that rule are wrong, and USA Hockey's Casebook says so in terms.** The first is *"only the first man off gets it"* — **Rule 629, Situation 2** answers that the major plus game misconduct *"is assessed to any player leaving the bench during an altercation, not just the first player to do so."* The second is *"my penalty was up, so I was entitled to leave."*
- **That second one is the sharper of the two, because the player it penalises is the one who skated away.** **Casebook Rule 629, Situation 1** puts exactly that case: two players' coincident penalties expire, but they must wait for a stoppage — and the stoppage that comes is *"because of an altercation."* Both leave the box. One joins in; the other *"does not enter the altercation, but skates to their players' bench."* The ruling covers them both — *"Each player would be assessed a major plus game misconduct penalty"* — and the reason is stated flat: *"No player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation."* ⚠️ **The stoppage that released you was the fight, and that is the trap.** What ends it is written into 629(a) itself: the altercation is concluded *"when the referee enters the referee's crease or, in the absence of penalties, signals a face-off location."* Wait for that, and let the officials wave you out.
- **The IIHF answers that same fact pattern the same way, which is the version a British player needs.** Its Situation Handbook puts a player with *"0:45 to serve on the hooking minor"* leaving the penalty box during a fight who *"does not start another fight"* and *"just skates around the altercation"*. The ruling is *"an additional minor penalty plus a game misconduct"*, with the coach required to put a replacement in the box to serve both the remaining 0:45 and the new minor, leaving the team short-handed for 2:45 (Situation 70.1, on Rule 70.4). ⚠️ **Quoted as printed — and the printing is inconsistent.** The question names A5 as the player leaving the box; the answer penalises B5 and short-hands Team B. That mismatch is in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 handbooks alike. The ruling is unambiguous; only the team letters are not.
- **The prohibition is in every book read here, but not under the same number and not on the same trigger.** The CARHA adult book writes it at **Rule 70(a)**: *"No player may leave the players' bench or penalty bench at any time during a fight on the ice or for the purpose of starting a fight."* ⚠️ **Note the word — CARHA's trigger is a *fight*, not an altercation**, the same narrower word Hockey Canada uses below, and narrower than the NHL's, the IIHF's, USA Hockey's and the PWHL's; its 70(b) double minor plus game misconduct for the first player off either bench follows that same word. ⚠️ **CARHA does not stop at the first player off, though, and that is the inference the narrower word invites.** Its **70(c)** adds an automatic game misconduct to any *other* player off either bench who is assessed *“a Minor, Major, or Misconduct penalty for their actions”* — and it reaches the penalty box in terms, including a player who leaves it, *“for which they were to be assessed a Minor penalty in addition to the Game Misconduct penalty, such player would not have to be the first to leave a bench”* (the minor itself is 70(d)). **So the second and third players out are caught there too**, to a ceiling of five game misconducts per team in one stoppage, counting any assessed under Rule 59 (Note 1) — and Note 2 allows a sixth. ⚠️ **Do not read the narrower trigger as room to skate in and break one up either, because both those books price the peacemaker in a different rule.** CARHA **Rule 59(b)**: *"Any player joining in a fight, acting as a peacemaker, or taking part in another fight during the same stoppage of play will be assessed a Game Misconduct penalty plus any other penalty the offender incurs under the rules."* Hockey Canada **7.10(e)(ii)** assesses a game misconduct to a player who *"joins in a fight or acts as a peacemaker"*, and its Interpretation 6 scopes that to fights where at least one player has already been assessed a fighting penalty. **Neither is discretionary, and neither asks what you meant by it.** **And they are not the only two books that reach you for it** — USA Hockey's **615(d)** game misconduct for *"the first to intervene in an altercation then in progress"* is mandatory as well, and its Casebook applies that to a peacemaker *"[r]egardless of the reason"* (Rule 615, Situation 24). The **PWHL** writes the bench-leaving bar at **72.1**, not 70.1 — its Rule 70 is *Illegal Substitution* — and the words are the NHL's exactly, with a pointer added to its own altercation rule: *"No player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation (refer to Rule 46.3 - Altercation)."* ⚠️ **Who it catches there is the NHL's rule; what it costs afterwards is not.** PWHL **72.6** gives the game misconduct to the first or second player off either bench, and to any penalised player who leaves the box during a stoppage and during an altercation, exactly as the NHL does — but **72.10** suspends the first player off **four** games and the second **three**, where the NHL's 70.10 is ten and five. The penalty-box clause is one place they do agree: five games for any player off the box who is *"not the first player to do so"* (PWHL 72.10, NHL 70.10).
- **What it costs, and who it catches, is not the same in any two of the four books compared through the rest of this section — the NHL, the IIHF, USA Hockey and Hockey Canada.** The **NHL** and the **IIHF** bar the same act, in wording that is close but not identical. NHL 70.1: *"No player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation."* The IIHF's 70.1 names the two places separately: *"No Player may leave the Players' bench or penalty box at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation."* In both books the automatic game misconduct that follows is written for the first or second player off — the NHL for *"[t]he player who was the first or second player to leave the players' (or penalty bench) during an altercation"* (70.3, restated at 70.6), the IIHF for *"the first or second Player to leave the Players' bench (or penalty box)"* (70.3 and 70.6). ⚠️ **So even in the two books the folklore comes from, it is the first *or second*, not the first** — and the NHL adds automatic suspensions of ten games for the first player off and five for the second (70.10). ⚠️ **That is not the whole of 70.10, and the part usually left off is the part that reaches a third player.** The same rule carries a separate clause for the penalty box alone: *"Any player who leaves the penalty bench illegally during an altercation and **is not the first player to do so**, shall be suspended automatically without pay for the next"* five *"regular League and/or Play-off games"*. **Not the second — any of them.** The IIHF writes no suspension tariff of its own here at all: its 70.10 is *"Disciplinary Measures"*, which refers the matter to the Proper Authorities under Rule 28, so outside the Elite League a British player's exposure is discretionary rather than counted in games. ⚠️ **Inside it they are counted, and the EIHL Casebook prints the number: three games, automatic.**
- **The IIHF-edition question does arise here — but not as a renumbering.** Rule 70 keeps all ten of its sub-rules, in the same order and under the same headings, in the 2025/26 (Version 1.1) and 2026/27 books, and the penalties themselves are identical. So unlike Rule 46 above, a rule number quoted here is safe in either edition. ⚠️ **What the newer book changes is what happens after the final buzzer.** Its 70.3, 70.4 and 70.6 each carry the same added sentence, which appears nowhere in the 2025/26 book: *"When a player has been penalized under this rule, the player's coach shall also be subject to potential supplementary discipline under Rule 28."* That sentence ends 70.3, and ends 70.4 bar a pointer to Rule 28 — but in 70.6 it sits mid-rule, with three further paragraphs after it, including the one that prices a penalised player leaving the box. In the same edition, 70.3's game misconduct now ends *"and shall be subject to potential supplementary discipline"*, 70.6 adds *"Those players shall also be subject to potential supplementary discipline"*, and a new **46.15** lists *"leaving the players' bench or penalty box"* among the acts that can draw it. **The penalty on the night is the same in both books. The exposure afterwards is wider in the current one, and it now reaches your coach.**
- **And one British competition does write a suspension tariff here, which is the half a British reader is likeliest to miss.** The **EIHL Casebook 26-27** supersedes the IIHF where it speaks, and its *Fines and Suspensions* section carries an item headed for leaving the players’ or penalty bench, which points the reader on to Rule 70: *"A Player who leaves the players’ or penalty bench to join an altercation will receive either a 20 (Game Misconduct) penalty or 2 + 20 (Game Misconduct) penalty and shall be automatically suspended for the minimum of three"* games, *"pending a review of the incident"*. ⚠️ **Read the direction before you read the number: the penalty on the night is the IIHF's, unchanged. What the Casebook adds is the automatic three-game minimum afterwards** — so the Elite League is heavier here, where on fighting it is [lighter on the sheet](#the-eihls-fighting-rules--lower-on-the-sheet-not-lower-in-cost). ⚠️ **And note what the Casebook's own words are scoped to.** Its suspension attaches to leaving *"to join an altercation"*. It says nothing about the player the IIHF's Situation Handbook catches — the one who leaves the box and *"just skates around the altercation"* — who is still exposed to IIHF 70.4's minor plus game misconduct on the night. **Do not read the narrower suspension clause as a narrower prohibition.** **This reaches the Elite League and nowhere else in Britain:** the word *altercation* does not appear at all in the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27, nor in the junior, U10, NIHL or WNIHL Rules of Competition, all five searched for this — so outside the EIHL the IIHF's discretionary Rule 28 route is what a British player has.
- **Hockey Canada bars it in general terms and then tiers the fight case — but read its opening sentence before its sub-clauses.** Rule 10.4 opens by allowing a player off the bench only *"for the purposes of a legal line change or at the end of a period"*, and off the penalty bench only *"upon the expiry of their penalty"*, so leaving is never simply permitted there either. Then **10.4(e)** begins: *"A Game Misconduct penalty will be assessed to any player who leaves the players' or Penalty Benches while a fight is occurring on the ice or for the purposes of starting a fight."* The tiers follow that sentence — a **double minor plus a game misconduct** for the first player off either bench (10.4(e)(i)), and a game misconduct for a later player who leaves during a fight *"and is subsequently assessed a penalty"* (10.4(e)(ii)), with a ceiling of five such game misconducts per team in a single stoppage (10.4(e)(iii)). ⚠️ **Whether that later-player tier narrows the opening sentence or simply prices one case of it is not settled anywhere in the book, so do not play the gap.** What Hockey Canada writes first is that leaving while a fight is on draws a game misconduct. What it will not assess for this offence is a **major**, which 10.4(b) rules out outright. ⚠️ **And under Hockey Canada you do not have to leave the bench at all to be ejected.** Its Interpretation 8 prices a player who *"intervenes as a peacemaker in the fight while still on the bench"* at a bench minor plus a game misconduct for Interference from the Bench under Rule 8.4(a) and (e), **plus a further game misconduct** under 7.10(e)(ii) — that player being *"classified as the third player to enter a fight."*
- **So the four books catch different sets of players, and the NHL and the IIHF stop at two only on the *players'* bench.** ⚠️ **Out of the penalty box those two books catch everybody, and that is the sentence to carry.** Both write the same paragraph twice, one word apart — the NHL's, at 70.4 and again at 70.6, is *"[a]ny penalized player leaving the **penalty bench** during a stoppage of play and during an altercation shall incur a minor penalty plus a game misconduct penalty"*, and the IIHF's, at its own 70.4 and 70.6 and identical in both editions, writes *"**penalty box**"* for the same place — with **no first-or-second limit in either**, and the NHL's 70.10 then adds a suspension in the same shape: *"Any player who leaves the penalty bench illegally during an altercation and **is not the first player to do so**, shall be suspended automatically without pay for the next"* five *"regular League and/or Play-off games"*. **Not the second — any of them.** So the third player out of the box is not somebody else's problem, and neither is the fourth. USA Hockey reaches every player who leaves during *any* altercation, at the top tier — a major plus a game misconduct — whether or not they do anything once they are out there. Hockey Canada's opening sentence also reaches every player who leaves, but only during a *fight*, and at a game misconduct. The NHL and the IIHF write their automatic game misconduct for the first or second player off. **Under none of the four is stepping onto the ice a neutral act while an altercation is going on.**
- **The penalty box is the limb worth memorising, because it catches ordinary players rather than fighters.** Under the **NHL** and the **IIHF**, *"[a]ny penalized player leaving the penalty bench during a stoppage of play and during an altercation shall incur a minor penalty plus a game misconduct penalty"* (NHL 70.4, restated at 70.6; IIHF 70.4, again writing *"penalty box"*). Under **USA Hockey** the same act is 629(a)'s major plus game misconduct. **Hockey Canada is harsher again for the player who was already serving time:** its Interpretation 6 to Rule 10.4 says that where the first player to leave a bench during a fight comes from the penalty bench, *"they will be assessed three minor penalties, plus a Game Misconduct penalty, plus any other penalties they may be assessed under the rules"* — the double minor and game misconduct for being first off the bench, plus a further minor for leaving before the penalty had expired. ⚠️ **That interpretation is printed under the heading *"Rule 10.4 (d)"*, as is Interpretation 5 before it — and 10.4(d) is the clause saying that no misconduct penalty may be assessed for leaving a bench, which is not what either interpretation is about. Both are quoted here under the number the book prints; what they interpret is 10.4(e).** **And a USA Hockey game misconduct does not end with tonight** — Rule 404(b) suspends you for *"the team's next game"* as well. The ladder is in [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight).
