- **What the rule does not catch.** USA Hockey 629(a) protects a change that was already under way: *"Substitutions made prior to the altercation shall not be penalized under this rule provided the players so substituting do not enter the altercation."* So a line that changed before it started is not caught — as long as they stay out of it. Everything after that point is the offence.
- **And keep this rule well away from the one it can be confused with.** Everything above is about a bench emptying during an altercation, and it is entirely a question of the penalty sheet. ⚠️ **A player lying on the ice after a hit is a different situation with a different instruction, and nothing here bears on it** — there the answer is medical, and it is to leave them where they are, leave the helmet on and get help. See [If it happens anyway](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice).

### Helmets in an altercation — keep yours on, and never touch theirs

```facts
Rule: USA Hockey 615(c) is a match penalty "(for all age classifications)" for deliberately removing your own helmet/facemask "prior to an altercation", and a second one for removing an opponent's "prior to or during" one
Rule: USA Hockey's Casebook applies that match penalty to your own helmet removed during an altercation too (Rule 615, Situation 14), and adds "Fighting penalties do not have to be assessed when applying Rule 615(c)"
Rule: USA Hockey counts as an altercation "[a]ny physical interaction between two or more opposing players where at least one penalty assessed" (Casebook, Rule 615, Situation 21) — a post-whistle shove drawing one minor is enough
Rule: USA Hockey 615(c) also assesses a game misconduct with an express two-game suspension to a player "whose actions during an altercation causes the removal of an opponent's helmet and facemask"
Rule: USA Hockey 615(c) assesses a further game misconduct where your own helmet comes off because it was not worn as designed — Casebook Situation 19 measures a loose chin strap at more than one finger between strap and jaw
Rule: Hockey Canada makes it a Gross Misconduct — ordered to the dressing room and reported to the league (Rule 10.6(f), with 4.9(a)) — to intentionally remove your own or another player's helmet before or during a fight, or to undo either chinstrap so it comes off
Rule: The NHL and the IIHF are the lenient pair — a minor for unsportsmanlike conduct for removing your own helmet before a fight, and no penalty at all for helmets that come off in the course of it (NHL 46.6; IIHF 46.12 in 2025/26, 46.6 in 2026/27)
Rule: The roughing minor for removing an opponent's helmet is the NHL's, the IIHF's and the PWHL's and is written for removal "during play" (NHL 51.1 and 51.2 via Rule 9.6; IIHF 51.1 and 51.2; PWHL the same via its own Rule 9.6) — it is not the rule for a helmet that comes off in a fight
Rule: Hockey Canada 10.6(d) reaches the gesture on its own — a Misconduct penalty for a player who "undoes their chinstrap to incite or challenge an opponent but does not actually participate in a fight"
Never: Take your own helmet off, or reach for an opponent's, in an altercation. Gloves on and helmet on instead — skate away and let the officials sort it out
```

**This is one of the widest four-book divergences anywhere in this document, and under one of the books it does not need a fight to catch you.** Most players know the televised version: helmets come off in NHL fights constantly and nothing happens. That is a real rule, and it is one pair of books' rule only.

- **USA Hockey starts at a match penalty, and says so for every age group.** Rule 615(c) assesses one *"(for all age classifications)"* against *"any player who deliberately removes their helmet/facemask prior to an altercation"*, and a second against *"any player who deliberately removes their opponent's helmet/facemask prior to or during an altercation."* It then adds **a game misconduct carrying an express two-game suspension** for *"any player whose actions during an altercation causes the removal of an opponent's helmet and facemask"*, and a further game misconduct for *"any player who doesn't wear their helmet/facemask as designed and the helmet/facemask is removed during an altercation."* A match penalty is not a five-minute inconvenience — you are gone, and a hearing follows.
- **And USA Hockey's Casebook widens it twice, in the directions that reach ordinary players rather than fighters.** First, the printed clause reaches *your own* helmet only *"prior to"* an altercation; **Rule 615, Situation 14 applies the match penalty *during* one as well** — *"any time a player involved in that altercation deliberately removes their own helmet, a match penalty must be assessed"* — and states plainly that *"[f]ighting penalties do not have to be assessed when applying Rule 615(c)."* Second, the **Note applying to Situations 14-20** says *"an altercation does not need to be considered a fight for Rule 615(c) to be applied. This rule would apply in any altercation where at least one player will receive a penalty"*, and **Situation 21** defines an altercation as *"[a]ny physical interaction between two or more opposing players where at least one penalty assessed."* ⚠️ **So the trigger is one penalty, not one punch.** A post-whistle shove that gets somebody two minutes is an altercation, and pulling your own lid off in that moment is a match penalty on its own.
- **The rule reaches you even when you did nothing deliberate — which is why the chin strap matters.** Casebook **Situation 16** has a player whose chin strap was not properly fastened lose their helmet in the ordinary course of an altercation, with no deliberate act by anyone: *"A game misconduct penalty to Player A."* **Situation 12** answers the same way where it simply cannot be determined how the helmets came off. And **Situation 19** gives the measure: *"If the space between the player's lower jaw and the chin strap allows for more than one finger to be inserted into that space, then the chin strap is not being properly worn."* Do it up.
- **Hockey Canada files the same act under equipment rather than fighting, and still ends your night.** Its roughing and fighting rules, 7.9 and 7.10, say nothing about helmets; **Rule 10.6(f)** does, and the tier is a **Gross Misconduct** — under Rule 4.9(a) an order to the dressing room for the rest of the game and a report to the league, with an automatic suspension on top under 4.9(b) if it happens in the last ten minutes, in overtime or after the game. It reaches a player who *"intentionally removes their helmet before or during a fight OR undoes their chinstrap before or during a fight and that helmet subsequently comes off"*, and the same for *"another player's"*. **10.6(d) reaches the gesture on its own**: a misconduct for a player who *"undoes their chinstrap to incite or challenge an opponent but does not actually participate in a fight."* Where nobody acted deliberately, **Interpretation 15** splits it three ways — nothing for an incidental removal, a double minor under Rule 7.1 for grabbing a helmet without gaining an advantage, and a **match penalty** under 7.1(c)(iv) for grabbing one and using it *"to gain an advantage or to inflict punishment"*.
- **The NHL and the IIHF are the pair that price it lowest, and they are the pair on television.** **NHL Rule 46.6** and **IIHF Rule 46.12** — renumbered **46.6** in the 2026/27 IIHF book — both read that no player may remove their own helmet before engaging in a fight, on pain of *"a minor penalty for unsportsmanlike conduct"*, and both then add the sentence that produces the folklore — the NHL's wording, which the IIHF carries in the same words bar its capital *P*: *"Helmets that come off in the course of and resulting from the altercation will not result in a penalty to either player."* **That sentence has no counterpart in USA Hockey's book**, where the same helmet coming off is a game misconduct for somebody. **Hockey Canada does reach the same result, but only for a genuinely incidental removal**: its Interpretation 15 to Rule 10.6(f) opens *"[i]f neither player intentionally removes a helmet or undoes a chinstrap prior to or during a fight but the helmets come off during the fight"*, and answers that where the referee judges the helmets came off incidentally, *"no further penalties should be assessed."* **A chin strap that was simply not fastened properly is the case the two North American books split on** — it is nowhere in that interpretation, and it is a game misconduct under USA Hockey 615(c). Which IIHF edition binds a British player is the same unresolved question as everywhere else in this section, but here it does not matter: the tier is a minor either way, and the 2026/27 text differs only in spelling it *"a two minute minor penalty"*.
- **And the roughing minor you may have heard of is a different rule about a different moment.** NHL 51.1 and 51.2 — and IIHF 51.1 and 51.2, identical in both editions — make it roughing, a minor, to intentionally remove *an opponent's* helmet **during play**, routed through each book's Rule 9.6. That is the open-ice snatch, not the scrum. **Do not carry it into an altercation, and do not carry it into USA Hockey or Hockey Canada at all**, whose roughing rules contain no helmet clause. The four-book comparison is in [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md).
- **One thing this section is not about, and the two must not run together.** Everything above concerns a helmet that comes off in a scrum, and what it costs on the penalty sheet. A player lying on the ice after a hit is a different situation entirely, and there the instruction is medical rather than disciplinary: ⚠️ **leave the helmet on, do not move them, get help.** See [If it happens anyway](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice).
### Head-butting — and the net-front push that counts as one

```facts
Rule: Head-butting is not a scrum technicality under USA Hockey — Rule 619(a) assesses "a major plus a game misconduct penalty" for head-butting an opponent, and 619(b) adds that "[a] match penalty for reckless endangerment may also be assessed for head-butting."
Rule: IIHF Rule 47.1 calls head-butting "making deliberate contact, or attempting to make contact, with an opponent by leading with their head and/or helmet, whether contact is made or not" — a double minor for an attempt (47.2), a major plus automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment (47.3)
Rule: Hockey Canada files head-butting under attempt to injure — a double minor at 7.1(a)(iii) for a minimal degree of violence, and a match penalty at 7.1(c)(i) for "[h]ead-butting an opponent with force, or injuring an opponent with this action"
Rule: Hockey Canada writes the scrum case into its head-butting double minor — Interpretation 1 to Rule 7.1(a) gives "helmet-to-helmet or facial-protector-to-facial-protector contact, without the use of excessive force" as its example
Rule: EIHL Casebook Rule 47 gives the head-butting double minor two examples of its own, Elite League only — a double minor for "a player who fakes a head butt at an opponent", and for one who "forces his head, helmet or visor and pushes his head with forces against an opponent’s head, helmet, or visor"
Never: Put your head or helmet into an opponent’s at the net front or in a scrum — the EIHL reaches a faked head-butt, and under USA Hockey head-butting is an ejection rather than four minutes
```

**This is here because of where it happens.** Head-butting sounds like something out of a fight, and the tariffs below are written as though it were. **The act the books actually describe is a push, delivered with the head, by two players standing chest to chest** — which is the net front in §9, the corner in §8 and the shove-fest after a whistle. You do not have to swing anything or drop a glove to arrive at it.

- **Start with the book most amateurs are under, because it has no low tier at all.** **USA Hockey Rule 619** defines head-butting in its Note as *"the physical use of one's head in the course of delivering a body check (head first) in the chest, head, neck or back area or the physical use of the head to strike an opponent"*, and then **619(a)**: *"A major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed for head-butting an opponent."* **619(b)** adds that *"[a] match penalty for reckless endangerment may also be assessed for head-butting."* ⚠️ **There is no double minor in that rule.** A head-to-head shove that costs four minutes in one book ends your game in this one, and a USA Hockey game misconduct does not end with tonight — the ladder is in [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight).
- **The IIHF's definition is the widest of the four, and it does not need contact.** **Rule 47.1**: *"The act of head-butting involves a Player making deliberate contact, or attempting to make contact, with an opponent by leading with their head and/or helmet, whether contact is made or not."* **47.2** assesses a double minor, at the referee's discretion, *"to any Player who attempts to 'head-butt' an opponent"*, and **47.3** a major plus an automatic game misconduct where the player *"recklessly endangers their opponent"*, judged on *"severity of the infraction, severity of the contact, the degree of violence and the general reprehensibility involved."* **47.4** leaves supplementary discipline open. The rule is identical in the 2025/26 (Version 1.1) and 2026/27 editions, so the edition question that runs through the fighting section above does not arise here. ⚠️ **What the IIHF does not do is give an example** — its rule names the tiers and stops — so a British reader working from the rule book alone has no picture of what the double minor looks like.
- **Hockey Canada supplies one, and files the whole offence somewhere a reader would not look for it.** It is not in the fighting or roughing rules but under **Rule 7.1, Attempt to Injure or Deliberate Injury**. **7.1(a)(iii)** puts a player who *"head-butts or attempts to head-butt an opponent"* among the acts drawing a **double minor** where done *"with a minimal degree of violence and without using such an action to gain an advantage or inflict punishment or injury"*, and **7.1(c)(i)** raises it to a **match penalty** for *"[h]ead-butting an opponent with force, or injuring an opponent with this action."* ⚠️ **Its Interpretation 1 to Rule 7.1(a) is the sentence to carry onto the ice**, because it describes the net front rather than a fight: examples of the double minor *"could include helmet-to-helmet or facial-protector-to-facial-protector contact, without the use of excessive force"*, with *"[t]he degree of force"* the factor deciding between the double minor and the match penalty. **So the cage-to-cage lean that feels like nothing is the rule's own example of the offence.**
- **The EIHL writes two examples of its own, and they are the Elite League's alone.** The **EIHL Casebook 26-27** supersedes the IIHF where it speaks, and under **Rule 47** it prints *"Examples of a double minor"*: *"a player who fakes a head butt at an opponent"*, and *"a player that forces his head, helmet or visor and pushes his head with forces against an opponent's head, helmet, or visor."* ⚠️ **The first of those is the one worth knowing, because it prices a gesture at four minutes** — and the second is a scrum, not a fight. *(Quoted as printed; the second example's grammar is the Casebook's.)* **This is one British competition. It is not the NIHL's rule, the SNL's, BUIHA's, the junior or women's game's, or any recreational league's** — those are under IIHF Rule 47 unmodified. ⚠️ **And do not read the tier across with the examples, because the IIHF does not write a double minor for what the EIHL's second example describes.** IIHF **47.2** assesses its double minor only for an *attempt* — *"assess a double-minor penalty to any Player who attempts to 'head-butt' an opponent"* — while the Casebook's second example is a **completed** forceful push of head, helmet or visor against an opponent's. **For a completed head-butt the IIHF book writes no double-minor tier at all**; what the referee has is **47.3**, a major plus an automatic game misconduct. **Hockey Canada is the book that genuinely does cover both at the lower tier**, because 7.1(a)(iii) reaches a player who *"head-butts or attempts to head-butt an opponent"* in terms. See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).
- **The practical version is the same instruction the rest of this document gives, applied to your head.** In a scrum or at the net front, keep your feet under you, keep your gloves on and keep your head out of theirs. **Pushing with your head is never the cheap option, and none of these books stops at four minutes.** The double minor exists only at the bottom of two of them — Hockey Canada's, for minimal violence, and the EIHL's, for its two printed examples. **Under USA Hockey there is no lower tier at all: head-butting is a major plus a game misconduct, and a match penalty may be added. Under the IIHF the double minor is written for an attempt, and a completed head-butt sits at 47.3 — a major plus an automatic game misconduct. Under Hockey Canada, force makes it a match penalty.** The stance that wins the net front is in [Defending the net front](#defending-the-net-front), and it is made of your feet and your hips.

---

## Common Mistakes

- **Assuming your league's contact rules instead of checking them.** The same age group can be checking in one association and non-checking in the next town. **And getting it wrong is not capped at two minutes:** where checking is barred, USA Hockey 604 runs minor, major plus game misconduct, match — and Rule 604 is on Rule 411's aggressive-infractions list, so a third major in a season costs three further games. Hockey Canada 7.3 is the same shape, with the major mandatory on injury. And a checking team playing a non-checking team means nobody checks.

  **Under USA Hockey, clearing 14U does not settle it** — Rule 604(a)'s own next sentence provides that *"[a] local governing body may prohibit body checking in any classification"*, so your association may have made your class non-check whatever the national age list says, and 604(b) makes any fixture against a non-checking team a no-checking game whatever your own class is. **And one USA Hockey prohibition is not in Rule 604 at all and has no age limb** — body checking is *"prohibited during all Blind Hockey games"*, penalised *"under the guidelines within Rule 604"*, on rosters the book allows to be all-adult, so reading yourself off the age list misses it. **Under Hockey Canada, clearing U13 does not settle it either** — Rule 7.3 also applies in "any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada," and the playing rules do not say which those are. **In Britain this is not answerable from a rulebook at all** — neither the IIHF Rule Book nor the In-House Rules sets a body-checking age. It is in your competition's Rules of Competition instead: IHUK's put the junior checking line between U12 and U14, so look yours up rather than assuming Britain copies USA Hockey's 12U line or Hockey Canada's U13 line. ⚠️ **And "IHUK runs my league" is not the test** — only four of IHUK's *Rules of Competition* were read here (junior, U10, NIHL 1 and 2, WNIHL), while IHUK's In-House Rules legislate by name for NIHL National, the SNL and BUIHA Varsity, which none of the four covers. Until you have an answer, do not hit anyone. **One adult book does answer it, and the answer is no:** the CARHA rule book has no checking division, Rule 49(a) makes a body check a minor or a major anywhere on the ice and a major where it injures, Rule 30(a) rules that major off the ice for the rest of the game with Rule 32(d) adding an automatic one-game suspension, and Rule 32(a) ejects you on three penalties in one game, tallying any combination of minors, bench minors and majors — with Rule 49(b)'s Note counting a boarding double minor as two of those three, so a boarding call plus one more minor is your night. CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only. Ask your league.
- **Treating the warm-up as off the record.** Skate it as though the game had started, because nothing you do there is free. It is inside the rules in every book here, and only the route differs. **USA Hockey 502(b)** allows penalties *"during the warm-up period"*; **Hockey Canada 2.2(j)** has them *"served at the start of the game"*, so your team opens short-handed; **CARHA 27(b)** assesses them where the Referee sees the offence. ⚠️ **The IIHF's Rule 5.6 reads like an amnesty and is not one.** Its *"No penalties are to be assessed but a report of the incident must be submitted to the Proper Authorities"* is the enforcement clause for that rule's own warm-up terms, and **a warm-up fight is penalised on the night anyway** — a major plus an automatic game misconduct under Rule 46.8 in the 2026/27 book, numbered 46.9 in 2025/26. For the warm-up conduct the fighting rule does not reach, the route is the report, which is supplementary discipline rather than a let-off. **And in the EIHL the warm-up is a non-contact period by rule**, under Casebook 86.6(i), with the head coach exposed to a suspension or fine alongside the player. That last one is the Elite League only.
- **Thinking "non-contact" means nobody touches you.** It does not — but the four books do not permit the same amount of it, so learn yours. **USA Hockey** is the widest: angling, physical engagement, standing your ground and *"simply maintaining established body position"* are all named as legal competitive contact, and it says the game *"can be very physical."* **IIHF Rule 101.1 — its women's-hockey rule, not its general one** — has two players in pursuit of the puck *"reasonably allowed to push and lean into each other provided that 'possession of the puck' remains the sole object of the two"* Players; holding ice you have established is protected for everyone by the general Rule 56.1. **Hockey Canada is narrower** — Rule 7.3 defines legal body contact as *"incidental contact of two opposing players in pursuit of the puck on the ice in the same direction"*, its Interpretation 1 permits steering *"without actually touching"*, and **7.3(a)** writes a minor — **but not for anyone, and the scope is inside the same sentence**: *"A Minor penalty will be assessed to any player who, in the opinion of the Referee, intentionally body-checks, bumps, shoves, or pushes any opposing player, **in divisions of U13 and below, female hockey, and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada**."* That is the same list of divisions the checking table above gives, so a Hockey Canada player above the U13 line in a division nobody has restricted is not reading their own rule there. **And a fifth book is narrower still** — CARHA's Rule 49(a) penalises a player who *"intentionally bodies, pushes, shoves, stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent"*, which is a duty to get out of the way and not merely a ban on initiating one; what keeps a battle legal there is **49(c)**, under which *"[w]here, in the opinion of the Referee, accidental contact has taken place, no penalty shall be assessed"*. ⚠️ **But standing your ground is protected in that book too** — Note 2 to its Rule 66(a) entitles defending players *"to 'stand their ground' or 'shadow' the attacking players"*, so the line runs between holding a spot you took and leaning or bodying to keep it. CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only. Players who expect a bubble get knocked off the puck all night; players who assume the widest book is theirs sit in the box.
- **Skating with your head down.** Get your eyes up before you arrive at the puck, not as you get there — you cannot brace for a hit you cannot see, and a body that is surprised does not absorb contact. USA Hockey teaches this as Rule One of *Heads Up Hockey* — *"Keep your head up on the ice, especially when it looks like you're going to take a hit"* — and calls it *"the single most important thing to know and do to prevent head injuries"*, which is their ranking of the habit rather than of any one moment. **And three of the six books read here protect you less for it.** Under **IIHF Rule 48.1** a square check with no upward motion on a puck carrier skating head down **toward the checker** is not penalised as an illegal check to the head at all. The NHL and the PWHL stop short of that, but neither ignores your posture: **NHL Rule 48.1(ii)** and **PWHL Rule 48.3(i)** each ask whether the opponent assumed *"a posture that made head contact on an otherwise full body check unavoidable"* — a factor weighed rather than a bar, and under the PWHL one that cuts a major to a minor on video review. **That direction limb is part of the rule, so a head-down player hit from the side or the blind side is outside the carve-out**, and the checker is still obliged not to target your head. **The sentence immediately before the carve-out in that rule states the half that runs in your favour:** where a player is skating with their head up and may reasonably be expecting contact, *"an opponent does not have the right to hit them to the head or neck."* Seeing the hit coming is not consent to be hit in the head. **No such exception was found in USA Hockey's Playing Rules, nor anywhere in Hockey Canada's rules, in any division** — Rule 7.6 and Rule 7.7 both, with their Interpretations — **and none in CARHA's Rule 50 either.** Carrying the puck head down is not the same as **ducking** a check, which has its own rules and its own penalty for the player who ducks.
- **Checking a player who has already passed or shot.** Once the puck has left them, take the puck or take the ice they are skating into — do not finish the hit. Common, and **not capped at two minutes**. NHL 56.2(iii) writes a minor for a deliberate check on *"an opponent, including the goalkeeper, who is not in possession of the puck"*, and USA Hockey 640(b) writes one too — written *"except Adult Male Classifications"*, where 640 Note 2 reaches it as roughing instead. Neither is the ceiling, and **Hockey Canada 8.3(b) reaches a major plus a game misconduct on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* with nobody hurt** — 8.3 also puts a measurable distance on it, an arm and stick length at the instant the puck is released, and **USA Hockey's Casebook writes the same measure** — though there it is one of four conditions rather than the whole test, so only under Hockey Canada does the gap decide it on its own. The NHL's window is *"rendered immediately following his loss of possession"* (56.1) and nothing wider; the IIHF adds a second condition on top of that — you must also still be inside an *"objectively reasonable window of opportunity"* (56.1) — and calls a late hit *"reckless endangerment"*, which 56.5 takes to a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt. ⚠️ **The window is not the whole of that book’s test, either:** a check on an opponent who has lost the puck and either has not seen you coming or is not set for it is *“automatically considered reckless”* however legal it would be on a non-vulnerable opponent (IIHF glossary, *Vulnerable Position*).

  **Under USA Hockey the window is the narrowest of the four**: 640(b) makes a minor of every *avoidable* check on a player who has released a shot or pass — the rule says they *"are not eligible to be body checked"*, and its Casebook — offering a *"general rule of thumb"* rather than a test — allows one to be finished where the checker made a prior effort to gain possession, was an arm's length plus stick length away when the puck was dumped, had the check already in the process of being delivered, and *"minimize[s] the force used in this instance and [does] not take advantage of a vulnerable or defenseless opponent"* — four conditions, though ⚠️ **the passage numbers none of them**, and the last of the four is a separate sentence carrying two obligations at once (Standard of Play, Situation 10). ⚠️ **Those four govern finishing a check and are not the whole passage** — it gives a second route to legal contact, where the defender simply *"maintains their normal skating lane and reasonable foot speed"* and *"the obligation is on the attacking player to go around the defender"*, forfeited the moment that defender stops or changes lane to cut the opponent off. It rules lateness out in terms: *"It is not acceptable to skate one or two more strides after the puck is gone and then decide to deliver the check."* **And USA Hockey's own tier is written into Rule 640**: 640(g) is a major plus a game misconduct where an action under 640(b, c, d, e or f) *"recklessly endangers an opponent"*, and 640(h) a match penalty. Both reach the late hit only through (b), the sub-section excepting adult men, so whether they reach adult men's hockey the book does not say — but 602(a)'s match penalty for a player who *"recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player"* is tied to no named foul at all and does not depend on the answer. **And Rule 640 is on Rule 411(a)'s aggressive-infractions list**, so a 640(g) major counts on the season tally too — a third major costs three further games, a fourth five, and a fifth a suspension until a hearing. Check the carrier, or take the ice they need.
- **Finishing a check into a player who is facing the wall, or hitting anyone from behind.** Take the puck, take the ice, or angle them off it — when their numbers are toward you the hit is off, whatever you had lined up. There is **no minor-penalty option** for checking from behind in the NHL, the IIHF or the PWHL (NHL Rule 43.2; IIHF Rule 43.2; PWHL Rule 43.2) — the lightest call available there is a major with an automatic game misconduct, anywhere on the ice. **Hockey Canada does have a minor for it, but never on its own:** Hockey Canada Rule 7.5(a) assesses a minor *and* a game misconduct together, so no book here lets you off with two minutes. **USA Hockey's floor is the lowest of the six and still not a bare minor** — 608(a) writes *"A minor plus a misconduct penalty, or a major plus a game misconduct penalty"*, so the lightest outcome there puts you in the box rather than out of the game. ⚠️ **And one more book has no rung like that at all.** **CARHA Rule 53(a)** — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — writes its floor as *"[a] Minor penalty plus a Game Misconduct"*, so under CARHA checking from behind takes you out of the game even at its lightest, and 53(b) is a flat match penalty where the player driven from behind into the boards or goal net could not protect themselves. **The onus is stated in the two minor-hockey books and nowhere else on the shelf** — USA Hockey Rule 608 (*"[t]he onus is on the player delivering the check to not hit from behind"*) and Hockey Canada Rule 7.5 (*"[i]n all circumstances the responsibility is on the player delivering the check to avoid"* contact with the back); NHL Rule 43, IIHF Rule 43 and PWHL Rule 43 state none, and the only sentence in CARHA's book carrying the word is about maintaining your equipment. And **"he turned at the last second" is a defence only under the NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL, and only for a deliberate turn** — NHL Rule 43.1 waives the penalty for a player who *"intentionally turns his body to create contact with his back"*, and IIHF Rule 43.1 for one who *"intentionally turns their back towards an opponent and puts themself in a vulnerable position"*, adding that other penalties may still be assessed; **PWHL Rule 43.1** writes the NHL's version in the feminine — *"When a player intentionally turns her body to create contact with her back, no penalty shall be assessed."* **USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and CARHA write no such waiver** — nor does USA Hockey's separate Casebook, whose Rule 608 situations attach no exception for a player who turned — and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.5(a) puts that exact question — what happens when a player about to be checked turns and creates contact with the back — and answers that the penalty goes to the checker, because *"[t]he responsibility is always on the player delivering the check **to avoid making contact with the back**."* **Read the object, because it is what the duty is about** — the book states a specific duty about the back, not strict liability for every check; [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) sets out how far an onus clause does and does not go. **CARHA has no waiver either:** its Rule 53(a) prices an intentional push, body check or hit from behind *"anywhere on the ice"*, with no turn exception of any kind.
- **Assuming checking from behind, head contact, charging and boarding are scoped to checking leagues like body checking is.** They are not, and the four instructions are the same whatever division you are in: never hit from behind, never finish a check into a player whose numbers are turned to the wall, never make contact with an opponent's head, face or neck, and arrive with speed you already had rather than speed you built for the hit. **All four bind every division, yours included** — the division scope is in the body-checking rules, Hockey Canada 7.3 and USA Hockey 604, and in none of these four. Boarding (7.2, 603), charging (7.4, 607) and checking from behind (7.5, 608) carry no division clause at all, and where Hockey Canada does write head contact by division it writes it twice so that both halves are covered — 7.6(a) for minor and female hockey, 7.7 for Junior and Senior — each opening by making it the player's responsibility to avoid an opponent's head, face or neck *"at all times"*.
- **Reading "above the knees" as the only limit that matters, and getting lower than your opponent to hit them.** Hit from your own height, not from under theirs — if you have to drop your body to reach them you have lost the hit, so take the ice instead. Lowering your body position to check at or below an opponent's knees is **clipping** — NHL and IIHF Rule 44.1 in the same words, USA Hockey Rule 639, and Hockey Canada Rule 8.7, which draws the line at the *hips* and puts the onus on the player lowering themselves. **And your knee arriving first is a separate foul again:** under **Hockey Canada 7.8** kneeing reaches contact with an opponent's knee *"on an otherwise legal hit"*, at a **double** minor, and **USA Hockey 628(a)** is a minor **plus a misconduct** — so neither of those two books gives you a bare minor for it. Neither foul is scoped to a division in any of the four books.
- **Treating boarding as a foul that stops at two minutes — or as something you can only do with your body.** Along the wall, play the puck and ease off as their numbers turn toward you — and a trip or a push can put a player into the boards just as a check can. ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey it is also a stick foul:** Rule 603's Note reaches a player who *"pushes, trips or body checks"* an opponent dangerously into the boards, and its Casebook at Rule 603, Situation 3 says that where a trip sends an opponent into the end boards, *"[e]ven though a tripping penalty may also be appropriate, the boarding call is preferred to draw attention to the more aggressive infraction."* The other three books define boarding by the check alone (NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1, Hockey Canada 7.2). **The minor is the base tier in three books** — NHL 41.2 leaves it to the referee *"based on the degree of violence of the impact with the boards"* and IIHF 41.2 does the same on the degree of *"force and violence"*, and Hockey Canada 7.2(a) writes one — **but none of the four caps it there.** **USA Hockey 603(a) writes no bare minor at all** — it reads *"A minor plus a misconduct or major plus game misconduct penalty shall be assessed for boarding an opponent"*; **Hockey Canada 7.2(b)** reaches a major plus a game misconduct *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"* **with nobody hurt**, and makes that same pair *mandatory* where a board that would otherwise be a minor injures; and under the IIHF book — the British one — **41.4 reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt at all**. **Three of the four reach a match penalty** — NHL 41.4, USA Hockey 603(c), Hockey Canada 7.2(c) — and the IIHF is the exception because its book writes none.
- **Treating "the onus is on the checker" as a rule of strict liability — in either direction.** Whichever way the rule cuts, do the same two things: give a turned player the ice, and never turn into a hit. As the checker, it does not mean every collision with a turned player is automatically yours — **in four of the six boarding rules read here**: NHL Rule 41.1 states the onus and then says the circumstances, including whether the opponent made himself vulnerable immediately before the check and whether the check was unavoidable, "can be considered," and that "this balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule." **IIHF Rule 41.1 — the British reader's — Hockey Canada Rule 7.2 and PWHL Rule 41.1 write the same however-clause**, and Hockey Canada's carries no division scope. **Two books do not write it: USA Hockey Rule 603 states the onus and stops, and CARHA Rule 49 writes neither an onus sentence nor a balancing clause.** **Do not carry that qualification across to checking from behind.** The NHL's checking-from-behind rule (43) states no onus at all, and the books that do state one for that offence — USA Hockey 608, and Hockey Canada's, which says "in all circumstances" — attach no such balancing clause. As the player being hit, it does not mean turning your back buys you a power play: NHL, IIHF and PWHL Rule 43.1 all waive the checking-from-behind penalty when the turn was intentional — outright in the NHL's book and the PWHL's, while the IIHF adds that other penalties may still be assessed. Neither point changes what you should do. But a player who thinks the rule is absolute is surprised by the call either way.
- **Turning your back to the boards when contact is coming.** It feels protective. It gives the checker your unprotected spine, puts your head first into the boards, and makes the contact a hit from behind — which USA Hockey warns could inflict a severe head or neck injury even when the hit is light. **Get skates parallel to the wall and take it on the forearm and hip** — that is the target orientation, and **head up with your chin off your chest** goes with it however you meet the wall. Squaring your chest to the boards is the Case 3 fallback for when you cannot turn, not something to aim for.
- **Arriving at the boards perpendicular, on the point of your shoulder, or upright with your feet together.** Go in on an angle, get your skates parallel, feet apart and knees bent, and meet the wall on your forearm and hip. **Never the point of the shoulder.** USA Hockey lists *"[s]tanding too upright with your feet together (not having a low center of gravity)"* as a Common Error of its own; it writes *never* for the shoulder tip, and its checklist for taking a check asks for *"No shoulder contact on the boards, if possible"* for the whole shoulder. If you are stuck facing the wall, get your gloves and stick up and spread out on the glass rather than taking it on one arm.
- **Angling at the player instead of at the ice.** If you skate at the body, a good carrier changes the puck to their far side and goes around you. Skate at the space.
- **Angling flat across the ice.** Take the angle so your momentum is heading back toward your own net rather than sideways across it — without that, one pass leaves you facing the wrong way.
- **Veering into them at the last instant to finish the angle.** The contact can be light and it is still a body check, because the route is part of the test. Hockey Canada's test is met *"when a player leaves their skating lane to make contact"*, and USA Hockey's Casebook clears the angler only where they use no *"overt hip, shoulder or forearm action"* and *"simply maintains their skating lane"* (Standard of Play, Situation 15). Pick the line early and hold it — and do not add an arm, shoulder or hip once you arrive.
- **Committing your feet on a poke check.** Extend from the shoulder rather than lunging with your body — poke, recover, stay skating. A missed poke with your weight forward means you are out of the play.
- **Never learning the stick lift.** Ask to be shown it, and use it in place of the shove. Lower-risk than a poke or a check when the lift is taken up rather than sideways and below the bottom hand, and most amateurs have simply never been shown it.
- **Taking the lift on an opponent bent low over the puck as if it were any other lift.** Take that one under the shaft, or leave it alone. Their face is down at stick height, and a lift that misses and catches it is high-sticking **however low your own hands were** — the reference is the struck player's waist, not yours (NHL Rule 60.3; IIHF Rule 60.3 in the 2026/27 book; IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27, Situation 60.13). **And it is not capped at the double minor:** IIHF 60.4 and USA Hockey 621(b) both reach a major plus a game misconduct for recklessly endangering an opponent, with nobody injured. **And a fifth book starts above the double minor rather than below it** — CARHA Rule 62(b), in CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, is a **major** for contact above an opponent's shoulders whether the stick got there accidentally or not and whether or not anyone is injured, with no minor rung above the shoulders and no windup carve-out anywhere in the book. ⚠️ **And a major there is usually the end of your night:** its Note adds a **game misconduct** where the high stick was intentional, or where an accidental one injures, and only an accidental one that injures nobody leaves you a major alone.
- **Cross-checking at the net front to create space.** An enforcement emphasis officials are specifically told to watch for — but not, as the folklore has it, the most-called penalty there. In 400 tracked games it is the **smallest of the seven infraction types broken out** in the net-front box, at 5.7% of the 265 penalties that fell inside that box out of 2,967 in the sample — essentially its league-wide rate (see §9). The stick fouls that really concentrate there are hooking and slashing. Tie the stick instead. And don't rely on the "blade on the ice" folklore — that phrase is USA Hockey Rule 609's alone and is in no other book here. **Nor is a cross-check capped at two minutes in any book here, and no major needs an injury** — NHL 59.3 turns on *"the severity of the contact"* with an automatic game misconduct at 59.5 and a match penalty at 59.4; IIHF 59.3 reaches a major plus an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment; USA Hockey 609(b) and 609(c) reach the same two tiers; Hockey Canada 9.2(b) has both limbs: the referee may assess a major plus a game misconduct *"based on the degree of violence of impact"* with nobody hurt, and the same pair *"will be assessed"* where the cross-check injures, with a match penalty at 9.2(c) — and its Interpretation 2 to 9.2(b) sends a cross-check above the shoulders out to Rule 7.6 Head Contact as a major plus game misconduct that *"must be assessed"*. **And under CARHA — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — the net-front case has no discretion in it at all:** 54(c) assesses a major plus a game misconduct to a player who *"crosschecks a goalkeeper while they are in the goal crease"*, and 54(b) the same for one above the shoulders *"whether or not injury results"*. ⚠️ **And a shaft shove into their numbers is a different foul from the one you are expecting — never put the stick into a back.** NHL 43.3, IIHF 43.1 and PWHL 43.3 name the cross-check inside checking from behind, where none of the three writes a minor rung at all; Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to 7.5(a) and CARHA 53(b) route it there too, and USA Hockey's *Casebook* at Rule 609, Situation 2 puts the line at the force used.
- **Setting the screen with a skate on the crease line.** Clear of the blue paint is not the same as clear of the crease, and on the line it is your teammate's goal that pays. The IIHF at Rule 1.7, USA Hockey in the Note to Rule 625(b) and Hockey Canada in its Glossary all count the red line itself as part of the crease — and under **USA Hockey 625(b)** *"No goal may be scored with an attacking player in the goal crease unless the puck has preceded the player(s) into the goal crease or the goalkeeper is out of the goal crease area"* — a position bar rather than an impairment test, and neither of those two exceptions is one a screener gets to use, because a screener plants ahead of the shot rather than following the puck in, and plants there precisely because the goaltender is home in their crease. Under **Hockey Canada 8.5** the goal survives only while the crease player does not *"obstruct the Goaltender's view or movements"*, which a screen does by definition. **The NHL's rule text does not say which side of the line the crease starts on** — only that the area is *"outlined by the crease lines"* — **but its Reference Table 14 answers it the other way**, allowing the goal at Situation 5 E to a player who *"plants himself on the crease line or outside the goal crease"* and obstructs the goalkeeper's vision. ⚠️ **That row is printed in both books**, so the IIHF answers the same play in two directions and nothing held here settles it. Standing a skate's width further out costs you nothing in any of the four. [Rink Map](../foundation/rink_map.md) owns the collision.
- **Bracing against the goaltender the way you brace against a defender.** At the net front you take a shove braced — wide base, knees low, giving rather than locked. Against a goalkeeper you do the opposite. Under **NHL and IIHF Rule 69.3**, once a goalkeeper attempting to establish position in the crease has made contact with you, an attacking player who *"does not immediately vacate his current position in the goal crease"* — which the rule itself glosses as *"give ground to the goalkeeper"* — loses any goal scored on the play, and the rule adds a penalty *"whether or not a goal is scored"* — a minor in the NHL's words, *"the appropriate penalty"* in the IIHF's, which caps nothing. **Both books' own reference tables then assess none where the goal went in** — Situation 6 D, *"A minor penalty is not assessed (loss of goal only)"* — and put the penalty in the no-goal case instead, where the NHL writes a minor and the IIHF again *"the appropriate penalty"*. So it is your team's goal or a trip to the box, and neither reading pays you for standing there. **Give ground the moment a goalie leans into you, then re-set the screen outside the paint and off its red line.** ⚠️ **Under the IIHF — Britain's book — Rule 1.7 puts the red line inside the crease, so a screen set on the line is set on the ice where this duty bites.** **And a minor is not those two books' ceiling either.** **Rule 69.2** in each book is a penalty *"(minor or major, as the Referee deems appropriate)"* for deliberate contact *"whether or not the goalkeeper is inside or outside the goal crease, and whether or not a goal is scored"*, so neither the crease line nor the puck going in bounds it — **PWHL 71.2** is the same rule renumbered. **And each book's Rule 42.1 reaches the contact again with no give-ground in it:** *"The appropriate penalty should be assessed in every case where an opposing player makes unnecessary contact with a goalkeeper"* — the NHL's words, the IIHF's the same but for a capital *"Player"* — with the NHL writing *"[a] minor, major or a major and a game misconduct"* there for a charge on a goalkeeper *"while the goalkeeper is within his goal crease"*, and IIHF 42.4 reaching a major plus a game misconduct for reckless endangerment. **And the NHL's own goalkeeper-interference rule carries a game misconduct where the IIHF's does not:** **Rule 69.8** assesses *"an appropriate penalty (minor or major and/or game misconduct)"* to an attacking player who initiates contact with a goalkeeper *"whether inside or outside the crease, in a fashion that would otherwise warrant a penalty"*, on top of supplementary discipline — **PWHL 71.8** carries that sentence word for word, while the IIHF's 69.8 is a bare cross-reference to its own supplementary-discipline rule, so under that book the game misconduct comes through the charging rule above instead. ⚠️ **And the other two books do not price the same shove at a bare minor.** Under USA Hockey **Note 1** to **Rule 607(d)** sends *"[a]ny deliberate body contact or check that is delivered to the goalkeeper"* to **charging**, and USA Hockey **607(c)** prices that at *"[a] minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct"* — *"while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or privileged area"*, which **Note 2** to the same rule makes the whole low zone between the end-zone face-off spots rather than a box around the paint — with **607(e)** adding that a match penalty for reckless endangerment *"may also be assessed for charging."* ⚠️ **And one rung there is not the referee's to weigh:** **607(b)** provides that a major plus a game misconduct *"shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of charging."* **Hockey Canada 8.5(a)** is a minor, but **8.5(b)** reaches a major plus a game misconduct at the referee's discretion *"based on the degree of violence of impact"* with nobody hurt, and mandatorily *"to any player who charges the goaltender"* — and Hockey Canada Rule 7.4 counts a player who *"[v]iolently and unnecessarily checks an opponent in any manner"* as charging, so no stride count is needed to get there. **8.5(c)** is a match penalty for a player who *"attempts to injure or deliberately injures a goaltender by Interference"*, and **8.5(d)** leaves no misconduct rung beneath the major, so there is no ten-minute step between the minor and the ejection. ⚠️ **A fifth book does price the braced shove itself at a minor — and then takes the referee's choice away twice.** **CARHA 66(b)** — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — imposes a minor on an attacking player *"who makes deliberate contact with a goalkeeper whether in or out of the goal crease"*, adding that *"[a]t the discretion of the Referee, a Major penalty may be imposed"*. But **CARHA 52(b)** — the same adult-league book — provides that a major plus a game misconduct *"shall be assessed to any player who charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease"* — and for any charge that injures an opponent, anywhere on the ice — while 52(a)'s Note counts *"more than two steps or strides"* as a charge, so that rung is reachable from the slot rather than from across the zone. And **54(c)** compels the same pair again where the contact is a cross-check *"while they are in the goal crease"*. **Its general body-contact rule reaches the shove as well, with no crease and no charge in it** — **49(a)** prices a player who *"intentionally bodies, pushes, shoves, stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent"* at a minor or a major on *"the intent and the violence of the impact"*, and makes the major mandatory *"[w]hen injury results from intentional body contact"*. **So a player who braces into a goaltender expecting the penalty box can leave the game instead.** ⚠️ **One relief does exist under USA Hockey and it is not the shove itself** — its *Casebook* at Rule 607, Situation 4 assesses no penalty *"[i]n the case where a defending player has forced the attacking player into their own goalkeeper"*, but only *"provided the attacking player has clearly made every attempt to avoid the contact with the goalkeeper."* ⚠️ **And one screening act is a minor with no contact and no crease at all:** a hand, a glove or a stick blade held up in front of the goaltender's face is unsportsmanlike conduct under **Rule 75**, which the **IIHF Situation Handbook** answers in the same words at **Situation 69.3** and **Situation 75.4**. ⚠️ **And bracing for a shove is not the only way to foul him — he is protected on the way back, too.** Once he has left the crease to play the puck and is trying to return, **NHL and IIHF Rule 69.4** lets an attacker be penalised for goalkeeper interference where *"the deliberate actions of an attacking player"* stop him getting there — a different foul from the give-ground duty above, turning on intent rather than on contact. [Offensive Zone Play](../systems/offensive_zone_play.md) sets out the rule and the two tables in full.
- **Going to the net front as though the screen were only a place to stand.** It is a place you are deliberately exposed: the job puts you in the shot lane at close range and upright, with a defender on your back, your eyes on the puck and your blade in that same lane to tip it, which is the reverse of everything a shot blocker is taught. That is not a reason to stop screening — it is a reason to know when the puck is about to be released, and to take that ice with your head and face protection right rather than nearly right, chin strap done up and a full cage's chin cup in contact with your chin rather than hanging below it ([Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md)). [Playing Without the Puck](../hockey-iq/playing_without_the_puck.md) sets the screener and the blocker side by side and gives the limits of what is known about the injury risk of each.
- **Clearing the net front by moving a body across your own goalmouth.** Whichever way is easier is not the safety question. That path has a steel goal frame at one edge and your own goaltender at the other, and the player travelling it is going sideways or backwards and cannot see either of them — and USA Hockey writes the goal post into the head-first spinal-injury mechanism alongside the boards, at a speed it says can be walking pace. **Take them toward the corner instead.** That direction is coaching caution drawn from the hazard rather than a rule anyone writes; what the rules reach is the worst version of it, and none of them at two minutes — a major plus a game misconduct under **USA Hockey 608(b)**, which reaches a check from behind that *"recklessly endangers an opponent, or causes them to go head first into the boards or goal frame"* — its first limb carrying no board or frame condition at all; a **match penalty** under **Hockey Canada 7.5(c)**, which does carry one, for the same hit *"into the boards or goal frame, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*; and no minor option for checking from behind at all in either the NHL's book or the IIHF's (**43.2** in both — the NHL's *"[t]here is no provision for a minor penalty for checking from behind"*, the IIHF's *"no option to award a minor penalty for 'Checking from Behind'"*). **And the hazard is not scoped to checking games even though the walk-out is** — a legal box-out moves a body along the same path. ⚠️ **And the same mistake has a second half — lifting them off their edges to move them.** A player whose skates are off the ice cannot brace and cannot choose what meets the post first, so drive from under the armpit with their skates staying down. **That one is coaching caution too: no book here writes a penalty for the lift on its own.**
- **Standing up in a board battle.** Get low — wide base, bent knees, body between opponent and puck. The lower player usually wins — not always, and a big enough strength gap still wins, but your base is the part you control and standing tall throws it away. That leverage beats strength is coaching consensus rather than a measured law.
- **Forgetting your feet exist.** In a real battle your stick is tied up and your skates are free. Kick the puck out — at the puck, never at a player: NHL 49.3 makes kicking or attempting to kick an opponent a match penalty whether or not an injury occurs, USA Hockey 627(b), Hockey Canada 7.1(c)(iii), PWHL 49.3 and CARHA 48(c) — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — reach a match penalty too, and under the IIHF — which writes none — the referee may assess a major, with the game misconduct automatic once they do. **No book here makes it a two-minute mistake.** And if you cannot see the puck, do not swing at where you think it is.
- **Putting a leg behind an opponent in a battle.** It is the shortest distance there is from a legal box-out to the most heavily punished foul in the book. **No book here makes slew-footing a two-minute mistake:** a mandatory match penalty under NHL 52.2 with no lesser tier, a major plus an automatic game misconduct under IIHF 52.2, and a major plus a game misconduct at the floor under USA Hockey 639(b) — whose Casebook says at Rule 639, Situation 5 that *"[a] minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing."* **Hockey Canada's double minor (8.8(a)) is a floor, not a ceiling** — 8.8(c) reaches a match penalty on the degree of violence and mandatorily where it injures. Keep the leg in front of you.
- **Pinning the player rather than the puck in a corner or a board battle.** The contact is legal because the *puck* is what you are going for; make the *player* the object of it and you lose the protection the books actually write. **One book carves out an exception and it is narrow:** Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.5(a) permits *"[p]inning a player to the boards at low speed and with minimal impact … even if contact is initiated from behind"* — but the same interpretation opens by confining it to accidental contact *"as a result of playing the puck"* that is *"marginal in nature and does not endanger the safety of the opponent"*, and it excepts *"Rule 7.3 — Body-checking, as applicable"*, which is U13 and below, all female hockey, and any division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved — so it is not available to any of those players at all. **USA Hockey Rule 604, Note 1** counts *"physically forcing the opponent off the puck and with no effort to legally play the puck"* as an illegal body check — a minor under **604(c)** at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's and all non-check adult classifications, with **604(d)** a major plus a game misconduct for reckless endangerment and **604(e)** a match penalty — and **Hockey Canada 7.3(a)** writes the same minor for a player who *"intentionally body-checks, bumps, shoves, or pushes any opposing player"* in the divisions Rule 7.3 names. **And in IIHF women's hockey the pin itself is barred, not merely the push:** IIHF Rule 101.1 does not allow two or more players competing for possession to use the boards *"to make contact with an opponent to eliminate her from the play, push her into the boards, or pin her along the boards"* — a minor, or a major (5') plus an automatic game misconduct, which In-House Rule 101 turns into an ejection *"in all cases"* in Britain. Go for the puck rather than the player: that offends none of the four.
- **Second man diving into the pile.** Take the space instead — seal the outlet pass and be first to the loose puck. Otherwise it is two teammates tangled with one opponent while a third opponent takes the puck.
- **Blocking a shot on a 2-on-1.** Your job is the pass. Let the goalie have the shooter.
- **Blocking a shot from a screening position.** Either commit fully and take the shot, or get out of the lane and let your goalie see it — never stand half in the way. A deflection off a body the goalie could not see is a goal.
- **Turning sideways or lifting a leg to block.** Stay square to the puck with both skates on the ice and your padded surfaces front-on — shin guards, thigh, hip. Either one exposes the unpadded parts of you and blinds you to the puck.
- **Bending forward to get your hands behind your shin pads while you are still on your feet.** That hand position belongs to a block taken on the ice. Reaching for it standing folds you at the waist and drops your head into the low shot lane — the one thing every part of this technique exists to keep it out of. **On your feet the hands go behind your body, backs of the gloves out**; behind the shin pads is what you have once you are down. [Defender](../positions/defender.md) sets out going to the ice.
- **Winding up from across the ice to deliver a check.** Arrive with speed you already had rather than speed you built for the hit — if you need extra strides to get there, you do not have the gap, so angle them off instead. **USA Hockey**, **Hockey Canada** and **CARHA** all put a stride count in writing, and **they are not all the same count** — USA Hockey (Standard of Play — Body Checking, and Rule 607) penalises taking *"more than two strides"*, and CARHA Rule 52(a)'s Note draws the same line — *"If more than two steps or strides are taken, it shall be considered a charge"* (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only), where the count picks the charging tier and never whether you may hit at all, because that book has no checking division — while Hockey Canada (Rule 7.4) defines charging as building up speed by taking *"two or more strides"*. So the two strides that are your ceiling under USA Hockey are already the offence under Hockey Canada. **IIHF Rule 42.1 — the British test** — names the same act without a count, penalising a player who *"builds up speed by taking multiple strides immediately prior to making contact."* The NHL's Rule 42.1 sets no number at all and judges charging on "distance traveled" instead. Anything more than a couple of strides of build-up is charging in any book. And for an **open-ice** check USA Hockey's own coaching manual is tighter still — one stride, or you do not have the gap. **And charging is not capped at a minor in any book:** NHL 42.2/42.3/42.4, USA Hockey 607(a) with no bare minor and 607(e) a match, Hockey Canada 7.4(a)/(b)/(c) — the IIHF again stopping at a major plus a game misconduct, and CARHA 52(a) making a major plus a game misconduct mandatory, anywhere on the ice, once the charge injures somebody.
- **Checking with your hands or forearms.** Hit with the shoulder or the hip, or do not hit. A hand or a forearm in a check is not a lighter version of a legal one; it is outside the definition of a legal check altogether. **USA Hockey's Glossary and its Declaration of Player Safety, Fair Play & Respect** both write, in the same words, that *"[t]he use of the hands, forearm, stick or elbow in delivering a body check is unacceptable and not within the guidelines of a legal body check"*, and the **IIHF glossary** — the British reader's — allows a legal check *"only with the trunk of the body (hips and shoulders)"*. **That is two books of the four saying it in terms**, the NHL writing no positive definition of a legal check and Hockey Canada prohibiting rather than defining. ⚠️ **Do not read USA Hockey Rule 640 Note 2 as pricing it at a roughing minor**, either: it names the foul a careless or reckless hand or arm in a check gets *called* under, and the check was already illegal before the carelessness.
- **Assuming a hit is safe because the player was facing you and had not turned.** Hit the trunk and nothing above it — if the only hit available would find their head, take the puck or the ice instead. Facing you does not make contact with their head legal, and there is no clean version of that hit to find: **IIHF Rule 48.1** — *"There is no clean check to the head or neck… This rule supersedes all similar actions regarding hits to the head and neck."* **USA Hockey Rule 620** goes wider than the NHL's main-point-of-contact test, reaching any contact to the head, face or neck with any part of the body, equipment or stick, and its Standard of Play puts the onus on the checker *"regardless as to size differential, to not make contact in the head/neck area of the opponent."* A shoulder that arrives from the front and finds a head is still the offence. **The six books read here split several ways on the player whose head is low when the check arrives, and two of them can penalise *you* — Hockey Canada with no location or consequence condition, under its Rule 8.7, and the IIHF only near the boards and only where your crouch causes an opponent to tumble over you, under its Rule 44.1.** **The IIHF writes the narrow version, and its trigger is carrying the puck with your head down.** IIHF Rule 48.1 provides that a player who bodychecks an opponent *"who is skating with the puck with their head down in the direction of the Player and does not use an upward motion or drive their body up into the opponent, shall not be penalized for an 'illegal check to the head'."* Even under the IIHF book the checker is still obliged not to target your head; what that book declines to penalise is only the square hit with no upward motion, on a player who has the puck and is skating **toward** the checker — so a head-down skater hit from the side or the blind side, or hit without the puck, is outside that sentence altogether. The IIHF book attaches no penalty of its own to the player carrying the puck with their head down. **The NHL and the PWHL write no such carve-out, but neither is silent on your posture:** **NHL Rule 48.1(ii)** weighs, in deciding whether the head contact was avoidable, *"[w]hether the opponent put himself in a vulnerable position by assuming a posture that made head contact on an otherwise full body check unavoidable"*, and **PWHL Rule 48.3(i)** writes the same test — there as a ground for reducing a major to a minor on automatic video review. The IIHF lists that factor as well. **So the difference is flat exculpation against weighed factor, not protection against none.** **CARHA writes neither**: its Rule 50 makes accidental head contact a minor and intentional head contact a double minor or, at the referee's discretion, a major plus a game misconduct, and its Rule 50(c) tops that with a match penalty for a player who *"deliberately attempts to injure or deliberately injures an opponent under this rule"* — with no posture clause of any kind. ⚠️ **Read the sentence immediately above that carve-out before you rely on it, because it is the one that constrains you:** IIHF 48.1 provides that *"When a Player is skating with their head up, whether they are in possession of the puck and may reasonably be expecting impending contact, an opponent does not have the right to hit them to the head or neck."* **A head that is up fails the carve-out's own condition, so the hit is outside it altogether** — and an opponent who can see you coming has not thereby given you their head. **Hockey Canada goes further in both directions, and on a different trigger — ducking to avoid the check rather than carrying the puck head down.** **The checker's amnesty is junior and senior only; Rule 8.7's crouching prohibition carries no division scope**, so the ducker is exposed in every division. Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) reads: *"In Junior and Senior divisions, if a player ducks to avoid a check and, as a result, is hit in the head area with an otherwise legal check, this will not be considered Head Contact and no penalty will be assessed. The referee may assess a penalty to the player who ducks to avoid a check under Rule 8.7 — Clipping, if appropriate."* Hockey Canada Rule 8.7 states that *"Players may not crouch down to avoid being bodychecked"*, and Hockey Canada Rule 8.7(a) makes that a Minor penalty and 8.7(b) and 8.7(c) reach a major plus a game misconduct and a match penalty — and because Rule 8.7 carries no division scope, ducking out of a check can put **you** in the box in **any** Hockey Canada division. ⚠️ **What changes with the division is the checker's exposure, not the ducker's.** In Hockey Canada junior and senior, Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) tells the referee that a check which catches a ducking opponent's head *"will not be considered Head Contact and no penalty will be assessed"* — the checker skates away. Below junior and senior that interpretation does not apply and Rule 7.6 governs — *"Regardless of the circumstances, players must be penalized for contact with an opponent's head, face, or neck at all times."* So in Hockey Canada minor and female hockey the ducker is still exposed under Rule 8.7 **and the checker must be penalised too**. **That Hockey Canada interpretation draws its own line, and it is the part to hold on to:** it *"does not apply to a player who is in a vulnerable position as the result of a legitimate hockey play (e.g. shooting, making or receiving a pass, etc.)"* — a head that is low because you are shooting or taking a pass is not a duck, and under Hockey Canada the checker still answers for it. **No such exception was found in USA Hockey's Playing Rules, nor anywhere in Hockey Canada's rules, in any division — Rule 7.6 and Rule 7.7 both, with their Interpretations.** USA Hockey Rule 620's Note puts the onus on the checker *"to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger of potential injury"*, and USA Hockey defines vulnerable or defenceless as *"unaware, unprepared, or unsuspecting of an impending hit"* — which is what a head-down carrier is. The Hockey Canada Rule 7.6 preamble — the head-contact rule that governs minor and female hockey, quoted earlier in this bullet — is flatter still, penalising head contact *"regardless of the circumstances"*; and Hockey Canada's ducking interpretation is confined by its own words to *"Junior and Senior divisions"*, so a minor or female player under Hockey Canada has no equivalent. *(That absence is a search of the USA Hockey Official Playing Rules, of the separate USA Hockey Rules and Casebook, of the Hockey Canada Playing Rules and of the CARHA rule book. The Casebook writes no such exception either, and its four Rule 620 situations run the other way — the only head contact they excuse is accidental contact between two players getting up after a fall (Situation 2). **Its Rule 607, Situation 2 goes further and points at the checker**, though read both halves of it: an opponent *"skating up the ice with their head down"* is there the *"vulnerable or defenseless"* player, and the check on them is charging **because** the checker *"traveled a great distance at full speed for the purpose of punishing"* them — it is the distance and the speed that make the call, not the head-down position on its own. No separate Hockey Canada casebook is held here, so the Hockey Canada half rests on its Playing Rules and the Interpretations printed in them.)* So under USA Hockey a square hit that finds a bent-over head is still your foul, at minimum a minor plus a misconduct (USA Hockey Rule 620(a)) and a mandatory major plus game misconduct where the contact is careless or reckless (USA Hockey Rule 620(b)) — **and where the force was excessive and meant to intimidate or punish, its Casebook makes the match penalty mandatory rather than optional, in the rule's "may" against the Casebook's "must" (Rule 620, Situation 4).** Do not plan on being the exception in any book, in either direction. Note which way Hockey Canada's exception points, and how far down it reaches. Keep your head up: under Hockey Canada, ducking is the version that can cost *you* the penalty. **And the penalty is not the reason not to duck.** A chin tucked to the chest removes the neck's natural curve and straightens the cervical spine, which is how a head-first collision produces a fracture — that is anatomy, not a rule, so it holds whether or not your book penalises the duck. The clipping rules on ducking are covered [above](#do-not-turn-your-back-to-an-oncoming-checker-near-the-boards).
- **Checking a player who is already in a battle with a teammate.** Leave that battle to your teammate and take the space instead — seal the outlet pass and be first to the loose puck rather than adding a third body to a pile. **USA Hockey defines them as defenceless** in so many words — its **Declaration of Player Safety, Fair Play & Respect** reads *"[w]hen two or more players are physically engaged for control of the puck along the boards, they are considered to be vulnerable or defenseless"* — and says the check "must be penalized". ⚠️ **The playing rule is wider than that sentence: the Declaration's definition is the half scoped to the boards, and the numbered rule is scoped nowhere, in either limb.** **Rule 640(e)** is a minor for a body check on an opponent *"who is physically engaged for possession of the puck with one or more other players"*, and its Note deems those players *"vulnerable or defenseless and are not eligible to be body checked"* — **naming no location**, and USA Hockey's Glossary puts the net front in terms, defining **Physical Engagement** to include *"opposing players competing for the puck in front of the goal or along the boards"* — so it reaches a net-front battle as much as a wall one, and **no classification** either, unlike 640(b) — and **640(g)** takes it to a major plus a game misconduct, **640(h)** to a match penalty. **The IIHF book has no board-battle clause but reaches the same hit by another route:** its glossary makes a player who has neither possession nor preparation for the hit *"vulnerable"*, and *"[a] bodycheck to an opponent who is vulnerable is automatically considered reckless even if that bodycheck would be considered legal to a non-vulnerable opponent"* — and reckless is the trigger for the major-and-game-misconduct tier. So under the British book it is not a free hit either. Only the **NHL** rulebook leaves a board battle purely to Boarding's judgement — Rule 41.1 reaches "a defenseless opponent" without a dedicated puck-battle clause of its own; away from the boards, its Interference doctrine carves out a legal "battle for the puck," and how far that reaches a net-front hit on an engaged player is not settled here.
- **Retaliating after the whistle.** Take the hit and skate away. You cannot count on the officials having seen the first one, and USA Hockey rewards *not* retaliating explicitly (Rule 615(a)).
- **Skating in to help a teammate who is already in a scrum.** The bench mistake below catches the player who was *off* the ice. This one catches the player who was on it, and it is the likelier of the two, because it feels like loyalty rather than like a penalty. **The third man in draws a game misconduct at the referee's discretion under NHL Rule 46.16 and IIHF Rule 46.14** — and both books extend it to the subsequent players who elect to intervene, so the fourth and fifth in are not clear either. ⚠️ **The two exceptions are not the same size, and neither is something you can plan around from outside a fight:** the NHL's applies *"except when a match penalty is being imposed in the original altercation"*, while the IIHF's needs an aggressor penalty in the original altercation **and** the referee's judgement that you came *"to stop the ongoing altercation with an 'unwilling combatant' and not to start a secondary altercation."* **And under USA Hockey you do not have to touch anybody to be penalised for arriving:** Rule 615(a) writes a minor for a player who *"drops their stick and/or removes their glove(s) during an altercation and is not a participant in the original altercation"*, and adds a game misconduct if you are deemed *"the instigator of a subsequent altercation"* — while its Note counts *"[a] punch, or an attempted punch, thrown by any player in the direction of an opponent, regardless as to whether contact is made"* as fighting, which 615(a) prices at a major plus a game misconduct. ⚠️ **And coming to break it up is nobody's excuse — three books name the peacemaker as the offence:** CARHA **59(b)** puts a game misconduct on a player *"acting as a peacemaker"* in a fight, Hockey Canada **7.10(e)(ii)** on one who *"acts as a peacemaker"*, and USA Hockey's Casebook answers the question by name at **Rule 615, Situation 24** — a player *"first to enter an altercation, acting only as a peacemaker"* is caught by 615(d) anyway, because *"[r]egardless of the reason, the first player to enter any altercation shall be assessed a game misconduct penalty."* **Those three are mandatory where the NHL's and the IIHF's third-man game misconduct is discretionary**, though the triggers differ — CARHA's and Hockey Canada's reach any player who joins in, USA Hockey's only the first — and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 6 needs one fighting penalty already assessed. **The NHL's and the PWHL's discretionary rules ask nothing about why you came either** — the only peacemaker waiver written in any of the six books read here is the 2026/27 IIHF book's, on that aggressor-penalty condition. Hands down, gloves on, skate away and let the officials sort it out. Full tiers in [Fighting](#fighting--and-why-the-amateur-rules-are-much-harsher-than-the-nhls).
- **Coming off the bench, or out of the penalty box, because it has kicked off.** Stay put until the referee enters the crease or signals a face-off — whatever it looks like out there, and whether your penalty is up or not. **Two beliefs get players ejected here, and both are wrong under USA Hockey.** *"Only the first man off gets it"* — its Casebook says the major plus game misconduct goes to *"any player leaving the bench during an altercation, not just the first player to do so"* (Rule 629, Situation 2). And *"my penalty was up, so I was entitled to go"* — **Situation 1 penalises the player who left the box and deliberately skated away from the fight to his own bench**, because the stoppage that released him was the altercation itself. **The prohibition is in every book read here, and these four price it differently:** the NHL's and the IIHF's automatic game misconduct is for the first *or second* player off (NHL 70.3 and 70.6; IIHF 70.3), Hockey Canada 10.4(e) gives the first a double minor plus a game misconduct and a later player a game misconduct only if they are penalised themselves, and USA Hockey 629(a) reaches everyone who leaves — a major **plus** a game misconduct, so your team kills five minutes short-handed with you already gone (Rule 403(a)). ⚠️ **And "first or second" is the players'-bench tier only — out of the penalty box it saves nobody.** NHL 70.4 and IIHF 70.4, each restated at 70.6, put a minor plus a game misconduct on *any* penalised player who leaves the box during a stoppage and during an altercation, and NHL 70.10 suspends for five games any player off the penalty bench who *"is not the first player to do so"*. **The third man out of the box is caught in every book read here** — CARHA's 70(c) game misconduct reaches any other player off either bench and says in terms that such a player *"would not have to be the first to leave a bench"*, and the PWHL's 72.6 writes the same penalty-box paragraph as the NHL's. ⚠️ **And if you play in the EIHL there is a fifth price none of those four writes:** its Casebook adds an automatic suspension of *"the minimum of three"* games to a player who leaves either bench *"to join an altercation"*, on top of whatever the referee gives you on the night. The tiers are in [Leaving the bench or the box](#leaving-the-bench-or-the-box-during-an-altercation).
- **Taking your own helmet off when it gets heated — or reaching for theirs.** Chin strap done up, hands down, skate away — your helmet stays on, and theirs is never yours to touch.
