The televised version of this is harmless: NHL Rule 46.6 and IIHF Rule 46.12 (46.6 in the 2026/27 book) put a minor for unsportsmanlike conduct on removing your own helmet before a fight and expressly nothing on *"[h]elmets that come off in the course of and resulting from the altercation"*. **Under USA Hockey the same act is a match penalty, *"(for all age classifications)"*, and it does not need a fight** — Rule 615(c), applied by its Casebook to your own helmet *during* an altercation as well as before one, in *"any altercation where at least one player will receive a penalty"*. **Hockey Canada makes it a Gross Misconduct** under Rule 10.6(f). **And a chin strap loose enough for more than one finger is a game misconduct of its own if the helmet then comes off.** The tiers are in [Helmets in an altercation](#helmets-in-an-altercation--keep-yours-on-and-never-touch-theirs).
- **Getting a hand on an opponent's cage.** Hands down. It comes out of a scrum as a reflex, and it is not priced like one. **USA Hockey 622(b)** assesses *"a major plus a game misconduct penalty"* to a player who *"uses their hand to rub, grab or hold the facemask of an opponent"*, and **622(c)** adds that a match penalty for reckless endangerment *"may also be assessed for grabbing or holding the facemask."* **Rule 622 is on Rule 411's aggressive-infractions list** — as *"Holding an Opponent (Grabbing Facemask)"* — so that major counts on the season tally, where a third costs three further games. **Hockey Canada does not file it under holding at all**, but under **Rule 7.1 – Attempt to Injure or Deliberate Injury**: a double minor at 7.1(a) even with *"a minimal degree of violence"*, a major plus a game misconduct at 7.1(b) on the degree of violence and mandatory where the grab injures, and a match penalty at 7.1(c). **And your own book prices it too if you play under the NHL's rules or the IIHF's** — **75.2** in each names *"grabbing hold of a face mask"* as unsportsmanlike conduct and assesses a minor, with the referee directed on to **Rule 21** where injury results or the force was serious and severe. **This is a different offence from taking a helmet off**, and it does not need an altercation to be under way. [The habits that keep you out of the box](#the-habits-that-keep-you-out-of-the-box) collects the rest of them.
- **Leaning cage-to-cage in a scrum, and thinking it is not head-butting because nobody swung.** Keep your head out of theirs — back it away rather than leaning into the contact. **Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 7.1(a) uses that exact picture as its example of the double minor** — *"helmet-to-helmet or facial-protector-to-facial-protector contact, without the use of excessive force"* — and the EIHL Casebook's Rule 47 examples reach a player who *"pushes his head with forces against an opponent's head, helmet, or visor"*, and a player who only *fakes* it. ⚠️ **And there is no four-minute option under USA Hockey at all:** Rule 619(a) is *"a major plus a game misconduct penalty"* for head-butting, with a match penalty available at 619(b).
- **Sending a player back out after a head knock because they were not knocked out — or once they say the headache has gone.** Loss of consciousness is not required for a concussion and is not typical of one, and CRT6 bars a return to any activity risking head contact, a fall or a collision until the player has been assessed medically, *"even if the symptoms resolve"*. Suspected concussion means off the ice, no return that day, **and a medical professional before they play again.**
- **Letting them drive home, or sending them home on their own.** CRT6 says a player with a suspected concussion should not be sent home by themselves — *"[t]hey need to be with a responsible adult"* — and should not drive *"until cleared to do so by a healthcare professional"*. In adult hockey this is the instruction that gets skipped, because it happens after everyone has left the rink.
- **Helping up a player who may have hurt their neck.** The most common and most damaging mistake made after a spinal impact. Do not move them and do not let them move. Call for medical help, check airway and breathing, reassure them, keep them warm and immobile — head and neck especially — and leave the helmet on. See [the on-ice spinal-injury response above](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice).

## Check yourself

*Answer each question before you go on — a real attempt, not a guess. Then go
and check, because the checking is the part that does the work. Producing an
answer and getting it wrong is worth more than recognising a right one, but only
when the right answer follows: the section named with each question, and the Key
Takeaways after them, are where you get it. Where the answer lives in another
document, that is named too.*

1. What is the difference between body contact and body checking, which of the two
   is legal in every league at every age, and what does "non-check" therefore not
   mean? *(§1. Which Contact Rules Are You Actually Playing Under?)*

2. You play in Britain and want to know the age at which body checking becomes
   legal. What do the IIHF Rule Book and the In-House Rules say about it, and
   where does the answer actually live? *(§If you play in Britain)*

3. You are closing on a puck carrier heading for the wall. Name the four things
   you are managing in the angle, and say why you must not cross over in the last
   few strides. *(§2. Angling — the four things you are managing)*

4. Contact is coming and you are near the boards. Turning your back feels like
   protecting yourself — why is it the opposite, and what are the three
   cases you should be aiming for instead, best first? *(§6. Do not turn your back
   to an oncoming checker near the boards)*

5. You want to take away a net-front player's tip and rebound. Why is a
   cross-check the wrong tool even with your blade on the ice — and where exactly
   on their stick must a lift be taken instead, and why there? *(§The cross-check
   problem, and §3. The stick lift)*

6. A teammate is already tangled with an opponent along the wall and you arrive
   third. Why is that hit not available to you? Give the route each of the two
   books takes to get there. *(§Boarding, and the player who cannot see you coming)*

7. A player is down after a hit and may have hurt their neck. What is the most
   common and most damaging thing bystanders do, and what are your actual steps in
   order? *(§If it happens anyway — a suspected neck injury on the ice)*

8. Your league does not permit checking and you still want to defend properly.
   What does Rule 56.1 entitle you to do with your body, and what are the two
   things that turn all of it into a penalty? *(§4. Body Position as Defence)*

9. "The onus is on the checker." Which rulebooks actually state that onus for
   checking from behind, and which well-known one does not state it at all? Then
   name the one thing the sentence does *not* mean for the player delivering the
   hit, and the one thing it does not mean for the player receiving it — and say
   why neither changes what you should do.
   *(§Checking from behind, and what the onus does and does not mean, and §6. Do not turn your back to an
   oncoming checker near the boards)*

10. Your penalty has just expired, play stops because a fight has broken out,
    and you step out of the box and skate to your own bench without going near
    it. What does USA Hockey assess you, and what has to happen before leaving
    stops being the offence? Then say how the answer differs in the other three
    books. *(§Leaving the bench or the box during an altercation)*

11. You are setting a screen in front of the net. Say where your skates go and
    why the blue paint is not the boundary; say what a skate on the red crease
    line costs you under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada, and which book answers
    the same play in two directions. Then say what you must do the moment the
    goalkeeper leans into you to establish position, and what holding your
    ground costs. Then say which of the five books writes no bare-minor rung
    for a braced shove into a goaltender at all, and how far the "privileged
    area" that rule is keyed to actually reaches — whether it reaches the corners, and what being outside it does *not* mean for the goaltender standing there. Then say which book stops leaving the shove to the referee once the contact becomes a charge or a cross-check in the crease. Last, name the one screening act that is a minor with no contact and no crease involved at all. *(§9. Net-Front Battles —
    Attacking the net front; Where you may stand when you screen;
    What it costs to touch the goaltender)*

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Find out whether your league permits body checking before you play a shift, get the answer in writing, and check the game in front of you as well as the age group.** Body *contact* is legal everywhere bar the CARHA adult book; body *checking* is not — and where it is barred, a check is **not** a two-minute mistake: under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada the ladder runs minor, major plus a game misconduct, and match penalty. **Age alone never settles it in any book.** USA Hockey Rule 604(a) lets a local governing body prohibit checking in any classification, so a nominally checking class tells you what the national rule allows rather than what your association has done with it; **604(b) then makes any fixture against a non-checking team a no-checking game whatever your own classification says — so check the game, not the age group**; Hockey Canada also bars it in any other division a Member has approved, and does not publish the list; and one USA Hockey prohibition sits outside Rule 604 altogether with no age or classification limb — body checking is *"prohibited during all Blind Hockey games"*, on rosters the book allows to be all-adult. **One adult book settles it in print and the answer is no** — the CARHA rule book has no checking division, and a CARHA major for a body check ends your night and costs you the next game (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). **In Britain you look it up in a different document, and it matches neither North American line.** Neither the IIHF Rule Book nor the In-House Rules states a body-checking age; your competition's *Rules of Competition* carry it, and the British junior line falls between U12 and U14 rather than at USA Hockey's 12U or Hockey Canada's U13. ⚠️ **Only four of IHUK's *Rules of Competition* were read for this guide — junior, U10, NIHL 1 and 2, WNIHL — and "IHUK runs my league" does not answer it**, because IHUK's In-House Rules reach competitions none of those four covers. **Women's hockey has two layers and the tighter one is the one to plan around:** the rule book permits a restricted form of bodychecking, and your league may forbid it outright, as WNIHL does. ⚠️ **And whatever your league allows, it does not start at the warm-up** — that is inside the rules in every book here, and under the IIHF a warm-up fight is a major plus an automatic game misconduct on the night. Until you have an answer, **do not hit anyone.** Every division, rule number and penalty ladder is in [Which Contact Rules Are You Actually Playing Under?](#1-which-contact-rules-are-you-actually-playing-under), the ladder again in [Delivering a Legal Body Check](#5-delivering-a-legal-body-check), and the British detail in [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).
2. **Angling is legal in every league, and it is how good defenders take the puck without hitting anyone.** Steer opponents with your route, not your body: take away the middle, keep your momentum heading back toward your own net, close under control, and arrive stick-on-puck. **Then hold the lane you chose.** Leaving your skating lane to make the contact, or adding an arm, shoulder or hip once you arrive, is what turns a legal angle into a body check (Hockey Canada Rule 7.3; USA Hockey Casebook, Standard of Play Situation 15) — and an illegal hit under IIHF Rule 101.1 in women's hockey, which makes the foul the move itself. **And if your league runs the CARHA book** — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — **49(a) reaches a player who *"does not avert body contact with an opponent"***, so ask before you decide the seal at the end of the angle is free.
3. **Stick on the puck is legal; stick on their hands or body is a penalty — and the stick lift is the best application of it.** Take the lift **up rather than sideways, and below their bottom hand**: at or near the hands the IIHF's Rule 55.1 reaches it on the location alone — subject to an unresolved carve-out in that same rule, below — and USA Hockey and Hockey Canada reach it where it stops the opponent playing the puck. Low is the answer under every book here either way. **The NHL is not silent on the hands either, and the slashing route is not the NHL's alone** — its hooking rule writes no hands sentence, and neither do the PWHL's or CARHA's, but NHL Rule 61.1 makes a forceful chop on or near them **slashing** unless it is an attempt to play the puck, which a clean lift for the puck is; IIHF 61.1 and PWHL 63.1 are that sentence word for word and USA Hockey 634 a shortened form of it with no *"attempt to play the puck"* let-out at all. **And a chop is never the cheaper option:** it can reach a **major plus a game misconduct with nobody injured** in every book read here (NHL 61.3 with 61.5, IIHF 61.3, PWHL 63.3 with 63.5, USA Hockey 634(b), Hockey Canada 9.3(b), and CARHA 80(a) with Rule 30(a), which makes that major an ejection), where a hooking major needs an injury under the NHL (55.3) and Hockey Canada (8.2(b)) but not under the IIHF (55.3) or USA Hockey (623(b)), whose hooking rules reach that same tier for recklessly endangering an opponent. Taken low it costs far less penalty risk than a poke check or a body check, **but not none**. **And one lift is worth passing on:** against an opponent bent low over the puck their face is down at stick height, and a lift that misses and catches it is high-sticking however low your own hands were — the reference is the waist of the player you hit, not yours (NHL 60.3; IIHF 60.3 in the 2026/27 book; IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27, Situation 60.13) — and it is not capped at the double minor either (IIHF 60.4; USA Hockey 621(b)) — **and a fifth book on disk starts above it**, CARHA Rule 62(b) making contact above an opponent's shoulders a **major** whether accidental or not and whether or not anyone is injured, with a **game misconduct** on top where it was intentional or where an accidental one injures, in CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only. Take that one under the shaft, or leave it alone. Two things people get wrong here are book-specific rather than laws of hockey: *"stick on their stick is legal"* is the NHL's, the IIHF's and the PWHL's rule and nobody else's, and there only while you are checking, and the folklore that a blade on the ice makes a two-handed shaft shove legal is **USA Hockey Rule 609's wording, absent from every other book here** — and a cross-check is not a two-minute foul in any of them either. The rule numbers and the divergences are in [Stick Checks](#3-stick-checks--legal-effective-and-under-used), and what actually gets called is in [What actually gets called](#what-actually-gets-called).
4. **You are entitled to the ice you are standing on — written in full in four of the six rulebooks read here, and in a CARHA adult league to the standing half only: Rule 66(a) Note 2 lets a defender stand their ground, while 49(a) reaches leaning or bodying to keep it, and failing to avert contact.** You can block a path, stand your ground, box out and lengthen an opponent's route to the puck (NHL Rule 56.1) — as long as you do not use your stick to make yourself wider, do not use your free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab, and — the third condition in the same sentence, and the one that gets called at the wall — **do not use the position as cover for a check that would otherwise be illegal**: a legal seal does not make a late hit, an arrival from behind or a charge legal. **And unless the game you are in permits full body checking, only as long as you hold your position rather than drive them off theirs:** walking an opponent out at the net front is a push — **and a full-checking game does not make it free either**, because the screener has no puck and interference reaches it. **And the lateral-movement clause needs *both* its limbs together (NHL and IIHF 56.1)** — what they penalise is *"Moving laterally **and** without establishing body position, then making contact with the non-puck carrier"*, so contact made from a position you did establish is outside it and driving from one you never established is inside it, **which is why taking the position first is not optional**; the quoted wording is the NHL’s, the IIHF’s the same but for quotation marks round its defined terms. And **USA Hockey 625(a)(4)** names the same act — *"[a] defending player who changes their skating lane or foot speed in an effort to play the body of an opponent who is no longer in control of the puck"* — with no Competitive Contact scope in Rule 625’s title at all, unlike Rule 604. Where checking is barred it is a minor under USA Hockey Rule 604(c) and Hockey Canada Rule 7.3(a) — the latter inside the scope Rule 7.3 sets for itself, *"divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey, and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada"* — **and neither of those minors is the ceiling**: USA Hockey 604(d) reaches a major plus a game misconduct for recklessly endangering an opponent and 604(e) a match penalty, while Hockey Canada 7.3(b) reaches the same pair on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* with nobody hurt, mandatorily where the check injures, and 7.3(c) a match penalty. **In women's hockey, where checking is restricted rather than barred, driving an opponent off their position is an illegal hit under IIHF Rule 101.1** — *"[a]ny move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player will be assessed at least a minor penalty (2') for an 'illegal hit'"*, with a major (5') plus an automatic game misconduct above it.
5. **A legal check is trunk on trunk, above their knees and at or below their shoulders, on a player who has the puck, to win the puck.** **USA Hockey's book and the IIHF's state that envelope in those words; the other two do not** — the NHL defines no legal check at all, and Hockey Canada works by directional force instead. **Play the envelope anyway:** it is the stricter standard, and a check that satisfies it offends neither of those two descriptions — a comparison drawn here between the four books' definitions, not a rule any of them states, and it does not travel past those four. ⚠️ **And that is about their definitions of a legal check, not about clipping and kneeing** — separate rules in all four books, scoped to no division, and under Hockey Canada 7.8 your knee arriving is kneeing even *"on an otherwise legal hit"*. Item 6 prices both. ⚠️ **That is not the same as being allowed to throw it.** §5's bar governs every word of this — **find out whether the game you are in permits body checking at all**, because where it does not, this whole item describes a penalty. Add two habits to it. Start with **your stick blade below the knees** — that is one book's written enforcement standard rather than a universal rule, but a stick that is down means hands, elbows and shoulder all arrive where they should. And **never leave your feet**: jumping into a check is charging in its own right, however short the approach. The wind-up counts vary by book — two strides written, "multiple" unnumbered, or no figure at all — and are in [Delivering a Legal Body Check: Technique](#delivering-a-legal-body-check-technique).
6. **Never hit a player from behind, near the head, into the boards, with speed built up to punish, or after they have already passed or shot — take the puck, take the ice, or angle them off it instead.** Checking from behind, head contact, boarding and charging **bind every division, including yours if you play non-check.** The body-checking rules are the ones scoped to a division; these four are not, and where head contact is split by division as it is under Hockey Canada, both halves are covered and nobody is exempt. **Of the five acts in that instruction, the late hit is the one with a scope of its own** — USA Hockey writes its named minor *"except Adult Male Classifications"*, where a roughing penalty with no such exception takes over instead, and none of the four books caps a late hit at a minor. **Checking from behind is the one that is never just two minutes** — no book here lets you off with a bare minor for it, and under CARHA its floor takes you off the ice for the rest of the game. ⚠️ **Two more fouls bind every division and are not on that list of four: kneeing and clipping.** Lowering your body to check at or below an opponent's knees is clipping, and under Hockey Canada your knee arriving first is kneeing even *"on an otherwise legal hit"* — neither of the two minor-hockey books gives you a bare minor for it. ⚠️ **Leading with your head belongs on that list and is the one most likely to happen standing still** — a helmet-to-helmet or cage-to-cage push in a scrum counts as head-butting — Hockey Canada's own example of a double minor for it, and under USA Hockey there is no minor tier at all. **Boarding, charging and head contact do start at a minor in most books, and none of the four caps them there:** a major, a game misconduct or a match penalty sits above, on violence, injury and which book you are under — the IIHF writes no match penalty at all, so a British reader's ceiling is a major plus a game misconduct — **and a game misconduct is the balance of the game**, so that ceiling is not the lighter answer it sounds like. **And the cost need not end tonight:** every book read here lets a game misconduct reach the next game or beyond, CARHA 32(d) and PWHL 23.6 included. **The onus is on you**, and where a book does soften it, that clause is written for boarding and may not exist in the rule you are actually judged under — so play it as though it were absolute, because a spinal injury is not undone by a favourable call. The rule numbers and the floors are spread across the last four sections of §5 — head contact and charging in [Four fouls no division is exempt from](#four-fouls-no-division-is-exempt-from), boarding in [Boarding, and the player who cannot see you coming](#boarding-and-the-player-who-cannot-see-you-coming), checking from behind and the onus in [Checking from behind](#checking-from-behind-and-what-the-onus-does-and-does-not-mean), what a game misconduct costs beyond tonight in [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight), kneeing and clipping in [Delivering a Legal Body Check: Technique](#delivering-a-legal-body-check-technique), and head-butting in [Head-butting](#head-butting--and-the-net-front-push-that-counts-as-one).
7. **Never skate with your head down** — USA Hockey calls keeping it up *"the single most important thing to know and do to prevent head injuries"*, a ranking of the habit rather than of any one moment. **Taking a hit near the wall: head up, chin off your chest, never your back to the boards, never duck.** Go in on an angle. If contact is coming, get your **skates parallel to the wall**, feet apart and knees bent for a low centre of gravity, and take it on your **forearm and hip**, not your shoulder — the tip never, and no part of the shoulder if you can help it, which is the way USA Hockey hedges it. If you are already facing the wall and cannot turn, get your gloves and stick up and spread out on the glass, **arms out as a shock absorber** — USA Hockey's own drill has players meet the boards with their *"extended arms, forearms, side of body and legs"* and no head contact at all, and a shock absorber is a thing that gives. That is damage limitation, not the position you were aiming for. **The two prohibitions exist for two different injuries.** Turning your back makes the contact a hit from behind, which USA Hockey warns can cause severe head or neck injury *even from a light hit*. Ducking straightens the cervical spine, and it is the **injury** from that head-down, head-first collision with the boards or goal post that USA Hockey says *"can occur at walking speed"* — there is no speed slow enough to make it safe. Crouching to avoid a check is itself penalised in two of the six books read here — under Hockey Canada in every division, and under the IIHF only where you crouch near the boards and an opponent tumbles over you: see [Receiving a Hit Safely](#6-receiving-a-hit-safely).
8. **If a player may have hurt their head or neck, nobody moves and somebody phones.** Do not help them up. Call for immediate medical help, observe the ABCs, reassure them, keep them warm, and instruct and immobilise them so they do not move — head and neck especially. Leave the helmet on. Assume a possible spinal injury in every head injury: neck pain or tenderness is itself an ambulance. Full response in [If it happens anyway](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice). **And the commoner case — they get up.** A suspected concussion means off the ice, no return that day, **and a medical professional before they play again**, whether or not they were knocked out; do not let the player decide. ⚠️ **Symptoms clearing up is not the all-clear** — CRT6 bars a return to any activity risking head contact, a fall or a collision until the player has been assessed medically, *"even if the symptoms resolve"*. **And somebody drives them and somebody stays with them:** CRT6 says a player with a suspected concussion should not be sent home by themselves and should not drive until a healthcare professional clears them.
9. **Battles are usually won by the lower player, not the stronger one, and by the second man staying out of the pile.** Wide base, bent knees, body between opponent and puck, wall on the far side — though leverage does not always beat strength, and a large enough strength gap wins anyway. That leverage beats strength is coaching consensus rather than a measured law. **Use your skates to kick the puck free when your stick is tied up — at the puck, never at a player.** Kicking or attempting to kick an opponent is a match penalty in five of the six books here whether or not an injury occurs, and under the IIHF — Britain's book, which writes no match penalty — a major at the referee's discretion, with the game misconduct automatic once they assess it. **No book here makes it a two-minute mistake.** And if you cannot see the puck, do not swing at where you think it is. **In the corner, first man engages and stops the *puck*** — pin the puck, not the player, because the puck being the object of the contact is what keeps it legal — **and second man reads where it squirts.** **A third player checking either of you while you are tied up is not a free hit, even where the check would otherwise be legal:** USA Hockey makes a body check on an opponent *"physically engaged for possession of the puck with one or more other players"* a minor in its own right — naming no location and no classification — with a major plus a game misconduct and a match penalty above it; and the IIHF reaches the same hit by deeming a check on a vulnerable opponent automatically reckless, which is its trigger for the major-and-game-misconduct tier. Only the NHL leaves a board battle to Boarding's judgement, and how far its interference doctrine reaches the same hit away from the wall is not settled here. **And never put a leg behind them:** slew-footing is the shortest distance there is from a legal box-out to an ejection, and no book here makes it a two-minute mistake. **At the net front, control sticks before you get physical — and when you do move a body there, move it toward the corner and not across your own goalmouth**, because that path has a steel goal post at one edge and your goaltender at the other and it is the player being moved who meets the post. **Keep their skates on the ice while you move them** — a player whose edges are gone cannot brace and cannot choose what meets the post first. **Head up and chin off your chest is the instruction at both ends of it**: USA Hockey puts the goal post in the head-first spinal-injury mechanism alongside the boards, at what it says can be walking pace. The corner direction is coaching caution rather than a rule, and so is keeping their skates down, because no book here writes a penalty for the lift on its own — but the worst version of it is priced, and never at two minutes. **When you are the one screening, keep your skates off the crease's red line as well as out of the blue paint** — under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada a skate on that line takes your teammate's goal off the board. ⚠️ **Under the IIHF — Britain's book — the rule text and its own reference table answer that play in opposite directions, and nothing held here settles it.** Standing a skate's width further out costs you nothing in any of them. **If a goalkeeper leans into you to establish position in the crease, give ground at once**, then re-set the screen outside the paint: holding your spot loses the goal *and* draws a penalty whether or not it went in, and the two minor-hockey books price that shove above a bare minor. **A separate rule protects his trip home** once he has left the crease to play the puck — that one turns on whether your action was deliberate, not on whether you made contact at all. **And a screen never has to touch anybody** — though a hand, glove or stick blade held up in front of the goaltender's face is an unsportsmanlike minor on its own. **Whose corner it is in your own end is a coaching choice** — find out which system you play. Rule numbers and tiers in [Board Battles](#7-board-battles), [Net-Front Battles](#9-net-front-battles) and [Whose corner is it?](#whose-corner-is-it).
10. **Block shots close, on your feet, hands tucked in behind your body, head out of the lane — and do not block the shot on a 2-on-1 or from in front of your own goalie's eyes.** Behind your shin pads is the hand position for a block taken on the ice, not one taken standing. How much your team blocks at all is a coaching choice, so ask your goalie what they want. **Screening at the other end is that same exposure reversed on purpose** — a screen stands you in the shot lane at close range and upright, with a defender on your back, your eyes on the puck and your blade in that lane to tip — so knowing when the puck is about to be released, and having your head and face protection right rather than nearly right, are part of the job rather than extras ([Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md)); [Playing Without the Puck](../hockey-iq/playing_without_the_puck.md) gives the limits of what is known about the injury risk of each. **If you lost position, the only legal way to get it back is with your feet. Skate more, reach less.** The standard coaching account of amateur penalties is that most are late, frustrated or lazy rather than dirty — **a lens for your own game rather than a measured fact, because no published study codes *why* a penalty was taken** ([Game Management, "Frustration penalties"](../systems/game_management.md#frustration-penalties)). **And if it does boil over: gloves on, helmet on, hands down, skate away.** **None of those is decorum, and none of them is priced at two minutes.** Under USA Hockey an *attempted* punch is fighting whether or not it lands; dropping your stick or gloves in an altercation you were not part of is a penalty in its own right; taking your own helmet off, or reaching for an opponent's, is a match penalty at every age classification and needs no punch at all — a Gross Misconduct under Hockey Canada, though only an unsportsmanlike minor under the NHL and the IIHF, which is exactly why it is worth knowing which is your book; and a hand on an opponent's cage is a separate offence again that needs no altercation to be under way. Skating over to help a teammate is not free either — the third man into an altercation can take a game misconduct at the referee's discretion under both the NHL's book and the IIHF's, and so can the players who follow him in — **and breaking it up is nobody's excuse in five of the six rulebooks read here: CARHA 59(b) and Hockey Canada 7.10(e)(ii) each give a game misconduct to a player who steps in as a peacemaker — Hockey Canada's only where a fighting penalty has already been assessed — USA Hockey's 615(d) game misconduct for the first player into an altercation is applied to a peacemaker by its Casebook "[r]egardless of the reason", and the NHL's and the PWHL's third-man rules ask nothing about why you came — while the sixth, the 2026/27 IIHF book, is the only one that asks at all, and its 46.14 waiver reaches a third player in who came to stop the fight only where an aggressor penalty is being imposed in the original altercation, which is not something you can plan on from outside a fight.** **And if it kicks off while you are on the bench or in the box, stay there.** Every book read here bars leaving a bench during an altercation or a fight, and under USA Hockey it is a major plus a game misconduct for *every* player who leaves rather than only the first — including the player who steps out on an expired penalty. **The NHL's and the IIHF's "first or second player off" is their players'-bench tier and nothing more; there is no book in which the third man out of the penalty box is clear.** Wait until the referee enters the crease or signals a face-off. Tiers and rule numbers in [Playing Physical Without Taking Penalties](#12-playing-physical-without-taking-penalties).

---

*Sources — retrieved 27 July 2026:*

*Rulebooks: [NHL Official Rules 2025-2026 (PDF)](https://media.d3.nhle.com/image/private/t_document/prd/slwjuaqwmuvj5bkplixo.pdf) — **Rule 21 Match Penalties** (21.1, *"A match penalty shall be imposed on any player who deliberately attempts to injure or who deliberately injures an opponent in any manner"* — tied to no named foul, read 19 September 2026), **Rule 54 Holding** (54.1–54.4, which write a minor, a penalty shot and an awarded goal and no tier above them, read 19 September 2026), Rule 41 Boarding, 42 Charging, 43 Checking from Behind, 44 Clipping, 45 Elbowing, **46 Fighting** (46.2's aggressor definition and tier; 46.11's instigator tier — *"an instigating minor penalty, a major penalty for fighting and a ten-minute misconduct"* — with its escalation to a game misconduct for a second instigator penalty in the same game and a third in one regular season; 46.14's major; and 46.16's third-man game misconduct, including its exception, *"except when a match penalty is being imposed in the original altercation"*, all read 31 August 2026), 48 Illegal Check to the Head, **49 Kicking** (49.1's definition, 49.2's four kicked-puck clarifiers, and 49.3's match penalty), 51 Roughing, **52 Slew-footing** (52.1's two-limbed definition, ending on a player made to *"fall violently to the ice"*, and 52.2's flat, mandatory match penalty with no tier beneath it), 55 Hooking (with **55.3**, whose major requires that the player *"injures an opponent"*, and 55.4), 56 Interference, **57 Tripping** (57.1's definition — a stick, knee, foot, arm, hand or elbow placed *"in such a manner that causes his opponent to trip or fall"* — and 57.3's puck-first carve-out, which converts the penalty shot to a minor and does not remove the minor), **59 Cross-checking** (59.1's definition; 59.2 and 59.3, whose minor and major both turn on *"the severity of the contact"* and not on injury; 59.4's match penalty where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent by cross-checking"*; and 59.5, *"When a major penalty is assessed for cross-checking, an automatic game misconduct penalty shall be imposed"*, all read 30 August 2026), 60 High-sticking, 61 Slashing (with **61.3**, whose major turns on *"the severity of the contact"* and not on injury, and 61.5), 63.2(i) Delaying the Game (freezing the puck), **69 Interference on the Goalkeeper** — 69.1, the premise that position inside or outside the crease does not by itself decide the goal, its clause (1) reaching an attacker who *"either by his positioning or by contact, impairs the goalkeeper's ability to move freely within his crease or defend his goal"*, and its permission for incidental contact *"initiated outside of the goal crease, provided the attacking player has made a reasonable effort to avoid such contact"*; 69.3 on contact inside the crease; **69.4**, whose separate permission is keyed to the goalkeeper being *"in the act of playing the puck outside his goal crease"*; and **69.6**, whose neutral-zone face-off follows only a goal already disallowed — with **Reference Table 14 — Interference on the Goalkeeper Situations**, read 18 September 2026: **5 C** and **5 E**, each *"Goal is allowed"*, and **6 D**, *"Goal is disallowed. A minor penalty is not assessed (loss of goal only)"*, **70 Leaving the Bench** — 70.1's flat bar, *"No player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation"*; 70.3 and 70.6, whose game misconduct is written for *"the first or second player to leave"*; 70.4 and 70.6's minor plus game misconduct for *"[a]ny penalized player leaving the penalty bench during a stoppage of play and during an altercation"*; and 70.10's automatic ten-game and five-game suspensions for the first and second player off, all read 1 September 2026 in the 2025-2026 edition and found word for word identical in the 2024-2025 one · [NHL Official Rules 2024-2025 (PDF)](https://media.d3.nhle.com/image/private/t_document/prd/yikcdsxofkmgsrhjl3di.pdf) — **Rule 60 High-sticking only**, read against the 2025-2026 text on 31 August 2026, which is what dates the high-sticking rewrite stated above. Its **60.1** reads *"A 'high stick' is one which is carried above the height of the opponent's shoulders"* and its **60.3** *"When a player carries or holds any part of his stick above the shoulders and makes contact with his opponent's neck, face or head so that injury results"* — so the waist floor is absent from both clauses in the 2024-2025 edition and present in both in the 2025-2026 one, and the 2025/26 IIHF wording quoted above is word for word the NHL's superseded 60.1. **60.2 is identical in the two editions.** No other rule was compared across them, and no NHL edition earlier than 2024-2025 was consulted, so *when* before 2025-26 the older wording had stood is not established here. The NHL book carries no summary of rule changes and dates no rule · [USA Hockey 2025-29 Official Playing Rules](https://cdn2.sportngin.com/attachments/document/945a-3442848/2025-29_USAH_Playing_Rules.pdf) — its two **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis** Summaries, read 7 September 2026 — Body Checking (*"A player is entitled to use proper body position and competitive contact in all age classifications"*) and Restraining Fouls (*"Players are entitled to the ice they occupy as long as they are able to maintain their own foot speed and body position between opponent and puck"*), neither of which announces an age or category scope, against the **Note to Rule 604(c)** (*"simply maintaining established body position … in front of the goal or along the boards"*), which sits under a rule scoped to the Competitive Contact categories, and the **Note to Rule 625 Interference**, whose title announces none; the Declaration of Player Safety glossary (Body Checking, Competitive Contact, Angling) and its **Late Avoidable Body Check** paragraph, which is where *"[t]he concept of 'finishing the check' is an unacceptable action as it is one that is meant to intimidate or punish the opponent with no intent to gain possession of the puck"* is written — the book's own **Glossary** repeats it under *"Late Body Check"* in the wider form, *"no intent, or possibility, to gain possession"*; **Rule 603 Boarding**, whose Note states the onus flat — *"The onus is on the player delivering the check to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger"*, with no balancing sentence after it, unlike NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2, read 31 August 2026, and **read again in full on 1 September 2026** for two clauses not previously carried: the Note's **first** paragraph, which defines boarding as the action where a player *"pushes, trips or body checks an opponent causing them to go dangerously into the boards"* — the only one of the four books to name a trip, against *"checks or pushes"* in NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 — and the sentence immediately after 603(a), *"'Rolling' an opponent along the boards where they are attempting to go through too small an opening is not considered boarding"*, which likewise has no counterpart in the other three books; **Rule 602(a) Attempt to Injure or Recklessly Endanger an Opponent** (*"A match penalty shall be assessed to any player or team official who recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player or team official"* — tied to no named foul at all); **Rule 640 Unnecessary Roughness (Roughing)** — **640(b)**, the late-hit minor written *"(except Adult Male Classifications)"*; **640 Note 2**, which directs a roughing penalty for *"an avoidable body check to an opponent after the whistle or who is no longer in control of the puck"* with no classification exception; and **640(g)** and **640(h)**, the major plus game misconduct and the match penalty for reckless endangerment, both of which reach a late hit only through sub-section (b), while (d) and (e) carry no classification limit; **Rule 629 Leaving the Players' Bench or Penalty Bench** — 629(a) read in full on 1 September 2026, including the proviso that *"[s]ubstitutions made prior to the altercation shall not be penalized under this rule provided the players so substituting do not enter the altercation"* and the clause concluding an altercation *"when the referee enters the referee's crease or, in the absence of penalties, signals a face-off location"*; **Rule 404(b)**, whose game misconduct suspends the player for *"the team's next game"*; **Rule 411 Progressive Suspensions** — 411(a) read in full on 31 August 2026, including its three-, four- and five-major rungs and the seventeen-rule aggressive-infractions list, which **runs across a page break and is complete only across both pages**, its last two entries being Rule 639 and Rule 640; **Rule 615 Fighting** — 615(a), whose third paragraph reads *"A minor, double minor or major plus game misconduct penalty, at the discretion of the Referee, shall be assessed to any player who, having been struck, continues the altercation by retaliating. A player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be assessed a penalty for fighting under this rule"* — note the protection is written *"under this rule"* and so does not reach roughing or misconduct; **Rule 617(c)(1)**, which disallows a goal where *"[a]n attacking player kicks the puck directly into the goal or the kicked puck deflects off any player, including goalkeeper, prior to entering the goal"*; **Rule 627 Kicking Opponent or Puck** — 627(a) the push-off, 627(b) the match penalty, 627(c) the kicked-puck goal, which writes no stick-versus-body distinction; all read 30 August 2026. Rule 604 Body Checking (604(a)–(e) read in full on 10 September 2026, including the sentence that follows 604(a)'s classification list — *"A local governing body may prohibit body checking in any classification and is encouraged to provide Competitive Contact options at all levels of recreational play"* — which is the basis for saying an age class nominally permitting checking does not settle whether your league does; the **Glossary**, under *Age Classifications*, **Note 3** — *"Adult classifications shall include Adult non-check, Adult U.S., Adult Elite and Adult Women, and shall be governed by these rules, except where otherwise noted"* — read 10 September 2026, which is the whole of the basis for §1's statement that an adult classification is not a checking classification by default; **the book gives no count of how much adult hockey is played in each of the four, and none is claimed here**; ⚠️ **one further USA Hockey body-checking prohibition sits outside Rule 604's own structure**, in the book's *Disabled Hockey* section and so not reachable by reading Rule 604 alone — *"Body checking is prohibited during all Blind Hockey games. Body checking shall be penalized under the guidelines within Rule 604 in the Official Rules of Ice Hockey."* It writes **no age or classification limb**, and the same section provides that a Blind Hockey roster *"can comprise all youth-aged players (players under 18), all adult-aged players (18 and older), or mixed youth and adult-aged players"*, which is the whole of the basis for saying it reaches adult rosters that 604(a)'s list does not; read 2 September 2026. ⚠️ **Only the Blind Hockey body-checking provision was read** — the Sled Hockey rules carry a separate Non-Contact Player provision that this document does not describe), 608 Checking from Behind, **609 Cross-Checking** (the Note's *"with no portion of the stick on the ice"* wording, and 609(b) and 609(c) — the major plus game misconduct that *"shall be assessed"* and the match penalty that *"may also be assessed"*, both for recklessly endangering an opponent by cross-checking, both read 30 August 2026), 610(a) Delaying the Game (deliberately freezing the puck along the boards), 620 Head Contact, **621 High Sticks** (621(b), the major plus game misconduct that *"shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of high sticking"*, and 621(c), the match penalty available for the same, both read 1 September 2026; the 621 Note measures against *"the normal height of the opponent's shoulders"*, and a de-hyphenated whole-book search of the playing rules **and** the Casebook on 1 September 2026 finds *"normal"* defined in neither), **639 Tripping/Clipping/Leg Checking/Slew Footing** (Note 4's definition; 639(a), which writes the minor for the family *"except slew footing"*; 639(b), whose second sentence reads *"The minimum penalty to be assessed for slew footing is a major plus game misconduct penalty"*; and 639(c), the match penalty for reckless endangerment available *"under this rule"*), 623 Hooking (with **623(b)** and **623(c)**, the major plus game misconduct that *"shall be assessed"* for recklessly endangering an opponent by hooking, and the match penalty above it), 625(b) (its first paragraph, under which a face-off *"shall be conducted at the nearest neutral zone face-off spot any time an attacking player stands, holds their stick, or skates through the goal crease provided the puck is in the attacking zone, the attacking team has possession of the puck and the goalkeeper is in contact with the crease"*, read 18 September 2026; the position bar on a goal with an attacking player in the goal crease, and the push-in relief in its third paragraph — "if the attacking player has been physically interfered with by the actions of a defending player that causes them to be in the goal crease, play shall not be stopped and any legal goal scored shall be allowed" — read 31 August 2026; the book was read in full for a screening or goaltender-vision provision and has none), **607 Charging** — **607(c)**, *"A minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to a player who body checks or charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or privileged area"*; **607(e)**, *"A match penalty for reckless endangerment may also be assessed for charging"*; **607(b)**, *"A major penalty plus game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of charging"*; **607(d) Note 1**, which routes *"any accidental or unavoidable contact"* with the goalkeeper to interference and *"[a]ny deliberate body contact or check that is delivered to the goalkeeper"* to charging; and **607(d) Note 2**, defining the goalkeeper's *"Privileged Area"* as *"an area outlined by connecting the end zone face-off spots with an imaginary line and imaginary lines from each face-off spot running perpendicular to the end boards"*, all read 18 September 2026, 634 Slashing (its Note — *"Any forceful chop with the stick on an opponent's body or opponent's stick, on or near the opponent's hands, shall be considered slashing"* — carrying no *"attempt to play the puck"* let-out, with **634(b)** and **634(c)** matching 623(b) and (c)) · [USA Hockey Standard of Play — Body Checking 2025-29](https://cdn1.sportngin.com/attachments/document/ef49-3383370/USAH_Playing_Rules_SOP_Body_Checking_2025-29.pdf) — its **CROSS-CHECKING** heading, read 30 August 2026, whose two worked examples are the net-front shove *"in a dangerous manner to create space"* and the same shove on a player *"protecting the puck along the boards"*; the playing rule at 609 carries none. Also its **ROUGHING**, **HEAD CONTACT** and **CHARGING** headings, quoted above · [USA Hockey Official Rules and Casebook 2025-29 (PDF)](https://cdn3.sportngin.com/attachments/document/4f1a-3442850/2025-29_USAH_Playing_Rules___Casebook.pdf) — **Rule 604, Situation 1**, read 10 September 2026 — a non-league fixture where *"[b]oth coaches agree to play the game under the Body Checking category rules"*, answered *"No. Rule Reference 604(b)"*, with *"Coaches do not have the authority to decide what rules they choose to play under on any given day"* and the direction that, absent specific information from *"a USA Hockey Local Supervisor or Registrar"*, the playing rules govern; **Standard of Play, Situation 10**, on when a body check may still be finished after the puck has gone: a prior effort to gain possession, *"an arm's length plus stick length"* at the instant the puck was dumped, the check already *"in the process of being delivered"*, and minimised force — offered there as a *"general rule of thumb"*, and followed by a second and separate route to legal contact where the defender *"maintains their normal skating lane and reasonable foot speed"* — with *"one or two more strides after the puck is gone"* ruled out in terms — corroborated at **Rule 640, Situation 3** and **Standard of Play, Situation 23**, which state the same test as avoidability and give *"finishing the check"* *"no place in youth hockey"*; and **Standard of Play, Situation 2** with **Rule 623, Situation 1**, which permit the stick lift *"at any time provided they do not impede their progress"* after Situation 1 has ruled that hooking an opponent's stick is itself a penalty. Also **Standard of Play, Situations 5, 6 and 7**, which are where USA Hockey writes its free-arm permission — absent from the playing rules — and its conditions; **Standard of Play, Situation 15**, read 18 September 2026 — the angling case, which asks whether a body-checking penalty should be called where a pursuing player establishes an angle, closes the gap along the boards and the carrier is knocked into them and loses the puck, and answers *"No, provided the Team B player does not use any overt hip, shoulder or forearm action to make contact with the opponent and simply maintains their skating lane and focuses on closing the gap and gaining possession of the puck"*; **Rule 620, Situations 1–4** and **Rule 607, Situation 2**, read for any exception excusing a check on a player whose head was low, of which they have none — and **read again on 1 September 2026 for their mandatory limbs**, which the playing rule does not carry: **Situation 4**, *"Any check using excessive force for the purpose of intimidating or punishing the opponent that makes direct contact with the head area of the opponent must be assessed as a match penalty"*, and **Situation 3**, *"Any time the Referee deems the contact to the head area to be intentional or a reckless act, a major plus game misconduct or match penalty must be assessed"*, both saying *must* where 620(c) says a match penalty *"may also be assessed"*; **Rule 608, Situation 1**, read for any exception for a player who turned into the check, of which it has none; **Rule 621, Situations 1–15** — the Casebook prints fifteen, and Situations 4–15 are all 621(d) high-sticking-the-puck cases — read in full on 1 September 2026 for a windup or follow-through carve-out and for anything addressing a crouching opponent, of which they have none, and where Situation 2 rules that contact is not required at all and that *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"*; **Rule 603, Situations 1 and 2** with **Standard of Play, Situation 19**, read 1 September 2026 — Situation 2 restates the onus scoped *"[f]or USA Hockey youth games"*, asks *"was the check reckless and/or what was the vulnerability of the player being checked?"*, and runs the force-and-preparedness example both ways; **Standard of Play, Situation 19 prints the same weighing a second time**, asking instead *"was the check necessary and/or what was the vulnerability of the player being checked?"* and conditioning its no-penalty example on a checker who *"clearly attempted to gain possession of the puck"*, a condition Situation 2 does not carry; **Rule 603, Situation 3**, read 1 September 2026, which answers *"Yes"* to whether boarding may be called where a trailing player *"trips the opponent and causes them to lose control and crash into the end boards"*, and adds that *"[e]ven though a tripping penalty may also be appropriate, the boarding call is preferred to draw attention to the more aggressive infraction"*; **Rule 632, Situation 1**, read 18 September 2026, which puts a third player who *"sees the two players physically engaged and skates in their direction with the clear intent to deliver a check to the opposing player"*, asks whether the Referee should stop play, answers *"Yes"* on *"Rule Reference 632(a Note)"*, and directs that *"The Referee must stop play the instant they realize that unnecessary contact will result from allowing the play to continue"* — with a penalty to the third player if contact is made; **Rule 629, Situations 1 and 2**, read 1 September 2026 — Situation 1 assessing a major plus game misconduct to each of two players who left the penalty box on expired penalties at a stoppage caused by an altercation, including the one who *"does not enter the altercation, but skates to their players' bench"*, on the ground that *"[n]o player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation"*, and Situation 2 stating that the penalty is assessed *"to any player leaving the bench during an altercation, not just the first player to do so"*; **Rule 639, Situations 1 and 4**, read 18 September 2026 — Situation 1 answering *"No"* to whether the fouled player must fall to the ice for tripping to be assessed, on *"Rule Reference 639(Note)"*, reasoning that the rule uses the words *"trip or fall"* and that where the player does not fall *"it must be clearly obvious their progress was impeded and a competitive advantage was gained as a result of the tripping action"*; and Situation 4 ruling that where a player leaves their feet and slides into an opponent, *"a penalty for tripping must be called regardless of who gains possession of the puck"*, with the single exception *"when a player drops to their knees to block a shot and their momentum carries them into the player shooting the puck, causing them to fall"*; **Rule 639, Situation 5**, which names leg checking, clipping and slew footing as the tripping actions calling for a more severe penalty, states *"A minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing"*, and closes, after a further sentence on clipping, *"Any time one of these actions has been deemed to recklessly endanger the opponent, a match penalty must be considered"*; **Rule 627, Situations 4 and 5**, read 30 August 2026, which put the kicked-puck deflection directly — *"The puck is kicked by an attacking player, deflects off their stick and enters their opponent's goal. Should the goal be allowed?"* — and answer *"No. Rule Reference 627(c)"*, the same answer following for a deflection off a teammate; **Rule 607, Situations 4, 5 and 6**, read 18 September 2026 for contact with the goalkeeper — Situation 4 runs three rungs for charging a goalkeeper *"while in their crease or privileged area"*, from *"no penalty should be assessed"* where *"a defending player has forced the attacking player into their own goalkeeper"* and the attacker has *"clearly made every attempt to avoid the contact with the goalkeeper"*, through *"a minor plus misconduct penalty is deemed appropriate"*, up to *"the major plus game misconduct, or match penalty option"*; Situation 5 holds that a goalkeeper *"can be legally checked when outside the privileged area"* but is not *"fair game"* there, a penalty being due *"in every instance where unnecessary or avoidable contact is made with the goalkeeper – even when outside the privileged area"*; and Situation 6 answers *"Yes"* to whether the Privileged Area includes the area behind the goal line, which *"extends to the closer end boards"*; and **Rule 625, Situations 1 to 17**, read for a screening or goaltender-vision provision, of which they have none — **Situation 10** is written on a different question, whether the Referee must stop play *"every time an attacking player places their stick and/or skates in the opposing team’s goal crease"*, and answers *"No"*, since play *"should not be stopped because a player momentarily places their stick and/or skates in the goal crease, unless they interfere with the goalkeeper in any manner"*; and **Rule 621, Situations 1 to 15**, read in full on 1 September 2026 — **Situation 2** answers *"No."* to *"Must contact with an opponent occur in order for the Referee to assess a penalty for high sticking?"*, adds that *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"*, and requires the call *"[w]henever a player raises their stick above their shoulders in an attempt to intimidate an opponent or is careless in their actions"*, all of which the playing rule's 621 Note does not say; **Situation 1** routes a stick to the head *"during the process of delivering a check"* to Rule 620 Head Contact instead; **Situation 3** confirms the 621(b) and 621(c) tiers on a player who recklessly endangers an opponent with a stick while falling; and **Situations 4 to 15** are all 621(d), high-sticking the puck, and bear on none of the above. *or* — and quoting it would reproduce the book's typo while correcting it inside quotation marks would be a fabricated quotation**; a whole-book search on 1 September 2026 returns that Note as the only definition of the word — with **Interpretation 1 to 9.5(a)** (the minor where a follow-through after high-sticking the puck hits an opponent above the shoulder with no injury) and **Interpretation 2 to 9.5(a)**, whose items i–vi treat attempted restraining fouls as accidental high-sticks — item ii the attempted stick lift causing injury (a double minor) and item vi a *"pitchfork action in lifting the stick"* causing injury (a major plus game misconduct under 7.7) — read 1 September 2026, and 4.8(b)(i), the Game Ejection at three minors for *"stick infractions"*, defined there as Rules 9.1 to 9.5 and so excluding hooking, with **Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b)** on the double minor counting as one infraction and Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.1(a) confirming the point for butt-end hooking — all three read 31 August 2026 *(citation-resolution note: Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.1(a) points the reader at "Rule 4.8 (a) – Game Ejection & Game Misconduct Penalties", and the Game Ejection provision is 4.8(**b**); 4.8(a) is the Game Misconduct. This document cites the body lettering rather than reproducing the book's pointer)*, 8.5 Interference with the Goaltender (its crease bar and the carve-out that follows it, under which a goal scored by a teammate outside the crease "WILL BE ALLOWED" if the crease player stays passive; the book's only two references to a goaltender's view both reach a player in the crease), **6.6(c) and 6.10 Kicking the Puck with Interpretation 1 to Rules 6.10(b) and 6.10(c)**, read 30 August 2026 — 6.10(b) states that *"no goal can be scored as the result of a kicking motion by an attacking player"* and extends that to a deflection *"off any other player or object"*, and Interpretation 1 answers the teammate case for *"skate, body or stick"* alike with *"no goal"*, and **7.1(c)(iii) Attempt to Injure**, the match penalty for *"Attempting to kick or deliberately kicking an opponent"*, and **Rule 10.4 Leaving the Players' or Penalty Bench**, read 1 September 2026 — 10.4(b) barring a Major penalty for it outright, and 10.4(e) triggering on a *fight* rather than an altercation, with (e)(i) assessing *"a double Minor penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty"* to *"[t]he first player of either team to leave their players' or Penalty Bench"* and (e)(ii) reaching a later player only where they are *"subsequently assessed a penalty"* *(the clauses quoted in this document, and Interpretation 1 to each of the three rules, are word-for-word unchanged from the superseded 2024-2026 edition. **The Rule 8.2(b), 8.2(c), 9.2, 9.3 and 4.8(b)(i) clauses are an exception to that statement: the 2024-2026 edition is not held here and those five were not compared across editions**. Rule 7.4 did change in one respect that this document does not rely on: the blindside-hit definition moved out of a Note under 7.4(a) into a new Interpretation 2, and gained the sentences "The 90° angle of approach will be determined based on the orientation of the player's body prior to receiving the hit. The direction of travel of the player receiving the hit is not a key consideration in determining if a 'blindside' hit has occurred." The old Interpretation 2 to 7.4(b) on goaltender contact is renumbered Interpretation 3.)* · [IIHF Official Rulebook 2025/26 (Version 1.1, July 2025)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/contentimages/3_the_iihf/2025-26_iihf_rulebook_22122025-v1.pdf) — **Rule 21 Dangerous Actions**, a major plus an automatic game misconduct confined to *"an action that is not covered by the playing rules"*, the book writing no match penalty anywhere, and **Rule 54 Holding**, whose 54.1–54.4 stop at a minor, a penalty shot and an awarded goal, both read 19 September 2026; **Rule 24.8** (the penalty-shot puck-first carve-out, in the NHL's words, which likewise leaves the tripping minor standing), **41 Boarding** — 41.1 states the responsibility on the checker and then the same *"However, in determining whether such contact could have been avoided…"* balancing sentence the NHL writes, closing *"This balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule."*, read 31 August 2026 — 42 Charging, 43 Checking from Behind, 44 Clipping, **46 Fighting** (46.1, 46.3 Instigator / Initiator, 46.4 Aggressor, 46.6 Defender – Unwilling Combatant and 46.7 Third Player In, all read 30 August 2026 — and see the 2026/27 entry below, which renumbers every one of them), 48 Illegal Check to the Head or Neck, **49 Kicking** (49.2, whose clauses (I) to (IV) reproduce the NHL's kicked-puck clarifiers word for word, and 49.3), **52 Slew-footing** (52.1 in the NHL's words, and 52.2, headed "Major Penalty and Game Misconduct Penalty", on a "shall, at their discretion, assess" frame rather than the "may assess" of its neighbours, with no lesser tier beneath it and no match tier above it because the IIHF book writes none), 55 Hooking (including **55.1**, whose second sentence — *"If the stick goes against the opponent's hands / or near the opponent's hands, it shall be penalized as 'hooking'"* — has no counterpart in NHL 55.1, and is present unchanged in the 2026/27 edition; and **55.3**, whose major and automatic game misconduct turn on reckless endangerment rather than injury; both read 31 August 2026), 56 Interference, **69 Interference on the Goalkeeper** — 69.1, whose clause (I) reaches an attacking Player who *"either by their positioning or by a “relevant contact”, impairs the goalkeeper's ability to move freely within their goal crease or defend their goal"*, whose incidental-contact permission is for contact *"initiated outside of the goal crease"*, and which alone among the four books gives the referee *"the possibility to stop the game"* for an attacking Player who is in the goal crease and *"does not leave immediately"*, sending the face-off to the nearest neutral zone spot; 69.3; and **69.4**, keyed to the goalkeeper being *"in the act of playing the puck outside their goal crease"* — with **Appendix IV, Table 16 — Interference on the Goalkeeper situations**, whose **5 C** and **5 E** each read *"Goal is allowed"* and whose **6 D** reads *"Goal is disallowed. A minor penalty is not assessed (loss of goal only)"*, all read 18 September 2026 — **59 Cross-checking** (59.1's definition and 59.3, headed "Major Penalty and Game Misconduct Penalty", whose major plus automatic game misconduct turns on recklessly endangering an opponent rather than on injury, read 30 August 2026), **61 Slashing** (61.1 reproducing the NHL's hands sentence word for word, and 61.3 pairing the major with an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment), 100 Women's Hockey Age Categories, 101 Women's Hockey Specific Playing Rules, 200–202 Men's Junior Hockey, and the Definitions appendix entries for "Body Checking", "Body Contact" and "Vulnerable Position". **Rule 70 Leaving the Players' Bench or Penalty Box was read in full on 1 September 2026 in this edition and in the 2026/27 one, and the two are substantively identical on every clause quoted in this document** — 70.1's flat bar, 70.3's game misconduct for *"[t]he Player who was the first or second Player to leave the Players' bench (or penalty box) during an altercation"*, and 70.4's minor plus game misconduct for *"[a]ny penalized Player leaving the penalty box during a stoppage of play and during an altercation"*. The 2026/27 text adds potential supplementary discipline at 70.3 and exposes the coach to it as well; neither changes any of the three. *(All cited rules were read in the 2025/26 text, which is the edition used as the baseline throughout this guide and the foundation adopted by England Ice Hockey, the SIHA and the BUIHA. Rules 42, 43, 44, 46, 48, 55 and 56 were compared against the May 2025 v1.0 text and are substantively identical; Rules 100, 101 and 200–202 were not compared across the two versions. Rule 101.1 was read in full against the statement of it in §1, and the wording there is correct.)* · [IIHF Official Rulebook 2026/27 (Version 1.0, June 2026)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/downloads/rule%20book/2026-27_iihf_rule_book.pdf) — **Rule 60 High-sticking**, read in full against the 2025/26 text: **60.1** carries the NHL's *"provided their shoulders are at waist level or higher"* sentence word for word where the 2025/26 book tests a stick *carried* above the shoulders, **60.3** gains the parenthesis *"(above the height of the opponent's waist)"*, and **60.2** is identical in both. **60.4 is identical in both as well** — the major and *"automatic game misconduct penalty"* the referee may assess where a Player *"recklessly endangers their opponent by 'high-sticking'"* — compared 1 September 2026, so that escalation does not turn on which edition a reader is under. The 60.1 windup and follow-through carve-out, including the bent-over centre at a face-off, is identical in both — and that carve-out is word for word the NHL's. **Rule 41.1 Boarding was compared across the two editions on 1 September 2026 and is unchanged**, ending on the same *"This balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule."* Also **Rule 46 Fighting**, read in full against the 2025/26 text on 30 August 2026 and **renumbered throughout** — instigator 46.3 to **46.10**, aggressor 46.4 to **46.2**, third player in 46.7 to **46.14**, and 46.6 changed from *Defender – Unwilling Combatant* to *Helmets*. Two changes of substance: **46.14** replaces 46.7's blanket exception with a waiver conditional on an aggressor penalty in the original altercation **and** the referee's judgment that the third player in was *"intervening to stop the ongoing altercation with an 'unwilling combatant' and not to start a secondary altercation"*; and **46.6's retaliation sentence is gone**, the unwilling-combatant idea surviving only as a note under 46.2, 46.8, 46.10 and 46.12 waiving *"the automatic game misconduct penalty"* at the referee's discretion. The instigator and aggressor tiers are unchanged. A de-hyphenated, whitespace-flattened whole-book search puts *"few punches"* at 2 occurrences in the 2025/26 text and **0** in the 2026/27 one. Also **Rule 49 Kicking** and **Rule 59 Cross-checking**, each compared across the two editions and word-for-word identical. *(Rules 46, 49, 59 and 60 were compared across the two editions; every other IIHF rule cited in this document is cited from the 2025/26 text above and was not re-checked against 2026/27.)* · [IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27 (Version 1.0, June 2026)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/downloads/officiating%20files/situation%20handbook/2026-27_iihf_situation_handbook.pdf) — **Situations 69.3 and 75.4**, which ask in all but one word whether an attacking player may *"hold the hand/glove or stick directly in front of the goalkeeper's eyes/face with the obvious intent of impairing the ability to see the puck/play"* — the form quoted is 75.4's, and 69.3 writes *"its hand/glove"* where 75.4 writes *"the"* — and both answer, in the same words, *"No. This shall be treated as unsportsmanlike conduct and penalized with the on a stick-lift attempt that catches a bent-over opponent in the face while the striker's own stick is *"only slightly above the height of their waist"*, and the Foreword's *"in case of any
